. Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa.

Homage to the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Enlightened One.

Aṅguttaranikāye

In the Aṅguttara Nikāya

Aṭṭhakanipāta-aṭṭhakathā

The Commentary to the Book of Eights

1. Paṭhamapaṇṇāsakaṃ

1. The First Fifty

1. Mettāvaggo

1. The Chapter on Loving-kindness

1. Mettāsuttavaṇṇanā

1. The Commentary on the Discourse on Loving-kindness

1. Aṭṭhakanipātassa [V 3.0193][M 3.0193][P 4.0068] paṭhame āsevitāyāti ādarena sevitāya. Bhāvitāyāti vaḍḍhitāya. Bahulīkatāyāti punappunaṃ katāya. Yānikatāyāti yuttayānasadisakatāya. Vatthukatāyāti patiṭṭhānaṭṭhena vatthu viya katāya. Anuṭṭhitāyāti paccupaṭṭhitāya. Paricitāyāti samantato citāya upacitāya. Susamāraddhāyāti suṭṭhu samāraddhāya sukatāya. Ānisaṃsāti guṇā. Sukhaṃ supatītiādīsu yaṃ vattabbaṃ, taṃ ekādasakanipāte vakkhāma.

1. In the first sutta of the Book of Eights, 'cultivated' means attended to with care. 'Developed' means increased. 'Made much of' means done repeatedly. 'Made a vehicle' means made similar to a fitting vehicle. 'Made a basis' means made like a basis, in the sense of a foundation. 'Established' means made present. 'Familiarized' means thoroughly accumulated and practiced. 'Well undertaken' means well begun and well done. 'Benefits' refers to the virtues. What should be said regarding 'sleeps happily,' etc., we will explain in the Book of the Elevens.

Appamāṇanti [V 3.0194] pharaṇavasena appamāṇaṃ. Tanū saṃyojanā honti, passato upadhikkhayanti mettāpadaṭṭhānāya vipassanāya anukkamena upadhikkhayasaṅkhātaṃ arahattaṃ pattassa dasa saṃyojanā pahīyantīti attho. Atha vā tanū saṃyojanā hontīti paṭighañceva paṭighasampayuttasaṃyojanā ca tanukā honti. Passato upadhikkhayanti tesaṃyeva kilesūpadhīnaṃ khayasaṅkhātaṃ mettaṃ adhigamavasena passantassa. Kusalī tena hotīti tena mettāyanena kusalo hoti. Sattasaṇḍanti [P 4.0069] sattasaṅkhātena saṇḍena samannāgataṃ, sattabharitanti attho. Vijetvāti adaṇḍena asatthena dhammeneva vijinitvā. Rājisayoti isisadisā dhammikarājāno. Yajamānāti dānāni dadamānā. Anupariyagāti vicariṃsu.

"Immeasurable" means immeasurable through pervasion. "Thin are the fetters, for one who sees the destruction of attachments" means that for one who has attained arahantship—which is reckoned as the destruction of attachments—the ten fetters are gradually abandoned by means of insight that has loving-kindness as its basis. Alternatively, "thin are the fetters" means that aversion and the fetters associated with aversion become thin. "For one who sees the destruction of attachments" means for one who sees, through the attainment of loving-kindness, the destruction of those very defilements which are the attachments. "One becomes skilled in that" means one becomes skilled through that loving-kindness. "A multitude of beings" means endowed with a multitude of beings; that is, filled with beings. "Having conquered" means having conquered by the Dhamma alone, without rod or weapon. "Royal sages" are righteous kings similar to sages. "Sacrificers" are those giving gifts. "They traveled around" means they wandered.

Assamedhantiādīsu porāṇakarājakāle kira sassamedhaṃ, purisamedhaṃ, sammāpāsaṃ, vācāpeyyanti cattāri saṅgahavatthūni ahesuṃ, yehi rājāno [M 3.0194] lokaṃ saṅgaṇhiṃsu. Tattha nipphannasassato dasamabhāgaggahaṇaṃ sassamedhaṃ nāma, sassasampādane medhāvitāti attho. Mahāyodhānaṃ chamāsikaṃ bhattavetanānuppadānaṃ purisamedhaṃ nāma, purisasaṅgaṇhane medhāvitāti attho. Daliddamanussānaṃ hatthato lekhaṃ gahetvā tīṇi vassāni vinā vaḍḍhiyā sahassadvisahassamattadhanānuppadānaṃ sammāpāsaṃ nāma. Tañhi sammā manusse pāseti hadaye bandhitvā viya ṭhapeti, tasmā sammāpāsanti vuccati. “Tāta, mātulā”tiādinā nayena pana saṇhavācābhaṇanaṃ vācāpeyyaṃ nāma, piyavācāti attho. Evaṃ catūhi saṅgahavatthūhi saṅgahitaṃ raṭṭhaṃ iddhañceva hoti, phītañca, bahuannapānaṃ, khemaṃ, nirabbudaṃ. Manussā mudā modamānā ure putte naccentā apārutagharā viharanti. Idaṃ gharadvāresu aggaḷānaṃ abhāvato niraggaḷanti vuccati. Ayaṃ porāṇikā paveṇi.

Regarding the 'grain sacrifice' and so forth, it is said that in ancient times there were four means of beneficence: the grain sacrifice, the human sacrifice, the right snare, and gentle speech, by which kings united the world. Among these, taking a tenth part of the harvested grain was called the grain sacrifice, meaning 'wisdom in providing grain.' The six-monthly provision of food and wages to great warriors was called the human sacrifice, meaning 'wisdom in rallying men.' Taking a written pledge from poor people and giving them wealth amounting to one or two thousand without interest for three years was called the right snare. For it rightly snares people, holding them as if bound to the heart; therefore, it is called the right snare. And then, speaking gentle words in the manner of 'My dear,' or 'Uncle,' and so forth, was called gentle speech, meaning 'amiable speech.' Thus, a kingdom united by these four means of beneficence became prosperous and flourishing, abundant in food and drink, safe, and free from trouble. People lived joyfully and happily, making their children dance on their chests, living with open doors. This is called 'unbolted' because of the absence of bolts on house doors. This was the ancient tradition.

Aparabhāge pana okkākarājakāle brāhmaṇā imāni cattāri [P 4.0070] saṅgahavatthūni imañca raṭṭhasampattiṃ parivattetvā uddhaṃmūlakaṃ katvā assamedhaṃ purisamedhantiādike pañca yaññe nāma akaṃsu. Tesu assamettha medhanti vadhentīti assamedho. Dvīhi pariyaññehi yajitabbassa ekavīsatiyūpassa ekasmiṃ pacchimadivaseyeva sattanavutipañcapasusataghātabhiṃsanassa ṭhapetvā bhūmiñca purise ca avasesasabbavibhavadakkhiṇassa yaññassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Purisamettha medhantīti purisamedho. Catūhi pariyaññehi yajitabbassa saddhiṃ bhūmiyā assamedhe vuttavibhavadakkhiṇassa yaññassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Sammamettha pāsantīti sammāpāso. Divase divase yugacchiggaḷe [V 3.0195] pavesanadaṇḍakasaṅkhātaṃ sammaṃ khipitvā tassa patitokāse vediṃ katvā saṃhārimehi yūpādīhi sarassatīnadiyā nimuggokāsato pabhuti paṭilomaṃ gacchantena yajitabbassa satrayāgassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Vājamettha pivantīti vājapeyyo. Ekena pariyaññena sattarasahi pasūhi yajitabbassa beluvayūpassa sattarasakadakkhiṇassa yaññassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Natthi ettha aggaḷāti niraggaḷo. Navahi pariyaññehi yajitabbassa [P 4.0071] saddhiṃ bhūmiyā ca purisehi ca assamedhe vuttavibhavadakkhiṇassa sabbamedhapariyāyanāmassa assamedhavikappassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ.

Later, however, during the time of King Okkāka, the brahmins, having inverted these four means of beneficence and the prosperity of the kingdom and turned them upside down, performed five sacrifices known as the horse sacrifice, the human sacrifice, and so on. Among these, assamedha is so called because 'they slaughter a horse here.' It is the designation of a sacrifice to be performed with two subsidiary offerings, involving twenty-one sacrificial posts, in which there is the horror of slaughtering five hundred and ninety-seven animals on the final day alone, with all remaining wealth given as offerings, excluding land and people. Purisamedha is so called because 'they slaughter a man here.' It is the designation of a sacrifice to be performed with four subsidiary offerings, along with land, and with wealth given as offerings as described in the horse sacrifice. Sammāpāsa is so called because 'they cast the yoke-pin here.' It is the designation of the satrayāga sacrifice to be performed day by day by casting the samma—which is reckoned as a rod inserted into the hole of a yoke—and, after making an altar at the spot where it falls, with movable posts and so on, by proceeding upstream from the submerged place of the Sarassatī River. Vājapeyya is so called because 'they drink strength here.' It is the designation of a sacrifice to be performed with one subsidiary offering and seventeen animals, involving a beluva sacrificial post and seventeen gifts. Niraggaḷa is so called because 'there are no bolts here.' It is the designation of a variant of the horse sacrifice, to be performed with nine subsidiary offerings, along with land and people, and with wealth given as offerings as described in the horse sacrifice, bearing the synonymous name sabbamedha.

Kalampi [M 3.0195] te nānubhavanti soḷasinti te sabbepi mahāyāgā ekassa mettācittassa vipākamahantatāya soḷasiṃ kalaṃ na agghanti, soḷasamaṃ bhāgaṃ na pāpuṇantīti attho. Na jinātīti na attanā parassa jāniṃ karoti. Na jāpayeti na parena parassa jāniṃ kāreti. Mettaṃsoti mettāyamānacittakoṭṭhāso hutvā. Sabbabhūtānanti sabbasattesu. Veraṃ tassa na kenacīti tassa kenaci saddhiṃ akusalaveraṃ vā puggalaveraṃ vā natthi.

'They are not worth a sixteenth part' means: all those great sacrifices, because of the greatness of the fruit of a single mind of loving-kindness, are not worth a sixteenth part; that is, they do not attain a sixteenth portion. 'He does not harm' means he does not himself cause harm to another. 'He does not cause others to harm' means he does not cause another to inflict harm on someone else. 'One of loving-kindness' means having become a component of a mind of loving-kindness. 'Towards all beings' means towards all creatures. 'He has no enmity with anyone' means for him there is no unwholesome enmity or personal enmity with anyone.

2. Paññāsuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Discourse on Wisdom

2. Dutiye ādibrahmacariyikāyāti maggabrahmacariyassa ādibhūtāya. Paññāyāti vipassanāya. Garuṭṭhāniyanti gāravuppattipaccayabhūtaṃ garubhāvanīyaṃ. Tibbanti bahalaṃ. Paripucchatīti atthapāḷianusandhipubbāparaṃ pucchati. Paripañhatīti pañhaṃ karoti, idañcidañca paṭipucchissāmīti vitakketi. Dvayenāti duvidhena. Anānākathikoti anānattakathiko hoti. Atiracchānakathikoti nānāvidhaṃ tiracchānakathaṃ na katheti. Ariyaṃ vā tuṇhībhāvanti ariyatuṇhībhāvo nāma catutthajjhānaṃ, sesakammaṭṭhānamanasikāropi vaṭṭati. Jānaṃ jānātīti jānitabbakaṃ jānāti. Passaṃ passatīti passitabbakaṃ passati. Piyattāyāti [P 4.0072] piyabhāvatthāya. Garuttāyāti garubhāvatthāya. Bhāvanāyāti bhāvanatthāya guṇasambhāvanāya vā. Sāmaññāyāti samaṇadhammatthāya. Ekībhāvāyāti nirantarabhāvatthāya.

2. In the second sutta, 'ādibrahmacariyikāya' means 'being the beginning of the noble practice of the path.' 'Paññāya' means 'by insight.' 'Garuṭṭhāniyaṃ' means 'being a condition for the arising of reverence and worthy of being highly developed.' 'Tibbaṃ' means 'strong.' 'Paripucchati' means 'one asks about the meaning, the Pāli connection, and the preceding and succeeding sequence.' 'Paripañhati' means 'one formulates a question; one thinks, “I will ask about this and that.”' 'Dvayena' means 'twofold.' 'Anānākathiko' means 'one is not a speaker on diverse topics.' 'Atiracchānakathiko' means 'one does not speak various kinds of worldly talk.' Regarding 'ariyaṃ vā tuṇhībhāvaṃ,' noble silence is the fourth jhāna; attention to the remaining meditation subjects is also suitable. 'Jānaṃ jānāti' means 'one knows what should be known.' 'Passaṃ passati' means 'one sees what should be seen.' 'Piyattāya' means 'for the sake of being beloved.' 'Garuttāya' means 'for the sake of venerableness.' 'Bhāvanāya' means 'for the sake of development' or 'for the honoring of virtues.' 'Sāmaññāya' means 'for the sake of the ascetic's state.' 'Ekībhāvāya' means 'for the sake of being uninterrupted.'

3-4. Appiyasuttadvayavaṇṇanā

3-4. Commentary on the Two Appiya Suttas

3-4. Tatiye appiyapasaṃsīti appiyajanassa pasaṃsako vaṇṇabhāṇī. Piyagarahīti piyajanassa [V 3.0196] nindako garahako. Catutthe anavaññattikāmoti “aho vata maṃ aññena avajāneyyu”nti anavajānanakāmo. Akālaññūti kathākālaṃ na jānāti, akāle katheti. Asucīti asucīhi kāyakammādīhi samannāgato.

In the third sutta, 'praising the unloved' (appiyapasaṃsī) means one who praises and speaks well of unloved people. 'Blaming the beloved' (piyagarahī) means one who blames and criticizes beloved people. In the fourth sutta, 'desiring not to be despised' (anavaññattikāmo) means one who desires not to be despised, thinking: “Oh, may others not despise me!” 'Not knowing the right time' (akālaññū) means one who does not know the proper time for speaking and speaks at the wrong time. 'Impure' (asucī) means one who is endowed with impure bodily actions and so on.

5. Paṭhamalokadhammasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the First Discourse on Worldly Conditions

5. Pañcame lokassa dhammāti lokadhammā. Etehi muttā nāma natthi, buddhānampi honti. Tenevāha – lokaṃ anuparivattantīti anubandhanti nappajahanti[M 3.0196], lokato na nivattantīti attho. Loko ca aṭṭha lokadhamme anuparivattatīti ayañca loko ete anubandhati na pajahati, tehi dhammehi na nivattatīti attho.

5. In the fifth sutta: because they are the nature of the world (lokassa dhammā), they are called 'worldly conditions' (lokadhammā). There is no one who is free from them; they occur even for the Buddhas. Therefore, it was said: 'They revolve along with the world,' meaning they follow it, do not abandon it, and do not turn back from the world. And 'the world revolves along with the eight worldly conditions' means that this world, too, follows them, does not abandon them, and does not turn back from those conditions.

Lābho alābhoti lābhe āgate alābho āgatoyevāti veditabbo. Ayasādīsupi eseva nayo. Avekkhati vipariṇāmadhammeti “vipariṇāmadhammā ime”ti evaṃ avekkhati. Vidhūpitāti [P 4.0073] vidhamitā viddhaṃsitā. Padañca ñatvāti nibbānapadaṃ jānitvā. Sammappajānāti bhavassa pāragūti bhavassa pāraṃ gato nipphattiṃ matthakaṃ patto, nibbānapadaṃ ñatvāva taṃ pāraṃ gatabhāvaṃ sammappajānātīti. Imasmiṃ sutte vaṭṭavivaṭṭaṃ kathitaṃ.

Regarding 'gain and loss': when gain arrives, it should be known that loss has also arrived. This same method applies to dishonor and so on. 'He observes their nature of change' means he reflects thus: 'These things have a nature of changing.' 'Vidhūpitā' means 'destroyed, demolished.' 'Padañca ñatvā' (having known the state) means 'having known the state of Nibbāna.' 'Sammappajānāti bhavassa pāragū' (fully comprehends, having gone beyond existence) means: having gone to the far shore of existence, having reached the culmination, it is only by knowing the state of Nibbāna that one fully comprehends the state of having gone to that far shore. In this sutta, the round of existence and the escape from the round are taught.

6. Dutiyalokadhammasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Second Discourse on Worldly Conditions

6. Chaṭṭhe ko visesoti kiṃ visesakāraṇaṃ. Ko adhippayāsoti ko adhikappayogo. Pariyādāyāti gahetvā pariniṭṭhapetvā. Idhāpi vaṭṭavivaṭṭameva kathitaṃ.

6. In the sixth sutta, 'ko viseso' means: what is the reason for distinction? 'Ko adhippayāso' means: what is the great exertion? 'Pariyādāya' means: having taken, having brought to completion. Here too, only the cycle and that which is free from the cycle are taught.

7. Devadattavipattisuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Discourse on the Ruin of Devadatta

7. Sattame acirapakkanteti saṅghaṃ bhinditvā na cirapakkante. Ārabbhāti āgamma paṭicca sandhāya. Attavipattinti attano vipattiṃ vipannākāraṃ. Sesapadesupi eseva nayo. Abhibhuyyāti abhibhavitvā madditvā.

7. In the seventh sutta, 'acirapakkante' means: having split the Saṅgha, not long departed. 'Ārabbha' means: with reference to, on account of, concerning. 'Attavipattiṃ' means: one's own misfortune, a state of misfortune. In the remaining terms as well, this is the method. 'Abhibhuyya' means: having overcome, having crushed.

8. Uttaravipattisuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Discourse on the Misfortune of Uttara

8. Aṭṭhame [V 3.0197] vaṭajālikāyanti evaṃnāmake vihāre. So kira vaṭavane niviṭṭhattā vaṭajālikāti saṅkhaṃ gato. Pāturahosīti imamatthaṃ devarañño ārocessāmīti gantvā pākaṭo ahosi. Ādibrahmacariyakoti sikkhattayasaṅgahassa sakalasāsanabrahmacariyassa ādibhūto.

8. In the eighth sutta, 'vaṭajālikāyaṃ' means: in the monastery of that name. It is said that because it was established in a banyan grove, it came to be known as Vaṭajālikā. 'Pāturahosi' means: thinking, “I will announce this matter to the king of the devas,” he went and became manifest. 'Ādibrahmacariyako' means: being the beginning of the entire holy life of the teaching, which is comprised of the threefold training.

9. Nandasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Nanda Discourse

9. Navame kulaputtoti jātikulaputto. Balavāti thāmasampanno. Pāsādikoti [P 4.0074] rūpasampattiyā pasādajanako. Tibbarāgoti bahalarāgo. Kimaññatrātiādīsu ayamattho – kiṃ aññena kāraṇena [M 3.0197] kathitena, ayaṃ nando indriyesu guttadvāro bhojane mattaññū jāgariyamanuyutto satisampajaññena samannāgato, yehi nando sakkoti paripuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ brahmacariyaṃ carituṃ. Sace imehi kāraṇehi samannāgato nābhavissa, na sakkuṇeyyāti. Itiha tatthāti evaṃ tattha. Imasmiṃ sutte vaṭṭameva kathitaṃ.

9. In the ninth sutta, 'kulaputto' means: a clansman by birth. 'Balavā' means: endowed with strength. 'Pāsādiko' means: inspiring confidence through accomplishment in form. 'Tibbarāgo' means: having intense lust. Regarding the passage beginning 'kimaññatra,' this is the meaning: What is the use of any other reason being stated? This Nanda has guarded sense-doors, knows the proper measure in eating, is devoted to wakefulness, and is endowed with mindfulness and clear comprehension, by which Nanda is able to practice the holy life completely and purely. If he were not endowed with these reasons, he would not be able. 'Iti ha tattha' means: thus there. In this discourse, only the cycle is taught.

10. Kāraṇḍavasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Kāraṇḍava Discourse

10. Dasame aññenāññaṃ paṭicaratīti aññena kāraṇena vacanena vā aññaṃ kāraṇaṃ vacanaṃ vā paṭicchādeti. Bahiddhā kathaṃ apanāmetīti bāhirato aññaṃ āgantukakathaṃ otāreti. Apaneyyesoti apaneyyo nīharitabbo esa. Samaṇadūsīti samaṇadūsako. Samaṇapalāpoti vīhīsu vīhipalāpo viya nissāratāya samaṇesu samaṇapalāpo. Samaṇakāraṇḍavoti samaṇakacavaro. Bahiddhā nāsentīti bahi nīharanti. Yavakaraṇeti yavakhette. Phuṇamānassāti ucce ṭhāne ṭhatvā mahāvāte opuniyamānassa. Apasammajjantīti [P 4.0075] sāradhaññānaṃ ekato dubbaladhaññānaṃ ekato karaṇatthaṃ punappunaṃ apasammajjanti, apasammajjanisaṅkhātena vātaggāhinā suppena vā vatthena vā nīharanti. Daddaranti daddarasaddaṃ.

10. In the tenth sutta, 'aññen'aññaṃ paṭicarati' means: one conceals another reason or statement with another reason or statement. 'Bahiddhā kathaṃ apanāmeti' means: one diverts the talk to another, extraneous topic from outside. 'Apaneyy'eso' means: this one is to be removed, to be expelled. 'Samaṇadūsī' means: a corrupter of ascetics. 'Samaṇapalāso' means: like chaff among paddy, due to being without substance, he is chaff among ascetics. 'Samaṇakāraṇḍavo' means: the rubbish of ascetics. 'Bahiddhā nāsenti' means: they carry him outside. 'Yavakaraṇe' means: in a barley field. 'Phuṇamānassa' means: of one who, standing in a high place, is being winnowed by a strong wind. 'Apasammajjanti' means: in order to separate the substantial grains from the weak grains, they repeatedly winnow; they carry them out with a winnowing fan or cloth that catches the wind. 'Daddaraṃ' means: the 'daddara' sound.

Saṃvāsāyanti saṃvāsena ayaṃ. Vijānāthāti jāneyyātha. Santavācoti saṇhavāco. Janavatīti janamajjhe. Raho karoti karaṇanti karaṇaṃ vuccati pāpakammaṃ, taṃ raho paṭicchanno hutvā [V 3.0198] karoti. Saṃsappī ca musāvādīti saṃsappitvā musāvādī, musā bhaṇanto saṃsappati phandatīti attho. Imasmiṃ sutte vaṭṭameva kathetvā gāthāsu vaṭṭavivaṭṭaṃ kathitanti.

'Saṃvāsāyaṃ' means: this person is known through co-residence. 'Vijānātha' means: you should know. 'Santavāco' means: having gentle speech. 'Janavati' means: in the midst of people. In the phrase 'raho karoti karaṇaṃ,' an evil deed is called 'karaṇaṃ' (an act); he does that in a secret place, having concealed himself. 'Saṃsappī ca musāvādī' means: being agitated and speaking falsehood; the meaning is that while speaking falsehood, he shifts and trembles. In this sutta, having taught only the cycle, in the verses the cycle and that which is free from the cycle are taught.

Mettāvaggo paṭhamo.

The First Mettā Chapter.

2. Mahāvaggo

2. The Great Chapter

1. Verañjasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Verañja Sutta

11. Dutiyassa paṭhame abhivādetīti evamādīni na samaṇo gotamoti ettha vuttanakārena yojetvā evamettha attho veditabbo [M 3.0198] “na vandati nāsanā vuṭṭhāti, nāpi ‘idha bhonto nisīdantū’ti evaṃ āsanena vā nimantetī”ti. Ettha hi vā-saddo vibhāvane nāma atthe [P 4.0076] “rūpaṃ niccaṃ vā aniccaṃ vā”tiādīsu viya. Evaṃ vatvā atha attano abhivādanādīni akarontaṃ bhagavantaṃ disvā āha – tayidaṃ, bho gotama, tathevāti. Yaṃ taṃ mayā sutaṃ, taṃ tatheva, taṃ savanañca me dassanañca saṃsandati sameti, atthato ekībhāvaṃ gacchati. Na hi bhavaṃ gotamo…pe… āsanena vā nimantetīti. Evaṃ attanā sutaṃ diṭṭhena nigametvā nindanto āha – tayidaṃ, bho gotama, na sampannamevāti taṃ abhivādanādīnaṃ akaraṇaṃ ayuttamevāti.

11. In the first sutta of the second chapter, words such as 'pays homage' should be combined with the negative particle 'na' as stated in 'the recluse Gotama is not...', and the meaning here should be understood thus: 'He does not pay respect, does not rise from his seat, nor does he invite with a seat, saying, "Venerables, please sit here."' Here indeed, the word 'vā' is in the sense of elucidation, as in such instances as 'Is form permanent or impermanent?' Having said this, then seeing the Blessed One not performing acts of homage and so forth towards him, he said: 'This, Venerable Gotama, is not proper at all,' meaning that the non-performance of homage and so forth is indeed improper.

Athassa bhagavā attukkaṃsanaparavambhanadosaṃ anupagamma karuṇāsītalena hadayena taṃ aññāṇaṃ vidhamitvā yuttabhāvaṃ dassetukāmo nāhaṃ taṃ brāhmaṇātiādimāha. Tatrāyaṃ saṅkhepattho – ahaṃ, brāhmaṇa, appaṭihatena sabbaññutaññāṇacakkhunā olokentopi taṃ puggalaṃ etasmiṃ sadevakādibhede loke na passāmi, yamahaṃ abhivādeyyaṃ vā paccuṭṭheyyaṃ vā āsanena vā nimanteyyaṃ. Anacchariyaṃ vā etaṃ, svāhaṃ ajja sabbaññutaṃ patto evarūpaṃ nipaccākārārahaṃ puggalaṃ na passāmi. Apica kho yadāpāhaṃ sampatijātova uttarena mukho sattapadavītihārena gantvā [V 3.0199] sakalaṃ dasasahassilokadhātuṃ olokesiṃ, tadāpi etasmiṃ sadevakādibhede loke taṃ puggalaṃ na passāmi, yamahaṃ evarūpaṃ nipaccakāraṃ kareyyaṃ. Atha kho maṃ soḷasakappasahassāyuko khīṇāsavamahābrahmāpi añjaliṃ paggahetvā “tvaṃ loke mahāpuriso, tvaṃ [P 4.0077] sadevakassa lokassa aggo ca jeṭṭho ca seṭṭho ca, natthi tayā uttaritaro”ti sañjātasomanasso patimānesi. Tadāpi cāhaṃ attanā uttaritaraṃ apassanto āsabhiṃ vācaṃ nicchāresiṃ – “aggohamasmi lokassa, jeṭṭhohamasmi lokassa, seṭṭhohamasmi lokassā”ti. Evaṃ sampatijātassāpi mayhaṃ abhivādanādiraho puggalo natthi, svāhaṃ idāni sabbaññutaṃ patto kaṃ abhivādeyyaṃ. Tasmā tvaṃ, brāhmaṇa, mā tathāgatā evarūpaṃ paramanipaccakāraṃ patthayi. Yañhi, brāhmaṇa, tathāgato abhivādeyya vā…pe… āsanena vā nimanteyya, muddhāpi tassa puggalassa rattipariyosāne paripākasithilabandhanaṃ vaṇṭā muttatālaphalaṃ viya gīvato chijjitvā sahasāva bhūmiyaṃ nipateyya.

Then the Blessed One, without falling into the fault of self-exaltation and disparagement of others, with a heart cooled by compassion, dispelled that ignorance and, wishing to show what is proper, spoke the words beginning with, 'I do not, brahmin…' Here is the brief meaning: 'O brahmin, even when I look with the unhindered eye of omniscience, I do not see in this world with its devas and so on, any person whom I should pay homage to, rise up for, or invite to a seat. It is not surprising that I, who have today attained omniscience, do not see any person worthy of such an act of respect. Moreover, when I was just born, facing north and walking seven steps, surveying the entire ten-thousandfold world-system, even then I did not see in this world with its devas and so on, any person to whom I should perform such an act of respect. Then the great Brahmā, an Arahant with a lifespan of sixteen thousand kappas, with hands reverently joined, delighted in mind, honored me, saying: "You are the great being in the world, the highest, eldest, and best in the world with its devas. There is none superior to you." Even then, not seeing anyone superior to myself, I made a noble utterance: "I am the highest in the world, the eldest in the world, the best in the world." Thus, for me even when just born, there was no person worthy of homage and so forth. Now that I have attained omniscience, whom should I pay homage to? Therefore, O brahmin, do not desire such an act of supreme respect from the Tathāgata. For, O brahmin, should the Tathāgata pay homage to someone… or invite someone to a seat, that person’s head, like a palmyra fruit detached from its stalk with its connection loosened by ripeness, would at the end of the night be severed from his neck and suddenly fall to the ground.'

Evaṃ [M 3.0199] vuttepi brāhmaṇo duppaññatāya tathāgatassa lokajeṭṭhabhāvaṃ asallakkhento kevalaṃ taṃ vacanaṃ asahamāno āha – arasarūpo bhavaṃ gotamoti. Ayaṃ kirassa adhippāyo – yaṃ loke abhivādanapaccuṭṭhānaañjalikammasāmīcikammaṃ “sāmaggiraso”ti vuccati, taṃ bhoto gotamassa natthi. Tasmā arasarūpo bhavaṃ gotamo, arasajātiko arasasabhāvoti. Athassa bhagavā cittamudubhāvajananatthaṃ ujuvipaccanīkabhāvaṃ pariharanto aññathā tassa vacanassa atthaṃ [P 4.0078] attani sandassento atthi khvesa, brāhmaṇa, pariyāyotiādimāha.

Even when this was said, the brahmin, due to his lack of wisdom, not discerning the Tathāgata's preeminence in the world, simply unable to bear that statement, said: 'Venerable Gotama is without taste.' This, it seems, was his intention: 'In the world, the acts of paying homage, rising up, making reverential gestures, and proper conduct are called “the taste of harmony.” Venerable Gotama lacks this. Therefore, Venerable Gotama is without taste, of a tasteless nature, of a tasteless character.' Then the Blessed One, to soften his mind, avoiding direct opposition, showing another meaning of that statement as referring to himself, said, beginning with: 'Indeed, brahmin, there is a way...'

Tattha atthi khvesāti atthi kho esa. Pariyāyoti kāraṇaṃ. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – atthi kho, brāhmaṇa, etaṃ kāraṇaṃ, yena kāraṇena maṃ “arasarūpo bhavaṃ gotamo”ti vadamāno puggalo sammā vadeyya, avitathavādīti saṅkhaṃ gaccheyya. Katamo pana soti? Ye te, brāhmaṇa, rūparasā…pe… phoṭṭhabbarasā, te tathāgatassa pahīnāti. Kiṃ vuttaṃ hoti? Ye te jātivasena vā upapattivasena vā seṭṭhasammatānampi puthujjanānaṃ rūpārammaṇādīni assādentānaṃ abhinandantānaṃ rajjantānaṃ uppajjanti kāmasukhassādasaṅkhātā rūparasā, saddarasā, gandharasā, rasarasā, phoṭṭhabbarasā, ye imaṃ lokaṃ gīvāya bandhitvā viya āviñchanti, vatthārammaṇādisāmaggiyañca uppannattā sāmaggirasāti vuccanti. Te sabbepi tathāgatassa pahīnā. “Mayhaṃ pahīnā”ti [V 3.0200] vattabbepi mamākārena attānaṃ anukkhipanto dhammaṃ deseti, desanāvilāso vā esa tathāgatassa.

Here, 'atthi khvesa' is to be parsed as 'atthi kho esa.' 'Pariyāya' means 'reason.' This is what is meant: 'Indeed, brahmin, there is this reason, by which reason a person speaking of me as "Venerable Gotama is without taste" would speak rightly and would be counted as a speaker of truth.' And what is that reason? 'Brahmin, those tastes for forms… tastes for tangible objects—those have been abandoned by the Tathāgata.' What is meant by this? Those tastes for forms, sounds, smells, flavors, and tangible objects—which are known as the enjoyment of sensual pleasure, and which arise for ordinary people, even for those considered superior by way of birth or rebirth, as they enjoy, delight in, and become attached to sense objects such as forms; those which drag this world along as if tying it by the neck; and because they arise from the conjunction of bases, objects, and so on, they are called 'tastes of harmony.' All these have been abandoned by the Tathāgata. Although it could be said, 'They have been abandoned by me,' he teaches the Dhamma without exalting himself with the notion of 'mine.' Or, this is the Tathāgata’s elegance of exposition.

Tattha pahīnāti cittasantānato vigatā, pajahitā vā. Etasmiṃ panatthe karaṇe sāmivacanaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Ariyamaggasatthena ucchinnaṃ taṇhāvijjāmayaṃ mūlaṃ etesanti ucchinnamūlā. Tālavatthu viya nesaṃ vatthu katanti tālāvatthukatā. Yathā hi tālarukkhaṃ samūlaṃ uddharitvā tassa vatthumatte tasmiṃ padese kate na puna tassa tālassa uppatti paññāyati, evaṃ ariyamaggasatthena [P 4.0079] samūle rūpādirase uddharitvā tesaṃ pubbe uppannapubbabhāvena vatthumatte cittasantāne kate sabbepi te tālāvatthukatāti vuccanti. Aviruḷhidhammattā vā matthakacchinnatālo viya katāti tālāvatthukatā. Yasmā pana evaṃ tālāvatthukatā anabhāvaṃkatā honti[M 3.0200], yathā nesaṃ pacchābhāvo na hoti, tathā katā honti. Tasmā āha – anabhāvaṃkatāti. Āyatiṃ anuppādadhammāti anāgate anuppajjanakasabhāvā.

Herein, 'abandoned' (pahīna) means departed from the mental continuum, or given up. Furthermore, in this context, the genitive case should be understood in the sense of the instrumental. 'Their root, which consists of craving and ignorance, has been cut off by the weapon of the noble path'—therefore, they are 'those with roots cut off' (ucchinnamūlā). 'Their base has been made like a palm-stump'—therefore, they are 'those made into a palm-stump' (tālāvatthukatā). For just as when a palm tree is uprooted along with its root, and in that place only its site remains, the arising of that palm tree is not perceived again, so too, when the flavors of form and so on are uprooted by the weapon of the noble path, together with their root, and in the mental continuum only their site remains by virtue of their having previously arisen, all of them are called 'those made into a palm-stump.' Or, because they have a nature of not growing, they are made like a palm tree whose top has been cut off; thus they are 'those made into a palm-stump.' And since, being thus made into a palm-stump, they are brought to a state of non-existence—they are made in such a way that their future existence does not occur—therefore, it is said, 'brought to a state of non-existence' (anabhāvaṃkatā). 'Those with a nature of non-arising in the future' (āyatiṃ anuppādadhammā) means they have the nature of not arising in the time to come.

No ca kho yaṃ tvaṃ sandhāya vadesīti yañca kho tvaṃ sandhāya vadesi, so pariyāyo na hoti. Nanu ca evaṃ vutte yo brāhmaṇena vutto sāmaggiraso, tassa attani vijjamānatā anuññātā hotīti? Na hoti. Yo hi naṃ sāmaggirasaṃ kātuṃ bhabbo hutvā na karoti, so tadabhāvena arasarūpoti vattabbataṃ arahati. Bhagavā pana abhabbova etaṃ kātuṃ, tenassa kāraṇe abhabbataṃ pakāsento āha – “no ca kho yaṃ tvaṃ sandhāya vadesī”ti. Yaṃ pariyāyaṃ sandhāya tvaṃ maṃ “arasarūpo”ti vadesi, so amhesu neva vattabboti.

Regarding 'But it is not in the way you mean': The reason you refer to when you speak does not apply. 'But when this is said, is it not that the existence in oneself of that agreeable flavor of communion spoken of by the brahmin is admitted?' It is not. For one who, being capable of producing that agreeable flavor of communion, does not do so, deserves to be called 'one without flavor' due to its absence. The Blessed One, however, is incapable of producing it. Therefore, wishing to show his incapability in this matter, he said: 'But it is not in the way you mean.' The intention is: 'The reason with reference to which you call me "one without flavor" should not be applied to us.'

Evaṃ brāhmaṇo attanā adhippetaṃ arasarūpataṃ āropetuṃ asakkonto athāparaṃ nibbhogo bhavantiādimāha. Sabbapariyāyesu cettha vuttanayeneva yojanākkamaṃ viditvā sandhāyabhāsitamatthaṃ evaṃ veditabbaṃ – brāhmaṇo tadeva vayovuddhānaṃ abhivādanādikammaṃ loke [P 4.0080] “sāmaggiparibhogo”ti maññamāno tadabhāvena ca bhagavantaṃ “nibbhogo”tiādimāha. Bhagavā ca yvāyaṃ rūpādīsu sattānaṃ chandarāgaparibhogo, tadabhāvaṃ attani sampassamāno aparaṃ pariyāyamanujāni.

Thus the brahmin, being unable to impose the state of being without flavor intended by him, then said another thing: 'The venerable one is without enjoyment,' and so on. And here, in all these reasons, having understood the method of application in the way already stated, the meaning spoken with reference should be understood thus: The brahmin, considering that very act of paying homage and so on to elders in the world to be 'the enjoyment of communion,' and due to its absence, called the Blessed One 'without enjoyment,' and so on. And the Blessed One, perceiving in himself the absence of that enjoyment through passionate desire which beings have in forms and so on, permitted another reason.

Puna brāhmaṇo yaṃ loke vayovuddhānaṃ abhivādanādikulasamudācārakammaṃ lokiyā karonti, tassa akiriyaṃ sampassamāno bhagavantaṃ akiriyavādoti āha. Bhagavā pana yasmā kāyaduccaritādīnaṃ [V 3.0201] akiriyaṃ vadati, tasmā taṃ akiriyavāditaṃ attani sampassamāno aparaṃ pariyāyamanujāni. Tattha ṭhapetvā kāyaduccaritādīni avasesā akusalā dhammā anekavihitā pāpakā akusalā dhammāti veditabbā.

Again, the brahmin, seeing the non-performance of that customary family practice of paying homage and so on to elders, which worldly people perform in the world, called the Blessed One a teacher of inaction. The Blessed One, however, because he teaches the non-doing of bodily misconduct and so on, therefore, perceiving that teaching of inaction in himself, permitted another reason. Therein, setting aside bodily misconduct and so on, the remaining unwholesome states should be understood as 'the many kinds of evil, unwholesome states'.

Puna brāhmaṇo tadeva abhivādanādikammaṃ bhagavati apassanto “imaṃ āgamma ayaṃ lokatanti lokapaveṇī ucchijjatī”ti maññamāno bhagavantaṃ ucchedavādoti āha. Bhagavā pana yasmā pañcakāmaguṇikarāgassa ceva akusalacittadvayasampayuttassa ca dosassa anāgāmimaggena ucchedaṃ vadati, sabbākusalasambhavassa pana mohassa arahattamaggena ucchedaṃ [M 3.0201] vadati, ṭhapetvā te tayo avasesānaṃ pāpakānaṃ akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ yathānurūpaṃ catūhi maggehi ucchedaṃ vadati, tasmā taṃ ucchedavādaṃ attani sampassamāno aparaṃ pariyāyamanujāni.

Again, the brahmin, not seeing that very act of paying homage and so on in the Blessed One, and thinking, 'On account of this one, this world-lineage, this world-tradition, is cut off,' called the Blessed One a teacher of annihilation. The Blessed One, however, because he teaches the cutting off of lust for the five strands of sensual pleasure and of hatred associated with the two types of unwholesome consciousness by the path of the non-returner, and the cutting off of delusion, which is the origin of all that is unwholesome, by the path of arahantship, and—setting aside those three—he teaches the cutting off of the remaining evil, unwholesome states by the four paths as is appropriate; therefore, perceiving that teaching of annihilation in himself, he permitted another reason.

Puna brāhmaṇo “jigucchati maññe samaṇo gotamo idaṃ vayovuddhānaṃ abhivādanādikulasamudācārakammaṃ, tena taṃ na karotī”ti maññamāno bhagavantaṃ jegucchīti āha. Bhagavā pana yasmā jigucchati kāyaduccaritādīhi, yāni kāyavacīmanoduccaritāni ceva yāva ca akusalānaṃ [P 4.0081] lāmakadhammānaṃ samāpatti samāpajjanā samaṅgibhāvo, taṃ sabbampi gūthaṃ viya maṇḍanakajātiko puriso jigucchati hirīyati, tasmā taṃ jegucchitaṃ attani sampassamāno aparaṃ pariyāyamanujāni. Tattha kāyaduccaritenātiādi karaṇavacanaṃ upayogatthe daṭṭhabbaṃ.

Again, the brahmin, thinking, 'I suppose the ascetic Gotama is disgusted by this customary family practice of paying homage to elders; for that reason he does not do it,' called the Blessed One 'one who is disgusted.' The Blessed One, however, because he is disgusted by bodily misconduct and so on—that is, bodily, verbal, and mental misconduct, and whatever attainment, undertaking, and possession there is of base, unwholesome states—all this he is disgusted by and is ashamed of, just as a person fond of adornment is disgusted by excrement. Therefore, perceiving that quality of being disgusted in himself, he permitted another reason. Therein, in 'by bodily misconduct' and so on, the instrumental case should be understood in the sense of the accusative.

Puna brāhmaṇo tadeva abhivādanādikammaṃ bhagavati apassanto “ayaṃ idaṃ lokajeṭṭhakakammaṃ vineti vināseti, atha vā yasmā etaṃ sāmīcikammaṃ na karoti, tasmā ayaṃ vinetabbo niggaṇhitabbo”ti maññamāno bhagavantaṃ venayikoti āha. Tatrāyaṃ padattho – vinayatīti vinayo, vināsetīti vuttaṃ hoti. Vinayo eva venayiko. Vinayaṃ vā arahatīti venayiko, niggahaṃ arahatīti vuttaṃ hoti. Bhagavā pana yasmā rāgādīnaṃ vinayāya vūpasamāya dhammaṃ deseti, tasmā venayiko hoti. Ayameva cettha padattho – vinayāya dhammaṃ desetīti venayiko. Vicitrā hi taddhitavutti. Svāyaṃ taṃ venayikabhāvaṃ attani sampassamāno aparaṃ pariyāyamanujāni.

Again, the brahmin, not seeing the Blessed One perform that very act of paying homage and so on, and thinking, 'He removes and destroys this practice for the world's elders; or, because he does not perform this proper practice, he should be disciplined and censured,' called the Blessed One a 'disciplinarian' (venayiko). Herein, this is the meaning of the word: one who removes (vinayati) is a 'remover' (vinayo); this means one who destroys. A 'remover' itself is a 'disciplinarian.' Or, one who deserves discipline (vinayaṃ) is a 'disciplinarian'; this means one who deserves censure. The Blessed One, however, because he teaches the Dhamma for the removal and calming of lust and so on, is a 'disciplinarian.' And this is the meaning here: 'He teaches the Dhamma for removal,' thus he is a 'disciplinarian.' Indeed, the formation of derivatives is varied. He, perceiving that quality of being a 'disciplinarian' in himself, permitted another reason.

Puna brāhmaṇo yasmā abhivādanādīni sāmīcikammāni karontā vayovuddhe tosenti hāsenti[V 3.0202], akarontā pana tāpenti vihesenti domanassaṃ nesaṃ uppādenti, bhagavā ca tāni na karoti, tasmā “ayaṃ vayovuddhe tapatī”ti maññamāno sappurisācāravirahitattā vā “kapaṇapuriso aya”nti maññamāno bhagavantaṃ tapassīti āha. Tatrāyaṃ padattho – tapatīti tapo, roseti vihesetīti attho. Sāmīcikammākaraṇassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Tapo assa atthīti tapassī. Dutiye atthavikappe byañjanāni avicāretvā loke kapaṇapuriso [P 4.0082] tapassīti [M 3.0202] vuccati. Bhagavā pana ye akusalā dhammā lokaṃ tapanato tapanīyāni vuccanti, tesaṃ pahīnattā yasmā tapassīti saṅkhaṃ gato. Tasmā taṃ tapassitaṃ attani sampassamāno aparaṃ pariyāyamanujāni. Tatrāyaṃ vacanattho – tapantīti tapā, akusaladhammānametaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Te tape assi nirassi pahāsi viddhaṃsīti tapassī.

Again, the brahmin, because those who perform customary acts such as paying homage please and delight the elders, whereas those who do not perform them torment and harass them, causing them sorrow, and the Blessed One does not perform those acts; therefore, thinking, “This one torments the elders,” or, due to his being devoid of the conduct of good people, thinking, “This is a wretched person,” he called the Blessed One “Tapassī.” Herein, this is the meaning of the word: that which torments is ‘tapa’; the meaning is that it oppresses and harasses. This is a designation for the non-performance of customary acts. He has this ‘tapa,’ therefore he is a ‘Tapassī.’ In the second alternative meaning, without analyzing the syllables, in the world a wretched person is called a ‘Tapassī.’ But the Blessed One, because he has abandoned those unwholesome states which, because they torment the world, are called ‘tapanīya,’ has come to be reckoned as a ‘Tapassī.’ Therefore, seeing in himself that state of being a ‘Tapassī,’ he permitted another explanation. Herein, this is the etymological explanation: they torment, therefore they are ‘tapā’; this is a designation for unwholesome states. He has dispelled, rejected, abandoned, and destroyed those ‘tapā,’ therefore he is a ‘Tapassī.’

Puna brāhmaṇo taṃ abhivādanādikammaṃ devalokagabbhasampattiyā devalokapaṭisandhipaṭilābhāya saṃvattatīti maññamāno bhagavati cassa abhāvaṃ disvā bhagavantaṃ apagabbhoti āha. Kodhavasena vā bhagavato mātukucchismiṃ paṭisandhiggahaṇe dosaṃ dassentopi evamāha. Tatrāyaṃ vacanattho – gabbhato apagatoti apagabbho, abhabbo devalokūpapattiṃ pāpuṇitunti adhippāyo. Hīno vā gabbho assāti apagabbho. Devalokagabbhaparibāhirattā āyatiṃ hīnagabbhapaṭilābhabhāgīti. Hīno vāssa mātukucchismiṃ gabbhavāso ahosīti adhippāyo. Bhagavato pana yasmā āyatiṃ gabbhaseyyā apagatā, tasmā so taṃ apagabbhataṃ attani sampassamāno aparaṃ pariyāyamanujāni. Tatra ca yassa kho, brāhmaṇa, āyatiṃ gabbhaseyyā punabbhavābhinibbatti pahīnāti etesaṃ padānaṃ evamattho daṭṭhabbo – “brāhmaṇa, yassa puggalassa anāgate gabbhaseyyā punabbhave ca abhinibbatti anuttarena maggena vihatakāraṇattā pahīnā. Gabbhaseyyāgahaṇena cettha jalābujayoni gahitā, punabbhavābhinibbattiggahaṇena itarā tisso”pi.

Again, the brahmin, thinking that the act of paying homage and so forth leads to the fulfillment of conception in a deva world and to the attainment of rebirth in a deva world, and seeing its absence in the Blessed One, called him 'Apagabbha.' Or, by the power of anger, while pointing out a fault in the Blessed One's taking of conception in his mother's womb, he spoke thus. Herein, this is the etymological explanation: 'He is departed from conception,' therefore he is 'Apagabbha'; the intention is that he is unable to attain birth in a deva world. Or, 'He has an inferior conception,' therefore he is 'Apagabbha.' Because he is external to conception in a deva world, in the future he will have the portion of obtaining an inferior conception. Or, the intention is: 'His dwelling in his mother's womb was inferior.' But for the Blessed One, because in the future, lying in a womb is departed from him, he, seeing in himself that state of being departed from the womb, permitted another explanation. And herein, the meaning of these words—'For whom, O brahmin, future lying in a womb and new arising in renewed existence is abandoned'—should be seen thus: 'O brahmin, for whichever person, in the future, lying in a womb and new arising in renewed existence are abandoned because the cause has been destroyed by the unexcelled path. And here, by the taking up of 'lying in a womb,' womb-born birth is taken; by the taking up of 'new arising in renewed existence,' the other three types are also taken.'

Apica gabbhassa seyyā gabbhaseyyā. Punabbhavo [P 4.0083] eva abhinibbatti punabbhavābhinibbattīti evamettha attho daṭṭhabbo. Yathā ca viññāṇaṭṭhitīti vuttepi na viññāṇato aññā ṭhiti atthi, evamidhāpi na gabbhato aññā seyyā veditabbā. Abhinibbatti ca nāma yasmā punabbhavabhūtāpi apunabbhavabhūtāpi atthi, idha ca punabbhavabhūtā adhippetā, tasmā vuttaṃ – “punabbhavo eva abhinibbatti punabbhavābhinibbattī”ti.

Moreover, 'the lying of the fetus' is `gabbhaseyyā`. 'Renewed existence itself is new arising' is `punabbhavābhinibbatti`; thus should the meaning be understood here. And just as when 'station of consciousness' is spoken of, there is no station other than consciousness, so too here, no lying other than the fetus should be understood. And as for 'new arising,' since it exists both as that which has become renewed existence and as that which has not become renewed existence, and here that which has become renewed existence is intended, therefore it is said: 'Renewed existence itself is new arising; `punabbhavābhinibbatti`.'

Evaṃ [V 3.0203] āgatakālato paṭṭhāya arasarūpatādīhi aṭṭhahi akkosavatthūhi akkosantampi brāhmaṇaṃ bhagavā dhammissaro dhammarājā dhammasāmī tathāgato anukampāya sītaleneva cakkhunā brāhmaṇaṃ olekento yaṃ dhammadhātuṃ paṭivijjhitvā desanāvilāsappattā nāma hoti, tassā [M 3.0203] dhammadhātuyā suppaṭividdhattā vigatavalāhake nabhe puṇṇacando viya ca saradakāle sūriyo viya ca brāhmaṇassa hadayandhakāraṃ vidhamento tāniyeva akkosavatthūni tena tena pariyāyena aññathā dassetvā punapi attano karuṇāvipphāraṃ aṭṭhahi lokadhammehi akampiyabhāvena paṭiladdhatādiguṇalakkhaṇaṃ pathavisamacittataṃ akuppadhammatañca pakāsento “ayaṃ brāhmaṇo kevalaṃ palitasirakhaṇḍadantavalittacatādīhi attano vuddhabhāvaṃ sallakkheti, no ca kho jānāti attānaṃ jātiyā anugataṃ jarāya anusaṭaṃ byādhino adhibhūtaṃ maraṇena abbhāhataṃ ajja maritvā puna sveva uttānaseyyadārakabhāvagamanīyaṃ[P 4.0084]. Mahantena kho pana ussāhena mama santikaṃ āgato, tadassa āgamanaṃ sātthakaṃ hotū”ti cintetvā imasmiṃ loke attano appaṭisamaṃ purejātabhāvaṃ dassento seyyathāpi, brāhmaṇātiādinā nayena brāhmaṇassa dhammadesanaṃ vaḍḍhesi.

Thus, from the time he came, though the brahmin was abusing him with the eight grounds for abuse, beginning with being of a disagreeable nature, the Blessed One—the Lord of the Dhamma, the King of the Dhamma, the Master of the Dhamma, the Tathāgata—gazing upon the brahmin with an eye cool with compassion, was one who had attained grace in teaching by having penetrated the Dhamma-element. Due to his thorough penetration of that Dhamma-element, wishing to dispel the darkness of delusion in the brahmin’s heart like the full moon in a cloudless sky or the sun in the autumn season, he showed those very grounds for abuse in another way with various reasons. And again, revealing his own pervasion of compassion, the characteristic of the 'tādi' quality obtained through being unshaken by the eight worldly conditions, his state of having a mind like the earth, and his imperturbable nature, he thought: 'This brahmin merely perceives his own old age through signs like gray hair, broken teeth, and wrinkled skin, but he does not know that he is followed by birth, pursued by aging, overwhelmed by sickness, and struck down by death; that having died today, tomorrow he is destined to reach the state of an infant lying on its back. Yet he has come to my presence with great effort. May his coming be fruitful!' Having thought thus, wishing to show his own incomparable state of being born first in this world, he expanded the Dhamma teaching for the brahmin with the method beginning, 'Just as, O brahmin...'

Tattha seyyathāpītiādīnaṃ heṭṭhā vutanayeneva attho veditabbo. Ayaṃ pana viseso – heṭṭhā vuttanayeneva hi te kukkuṭapotakā pakkhe vidhunantā taṃkhaṇānurūpaṃ viravantā nikkhamanti. Evaṃ nikkhamantānañca tesaṃ yo paṭhamataraṃ nikkhamati, so jeṭṭhoti vuccati. Tasmā bhagavā tāya upamāya attano jeṭṭhabhāvaṃ sādhetukāmo brāhmaṇaṃ pucchati – yo nu kho tesaṃ kukkuṭacchāpotakānaṃ…pe… kinti svāssa vacanīyoti. Tattha kukkuṭacchāpakānanti kukkuṭapotakānaṃ. Kinti svāssa vacanīyoti so kinti vacanīyo assa, kiṃ vattabbo bhaveyya jeṭṭho vā kaniṭṭho vāti.

Herein, the meaning of 'Just as...' and so on should be understood in the manner already stated below. This, however, is the distinction: indeed, in the manner already stated below, those chicks emerge, shaking their wings and chirping in a manner appropriate to that moment. And among those emerging thus, the one who emerges first is called the eldest. Therefore, the Blessed One, wishing to establish his own seniority by means of that simile, asks the brahmin: 'Now, among those young chicks… what should be said of him?' Here, 'of the young chicks' (`kukkuṭacchāpakānaṃ`) means 'of the young chicks' (`kukkuṭapotakānaṃ`). 'What should be said of him?' means: 'What should he be called? How should he be spoken of—as the eldest or as the youngest?'

“Jeṭṭho”tissa, bho gotama, vacanīyoti, bho gotama, so jeṭṭho iti assa vacanīyo. Kasmāti ce? So hi nesaṃ jeṭṭhoti, yasmā so nesaṃ vuddhataroti attho. Athassa bhagavā opammaṃ sampaṭipādento evameva khoti āha, yathā so kukkuṭapotako, evaṃ ahampi. Avijjāgatāya [P 4.0085] pajāyāti avijjā vuccati aññāṇaṃ, tattha gatāya. Pajāyāti sattadhivacanametaṃ, avijjākosassa anto paviṭṭhesu sattesūpi vuttaṃ hoti. Aṇḍabhūtāyāti aṇḍe bhūtāya pajātāya sañjātāya. Yathā hi aṇḍe nibbattā ekacce sattā aṇḍabhūtāti vuccanti, evamayaṃ [M 3.0204] sabbāpi pajā avijjaṇḍakose nibbattattā aṇḍabhūtāti vuccati. Pariyonaddhāyāti [V 3.0204] tena avijjaṇḍakosena samantato onaddhāya baddhāya veṭhitāya. Avijjaṇḍakosaṃ padāletvāti taṃ avijjāmayaṃ aṇḍakosaṃ bhinditvā. Ekova loketi sakalepi lokasannivāse ahameva eko adutiyo. Anuttaraṃ sammāsambodhiṃ abhisambuddhoti uttararahitaṃ sabbaseṭṭhaṃ sammā sāmañca bodhiṃ, atha vā pasatthaṃ sundarañca bodhiṃ. Arahattamaggañāṇassetaṃ nāmaṃ, sabbaññutaññāṇassāpi nāmameva. Ubhayampi vaṭṭati. Aññesaṃ arahattamaggo anuttarā bodhi hoti, na hotīti? Na hoti. Kasmā? Asabbaguṇadāyakattā. Tesañhi kassaci arahattamaggo arahattaphalameva deti, kassaci tisso vijjā, kassaci cha abhiññā, kassaci catasso paṭisambhidā, kassaci sāvakapāramiñāṇaṃ. Paccekabuddhānampi paccekabodhiñāṇameva deti, buddhānaṃ pana sabbaguṇasampattiṃ [P 4.0086] deti abhiseko viya rañño sabbalokissarabhāvaṃ. Tasmā aññassa kassacipi anuttarā bodhi na hotīti. Abhisambuddhoti abbhaññāsiṃ paṭivijjhiṃ, pattomhi adhigatomhīti vuttaṃ hoti.

Regarding 'He should be called the eldest, Venerable Gotama': this means, Venerable Gotama, that chick should be called 'the eldest.' If it is asked, 'Why so?' Because he is the eldest among them; the meaning is that he is the most senior among them. Then the Blessed One, presenting a simile, said, 'Even so...' Just as that chick, so am I. Regarding 'among a generation gone to ignorance': ignorance is called unknowing; 'gone to' means existing in that. Regarding 'generation': this is a term for beings; it is also said of beings who have entered within the shell of ignorance. Regarding 'become like an egg': it means having come to be, been born, or arisen in an egg. For just as some beings born in an egg are called 'egg-born,' so too this entire generation is called 'egg-born' because they are born in the eggshell of ignorance. Regarding 'enveloped': it means covered, bound, and wrapped all around by that eggshell of ignorance. Regarding 'having broken the eggshell of ignorance': it means having shattered that eggshell made of ignorance. Regarding 'alone in the world': in the entire abode of the world, I alone am without a second. Regarding 'having awakened to the unsurpassed perfect enlightenment': it means the awakening that is without a superior, the best of all, rightly and by oneself; or, the praised and beautiful awakening. This is a name for the knowledge of the Arahant path; it is also a name for the knowledge of omniscience. Both are applicable. For others, is the Arahant path the unsurpassed awakening, or is it not? It is not. Why? Because it does not bestow all the noble qualities. For, in the case of some, their Arahant path bestows only the fruit of Arahantship; for some, the three true knowledges; for some, the six higher knowledges; for some, the four analytical knowledges; and for some, the knowledge of a disciple's perfections. For Paccekabuddhas, it bestows only the knowledge of a Paccekabuddha. But for Buddhas, it bestows the accomplishment of all noble qualities, just as a king's consecration bestows sovereignty over the entire world. Therefore, for anyone else, there is no unsurpassed awakening. Regarding 'having awakened': this means 'I have directly known, I have penetrated, I have attained, I have realized.'

Idāni yadetaṃ bhagavatā “evameva kho”tiādinā nayena vuttaṃ opammasampaṭipādanaṃ, taṃ evaṃ atthena saṃsanditvā veditabbaṃ – yathā hi tassā kukkuṭiyā attano aṇḍesu adhisayanāditividhakiriyākaraṇaṃ, evaṃ bodhipallaṅke nisinnassa bodhisattabhūtassa bhagavato attano santāne aniccaṃ, dukkhaṃ, anattāti tividhānupassanākaraṇaṃ. Kukkuṭiyā tividhakiriyāsampādanena aṇḍānaṃ apūtibhāvo viya bodhisattabhūtassa bhagavato tividhānupassanāsampādanena vipassanāñāṇassa aparihāni. Kukkuṭiyā tividhakiriyākaraṇena aṇḍānaṃ allasinehapariyādānaṃ viya bodhisattabhūtassa bhagavato tividhānupassanāsampādanena bhavattayānugatanikantisinehapariyādānaṃ. Kukkuṭiyā tividhakiriyākaraṇena aṇḍakapālānaṃ tanubhāvo viya bodhisattabhūtassa bhagavato tividhānupassanāsampādanena avijjaṇḍakosassa tanubhāvo, kukkuṭiyā tividhakiriyākaraṇena kukkuṭapotakassa pādanakhatuṇḍakānaṃ thaddhakharabhāvo viya bodhisattabhūtassa bhagavato tividhānupassanāsampādanena vipassanāñāṇassa tikkhakharavippasannasūrabhāvo. Kukkuṭiyā tividhakiriyākaraṇena kukkuṭapotakassa pariṇāmakālo viya bodhisattabhūtassa [M 3.0205][P 4.0087] bhagavato tividhānupassanāsampādanena vipassanāñāṇassa pariṇāmakālo vaḍḍhikālo gabbhaggahaṇakālo. Kukkuṭiyā tividhakiriyākaraṇena kukkuṭapotakassa pādanakhasikhāya vā mukhatuṇḍakena vā aṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā pakkhe papphoṭetvā sotthinā abhinibbhidākālo viya bhagavato tividhānupassanāsampādanena vipassanāñāṇagabbhaṃ gaṇhāpetvā anupubbādhigatena [V 3.0205] arahattamaggena avijjaṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā abhiññāpakkhe papphoṭetvā sotthinā sakalabuddhaguṇasacchikatakālo veditabbo.

Now, the establishment of the simile spoken by the Blessed One beginning with 'Even so...' should be understood by connecting it with its meaning in this way. Just as that hen performs the threefold action, such as brooding over her eggs, so the Blessed One, as a Bodhisatta seated on the seat of awakening, performs the threefold contemplation of impermanence, suffering, and not-self within his own continuity. Just as the eggs are kept from rotting through the hen's accomplishment of the threefold action, so the knowledge of insight does not decline through the Blessed One's accomplishment of the threefold contemplation. Just as the wet stickiness of the eggs is exhausted through the hen's performance of the threefold action, so the sticky craving that follows the three realms of existence is exhausted through the Blessed One's accomplishment of the threefold contemplation. Just as the eggshells become thin through the hen's performance of the threefold action, so the eggshell of ignorance becomes thin through the Blessed One's accomplishment of the threefold contemplation. Just as the chick's claws and beak become hard and rough through the hen's performance of the threefold action, so the knowledge of insight becomes sharp, rough, very clear, and courageous through the Blessed One's accomplishment of the threefold contemplation. Just as the time of maturation for the chick arrives through the hen's performance of the threefold action, so the time of maturation, the time of growth, and the time of grasping the embryo of the path arrives for the knowledge of insight through the Blessed One's accomplishment of the threefold contemplation. Just as the time arrives for the chick to break out safely—having broken the eggshell with the tip of its claw or beak and flapped its wings—through the hen's performance of the threefold action, so too should be understood the time of safely realizing all the qualities of a Buddha, which occurs when, through the Blessed One's accomplishment of the threefold contemplation, he causes the embryo of the path to be grasped by the knowledge of insight, breaks the eggshell of ignorance with the successively attained Arahant path, and flaps the wings of the higher knowledges.

Ahañhi, brāhmaṇa, jeṭṭho seṭṭho lokassāti, brāhmaṇa, yathā tesaṃ kukkuṭapotakānaṃ paṭhamataraṃ aṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā abhinibbatto kukkuṭapotako jeṭṭho hoti, evaṃ avijjāgatāya pajāya taṃ avijjaṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā paṭhamataraṃ ariyāya jātiyā jātattā ahañhi jeṭṭho vuddhatamoti saṅkhaṃ gato, sabbaguṇehi pana appaṭisamattā seṭṭhoti.

Regarding 'For I, brahmin, am the eldest, the best in the world': Brahmin, just as among those chicks, the chick that first breaks the eggshell and hatches is the eldest, so too, among a generation that has gone to ignorance, because I was the very first to be born with the noble birth after breaking that eggshell of ignorance, I have attained the reckoning of 'eldest' and 'most senior.' And because I am unequalled in all qualities, I am the 'best.'

Evaṃ bhagavā attano anuttaraṃ jeṭṭhaseṭṭhabhāvaṃ brāhmaṇassa pakāsetvā idāni yāya paṭipadāya taṃ adhigato, taṃ paṭipadaṃ pubbabhāgato pabhuti dassetuṃ āraddhaṃ kho pana me, brāhmaṇātiādimāha. Tattha āraddhaṃ kho pana me, brāhmaṇa, vīriyaṃ ahosīti, brāhmaṇa, na mayā ayaṃ anuttaro jeṭṭhaseṭṭhabhāvo kusītena muṭṭhassatinā sāraddhakāyena vikkhittacitena adhigato, apica kho tadadhigamāya āraddhaṃ kho pana me vīriyaṃ ahosi. Bodhimaṇḍe nisinnena mayā catusammappadhānabhedaṃ vīriyaṃ āraddhaṃ ahosi, paggahitaṃ asithilappavattitaṃ. Āraddhattāyeva ca me taṃ asallīnaṃ ahosi[P 4.0088]. Na kevalañca vīriyameva, satipi me ārammaṇābhimukhabhāvena upaṭṭhitā ahosi, upaṭṭhitattāyeva ca asammuṭṭhā. Passaddho kāyo asāraddhoti kāyacittappassaddhivasena kāyopi me passaddho ahosi. Tattha yasmā nāmakāye passaddhe rūpakāyopi passaddhoyeva hoti, tasmā “nāmakāyo rūpakāyo”ti avisesetvāva “passaddho kāyo”ti vuttaṃ. Asāraddhoti so ca kho passaddhattāyeva asāraddho, vigatadarathoti vuttaṃ hoti[M 3.0206]. Samāhitaṃ cittaṃ ekagganti cittampi me sammā āhitaṃ suṭṭhu ṭhapitaṃ appitaṃ viya ahosi, samāhitattā eva ca ekaggaṃ acalaṃ nipphandananti. Ettāvatā jhānassa pubbabhāgapaṭipadā kathitā hoti.

Thus, the Blessed One, having shown the brahmin his own unsurpassed state of being the most senior and excellent, now, in order to show the practice by which he attained it, beginning from the preliminary part, spoke the passage starting with, 'Indeed, brahmin, my energy was aroused...' Therein, as to 'Indeed, brahmin, my energy was aroused': Brahmin, this unsurpassed state of being the most senior and excellent was not attained by me through laziness, with lapsed mindfulness, with an agitated body, or with a scattered mind. But rather, for its attainment, my energy was aroused. It was upheld, its occurrence was not slack. And precisely because it was aroused, that energy of mine was unflagging. And not only was my energy aroused and unflagging, but my mindfulness, too, was established, facing its object. And precisely because it was established, it was unforgotten. As to 'The body was calmed, unagitated': by means of the calming of the mental and physical body, my body, too, was calmed. Therein, because when the mental body is calmed, the physical body is also calmed, it was said 'the body was calmed' without making a distinction such as 'mental body' or 'physical body.' As to 'unagitated': precisely because it was calmed, it was unagitated, meaning free from distress. As to 'The mind was concentrated, one-pointed': my mind, too, was well-placed, well-established, as if absorbed. And precisely because it was concentrated, it was one-pointed, unshaken, and still. By this much, the preliminary practice for jhāna has been described.

Idāni imāya paṭipadāya adhigataṃ paṭhamajjhānaṃ ādiṃ katvā vijjāttayapariyosānaṃ visesaṃ dassento so kho ahantiādimāha. Tattha yaṃ yāva vinicchayanayena vattabbaṃ siyā, taṃ visuddhimagge (visuddhi. 1.69) vuttameva.

Now, demonstrating the special attainment achieved by means of this practice, starting from the first jhāna and culminating in the threefold knowledge, he spoke the passage beginning with, 'So I...' Here, whatever should be said by way of determination has already been said by me in the Visuddhimagga.

Ayaṃ kho me, brāhmaṇātiādīsu pana vijjāti viditakaraṇaṭṭhena vijjā. Kiṃ viditaṃ karoti[V 3.0206]? Pubbenivāsaṃ. Avijjāti tasseva pubbenivāsassa aviditakaraṇaṭṭhena tappaṭicchādakamoho. Tamoti sveva moho tappaṭicchādakaṭṭhena tamo nāma. Ālokoti [P 4.0089] sā eva vijjā obhāsakaraṇaṭṭhena ālokoti. Ettha ca vijjā adhigatāti attho, sesaṃ pasaṃsāvacanaṃ. Yojanā panettha – ayaṃ kho me vijjā adhigatā, tassa me adhigatavijjassa avijjā vihatā, vinaṭṭhāti attho. Kasmā? Yasmā vijjā uppannā. Esa nayo itarasmimpi padadvaye. Yathā tanti ettha tanti nipātamattaṃ. Satiyā avippavāsena appamattassa vīriyātāpena ātāpino kāye ca jīvite ca anapekkhātāya pahitattassa pesitattassāti attho. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – yathā appamattassa ātāpino pahitattassa viharato avijjā vihaññeyya, vijjā uppajjeyya tamo vihaññeyya, āloko uppajjeyya, evameva mama avijjā vihatā, vijjā uppannā, tamo vihato, āloko uppanno. Etassa me padhānānuyogassa anurūpameva phalaṃ laddhanti.

In 'This indeed, brahmin, is my...' and so on: 'knowledge' (vijjā) is so called in the sense of making known. What does it make known? It makes known previous abodes. 'Ignorance' (avijjā) is the delusion that conceals that very previous abode, in the sense of not making it known. 'Darkness' (tamo) is that very delusion, which is called 'darkness' in the sense of concealing it. 'Light' (āloko) is that very knowledge, which is called 'light' in the sense of producing illumination. And here, the meaning is 'knowledge was attained'; the rest is a statement of praise. The construction here should be understood thus: 'This knowledge was attained by me. For me, for whom knowledge was attained, ignorance was dispelled, it perished'—this is the meaning. Why was ignorance dispelled? Because knowledge arose. This same method should be seen in the other pair of phrases as well. In the phrase `yathā taṃ`, the word `taṃ` is a mere particle. The meaning is: for a person who is heedful through not departing from mindfulness; ardent through the ardency of energy; and resolute, with a mind dispatched towards the goal, due to having no concern for body and life. This is what is meant: Just as for one dwelling heedful, ardent, and resolute, ignorance would be dispelled, knowledge would arise, darkness would be dispelled, and light would arise—so too for me, ignorance was dispelled, knowledge arose, darkness was dispelled, and light arose. For me, who was devoted to the striving, a fitting fruit was indeed obtained.

Ayaṃ kho me, brāhmaṇa, paṭhamā abhinibbhidā ahosi kukkuṭacchāpakasseva aṇḍakosamhāti ayaṃ kho mama, brāhmaṇa, pubbenivāsānussatiñāṇamukhatuṇḍakena pubbe nivutthakhandhappaṭicchādakaṃ avijjaṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā paṭhamā abhinibbhidā paṭhamā nikkhanti paṭhamā ariyājāti ahosi kukkuṭacchāpakasseva mukhatuṇḍakena vā pādanakhasikhāya vā aṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā tamhā aṇḍakosamhā abhinibbhidā nikkhanti kukkuṭanikāye paccājātīti. Ayaṃ tāva pubbenivāsakathāyaṃ nayo.

As to 'This, brahmin, was my first breakthrough, like that of a chick from its eggshell': Brahmin, for me, having split open the eggshell of ignorance that conceals the aggregates previously inhabited with the beak that is the knowledge of recollecting past abodes, there was the first breakthrough, the first emergence, the first noble birth. This was just like a chick's breakthrough, emergence, and birth among the flock of chickens, after splitting the eggshell with its beak or the tip of its claw and coming out from that eggshell. This, for now, is the method in the discourse on previous abodes.

Cutupapātakathāya [M 3.0207] pana vijjāti dibbacakkhuñāṇavijjā. Avijjāti cutupapātappaṭicchādikā [P 4.0090] avijjā. Yathā pana pubbenivāsakathāyaṃ “pubbenivāsānussatiñāṇamukhatuṇḍakena pubbe nivutthakkhandhappaṭicchādakaṃ avijjaṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā”ti vuttaṃ, evamidha “cutupapātañāṇamukhatuṇḍakena cutupapātappaṭicchādakaṃ avijjaṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā”ti vattabbaṃ.

Now, in the discourse on decease and rebirth: 'knowledge' is the knowledge of the divine eye. 'Ignorance' is the ignorance that conceals decease and rebirth. And just as in the discourse on previous abodes it was said, 'having split open the eggshell of ignorance that conceals the aggregates previously inhabited with the beak that is the knowledge of recollecting past abodes,' so here it should be said, 'having split open the eggshell of ignorance that conceals decease and rebirth with the beak that is the knowledge of decease and rebirth.'

Yaṃ panetaṃ paccavekkhaṇañāṇapariggahitaṃ āsavānaṃ khayañāṇādhigamaṃ brāhmaṇassa dassento ayaṃ kho me, brāhmaṇa, tatiyā vijjātiādimāha, tattha vijjāti arahattamaggavijjā. Avijjāti catusaccappaṭicchādikā avijjā. Ayaṃ kho me, brāhmaṇa, tatiyā abhinibbhidā ahosīti [V 3.0207] ettha ayaṃ kho mama, brāhmaṇa, āsavānaṃ khayañāṇamukhatuṇḍakena catusaccapaṭicchādakaṃ avijjaṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā tatiyā abhinibbhidā tatiyā nikkhanti tatiyā ariyajāti ahosi kukkuṭacchāpakasseva mukhatuṇḍakena vā pādanakhasikhāya vā aṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā tamhā aṇḍakosamhā abhinibbhidā nikkhanti kukkuṭanikāye paccājātīti.

Furthermore, showing the brahmin that attainment of the knowledge of the destruction of the taints which is comprehended by the reviewing knowledge, he spoke, starting with 'This, brahmin, is my third knowledge...' Therein, 'knowledge' is the knowledge associated with the path of arahantship. 'Ignorance' is the ignorance that conceals the four truths. Here, in 'This, brahmin, was my third breakthrough': Brahmin, for me, having split open the eggshell of ignorance that conceals the four truths with the beak that is the knowledge of the destruction of the taints, there was the third breakthrough, the third emergence, the third noble birth. This was just like a chick's breakthrough, emergence, and birth among the flock of chickens, after splitting the eggshell with its beak or the tip of its claw and coming out from that eggshell.

Ettāvatā kiṃ dassesīti? So hi, brāhmaṇa, kukkuṭacchāpako aṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā tato nikkhamanto sakimeva jāyati, ahaṃ pana pubbenivutthakkhandhappaṭicchādakaṃ avijjaṇḍakosaṃ bhinditvā paṭhamaṃ tāva pubbenivāsānussatiñāṇavijjāya jāto. Tato sattānaṃ cutipaṭisandhippaṭicchādakaṃ avijjaṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā dutiyaṃ dibbacakkhuñāṇavijjāya jāto, puna catusaccappaṭicchādakaṃ avijjaṇḍakosaṃ padāletvā tatiyaṃ āsavānaṃ khayañāṇavijjāya jāto. Evaṃ tīhi vijjāhi tikkhattuṃ jātomhi. Sā ca me jāti ariyā suparisuddhāti idaṃ dasseti. Evaṃdassento ca pubbenivāsañāṇena atītaṃsañāṇaṃ, dibbacakkhunā paccuppannānāgataṃsañāṇaṃ, āsavakkhayena sakalalokiyalokuttaraguṇanti [P 4.0091] evaṃ tīhi vijjāhi sabbepi sabbaññuguṇe pakāsetvā attano ariyāya jātiyā jeṭṭhaseṭṭhabhāvaṃ brāhmaṇassa dassesi.

By this much, what does he show? This is the question. Indeed, brahmin, that chick, having broken the eggshell and emerging from it, is born only once. But I, having broken the eggshell of ignorance that conceals the aggregates previously inhabited, was first born by the knowledge which is the recollection of past abodes. Then, having broken the eggshell of ignorance that conceals the passing away and rebirth-linking of beings, I was born a second time by the knowledge which is the divine eye. Again, having broken the eggshell of ignorance that conceals the Four Truths, I was born a third time by the knowledge which is the knowledge of the destruction of the taints. Thus, by these three knowledges, I have been born three times. And that birth of mine is noble and exceedingly pure—this is what he shows. And in showing this, by the knowledge of past abodes he reveals knowledge of the past; by the divine eye, knowledge of the present and future; and by the destruction of the taints, all worldly and supramundane qualities. Thus, by these three knowledges, having revealed all the qualities of an Omniscient One, he showed to the brahmin his state of being the eldest and most excellent through his noble birth.

Evaṃ vutte verañjo brāhmaṇoti evaṃ bhagavatā lokānukampakena brāhmaṇaṃ anukampamānena niguhitabbepi attano ariyāya jātiyā jeṭṭhaseṭṭhabhāve vijjāttayapakāsikāya dhammadesanāya vutte pītivipphāraparipuṇṇagattacitto verañjo brāhmaṇo taṃ bhagavato ariyāya jātiyā jeṭṭhaseṭṭhabhāvaṃ viditvā “īdisaṃ nāmāhaṃ sabbalokajeṭṭhaṃ [M 3.0208] sabbaguṇasamannāgataṃ sabbaññuṃ ‘aññesaṃ abhivādanādikammaṃ na karotī’ti avacaṃ, dhiratthu vata, bho, aññāṇa”nti attānaṃ garahitvā “ayaṃ dāni loke ariyāya jātiyā purejātaṭṭhena jeṭṭho, sabbaguṇehi appaṭisamaṭṭhena seṭṭho”ti niṭṭhaṃ gantvā bhagavantaṃ etadavoca – jeṭṭho bhavaṃ gotamo seṭṭho bhavaṃ gotamoti. Evañca pana vatvā puna taṃ bhagavato dhammadesanaṃ abbhanumodamāno abhikkantaṃ bho gotamātiādimāha. Taṃ vuttatthamevāti.

When this was said—the Blessed One, out of compassion for the world and for the brahmin, having delivered a Dhamma discourse illuminating the threefold knowledge, revealing his own state of being the eldest and most excellent through his noble birth, which ought to have been concealed—the brahmin Verañja, his body and mind filled with the pervasion of rapture, understood this state of being the eldest and most excellent. He reflected: 'Indeed, I spoke thus—“He does not perform acts such as paying homage to others”—about one who is the eldest in all the world, endowed with all qualities, the Omniscient One. Indeed, shame on this ignorance!' Having reproached himself, he came to the conclusion: 'Now, in the world, he is the eldest by reason of being born first through his noble birth; he is the most excellent by reason of being unequalled in all qualities.' He then said this to the Blessed One: 'The Venerable Gotama is the eldest! The Venerable Gotama is the most excellent!' And having said this, again greatly rejoicing in that Dhamma discourse of the Blessed One, he said the words beginning with, 'Excellent, Venerable Gotama!' The meaning of that has already been stated.

2. Sīhasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Sīha Sutta

12. Dutiye abhiññātāti ñātā paññātā pākaṭā. Santhāgāreti mahājanassa vissamanatthāya [V 3.0208] kate agāre. Sā kira santhāgārasālā nagaramajjhe ahosi, catūsu ṭhānesu ṭhitānaṃ paññāyati, catūhi disāhi āgatamanussā paṭhamaṃ tattha vissamitvā pacchā attano attano phāsukaṭṭhānaṃ gacchanti. Rājakulānaṃ rajjakiccasantharaṇatthāya [P 4.0092] kataṃ agārantipi vadantiyeva. Tattha hi nisīditvā licchavirājāno rajjakiccaṃ santharanti karonti vicārenti. Sannisinnāti tesaṃ nisīdanatthaññeva paññattesu mahārahavarapaccattharaṇesu samussitasetacchattesu āsanesu sannisinnā. Anekapariyāyena buddhassa vaṇṇaṃ bhāsantīti rājakule kiccañceva lokatthacariyañca vicāretvā anekehi kāraṇehi buddhassa vaṇṇaṃ bhāsanti kathenti dīpenti. Paṇḍitā hi te rājāno saddhā pasannā sotāpannāpi sakadāgāminopi anāgāminopi ariyasāvakā, te sabbepi lokiyajaṭaṃ chinditvā buddhādīnaṃ tiṇṇaṃ ratanānaṃ vaṇṇaṃ bhāsanti. Tattha tividho buddhavaṇṇo nāma cariyavaṇṇo, sarīravaṇṇo, guṇavaṇṇoti. Tatrime rājāno cariyāya vaṇṇaṃ ārabhiṃsu – “dukkaraṃ vata kataṃ sammāsambuddhena kappasatasahassādhikāni cattāri asaṅkheyyāni dasa pāramiyo, dasa upapāramiyo, dasa paramatthapāramiyoti samattiṃsa pāramiyo pūrentena, ñātatthacariyaṃ, lokatthacariyaṃ, buddhacariyaṃ matthakaṃ pāpetvā pañca mahāpariccāge pariccajantenā”ti aḍḍhacchakkehi jātakasatehi buddhavaṇṇaṃ kathentā tusitabhavanaṃ pāpetvā ṭhapayiṃsu.

2. In the second sutta, 'well-known' means known, renowned, and evident. 'In the assembly hall' means in a building constructed for the great populace to rest. It is said that this assembly hall was in the middle of the city, visible from four places. People arriving from the four directions would first rest there before going to their own comfortable places. Some also say it was a building made for the royal families to deliberate on the affairs of the kingdom. Indeed, sitting there, the Licchavi kings would carry out, conduct, and manage the affairs of the kingdom. 'Seated together' means they were well seated on seats prepared just for their sitting, which had excellent coverings worthy of great men and had white parasols raised high. 'In many ways, they spoke in praise of the Buddha' means that after deliberating on matters of the royal court and actions for the welfare of the world, they spoke of, related, and illuminated the praise of the Buddha for many reasons. For those kings were wise, had faith, and were devout; they were noble disciples—some stream-enterers, some once-returners, and some non-returners. All of them, having cut the worldly tangle, spoke of the praise of the Three Jewels, beginning with the Buddha. Therein, the praise of the Buddha is of three kinds: praise of his conduct, praise of his physical form, and praise of his qualities. These kings began with the praise of his conduct: 'Indeed, a difficult deed was done by the Perfectly Enlightened One, who, for four incalculable aeons and more than one hundred thousand kappas, fulfilled the thirty perfections—the ten perfections, the ten higher perfections, and the ten supreme perfections—and who, having brought to culmination the conduct for the welfare of his relatives, the conduct for the welfare of the world, and the conduct for the attainment of Buddhahood, made the five great renunciations.' Thus, speaking of the Buddha's praise through the six hundred and fifty Jātakas, they traced his story up to his rebirth in the Tusita heaven.

Dhammassa [M 3.0209] vaṇṇaṃ bhāsantā pana “tena bhagavatā dhammo desito, nikāyato pañca nikāyā, piṭakato tīṇi piṭakāni, aṅgato nava aṅgāni, khandhato caturāsītidhammakkhandhasahassānī”ti koṭṭhāsavasena dhammaguṇaṃ kathayiṃsu.

12. Furthermore, speaking in praise of the Dhamma, they said: 'The Dhamma has been taught by the Blessed One. By way of collection, there are five Nikāyas; by way of basket, there are three Piṭakas; by way of division, there are nine Aṅgas; and by way of aggregate, there are eighty-four thousand Dhammakkhandhas.' Thus, they spoke of the qualities of the Dhamma by way of its divisions.

Saṅghassa vaṇṇaṃ bhāsantā satthu dhammadesanaṃ sutvā “paṭiladdhasaddhā kulaputtā bhogakkhandhañceva ñātiparivaṭṭañca [P 4.0093] pahāya setacchattaṃ oparajjaṃ senāpatiseṭṭhibhaṇḍāgārikaṭṭhānantarādīni agaṇetvā nikkhamma satthu varasāsane pabbajanti. Setacchattaṃ pahāya pabbajitānaṃ bhaddiyarājamahākappinapukkusātiādīnaṃ rājapabbajitānaṃyeva buddhakāle asītisahassāni ahesuṃ. Anekakoṭisataṃ dhanaṃ pahāya pabbajitānaṃ pana yasakulaputtasoṇaseṭṭhiputtaraṭṭhapālakulaputtādīnaṃ paricchedo natthi. Evarūpā ca evarūpā ca kulaputtā satthu sāsane pabbajantī”ti pabbajjāsaṅkhepavasena saṅghaguṇe kathayiṃsu.

Speaking in praise of the Sangha, they said: 'Having heard the Teacher’s Dhamma discourse, sons of good families who have gained faith abandon their mass of wealth and circle of relatives. Disregarding the white parasol, the position of heir apparent, and ranks such as general, treasurer, steward, and so on, they go forth and take ordination in the Teacher’s excellent dispensation. In the Buddha's time, there were eighty thousand of just those who had gone forth from royalty, having renounced the white parasol, such as King Bhaddiya, Mahākappina, Pukkusāti, and others. As for those who went forth having abandoned wealth of many hundreds of crores, such as Yasa the clansman, Soṇa the treasurer's son, Raṭṭhapāla the clansman, and others, there is no limit. Sons of good families of these various kinds go forth in the Teacher’s dispensation.' Thus, by way of a summary of the going forth, they spoke of the qualities of the Sangha.

Sīho [V 3.0209] senāpatīti evaṃnāmako senāya adhipati. Vesāliyañhi satta sahassāni satta satāni satta ca rājāno. Te sabbepi sannipatitvā sabbesaṃ manaṃ gahetvā “raṭṭhaṃ vicāretuṃ samatthaṃ ekaṃ vicinathā”ti vicinantā sīhaṃ rājakumāraṃ disvā “ayaṃ sakkhissatī”ti sanniṭṭhānaṃ katvā tassa rattamaṇivaṇṇaṃ kambalapariyonaddhaṃ senāpaticchattaṃ adaṃsu. Taṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ – “sīho senāpatī”ti. Nigaṇṭhasāvakoti nigaṇṭhassa nāṭaputtassa paccayadāyako upaṭṭhāko. Jambudīpatalasmiñhi tayo janā nigaṇṭhānaṃ aggupaṭṭhākā – nāḷandāyaṃ, upāli gahapati, kapilapure vappo sakko, vesāliyaṃ ayaṃ sīho senāpatīti. Nisinno [P 4.0094] hotīti sesarājūnaṃ parisāya antarantare āsanāni paññāpayiṃsu, sīhassa pana majjhe ṭhāneti tasmiṃ paññatte mahārahe rājāsane nisinno hoti. Nissaṃsayanti nibbicikicchaṃ addhā ekaṃsena, na hete yassa vā tassa vā appesakkhassa evaṃ anekasatehi kāraṇehi vaṇṇaṃ bhāsanti.

'Sīha the general' means the commander of the army with that name. For in Vesālī, there were seven thousand, seven hundred, and seven kings. All of them gathered together and, taking everyone's consent, said, 'Seek out one capable person to administer the kingdom.' Searching, they saw Prince Sīha and, having decided, 'This one will be capable,' they gave him the general's parasol, which was the color of a red gem and wrapped with a woolen blanket. It is with reference to this that it is said, 'Sīha the general.' 'A disciple of the Nigaṇṭhas' means a provider of requisites and an attendant of the Nigaṇṭha Nāṭaputta. Indeed, on the surface of Jambudīpa, there were three chief attendants of the Nigaṇṭhas: in Nāḷandā, Upāli the householder; in Kapilapura, Vappa the Sakyan; and in Vesālī, this General Sīha. 'He was seated' means that seats were arranged for the other kings at intervals in the assembly, but for Sīha, a seat was prepared in the middle; thus, he was seated on that prepared, magnificent royal throne. 'Without doubt' means free from uncertainty, certainly, unequivocally. For they do not praise just anyone of little power with so many hundreds of reasons.

Yena nigaṇṭho nāṭaputto tenupasaṅkamīti nigaṇṭho kira nāṭaputto “sacāyaṃ sīho kassacideva samaṇassa gotamassa vaṇṇaṃ kathentassa sutvā samaṇaṃ gotamaṃ dassanāya upasaṅkamissati, mayhaṃ parihāni bhavissatī”ti [M 3.0210] cintetvā paṭhamataraṃyeva sīhaṃ senāpatiṃ etadavoca – “senāpati imasmiṃ loke ‘ahaṃ buddho ahaṃ buddho’ti bahū vicaranti. Sace tvaṃ kassaci dassanāya upasaṅkamitukāmo ahosi, maṃ puccheyyāsi. Ahaṃ te yuttaṭṭhānaṃ pesessāmi, ayuttaṭṭhānato nivāressāmī”ti. So taṃ kathaṃ anussaritvā “sace maṃ pesessati, gamissāmi. No ce, na gamissāmī”ti cintetvā yena nigaṇṭho nāṭaputto, tenupasaṅkami.

'He approached the Nigaṇṭha Nāṭaputta' means that the Nigaṇṭha Nāṭaputta, it seems, had previously spoken to General Sīha, thinking, 'If this Sīha hears someone praising the ascetic Gotama and then goes to see him, it will be my decline.' He had said: 'General, in this world many wander about claiming, "I am a Buddha, I am a Buddha." If you wish to visit anyone, you should ask me. I will send you to a suitable place and prevent you from going to an unsuitable one.' Remembering that conversation, Sīha thought, 'If he sends me, I will go. If not, I will not go.' Thinking thus, he approached the Nigaṇṭha Nāṭaputta.

Athassa vacanaṃ sutvā nigaṇṭho mahāpabbatena viya balavasokena otthaṭo “yattha dānissāhaṃ gamanaṃ na icchāmi, tattheva gantukāmo jāto, hatohamasmī”ti anattamano hutvā “paṭibāhanupāyamassa karissāmī”ti cintetvā kiṃ pana tvantiādimāha. Evaṃ vadanto vicarantaṃ goṇaṃ daṇḍena paharanto viya jalamānaṃ padīpaṃ nibbāpento viya bhattabharitaṃ pattaṃ nikkujjanto viya ca sīhassa uppannapītiṃ vināsesi. Gamiyābhisaṅkhāroti hatthiyānādīnaṃ yojāpanagandhamālādiggahaṇavasena pavatto payogo. So paṭippassambhīti [P 4.0095] so vūpasanto.

When the Nigaṇṭha heard his words, he was overwhelmed by a mighty sorrow, as if by a great mountain, thinking, “To the very place I do not wish him to go, there he has become desirous of going. I am ruined!” Displeased, he thought, “I will create an obstacle for him,” and said, “But what about you?” and so forth. Speaking thus, he destroyed the joy that had arisen in Sīha, as if striking a wandering ox with a stick, as if extinguishing a burning lamp, and as if overturning a bowl filled with food. ‘Preparation for going’ refers to the effort that proceeds by way of harnessing elephants and other vehicles, and taking perfumes, garlands, and so on. ‘That subsided’ means that effort ceased.

Dutiyampi khoti dutiyavārampi. Imasmiñca vāre buddhassa vaṇṇaṃ bhāsantā tusitabhavanato paṭṭhāya yāva mahābodhipallaṅkā dasabalassa heṭṭhā pādatalehi upari kesaggehi paricchinditvā [V 3.0210] dvattiṃsamahāpurisalakkhaṇaasītianubyañjanabyāmappabhānaṃ vasena sarīravaṇṇaṃ kathayiṃsu. Dhammassa vaṇṇaṃ bhāsantā “ekapadepi ekabyañjanepi avakhalitaṃ nāma natthī”ti sukathitavaseneva dhammaguṇaṃ kathayiṃsu. Saṅghassa vaṇṇaṃ bhāsantā “evarūpaṃ yasasirivibhavaṃ pahāya satthu sāsane pabbajitā na kosajjapakatikā honti, terasasu pana dhutaṅgaguṇesu paripūrakārino hutvā sattasu anupassanāsu kammaṃ karonti, aṭṭhatiṃsārammaṇavibhattiyo vaḷañjentī”ti paṭipadāvasena saṅghaguṇe kathayiṃsu.

‘For the second time’ means for the second occasion. And on this occasion, while speaking of the Buddha’s excellence, they described the bodily excellence of the Ten-Powered One, from the Tusita abode up to the great Bodhi throne, delineating it from the soles of his feet below to the tips of his hair above, in terms of the thirty-two marks of a great man, the eighty minor characteristics, and the fathom-wide aura.
