While speaking of the Dhamma’s excellence, they spoke of the Dhamma’s qualities by way of its being well-spoken, saying, “There is no such thing as a flaw in even a single word or a single phrase.” While speaking of the Sangha’s excellence, they spoke of the Sangha’s qualities by way of their practice: “Having abandoned such fame, glory, and wealth, those who have gone forth in the Teacher’s dispensation are not of a lazy nature. Rather, being fulfillers of the thirteen ascetic qualities, they undertake the practice in the seven kinds of contemplation, and they make use of the thirty-eight divisions of meditation objects.”

Tatiyavāre pana buddhassa vaṇṇaṃ bhāsamānā “itipi so bhagavā”ti suttantapariyāyeneva buddhaguṇe kathayiṃsu, “svākkhāto bhagavatā dhammo”tiādinā suttantapariyāyeneva dhammaguṇe, “suppaṭipanno bhagavato [M 3.0211] sāvakasaṅgho”tiādinā suttantapariyāyeneva saṅghaguṇe ca kathayiṃsu. Tato sīho cintesi – “imesañca licchavirājakumārānaṃ tatiyadivasato paṭṭhāya buddhadhammasaṅghaguṇe kathentānaṃ mukhaṃ nappahoti, addhā anomaguṇena samannāgatā [P 4.0096] so bhagavā, imaṃ dāni uppannaṃ pītiṃ avijahitvāva ahaṃ ajja sammāsambuddhaṃ passissāmī”ti. Athassa “kiṃ hi me karissanti nigaṇṭhā”ti vitakko udapādi. Tattha kiṃ hi me karissantīti kiṃ nāma mayhaṃ nigaṇṭhā karissanti. Apalokitā vā anapalokitā vāti āpucchitā vā anāpucchitā vā. Na hi me te āpucchitā yānavāhanasampattiṃ, na ca issariyavisesaṃ dassanti, nāpi anāpucchitā harissanti, aphalaṃ etesaṃ āpucchananti adhippāyo.

On the third occasion, while speaking of the Buddha’s excellence, they spoke of the Buddha’s qualities only in the manner of the sutta passages, saying, “Thus is the Blessed One…”; of the Dhamma’s qualities only in the manner of the sutta passages, saying, “The Dhamma is well-expounded by the Blessed One…,” and so forth; and of the Sangha’s qualities only in the manner of the sutta passages, saying, “The Sangha of the Blessed One’s disciples is practicing the good way…,” and so on. Then Sīha thought: “Starting from the third day, the mouths of these Licchavi princes have not been sufficient to describe the virtues of the Buddha, Dhamma, and Sangha. Surely, that Blessed One is endowed with sublime qualities. Without abandoning this joy that has now arisen, today I shall see the Perfectly Enlightened One.” Then the thought arose in him: “What indeed can the Nigaṇṭhas do to me?” Here, ‘What indeed can they do to me?’ means ‘What can the Nigaṇṭhas possibly do to me, whether I ask their leave or not?’ Indeed, if I ask them, they will not provide me with vehicles and conveyances, nor can they take anything away. Asking them is fruitless—this is the meaning.

Vesāliyā niyyāsīti yathā hi gimhakāle deve vuṭṭhe udakaṃ sandamānaṃ nadiṃ otaritvā thokameva gantvā tiṭṭhati nappavattati, evaṃ sīhassa paṭhamadivase “dasabalaṃ passissāmī”ti uppannāya pītiyā nigaṇṭhena paṭibāhitakālo. Yathāpi dutiyadivase deve vuṭṭhe udakaṃ sandamānaṃ nadiṃ otaritvā thokaṃ gantvā vālikāpuñjaṃ paharitvā appavattaṃ hoti, evaṃ sīhassa dutiyadivase “dasabalaṃ passissāmī”ti uppannāya pītiyā nigaṇṭhena paṭibāhitakālo. Yathā tatiyadivase deve vuṭṭhe udakaṃ sandamānaṃ nadiṃ otaritvā purāṇapaṇṇasukkhadaṇḍakaṭṭhakacavarādīni parikaḍḍhantaṃ vālikāpuñjaṃ bhinditvā samuddaninnameva hoti, evaṃ sīho tatiyadivase tiṇṇaṃ vatthūnaṃ guṇakathaṃ sutvā uppanne pītipāmojje “aphalā nigaṇṭhā nipphalā nigaṇṭhā, kiṃ me ime karissanti, gamissāmahaṃ satthusantika”nti manaṃ abhinīharitvā vesāliyā niyyāsi. Niyyanto ca “cirassāhaṃ dasabalassa santikaṃ gantukāmo [V 3.0211] jāto, na kho pana me yuttaṃ aññātakavesena [P 4.0097] gantu”nti “yekeci dasabalassa santikaṃ gantukāmā, sabbe nikkhamantū”ti ghosanaṃ kāretvā pañcarathasatāni yojāpetvā uttamarathe ṭhito tehi ceva pañcahi rathasatehi mahatiyā ca parisāya parivuto gandhapupphacuṇṇavāsādīni gāhāpetvā niyyāsi. Divā divassāti divasassa ca divā, majjhanhike atikkantamatte.

Regarding ‘He set out from Vesālī’: Just as in the summer season, when rain has fallen, the flowing water, having descended into a river, proceeds only a short distance, then stops and does not flow further; even so should be understood the time on the first day when the joy that arose in Sīha with the thought, “I will see the Ten-Powered One,” was obstructed by the Nigaṇṭhas. And just as on the second day, when rain has fallen, the flowing water, having descended into a river and proceeded a short distance, strikes a heap of sand and becomes non-flowing; even so should be understood the time on the second day when the joy that arose in Sīha with the thought, “I will see the Ten-Powered One,” was obstructed by the Nigaṇṭhas. And just as on the third day, when rain has fallen, the flowing water, having descended into a river, dragging along old leaves, dry sticks, wood, and rubbish, having broken through the heap of sand, becomes inclined only towards the ocean; even so, on the third day, Sīha, having heard the discourse on the qualities of the Three Jewels, with joy and gladness having arisen, set out from Vesālī, having directed his mind thus: “The Nigaṇṭhas are fruitless, the Nigaṇṭhas are without result. What can they do for me? I will go to the Teacher’s presence.” And as he was setting out, he thought: “For a long time I have wished to go to the presence of the Ten-Powered One, but it is not proper for me to go in the guise of an unknown person.” So he had it proclaimed, “Whoever wishes to go to the presence of the Ten-Powered One, let them all come forth!” He had five hundred chariots prepared, and standing in the finest chariot, surrounded by those five hundred chariots and a great assembly, and having caused perfumes, flowers, scented powders, and incenses to be taken, he set out. ‘By day, during the day’ means during the daytime of the day, just after midday had passed.

Yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkamīti ārāmaṃ pavisanto dūratova asīti-anubyañjana-byāmappabhā-dvattiṃsa-mahāpurisalakkhaṇāni chabbaṇṇaghanabuddharasmiyo ca disvā “evarūpaṃ nāma purisaṃ evaṃ āsanne vasantaṃ ettakaṃ kālaṃ nāddasaṃ, vañcito [M 3.0212] vatamhi, alābhā vata me”ti cintetvā mahānidhiṃ disvā daliddapuriso viya sañjātapītipāmojjo yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami. Dhammassa cānudhammaṃ byākarontīti bhotā gotamena vuttakāraṇassa anukāraṇaṃ kathenti. Kāraṇavacano hettha dhammasaddo “hetumhi ñāṇaṃ dhammapaṭisambhidā”tiādīsu (vibha. 720) viya. Kāraṇanti cettha tathāpavattassa saddassa attho adhippeto tassa pavattihetubhāvato. Atthappayutto hi saddappayogo. Anukāraṇanti eso eva parehi tathā vuccamāno. Sahadhammiko vādānuvādoti. Parehi vuttakāraṇehi sakāraṇo hutvā tumhākaṃ vādo vā tato paraṃ tassa anuvādo vā koci appamattakopi viññūhi garahitabbaṃ ṭhānaṃ kāraṇaṃ na āgacchati. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – kiṃ sabbākārenapi tava vāde gārayhaṃ kāraṇaṃ natthīti. Anabbhakkhātukāmāti na abhūtena vattukāmā. Atthi sīhapariyāyotiādīnaṃ attho verañjakaṇḍe āgatanayeneva veditabbo. Paramena [P 4.0098] assāsenāti catumaggacatuphalasaṅkhātena uttamena. Assāsāya dhammaṃ desemīti assāsanatthāya santhambhanatthāya dhammaṃ desemi. Iti bhagavā aṭṭhahaṅgehi sīhassa senāpatissa dhammaṃ desesi.

Regarding ‘He approached where the Blessed One was’: Entering the monastery and seeing from afar the eighty minor marks, the fathom-wide aura, the thirty-two major marks of a great man, and the six-colored dense Buddha-rays, he thought: “For so long I have not seen such a man dwelling so near; truly I have been deceived, what a loss for me!” With joy and gladness having arisen, like a poor man who has seen a great treasure, he approached where the Blessed One was. Regarding ‘And explaining the Dhamma in accordance with the Dhamma’: this means they speak in imitation of what was said by the venerable Gotama. Here, the word ‘Dhamma’ is used in the sense of ‘cause,’ as in passages such as “knowledge regarding cause is analytical knowledge of the Dhamma.” Here, ‘cause’ is the intended meaning for the word in that particular instance, because it is the reason for its occurrence. For the usage of words is connected with meaning. ‘Imitation’ means that others speak of it in the same way. Regarding ‘A discussion in accordance with the Dhamma’: this means that your argument, being supported by reasons spoken by others, or any subsequent elaboration of it, does not offer even the slightest ground for the wise to criticize. This is what is meant: Is there, in your argument, a blameworthy reason in any way at all? Regarding ‘Not wishing to speak falsely’: this means not wishing to speak what is not so. The meaning of ‘there is a discourse on Sīha’ and so forth should be understood in the same way as it appears in the Verañja chapter. Regarding ‘with the highest reassurance’: it means with the supreme reassurance designated as the four paths and four fruits. Regarding ‘I teach the Dhamma for reassurance’: it means I teach the Dhamma for the purpose of reassurance, for the purpose of making firm. Thus, the Blessed One taught the Dhamma to General Sīha with eight factors.

Anuviccakāranti anuviditvā cintetvā tulayitvā kattabbaṃ karohīti vuttaṃ hoti. Sādhu hotīti sundaro hoti. Tumhādisasmiñhi maṃ disvā maṃ saraṇaṃ gacchante nigaṇṭhaṃ disvā nigaṇṭhaṃ saraṇaṃ gacchante “kiṃ ayaṃ sīho diṭṭhadiṭṭhameva saraṇaṃ gacchatī”ti garahā uppajjati, tasmā anuviccakāro tumhādisānaṃ sādhūti dasseti. Paṭākaṃ parihareyyunti te kira evarūpaṃ sāvakaṃ labhitvā “asuko nāma rājā vā rājamahāmatto vā seṭṭhi vā amhākaṃ saraṇaṃ gato sāvako jāto”ti paṭākaṃ ukkhipitvā nagare ghosentā āhiṇḍanti. Kasmā? Evaṃ no mahantabhāvo [V 3.0212] āvibhavissatīti ca. Sace panassa “kimahaṃ etesaṃ saraṇaṃ gato”ti vippaṭisāro uppajjeyya, tampi so “etesaṃ me saraṇagatabhāvaṃ bahū jānanti, dukkhaṃ idāni paṭinivattitu”nti vinodetvā na paṭikkamissatīti ca. Tenāha – “paṭākaṃ parihareyyu”nti. Opānabhūtanti paṭiyattaudapāno viya ṭhitaṃ. Kulanti tava nivesanaṃ. Dātabbaṃ maññeyyāsīti pubbe dasapi vīsatipi saṭṭhipi jane āgate disvā [M 3.0213] natthīti avatvā desi, idāni maṃ [P 4.0099] saraṇaṃ gatakāraṇamatteneva mā imesaṃ deyyadhammaṃ upacchindi. Sampattānañhi dātabbamevāti ovadi. Sutaṃ metaṃ, bhanteti kuto sutanti? Nigaṇṭhānaṃ santikā. Te kira kulagharesu evaṃ pakāsenti “mayaṃ yassa kassaci sampattassa dātabbanti vadāma, samaṇo pana gotamo ‘mayhameva dānaṃ dātabbaṃ nāññesaṃ, mayhameva sāvakānaṃ dānaṃ dātabbaṃ, nāññesaṃ sāvakānaṃ, mayhameva dinnaṃ dānaṃ mahapphalaṃ, nāññesaṃ, mayhameva sāvakānaṃ dinnaṃ mahapphalaṃ, nāññesa’nti evaṃ vadatī”ti. Taṃ sandhāya ayaṃ “sutaṃ meta”nti āha.

Regarding ‘acting after investigation’: this means ‘having known, having thought, having weighed with wisdom, do what should be done.’ Regarding ‘it is good’: it means it is excellent. For in the case of a prominent person like you, when seeing me you go to me for refuge, or when seeing a Nigaṇṭha you go to him for refuge, the criticism arises: ‘Why does this Sīha go for refuge to whomever he sees?’ Therefore, it is shown that for a person like you, acting after investigation is good. Regarding ‘they would carry a banner’: it is said that upon gaining such a disciple, they raise a banner and wander about the city proclaiming, ‘Such and such a king, or royal minister, or wealthy man has gone for refuge to us and has become our disciple!’ Why? Thinking, ‘In this way our greatness will become manifest,’ and also, ‘If regret should arise in him, thinking, “Why have I gone to them for refuge?” he will dispel it, thinking, “Many people know that I have gone to them for refuge; it is difficult to turn back now,” and he will not retreat.’ Therefore, he said, ‘they would carry a banner.’ Regarding ‘being like a watering place’: it means standing like a prepared well. Regarding ‘household’: it means your residence. Regarding ‘you should deem it fit to give’: he advised thus: ‘Previously, when ten, twenty, or even sixty people arrived, you gave without saying, “There is nothing.” Now, merely for the reason that you have gone to me for refuge, do not cut off the alms-offerings due to them. For things should indeed be given to those who have arrived.’ Regarding ‘I have heard this, venerable sir’: the question is, ‘From where was it heard?’ The answer is: from the presence of the Nigaṇṭhas. It is said that they proclaim in the households of their supporters thus: ‘We say that one should give to anyone who has arrived. The ascetic Gotama, however, says: “Gifts should be given only to me, not to others; gifts should be given only to my disciples, not to the disciples of others; a gift given only to me is of great fruit, not to others; a gift given only to my disciples is of great fruit, not to the disciples of others.”’ Referring to this, Sīha said, ‘I have heard this.’

Anupubbiṃ kathanti dānānantaraṃ sīlaṃ, sīlānantaraṃ saggaṃ, saggānantaraṃ magganti evaṃ anupaṭipāṭikathaṃ. Tattha dānakathanti idaṃ dānaṃ nāma sukhānaṃ nidānaṃ, sampattīnaṃ mūlaṃ, bhogānaṃ patiṭṭhā, visamagatassa tāṇaṃ leṇaṃ gati parāyaṇaṃ, idhalokaparalokesu dānasadiso avassayo patiṭṭhā ārammaṇaṃ tāṇaṃ leṇaṃ gati parāyaṇaṃ natthi. Idañhi avassayaṭṭhena ratanamayasīhāsanasadisaṃ, patiṭṭhānaṭṭhena mahāpathavīsadisaṃ, ārammaṇaṭṭhena ālambanarajjusadisaṃ. Idañhi dukkhanittharaṇaṭṭhena nāvā, samassāsanaṭṭhena saṅgāmasūro, bhayaparittāṇaṭṭhena [P 4.0100] susaṅkhatanagaraṃ, maccheramalādīhi anupalittaṭṭhena padumaṃ, tesaṃ nidahanaṭṭhena aggi, durāsadaṭṭhena āsiviso, asantāsanaṭṭhena sīho, balavantaṭṭhena hatthī, abhimaṅgalasammataṭṭhena setavasabho, khemantabhūmisampāpanaṭṭhena valāhako assarājā. Dānaṃ nāmetaṃ mayā gatamaggo, mayheso vaṃso, mayā dasa pāramiyo pūrentena velāmamahāyañño, mahāgovindamahāyañño, mahāsudassanamahāyañño, vessantaramahāyaññoti, anekamahāyaññā pavattitā, sasabhūtena jalitaaggikkhandhe attānaṃ niyyādentena sampattayācakānaṃ cittaṃ gahitaṃ. Dānañhi loke sakkasampattiṃ deti mārasampattiṃ brahmasampattiṃ, cakkavattisampattiṃ, sāvakapāramīñāṇaṃ, paccekabodhiñāṇaṃ, abhisambodhiñāṇaṃ detīti evamādidānaguṇappaṭisaṃyuttaṃ kathaṃ.

A gradual discourse is a sequential discourse, thus: after generosity, virtue; after virtue, heaven; after heaven, the path. Herein, regarding the discourse on generosity: This which is called generosity is the source of happiness, the root of prosperity, the foundation of wealth, a protection, a refuge, a destination, and a final resort for one who has come to ruin. In this world and the next, there is no support, foundation, object, protection, refuge, destination, or final resort equal to generosity. Indeed, by its nature as a support, it is like a jeweled lion-throne; by its nature as a foundation, it is like the great earth; by its nature as an object to hold on to, it is like a supporting rope. Furthermore, this generosity, by its nature of delivering from suffering, is like a boat; by its nature of giving comfort, is like a hero in battle; by its nature of protecting from fear, is like a well-prepared city; by its nature of being unstained by the taints of stinginess and so on, is like a lotus; by its nature of burning them up, is like fire; by its nature of being difficult to approach, is like a venomous snake; by its nature of being fearless, is like a lion; by its nature of being powerful, is like an elephant; by its nature of being deemed highly auspicious, is like a white bull; by its nature of conveying one to the land of safety, is like the royal steed Valāhaka. This which is called generosity is the path I have trodden; this is my lineage. While fulfilling the ten perfections, I initiated many great sacrifices: the great sacrifice of Velāma, the great sacrifice of Mahāgovinda, the great sacrifice of Mahāsudassana, and the great sacrifice of Vessantara. As the hare, by offering myself into a blazing mass of fire, the hearts of the supplicants who had arrived were won. Indeed, in the world, generosity gives the prosperity of Sakka, the prosperity of Māra, the prosperity of Brahmā, and the prosperity of a wheel-turning monarch; it gives the knowledge of the perfection of a disciple, the knowledge of a Paccekabuddha, and the knowledge of perfect self-enlightenment. Thus is the discourse connected with the virtues of generosity.

Yasmā pana dānaṃ dento sīlaṃ samādātuṃ sakkoti, tasmā tadanantaraṃ sīlakathaṃ kathesi. Sīlakathanti [V 3.0213] sīlaṃ nāmetaṃ avassayo patiṭṭhā ārammaṇaṃ tāṇaṃ leṇaṃ gati parāyaṇaṃ. Sīlaṃ nāmetaṃ mama vaṃso[M 3.0214], ahaṃ saṅkhapālanāgarājakāle bhūridattanāgarājakāle campeyyanāgarājakāle sīlavarājakāle mātuposakahatthirājakāle chaddantahatthirājakāleti anantesu attabhāvesu sīlaṃ paripūresiṃ. Idhalokaparalokasampattīnañhi sīlasadiso avassayo patiṭṭhā ārammaṇaṃ tāṇaṃ leṇaṃ gati parāyaṇaṃ natthi, sīlālaṅkārasadiso alaṅkāro natthi, sīlapupphasadisaṃ pupphaṃ natthi, sīlagandhasadiso gandho natthi. Sīlālaṅkārena hi alaṅkataṃ sīlagandhānulittaṃ sadevakopi loko [P 4.0101] olokento tittiṃ na gacchatīti evamādisīlaguṇappaṭisaṃyuttaṃ kathaṃ.

Furthermore, since one who gives is able to undertake virtue, he therefore spoke the discourse on virtue immediately after that. Regarding the discourse on virtue: This which is called virtue is a support, a foundation, an object of attention, a refuge, a shelter, a destination, and a final resort. This which is called virtue is my lineage. In countless existences—at the time of the Nāga King Saṅkhapāla, the Nāga King Bhūridatta, the Nāga King Campeyya, the King Sīlavā, the elephant king who supported his mother, and the six-tusked elephant king Chaddanta—I perfected virtue. Indeed, for the attainments of this world and the next, there is no support, foundation, object of attention, refuge, shelter, destination, or final resort equal to virtue. There is no ornament like the adornment of virtue, no flower like the flower of virtue, no fragrance like the fragrance of virtue. Indeed, the world with its gods, gazing upon a person adorned with the adornment of virtue and anointed with the fragrance of virtue, does not reach satisfaction. Thus is the discourse connected with the virtues of virtue.

“Idaṃ pana sīlaṃ nissāya ayaṃ saggo labbhatī”ti dassetuṃ sīlānantaraṃ saggakathaṃ kathesi. Saggakathanti “ayaṃ saggo nāma iṭṭho kanto manāpo, niccamettha kīḷā, niccaṃ sampattiyo labbhanti, cātumahārājikā devā navutivassasatasahassāni dibbasukhaṃ dibbasampattiṃ anubhavanti, tāvatiṃsā tisso ca vassakoṭiyo saṭṭhi ca vassasatasahassānī”ti evamādisaggaguṇappaṭisaṃyuttaṃ kathaṃ. Saggasampattiṃ kathayantānañhi buddhānaṃ mukhaṃ nappahoti. Vuttampi cetaṃ – “anekapariyāyena khvāhaṃ, bhikkhave, saggakathaṃ katheyya”ntiādi (ma. ni. 3.255).

Furthermore, to show that, “By relying on this virtue, this heaven is attained,” he spoke the discourse on heaven immediately after the discourse on virtue. Regarding the discourse on heaven: “This which is called heaven is desirable, lovely, and pleasing. Here, there are constant amusements; here, constant attainments are obtained. The Cātumahārājika gods experience divine happiness and divine prosperity for nine million years, and the Tāvatiṃsa gods for thirty-six million years.” Thus is the discourse connected with the virtues of heaven. Indeed, the mouths of the Buddhas are not sufficient to describe the attainments of heaven. And this has been said: “In many ways, monks, I could give a discourse on heaven,” and so on.

Evaṃ saggakathāya palobhetvā pana hatthiṃ alaṅkaritvā tassa soṇḍaṃ chindanto viya “ayampi saggo anicco addhuvo, na ettha chandarāgo kattabbo”ti dassanatthaṃ “appassādā kāmā bahudukkhā bahupāyāsā, ādīnavo ettha bhiyyo”tiādinā (ma. ni. 1.235-236; 2.42) nayena kāmānaṃ ādīnavaṃ okāraṃ saṃkilesaṃ kathesi. Tattha ādīnavoti doso. Okāroti avakāro lāmakabhāvo. Saṃkilesoti tehi sattānaṃ saṃsāre saṃkilissanaṃ. Yathāha – “saṃkilissanti vata, bho, sattā”ti (ma. ni. 2.351).

Furthermore, having thus enticed with the discourse on heaven, then, just as one might cut off the trunk of an elephant after adorning it, to show that, “This heaven, too, is impermanent and unstable; desire and lust should not be practiced here,” he spoke of the danger, the inferiority, and the defilement of sensual pleasures in the manner of: “Sensual pleasures offer little satisfaction, are of much suffering and much despair; the danger in them is greater,” and so on. Herein, ‘danger’ means fault. ‘Inferiority’ means degradation, a base state. ‘Defilement’ means the defiling of beings in saṃsāra by them. As it was said: “Alas, sirs, beings are defiled.”

Evaṃ kāmādīnavena tajjetvā nekkhamme ānisaṃsaṃ pakāsesi. Kallacittanti arogacittaṃ. Sāmukkaṃsikāti sāmaṃ ukkaṃsikā attanāyeva uddharitvā gahitā, sayambhuñāṇena [P 4.0102] diṭṭhā asādhāraṇā aññesanti attho. Kā pana sāti? Ariyasaccadesanā. Tenevāha – dukkhaṃ samudayaṃ nirodhaṃ magganti. Virajaṃ vītamalanti rāgarajādīnaṃ abhāvā virajaṃ, rāgamalādīnaṃ [M 3.0215] vigatattā vītamalaṃ. Dhammacakkhunti idha sotāpattimaggo adhippeto. Tassa uppattiākāradassanatthaṃ [V 3.0214] yaṃkiñci samudayadhammaṃ sabbaṃ taṃ nirodhadhammanti āha. Tañhi nirodhaṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā kiccavasena evaṃ sabbasaṅkhataṃ paṭivijjhantaṃ uppajjati. Diṭṭho ariyasaccadhammo etenāti diṭṭhadhammo. Esa nayo sesesupi. Tiṇṇā vicikicchā anenāti tiṇṇavicikiccho. Vigatā kathaṃkathā assāti vigatakathaṃkatho. Visārajjaṃ pattoti vesārajjappatto. Kattha? Satthusāsane. Nāssa paro paccayo, na paraṃ saddhāya ettha vattatīti aparappaccayo.

Having thus warned with the danger in sensual pleasures, he declared the benefit in renunciation. 'A ready mind' means a healthy mind. 'The supreme teaching' means that which is personally drawn out, extracted and grasped by oneself, seen with self-arisen knowledge, and not common to others—this is the meaning. And what is that? The teaching of the Noble Truths. Therefore, he said: suffering, origin, cessation, path. 'Dustless and stainless' means dustless due to the absence of the dust of passion and so on; stainless due to the removal of the stains of passion and so on. By 'the eye of the Dhamma,' the path of stream-entry is intended here. To show the manner of its arising, he said: 'Whatever is of a nature to arise, all that is of a nature to cease.' For, taking cessation as its object, it arises by way of its function, thus penetrating all conditioned things. Because the Dhamma of the Noble Truths has been seen by him, he is `diṭṭhadhamma` (one who has seen the Dhamma). This method applies to the remaining terms as well. Because doubt has been crossed by him, he is `tiṇṇavicikiccha` (one who has crossed over doubt). Because questioning is gone for him, he is `vigatakathaṃkatha` (one for whom questioning is gone). He is `vesārajjappatta` (one who has attained confidence) because he has attained assurance. Where? In the Teacher's dispensation. He is `aparappaccaya` (not reliant on another) because there is no other person as his condition, nor does he proceed here by faith in another.

Pavattamaṃsanti pakatiyā pavattaṃ kappiyamaṃsaṃ mūlaṃ gahetvā antarāpaṇe pariyesāhīti adhippāyo. Sambahulā nigaṇṭhāti pañcasatamattā nigaṇṭhā. Thūlaṃ pasunti thūlaṃ mahāsarīraṃ gokaṇṇamahiṃsasūkarasaṅkhātaṃ pasuṃ. Uddissakatanti attānaṃ uddisitvā kataṃ, māritanti attho. Paṭiccakammanti svāyaṃ taṃ maṃsaṃ paṭicca taṃ pāṇavadhakammaṃ phusati. Tañhi akusalaṃ upaḍḍhaṃ dāyakassa, upaḍḍhaṃ paṭiggāhakassa hotīti nesaṃ laddhi. Aparo [P 4.0103] nayo – paṭiccakammanti attānaṃ paṭiccakataṃ. Atha vā paṭiccakammanti nimittakammassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ, taṃ paṭiccakammaṃ ettha atthīti maṃsampi paṭiccakammanti vuttaṃ. Upakaṇṇaketi kaṇṇamūle. Alanti paṭikkhepavacanaṃ, kiṃ imināti attho. Na ca paneteti ete āyasmanto dīgharattaṃ avaṇṇakāmā hutvā avaṇṇaṃ bhāsantāpi abbhācikkhantā na jiridanti, abbhakkhānassa antaṃ na gacchantīti attho. Atha vā lajjanatthe idaṃ jiridantīti padaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ, na lajjantīti attho.

'Pavattamaṃsaṃ' means: 'Take the money and search in the market for permissible meat that is readily available.' This is the intention of the general Sīha. 'Sambahulā nigaṇṭhā' means: about five hundred Nigaṇṭhas. 'Thūlaṃ pasuṃ' means: a stout, large-bodied, four-footed creature, that is to say, an ox, buffalo, or pig. 'Uddissakataṃ' means: done by designating oneself; that is, killed. This is the meaning. 'Paṭiccakammaṃ' means: that recluse Gotama, on account of that meat, comes into contact with that kamma of killing a living being. Indeed, that unwholesome kamma is half for the giver and half for the receiver; this is their doctrine. Another method: 'Paṭiccakammaṃ' means an evil deed done on account of oneself. Or else, 'paṭiccakammaṃ' is a designation for 'action based on a cause.' Because that 'action based on a cause' is present in this meat, the meat itself is also called 'paṭiccakammaṃ'. 'Upakaṇṇake' means: at the root of the ear. 'Alaṃ' is a word of rejection; 'What is the use of this?' is the meaning. 'Na ca pana ete' means: these venerable ones, for a long time having been desirous of dispraise, even while speaking dispraise and slandering, do not reach the end; they do not reach the end of their slander. This is the meaning. Or else, this word 'jiridanti' should be seen in the sense of shame; 'they are not ashamed' is the meaning.

3. Assājānīyasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Assājānīya Sutta

13. Tatiye aṅgehīti guṇaṅgehi. Tassaṃ disāyaṃ jāto hotīti tassaṃ sindhunadītīradisāyaṃ jāto hoti. Aññepi bhadrā assājānīyā tattheva jāyanti. Allaṃ vā sukkhaṃ vāti allatiṇaṃ vā sukkhatiṇaṃ vā. Nāññe asse ubbejetāti aññe asse na ubbejeti na paharati na ḍaṃsati na kalahaṃ karoti. Sāṭheyyānīti saṭhabhāvo. Kūṭeyyānīti kūṭabhāvo. Jimheyyānīti jimhabhāvo. Vaṅkeyyānīti vaṅkabhāvā. Iccassa catūhipi padehi asikkhitabhāvova kathito[M 3.0216]. Vāhīti vahanasabhāvo dinnovādapaṭikaro. Yāva [P 4.0104] jīvitamaraṇapariyādānāti yāva jīvitassa maraṇena pariyosānā. Sakkaccaṃ paribhuñjatīti amataṃ [V 3.0215] viya paccavekkhitvā paribhuñjati. Purisathāmenātiādīsu ñāṇathāmādayo kathitā. Saṇṭhānanti osakkanaṃ paṭippassaddhi.

13. In the third sutta, 'with limbs' means with the limbs of virtue. 'Born in that region' means born in the region on the bank of the Sindhu River. Other fine thoroughbreds are also born right there. 'Wet or dry' means wet grass or dry grass. 'He does not frighten other horses' means he does not frighten, strike, bite, or quarrel with other horses. 'Deceitfulness' means a deceitful nature. 'Cunning' means a cunning nature. 'Crookedness' means a crooked nature. 'Perverseness' means a perverse nature. Thus, by these four terms, his untrained nature is spoken of. 'A bearer' means having the nature of carrying, one who acts in accordance with given instruction. 'Until the end of life' means until the ending of life by death. 'He consumes carefully' means he consumes after reflecting on it as if it were ambrosia. In 'a man’s strength,' etc., the strengths of wisdom, etc., are spoken of. 'Steadiness' means the calming of shrinking back.

4. Assakhaḷuṅkasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Assakhaḷuṅka Sutta

14. Catutthe “pehī”ti vuttoti “gacchā”ti vutto. Piṭṭhito rathaṃ pavattetīti khandhaṭṭhikena yugaṃ uppīḷitvā pacchimabhāgena rathaṃ pavaṭṭento osakkati. Pacchā laṅghati, kubbaraṃ hanatīti dve pacchimapāde ukkhipitvā tehi paharitvā rathakubbaraṃ bhindati. Tidaṇḍaṃ bhañjatīti rathassa purato tayo daṇḍakā honti, te bhañjati. Rathīsāya satthiṃ ussajjitvāti sīsaṃ nāmetvā yugaṃ bhūmiyaṃ pātetvā satthinā rathīsaṃ paharitvā. Ajjhomaddatīti dvīhi purimapādehi īsaṃ maddanto tiṭṭhati. Ubbaṭumaṃ rathaṃ karotīti thalaṃ vā kaṇḍakaṭṭhānaṃ vā rathaṃ āropeti. Anādiyitvāti amanasikatvā agaṇitvā. Mukhādhānanti mukhaṭhapanatthāya dinnaṃ ayasaṅkhalikaṃ. Khīlaṭṭhāyīti cattāro pāde thambhe viya niccalaṃ ṭhapetvā khīlaṭṭhānasadisena ṭhānena tiṭṭhati. Imasmiṃ [P 4.0105] sutte vaṭṭameva kathitaṃ.

14. In the fourth sutta, 'when told "pehī"' means 'when told "gaccha"'. 'He moves the chariot backward' means: pressing down on the yoke with his shoulder bone, he retreats, causing the chariot to roll with its back part. 'He leaps backward, he strikes the chariot-rail' means: lifting his two hind feet and striking with them, he breaks the chariot's wheel rim. 'He breaks the three-poled structure' means: there are three poles in front of the chariot, and he breaks them. 'Striking the chariot-pole with his thigh' means: lowering his head, making the yoke fall to the ground, and striking the chariot-pole with his thigh. 'He tramples down' means: he stands trampling the pole with his two front feet. 'He makes the chariot go on uneven ground' means: he makes the chariot go up onto high ground or a thorny place. 'Not taking it to heart' means: not paying attention, not regarding. 'Mukhādhānaṃ' means: an iron bridle given for the purpose of placing on the mouth. 'Standing firm as a post' means: having placed his four feet motionless like pillars, he stands in a stance like a firm post. In this sutta, only the round of existence is spoken of.

5. Malasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Mala Sutta

15. Pañcame asajjhāyamalāti uggahitamantānaṃ asajjhāyakaraṇaṃ malaṃ nāma hoti. Anuṭṭhānamalā gharāti uṭṭhānavīriyābhāvo gharānaṃ malaṃ nāma. Vaṇṇassāti sarīravaṇṇassa. Rakkhatoti yaṃkiñci attano santakaṃ rakkhantassa. Avijjā paramaṃ malanti tato sesākusaladhammamalato aṭṭhasu ṭhānesu aññāṇabhūtā vaṭṭamūlasaṅkhātā bahalandhakāraavijjā paramaṃ malaṃ. Tato hi malataraṃ nāma natthi. Imasmimpi sutte vaṭṭameva kathitaṃ.

15. In the fifth sutta, 'the stain of non-recitation' means that for those with memorized verses, non-recitation is called a stain. 'The stain of indolence for households' means that the lack of effort and energy is called a stain for households. 'Of complexion' means of the body's complexion. 'Of one who protects' means of one who protects whatever belongs to himself. 'Ignorance is the supreme stain' means: compared to the other stains of unwholesome states, ignorance—which is not knowing in eight instances, is reckoned as the root of the round of existence, and is dense darkness—is the supreme stain. For there is nothing more of a stain than it. In this sutta too, only the round of existence is spoken of.

6. Dūteyyasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Dūteyya Sutta

16. Chaṭṭhe dūteyyanti dūtakammaṃ. Gantumarahatīti taṃ dūteyyasaṅkhātaṃ sāsanaṃ dhāretvā harituṃ arahati. Sotāti yo taṃ assa sāsanaṃ deti[M 3.0217], tassa sotā. Sāvetāti taṃ uggaṇhitvā “idaṃ nāma tumhehi vutta”nti paṭisāvetā. Uggahetāti suggahitaṃ katvā uggahetā. Dhāretāti sudhāritaṃ [V 3.0216] katvā dhāretā. Viññāti atthānatthassa atthaṃ jānitā. Viññāpetāti paraṃ vijānāpetā. Sahitā sahitassāti idaṃ sahitaṃ, idaṃ asahitanti evaṃ sahitāsahitassa kusalo, upagatānupagatesu cheko sāsanaṃ ārocento sahitaṃ sallakkhetvā āroceti. Na byathatīti vedhati na chambhati. Asandiddhanti [P 4.0106] nissandehaṃ vigatasaṃsayaṃ. Pucchitoti pañhatthāya pucchito.

16. In the sixth sutta, 'dūteyya' means the task of a messenger. 'He is worthy to go' means he is worthy to bear and convey that message designated as 'dūteyya'. 'A hearer' means he is a hearer for the one who gives him that message. 'One who makes others hear' means having learned it, he is one who reports back, saying, 'This is what you said.' 'One who learns' means having made it well-grasped, he is one who learns it. 'One who retains' means having made it well-retained, he is one who retains it. 'One who understands' means one who knows the meaning of what is beneficial and not. 'One who makes others understand' means he is one who makes others know. 'Of what is coherent and incoherent' means he is skilled in what is coherent and incoherent, thinking, 'this is coherent, this is incoherent'; he is adept in what is beneficial and not beneficial. When delivering the message, having discerned what is coherent, he delivers it. 'He does not tremble' means he does not shake, he is not frozen. 'Without doubt' means without uncertainty, with doubt removed. 'Questioned' means questioned for the purpose of a question.

7-8. Bandhanasuttadvayavaṇṇanā

Commentary on the Two Bandhana Suttas

17-18. Sattame ruṇṇenāti ruditena. Ākappenāti nivāsanapārupanādinā vidhānena. Vanabhaṅgenāti vanato bhañjitvā āhaṭena pupphaphalādipaṇṇākārena. Aṭṭhamepi eseva nayo.

In the seventh sutta, 'ruṇṇena' means with crying. 'Ākappena' means by the arrangement of such things as wearing lower garments and robes. 'Vanabhaṅgena' means with a gift of such things as flowers and fruits, brought after being plucked from the forest. In the eighth sutta too, this same method applies.

9. Pahārādasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Pahārāda Sutta

19. Navame pahārādoti evaṃnāmako. Asurindoti asurajeṭṭhako. Asuresu hi vepacitti rāhu pahārādoti ime tayo jeṭṭhakā. Yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkamīti dasabalassa abhisambuddhadivasato paṭṭhāya “ajja gamissāmi sve gamissāmī”ti ekādasa vassāni atikkamitvā dvādasame vasse satthu verañjāyaṃ vasanakāle “sammāsambuddhassa santikaṃ gamissāmī”ti cittaṃ uppādetvā “mama ‘ajja sve’ti dvādasa vassāni jātāni, handāhaṃ idāneva gacchāmī”ti taṅkhaṇaṃyeva asuragaṇaparivuto asurabhavanā nikkhamitvā divā divassa yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami, ekamantaṃ aṭṭhāsīti so kira “tathāgataṃ pañhaṃ pucchitvā eva dhammaṃ suṇissāmī”ti āgato, tathāgatassa pana diṭṭhakālato paṭṭhāya buddhagāravena pucchituṃ asakkonto api satthāraṃ vanditvā ekamantaṃ aṭṭhāsi. Tato satthā cintesi – “ayaṃ pahārādo mayi akathente paṭhamataraṃ kathetuṃ na sakkhissati, ciṇṇavasiṭṭhāneyeva naṃ kathāsamuṭṭhāpanatthaṃ ekaṃ pañhaṃ pucchissāmī”ti[P 4.0107].

19. In the ninth, 'Pahārāda' means one so named. 'Asurinda' means the chief of the asuras. Indeed, among the asuras, Vepacitti, Rāhu, and Pahārāda—these three are the chiefs. As for 'He approached the Blessed One': from the day of the Ten-Powered One’s enlightenment, thinking, 'I will go today, I will go tomorrow,' he let eleven years pass. In the twelfth year, while the Teacher was dwelling at Verañjā, he produced the thought, 'I will go to the presence of the Perfectly Enlightened One.' Thinking, 'For me, twelve years have passed with “today, tomorrow”; come now, I will go right now,' at that very moment, surrounded by a host of asuras, he departed from the asura realm and, in the daytime, approached the Blessed One where he was and stood to one side. It is said that he came thinking, 'I will listen to the Dhamma only after asking the Tathāgata a question.' However, from the time he saw the Tathāgata, being unable to ask out of reverence for the Buddha, he paid homage to the Teacher and stood to one side. Then the Teacher thought: 'If I do not speak, this Pahārāda will not be able to speak first. I will ask him a question on a familiar topic in order to initiate a conversation.'

Atha [M 3.0218] naṃ pucchanto api pana pahārādātiādimāha. Tattha abhiramantīti ratiṃ vindanti, anukkaṇṭhamānā vasantīti attho. So “pariciṇṇaṭṭhāneyeva maṃ bhagavā pucchatī”ti attamano hutvā abhiramanti, bhanteti āha. Anupubbaninnotiādīni sabbāni anupaṭipāṭiyā ninnabhāvassa vevacanāni. Na āyatakeneva papātoti na chinnataṭamahāsobbho viya āditova papāto[V 3.0217]. So hi tīrato paṭṭhāya ekaṅguladvaṅgulavidatthiratanayaṭṭhiusabhaaḍḍhagāvutagāvutaaḍḍhayojanādivasena gambhīro hutvā gacchanto sinerupādamūle caturāsītiyojanasahassagambhīro hutvā ṭhitoti dasseti.

Then, questioning him, he said, 'Pahārāda...' and so on. Therein, 'they delight' means they find joy; the meaning is that they live without discontent. He, thinking, 'The Blessed One is questioning me on a familiar topic,' became pleased and said, 'They delight, venerable sir.' 'Gradually sloping,' and so on, are all synonyms for the state of being inclined in sequence. 'Not a sheer drop' means it is not a drop from the very beginning like a great chasm with a sheer bank. For it shows that, starting from the bank, it proceeds becoming deep by degrees of one finger-breadth, two finger-breadths, a span, a cubit, a rod, an usabha, half a gāvuta, a gāvuta, half a yojana, and so on, until at the foot of Mount Sineru it stands eighty-four thousand yojanas deep.

Ṭhitadhammoti ṭhitasabhāvo. Kuṇapenāti yena kenaci hatthiassādīnaṃ kaḷevarena. Thalaṃ ussāretīti hatthena gahetvā viya vīcipahāreneva thalaṃ khipati.

'Of a stable nature' means of a stable nature. 'With a corpse' means with any carcass, such as that of an elephant or horse. 'It casts it onto the shore' means it throws it onto the shore just by the striking of a wave, as if seizing it with a hand.

Gaṅgāyamunāti idha ṭhatvā imāsaṃ nadīnaṃ uppattikathaṃ kathetuṃ vaṭṭati. Ayaṃ tāva jambudīpo dasasahassayojanaparimāṇo, tattha catusahassayojanaparimāṇo padeso udakena ajjhotthaṭo mahāsamuddoti saṅkhaṃ gato, tisahassayojanappamāṇe manussā vasanti, tisahassayojanappamāṇe himavā patiṭṭhito ubbedhena pañcayojanasatiko caturāsītikūṭasahassapaṭimaṇḍito samantato sandamānapañcasatanadīvicitto, yattha āyāmavitthārena ca gambhīrato ca paṇṇāsapaṇṇāsayojanā diyaḍḍhayojanasataparimaṇḍalā anotattadaho kaṇṇamuṇḍadaho [P 4.0108] rathakāradaho chaddantadaho kuṇāladaho mandākinidaho sīhappapātadahoti satta mahāsarā patiṭṭhahanti.

Regarding 'Ganges, Yamunā, etc.': here it is fitting to pause and tell the story of the origin of these rivers. First, this Jambudīpa is ten thousand yojanas in extent. Therein, a region with an extent of four thousand yojanas is covered by water and has come to be called the great ocean. Humans dwell in an area of three thousand yojanas. The Himālaya is established in an area of three thousand yojanas; it is five hundred yojanas in height, adorned with eighty-four thousand peaks, and variegated with five hundred rivers flowing all around. Therein are established the seven great lakes—Anotatta, Kaṇṇamuṇḍa, Rathakāra, Chaddanta, Kuṇāla, Mandākinī, and Sīhappapāta—which are each fifty yojanas in length, breadth, and depth, and have a circumference of one hundred and fifty yojanas.

Tesu anotatto sudassanakūṭaṃ cittakūṭaṃ kāḷakūṭaṃ gandhamādanakūṭaṃ kelāsakūṭanti imehi pañcahi pabbatehi parikkhitto. Tattha sudassanakūṭaṃ sovaṇṇamayaṃ dviyojanasatubbedhaṃ antovaṅkaṃ kākamukhasaṇṭhānaṃ tameva saraṃ paṭicchādetvā tiṭṭhati, cittakūṭaṃ sabbaratanamayaṃ, kāḷakūṭaṃ añjanamayaṃ, gandhamādanakūṭaṃ sānumayaṃ abbhantare muggavaṇṇaṃ, mūlagandho sāragandho pheggugandho tacagandho papaṭikāgandho rasagandho pattagandho pupphagandho phalagandho [M 3.0219] gandhagandhoti imehi dasahi gandhehi ussannaṃ, nānappakāraosadhasañchannaṃ kāḷapakkhauposathadivase ādittamiva aṅgāraṃ jalantaṃ tiṭṭhati, kelāsakūṭaṃ rajatamayaṃ. Sabbāni sudassanena samānubbedhasaṇṭhānāni tameva saraṃ paṭicchādetvā ṭhitāni. Tāni sabbāni devānubhāvena nāgānubhāvena ca vassanti, nadiyo cetesu sandanti. Taṃ sabbampi udakaṃ anotattameva pavisati. Candimasūriyā dakkhiṇena vā uttarena vā gacchantā pabbatantarena tattha obhāsaṃ karonti, ujuṃ gacchantā na karonti. Tenevassa anotatto tisaṅkhā udapādi.

Among these, Lake Anotatta is encircled by these five mountains: Sudassanakūṭa, Cittakūṭa, Kāḷakūṭa, Gandhamādanakūṭa, and Kelāsakūṭa. Among them, Sudassanakūṭa peak is made of gold, two hundred yojanas in height, curved inside, shaped like a crow’s beak, and stands covering that very lake. Cittakūṭa is made of all kinds of gems. Kāḷakūṭa is made of kohl. Gandhamādanakūṭa is made of plateaus, and its interior is the color of a green gram. It is abundant with these ten fragrances: root fragrance, heartwood fragrance, sapwood fragrance, bark fragrance, outer bark fragrance, sap fragrance, leaf fragrance, flower fragrance, fruit fragrance, and the fragrance of fragrant substances. It is covered with various kinds of medicinal herbs and, on the Uposatha day of the dark fortnight, it stands blazing like a burning ember. Kelāsakūṭa is made of silver. All of them have the same height and shape as Sudassana and stand covering that very lake. All of them exist by the power of devas and the power of nāgas, and rivers flow on them. All that water enters only Lake Anotatta. The moon and sun, when traveling on their southern or northern course, illuminate it through the space between the mountains, but they do not illuminate it when traveling straight. For that very reason, the designation 'Anotatta' arose for it.

Tattha manoharasilātalāni nimmacchakacchapāni phalikasadisanimmalodakāni nhānatitthāni supaṭiyattāni [V 3.0218] honti[P 4.0109], yesu buddhā khīṇāsavā ca paccekabuddhā ca iddhimantā ca isayo nhāyanti, devayakkhādayo udakakīḷaṃ kīḷanti.

There, there are delightful stone slabs, free of fish and turtles, with water as clear as crystal, and well-prepared bathing places. In these, Buddhas, Arahants, Paccekabuddhas, and powerful sages bathe, while devas, yakkhas, and others play water-games.

Tassa catūsu passesu sīhamukhaṃ, hatthimukhaṃ, assamukhaṃ, usabhamukhanti cattāri mukhāni honti, yehi catasso nadiyo sandanti. Sīhamukhena nikkhantanadītīre sīhā bahutarā honti, hatthimukhādīhi hatthiassausabhā. Puratthimadisato nikkhantanadī anotattaṃ tikkhattuṃ padakkhiṇaṃ katvā itarā tisso nadiyo anupagamma pācīnahimavanteneva amanussapathaṃ gantvā mahāsamuddaṃ pavisati. Pacchimadisato ca uttaradisato ca nikkhantanadiyopi tatheva padakkhiṇaṃ katvā pacchimahimavanteneva uttarahimavanteneva ca amanussapathaṃ gantvā mahāsamuddaṃ pavisanti. Dakkhiṇamukhato nikkhantanadī pana taṃ tikkhattuṃ padakkhiṇaṃ katvā uttarena ujukaṃ pāsāṇapiṭṭheneva saṭṭhi yojanāni gantvā pabbataṃ paharitvā uṭṭhāya parikkhepena tigāvutappamāṇā udakadhārā hutvā ākāsena saṭṭhi yojanāni gantvā tiyaggaḷe nāma pāsāṇe patitā, pāsāṇo udakadhārāvegena bhinno. Tattha paññāsayojanappamāṇā tiyaggaḷā nāma mahāpokkharaṇī jātā, pokkharaṇiyā kūlaṃ bhinditvā pāsāṇaṃ pavisitvā saṭṭhi yojanāni gatā. Tato ghanapathaviṃ bhinditvā ummaṅgena saṭṭhi yojanāni gantvā giñjhaṃ nāma tiracchānapabbataṃ paharitvā hatthatale pañcaṅgulisadisā pañca dhārā hutvā pavattati. Sā [M 3.0220] tikkhattuṃ anotattaṃ padakkhiṇaṃ katvā gataṭṭhāne āvattagaṅgāti vuccati[P 4.0110]. Ujukaṃ pāsāṇapiṭṭhena saṭṭhi yojanāni gataṭṭhāne kaṇhagaṅgā, ākāsena saṭṭhi yojanāni gataṭṭhāne ākāsagaṅgā, tiyaggaḷapāsāṇe paññāsayojanokāse ṭhitā tiyaggaḷapokkharaṇī, kūlaṃ bhinditvā pāsāṇaṃ pavisitvā saṭṭhi yojanāni gataṭṭhāne bahalagaṅgāti, umaṅgena saṭṭhi yojanāni gataṭṭhāne umaṅgagaṅgāti vuccati. Viñjhaṃ nāma tiracchānapabbataṃ paharitvā pañca dhārā hutvā pavattanaṭṭhāne pana gaṅgā, yamunā, aciravatī, sarabhū, mahīti pañca saṅkhaṃ gatā. Evametā pañca mahānadiyo himavantato pavattantīti veditabbā.

On its four sides are four outlets: a lion's mouth, an elephant's mouth, a horse's mouth, and a bull's mouth, from which four rivers flow. On the banks of the river emerging from the lion's mouth, lions are more numerous; from the outlets of the elephant, horse, and bull, elephants, horses, and bulls are more numerous. The river emerging from the eastern direction, having circled Lake Anotatta three times clockwise and without approaching the other three rivers, goes along the eastern Himalayas through a path of non-humans and enters the great ocean. The rivers emerging from the western and northern directions also, in the same way, having circled it clockwise, go along the western and northern Himalayas through a path of non-humans and enter the great ocean. The river emerging from the southern mouth, however, having circled the lake three times clockwise, goes straight north for sixty yojanas along a stone slab. Having struck a mountain, it rises up and, becoming a column of water with a circumference of three gāvutas, goes sixty yojanas through the sky and falls on a stone named Tiyaggaḷa. The stone is shattered by the force of the water column. There, a great pond named Tiyaggaḷā, fifty yojanas in size, is formed. Breaking the pond’s bank, it enters the stone and goes for sixty yojanas. Then, breaking through the solid earth, it goes sixty yojanas through a tunnel and, having struck the transverse mountain named Viñjha, it flows on, having become five streams like the five fingers on the palm of a hand. Where it has gone after circling Lake Anotatta three times, it is called the Āvattagaṅgā. Where it has gone sixty yojanas straight along the stone slab, it is called the Kaṇhagaṅgā. Where it has gone sixty yojanas through the sky, it is called the Ākāsagaṅgā. The water standing in the fifty-yojana expanse on the Tiyaggaḷa stone is called the Tiyaggaḷapokkharaṇī. Where it has gone sixty yojanas after breaking the bank and entering the stone, it is called the Bahalagaṅgā. Where it has gone sixty yojanas through the tunnel, it is called the Umaṅgagaṅgā. But at the place where it flows on after striking the transverse mountain named Viñjha and becoming five streams, it is reckoned as five: the Gaṅgā, Yamunā, Aciravatī, Sarabhū, and Mahī. Thus, it should be known that these five great rivers flow from the Himalayas.

Savantiyoti yā kāci savamānā gacchantī mahānadiyo vā kunnadiyo vā. Appentīti allīyanti osaranti. Dhārāti vuṭṭhidhārā. Pūrattanti puṇṇabhāvo. Mahāsamuddassa hi ayaṃ dhammatā – “imasmiṃ kāle devo mando jāto, jālakkhipādīni ādāya macchakacchape gaṇhissāmā”ti vā “imasmiṃ kāle mahantā vuṭṭhi, labhissāma nu kho piṭṭhipasāraṇaṭṭhāna”nti vā [V 3.0219] vattuṃ na sakkā. Paṭhamakappikakālato paṭṭhāya hi yaṃ sinerumekhalaṃ āhacca udakaṃ ṭhitaṃ, tato ekaṅgulamattampi udakaṃ neva heṭṭhā osīdati, na uddhaṃ uttarati. Ekarasoti asambhinnaraso.

'Savantiyo' means any flowing great rivers or small rivers. 'Appenti' means they cling to or descend. 'Dhārā' refers to a stream of rain. 'Pūrattaṃ' means fullness. For the great ocean has this nature: it is not possible to say, 'At this time the rain has become slight, so we will take nets and so on and catch fish and turtles,' or 'At this time there is great rain—will we find a place to stretch our backs?' Indeed, from the time of the first aeon, the water that has stood after striking the girdle of Mount Sineru neither sinks down even a finger's breadth nor rises up. 'Ekaraso' means having an unmixed taste.

Muttāti khuddakamahantavaṭṭadīghādibhedā anekavidhā[P 4.0111]. Maṇīti rattanīlādibhedo anekavidho. Veḷuriyoti vaṃsavaṇṇasirīsapupphavaṇṇādibhedo anekavidho. Saṅkhoti dakkhiṇāvaṭṭatambakucchikadhamanasaṅkhādibhedo anekavidho. Silāti setakāḷamuggavaṇṇādibhedo anekavidhā. Pavāḷanti khuddakamahantarattaghanarattādibhedaṃ anekavidhaṃ. Masāragallanti kabaramaṇi. Nāgāti ūmipiṭṭhavāsinopi vimānaṭṭhakā nāgāpi.

Pearls are of many kinds, with distinctions such as small, large, round, and long. Gems are of many kinds, with distinctions such as ruby and sapphire. Beryl is of many kinds, with distinctions such as bamboo-colored and sirisa-flower-colored. Conch shells are of many kinds, with distinctions such as right-spiraling, red-bellied, and blown. Crystal is of many kinds, with distinctions such as white, black, and mung-bean-colored. Coral is of many kinds, with distinctions such as small, large, red, and dense red. Masāragalla is a variegated gem. Nāgas are those dwelling on the surface of the waves as well as nāgas established in mansions.

Aṭṭha pahārādāti satthā aṭṭhapi dhamme vattuṃ sakkoti, soḷasapi bāttiṃsapi catusaṭṭhipi sahassampi, pahārādena pana aṭṭha kathitā, ahampi teheva sarikkhake katvā kathessāmīti cintetvā evamāha. Anupubbasikkhātiādīsu anupubbasikkhāya tisso sikkhā gahitā, anupubbakiriyāya terasa dhutaṅgāni, anupubbapaṭipadāya satta anupassanā aṭṭhārasa mahāvipassanā aṭṭhatiṃsa ārammaṇavibhattiyo sattatiṃsa bodhapakkhiyadhammā[M 3.0221]. Na āyatakeneva aññāpaṭivedhoti maṇḍūkassa uppatitvā gamanaṃ viya āditova sīlapūraṇādiṃ akatvā arahattappaṭivedho nāma natthi, paṭipāṭiyā pana sīlasamādhipaññāyo pūretvāva sakkā arahattaṃ pattunti attho.

Regarding 'Aṭṭha pahārādā': The Teacher is able to speak of eight wonderful and marvelous things, or sixteen, or thirty-two, or sixty-four, or a hundred, or a thousand. However, by the asura Pahārāda, eight were spoken of. Thinking, 'I too, making them similar to those, will teach,' he spoke thus. In 'Anupubbasikkhā' (gradual training), etc.: by 'gradual training,' the three trainings are taken up; by 'gradual action,' the thirteen ascetic practices; by 'gradual progress,' the seven contemplations, the eighteen great insights, the thirty-eight divisions of objects, and the thirty-seven factors of enlightenment. Regarding 'Not by a leap is there penetration to final knowledge': just like the going of a frog by leaping, there is no such thing as the penetration to Arahantship without having fulfilled morality, etc., from the very beginning. Indeed, the meaning is that it is possible to attain Arahantship only by fulfilling morality, concentration, and wisdom in sequence.

Ārakāvāti dūreyeva. Na tena nibbānadhātuyā ūnattaṃ vā pūrattaṃ vāti asaṅkhyeyyepi kappe buddhesu anuppannesu ekasattopi parinibbātuṃ na sakkoti, tadāpi “tucchā nibbānadhātū”ti na [P 4.0112] sakkā vattuṃ. Buddhakāle ca pana ekekasmiṃ samāgame asaṅkhyeyyāpi sattā amataṃ ārādhenti, tadāpi na sakkā vattuṃ – “pūrā nibbānadhātū”ti.

Regarding 'Ārakā': it is indeed far away. Regarding 'By that there is no deficiency or fullness of the Nibbāna-element': Even when for countless eons Buddhas do not arise, not even a single being is able to attain Parinibbāna; even then, it is not possible to say, 'The Nibbāna-element is empty.' But during the time of a Buddha, in a single assembly, countless beings accomplish the deathless; even then, it is not possible to say, 'The Nibbāna-element is full.'

10. Uposathasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Uposatha Sutta.

20. Dasame nisinno hotīti uposathakaraṇatthāya upāsikāya ratanapāsāde nisinno. Nisajja pana bhikkhūnaṃ cittāni olokento ekaṃ dussīlapuggalaṃ disvā “sacāhaṃ imasmiṃ puggale [V 3.0220] nisinneyeva pātimokkhaṃ uddisissāmi, sattadhā tassa muddhā phalissatī”ti tassa anukampāya tuṇhīyeva ahosi. Abhikkantāti atikkantā parikkhīṇā. Uddhaste aruṇeti uggate aruṇasīse. Nandimukhiyāti tuṭṭhamukhiyā. Aparisuddhā, ānanda, parisāti “asukapuggalo aparisuddho”ti avatvā “aparisuddhā, ānanda, parisā”ti āha. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

20. In the tenth sutta, 'he was seated' means he was seated in the laywoman's jeweled palace for the purpose of performing the Uposatha. And having sat down, while observing the minds of the bhikkhus, he saw one immoral individual and reflected: 'If I recite the Pātimokkha while this person is seated here, his head will split into seven pieces.' Therefore, out of compassion for him, he remained silent. 'Abhikkantā' means past, exhausted. 'Uddhaste aruṇe' means when the head of dawn has risen. 'Nandimukhiyā' means with a delighted face. Regarding 'The assembly is impure, Ānanda': without saying, 'Such-and-such a person is impure,' he said, 'The assembly is impure, Ānanda.' The rest is clear everywhere.

Mahāvaggo dutiyo.

The Second Great Chapter.

3. Gahapativaggo

3. The Chapter on Householders.

1. Paṭhamauggasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the First Ugga Sutta.

21. Tatiyassa paṭhame paññatte āsane nisīdīti tassa kira ghare pañcannaṃ bhikkhusatānaṃ pañca āsanasatāni niccaṃ paññattāneva honti, tesu aññatarasmiṃ āsane nisīdi. Taṃ suṇāhīti te suṇāhi, taṃ vā aṭṭhavidhaṃ acchariyadhammaṃ suṇāhi. Cittaṃ pasīdīti “buddho nu kho na buddho nu kho”ti vitakkamattampi na uppajji, ayameva buddhoti cittuppādo pasanno anāvilo ahosi. Sakāni vā ñātikulānīti attano yāpanamattaṃ dhanaṃ [M 3.0222] gahetvā ñātigharāni gacchatu. Kassa vo dammīti katarapurisassa tumhe dadāmi, ārocetha me attano adhippāyaṃ. Appaṭivibhattāti [P 4.0113] “ettakaṃ dassāmi ettakaṃ na dassāmi, idaṃ dassāmi idaṃ na dassāmī”ti cittaṃ uppādentena hi paṭivibhattā nāma hoti, mayhaṃ pana na evaṃ. Atha kho saṅghikā viya gaṇasantakā viya ca sīlavantehi saddhiṃ sādhāraṇāyeva. Sakkaccaṃyeva payirupāsāmīti sahatthā upaṭṭhahāmi, cittīkārena upasaṅkamāmi.

21. In the first sutta of the third chapter, regarding 'he sat on a prepared seat': it is said that in his house, five hundred seats were always prepared for five hundred bhikkhus. He sat on one of those seats. 'Listen to that' means listen to them, or listen to that eightfold marvelous quality. 'His mind became clear' means not even a thought like, 'Is he the Buddha or is he not the Buddha?' arose; rather, the thought that arose, 'This is indeed the Buddha,' was clear and unclouded. 'Or to his own relatives' means let him go to his relatives’ homes, taking just enough wealth for his own sustenance. 'To whom shall I give you?' means 'To which person shall I give you? Declare to me your own intention.' 'Undivided' means: one who generates the thought, 'I will give this much, I will not give that much; I will give this, I will not give that,' is called 'divided'; but for me it is not so. Rather, my wealth is held in common with the virtuous, like property belonging to the Sangha or to a group. 'I attend carefully' means I serve with my own hands; I approach with respect.

Anacchariyaṃ kho pana maṃ, bhanteti, bhante, yaṃ maṃ devatā upasaṅkamitvā evaṃ ārocenti, idaṃ na acchariyaṃ. Yaṃ panāhaṃ tatonidānaṃ cittassa uṇṇatiṃ nābhijānāmi, taṃ eva acchariyanti [V 3.0221] vadati. Sādhu sādhu, bhikkhūti ettha kiñcāpi bhikkhuṃ āmanteti, upāsakasseva pana veyyākaraṇasampahaṃsane esa sādhukāroti veditabbo.

'It is no wonder to me, venerable sir,' means: 'Venerable sir, that deities approach me and inform me thus, this is not marvelous. But that I do not, because of that, perceive any elation of mind, that indeed is marvelous,' he says. 'Good, good, bhikkhu,' means that here, although he addresses a bhikkhu, this approval should be understood as an appreciation of the layperson’s explanation.

2. Dutiyauggasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Second Ugga Sutta.

22. Dutiye nāgavaneti tassa kira seṭṭhino nāgavanaṃ nāma uyyānaṃ, so tattha purebhattaṃ gandhamālādīni gāhāpetvā uyyānakīḷikaṃ kīḷitukāmo gantvā paricāriyamāno bhagavantaṃ addasa. Saha dassanenevassa purimanayeneva cittaṃ pasīdi, surāpānena ca uppannamando taṅkhaṇaṃyeva pahīyi. Taṃ sandhāyevamāha. Oṇojesinti udakaṃ hatthe pātetvā adāsiṃ. Asukoti amuko. Samacittova demīti “imassa thokaṃ, imassa bahuka”nti evaṃ cittanānattaṃ na karomi, deyyadhammaṃ pana ekasadisaṃ karomīti dasseti. Ārocentīti [P 4.0114] ākāse ṭhatvā ārocenti. Natthi taṃ saṃyojananti iminā upāsako attano anāgāmiphalaṃ byākaroti.

22. In the second sutta, regarding 'Nāgavana': it is said that a wealthy man had a park named Nāgavana. He went there before the midday meal, intending to enjoy park pastimes after arranging for perfumes, garlands, and so forth. While being attended to, he saw the Blessed One. At the mere sight of him, his mind became serene as before, and the intoxication produced by drinking spirits vanished at that very moment. It is with reference to this that he spoke. 'I offered' means: 'I poured water on the hand and gave.' 'Such a one' means so-and-so. 'With an equal mind I give' shows that: 'I do not make distinctions in my mind, thinking, "A little for this one, much for that one," but I make the gift the same for all.' 'They announce' means they announce while standing in the sky. With the words, 'There is no fetter,' the lay disciple declares his attainment of the fruit of non-returning.

3. Paṭhamahatthakasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the First Hatthaka Sutta.

23. Tatiye hatthako āḷavakoti bhagavatā āḷavakayakkhassa hatthato hatthehi sampaṭicchitattā hatthakoti laddhanāmo rājakumāro. Sīlavāti pañcasīladasasīlena sīlavā. Cāgavāti cāgasampanno. Kaccittha, bhanteti, bhante, kacci ettha bhagavato byākaraṇaṭṭhāne. Appicchoti adhigamappicchatāya appiccho.

23. In the third sutta, 'Hatthaka of Āḷavī' refers to the prince who obtained the name 'Hatthaka' because he was received by the hands of the Blessed One from the hand of the yakkha Āḷavaka. 'Virtuous' means virtuous through the five precepts and the ten precepts. 'Generous' means endowed with generosity. 'Kaccittha, bhante' means: 'Venerable Sir, is it so here in the Blessed One's exposition?' 'Of few wishes' means he is of few wishes on account of having few wishes regarding his attainment.

4. Dutiyahatthakasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Second Hatthaka Sutta.

24. Catutthe [M 3.0223] pañcamattehi upāsakasatehīti sotāpannasakadāgāmīnaṃyeva ariyasāvakaupāsakānaṃ pañcahi satehi parivuto bhuttapātarāso gandhamālavilepenacuṇṇāni gahetvā yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami. Saṅgahavatthūnīti saṅgaṇhanakāraṇāni. Tehāhanti tehi ahaṃ. Taṃ dānena saṅgaṇhāmīti naṅgalabalibaddabhattabījādīni ceva gandhamālamūlādīni ca datvā saṅgaṇhāmi. Peyyavajjenāti amma, tāta, bhātara, bhaginītiādikena kaṇṇasukhena mudukena piyavacanena saṅgaṇhāmi. Atthacariyāyāti “imassa dānena vā piyavacanena vā kiccaṃ natthi, atthacariyāya saṅgaṇhitabbayuttako [V 3.0222] aya”nti ñatvā uppannakiccanittharaṇasaṅkhātāya atthacariyāya saṅgaṇhāmi. Samānattatāyāti “imassa dānādīhi kiccaṃ natthi, samānattatāya [P 4.0115] saṅgaṇhitabbo aya”nti ekato khādanapivananisajjādīhi attanā samānaṃ katvā saṅgaṇhāmi. Daliddassa kho no tathā sotabbaṃ maññantīti daliddassa kiñci dātuṃ vā kātuṃ vā asakkontassa, yathā daliddassa no tathā sotabbaṃ maññanti, mama pana sotabbaṃ maññanti, dinnovāde tiṭṭhanti, na me anusāsaniṃ atikkamitabbaṃ maññanti. Yoni kho tyāyanti upāyo kho te ayaṃ. Imesu pana dvīsupi suttesu satthārā sīlacāgapaññā missakā kathitāti veditabbā.

24. In the fourth sutta, 'with about five hundred lay followers' means: surrounded by five hundred noble lay disciples who were only stream-enterers and once-returners. Having eaten his morning meal and taken perfumes, garlands, unguents, and powders, he approached the Blessed One. 'The bases of benefaction' means the causes for cherishing. 'Tehāhaṃ' means 'by them, I.' 'I cherish them with giving' means: I cherish them by giving plows, oxen, food, seeds, and so on, as well as perfumes, garlands, roots, and so on. 'With kind speech' means: I cherish them with speech that is pleasant to the ear, gentle, and endearing, such as 'mother,' 'father,' 'brother,' 'sister,' and so on. 'With beneficial conduct' means: knowing, 'For this person, there is no need for giving or kind speech; he is fit to be cherished through beneficial conduct,' I cherish him with beneficial conduct, which is defined as carrying out a task that has arisen. 'With impartiality' means: knowing, 'For this person, there is no need for giving and so on; he is one who should be cherished through impartiality,' I cherish him by making him equal to myself in eating, drinking, sitting together, and so on. Regarding 'They do not think a poor person should be listened to in the same way': this refers to a poor person who is unable to give or do anything. They do not think such a person should be listened to in the same way, but they think that I should be listened to; they abide by the advice given; they do not think my instruction should be transgressed. 'Yoni kho tyāyaṃ' means: this is indeed a suitable method for you. Furthermore, it should be known that in both these suttas, the Teacher has taught virtue, generosity, and wisdom as mixed.

5-6. Mahānāmasuttādivaṇṇanā

5-6. Commentary on the Mahānāma Sutta and Others.

25-26. Pañcame atthūpaparikkhitā hotīti atthānatthaṃ kāraṇākāraṇaṃ upaparikkhitā hoti. Chaṭṭhe saddhāsīlacāgā missakā kathitā.

In the fifth sutta, 'one is an examiner of the meaning' means one investigates what is beneficial and unbeneficial, what is a cause and not a cause. In the sixth sutta, faith, virtue, and generosity are taught as mixed.

7. Paṭhamabalasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the First Sutta on Powers.

27. Sattame ujjhattibalāti ujjhānabalā. Bālānañhi “yaṃ asuko idañcidañca āha, maṃ so āha, na añña”nti evaṃ ujjhānameva balaṃ. Nijjhattibalāti “na idaṃ evaṃ, evaṃ nāmeta”nti atthānatthanijjhāpanaṃyeva balaṃ. Paṭisaṅkhānabalāti paccavekkhaṇabalā. Khantibalāti adhivāsanabalā.

27. In the seventh discourse, 'the power of scorn' (ujjhattibalā) means the power of scorning (ujjhānabalā). For fools, indeed, scorn itself is their power, thinking: 'So-and-so said this and that; he said it to me, not to another.' 'The power of contemplation' (nijjhattibalā) means the power which is precisely the contemplation of what is beneficial and not beneficial, thinking: 'This is not so; it is indeed so.' 'The power of reflection' (paṭisaṅkhānabalā) means the power of reviewing (paccavekkhaṇabalā). 'The power of patience' (khantibalā) means the power of forbearance (adhivāsanabalā).

8. Dutiyabalasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Second Discourse on Powers.

28. Aṭṭhame [M 3.0224] balānīti ñāṇabalāni. Āsavānaṃ khayaṃ paṭijānātīti arahattaṃ paṭijānāti. Aniccatoti hutvā abhāvākārena. Yathābhūtanti yathāsabhāvato. Sammappaññāyāti sahavipassanāya maggapaññāya. Aṅgārakāsūpamāti [P 4.0116] santāpanaṭṭhena aṅgārakāsuyā upamitā ime kāmāti. Vivekaninnanti phalasamāpattivasena nibbānaninnaṃ. Vivekaṭṭhanti kilesehi vajjitaṃ dūrībhūtaṃ vā. Nekkhammābhiratanti pabbajjābhirataṃ. Byantibhūtanti vigatantabhūtaṃ ekadesenāpi anallīnaṃ visaṃyuttaṃ visaṃsaṭṭhaṃ. Āsavaṭṭhāniyehīti sampayogavasena āsavānaṃ kāraṇabhūtehi, kilesadhammehīti [V 3.0223] attho. Atha vā byantibhūtanti vigatavāyanti attho. Kuto? Sabbaso āsavaṭṭhāniyehi dhammehi, sabbehi tebhūmakadhammehīti attho. Imasmiṃ sutte ariyamaggo lokiyalokuttaro kathito.

28. In the eighth discourse, 'powers' (balāni) means the powers of knowledge (ñāṇabalāni). 'He declares the destruction of the taints' (āsavānaṃ khayaṃ paṭijānāti) means he declares the fruit of Arahantship. 'As impermanent' (aniccatoti) means in the manner of non-existence after having been. 'As it really is' (yathābhūtaṃ) means in accordance with its nature. 'With right wisdom' (sammappaññāyāti) means with the path-wisdom that is together with insight. 'Like a pit of glowing coals' (aṅgārakāsūpamāti) means these sensual pleasures are compared with a pit of glowing coals because of their tormenting nature. 'Inclined to seclusion' (vivekaninnanti) means inclined to Nibbāna by the power of fruition attainment. 'Abiding in seclusion' (vivekaṭṭhanti) means devoid of defilements, or having become far from them. 'Delighting in renunciation' (nekkhammābhiratanti) means delighting in the going forth. 'Completely free' (byantibhūtanti) means having become free from the end, that is, base craving; not clinging even in part; disjoined; unmingled. 'From the states that are the basis for the taints' (āsavaṭṭhāniyehīti) means from the defiling dhammas which have become the cause of the taints by the power of association; this is the meaning. Alternatively, 'completely free' (byantibhūtanti) means having reached a state free from craving; this is the meaning. From what? From all the dhammas of the three planes which are the cause of the taints in every way; this is the meaning. In this sutta, the noble path, both worldly and supramundane, is taught.

9. Akkhaṇasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Akkhaṇa Sutta.

29. Navame khaṇe kiccāni karotīti khaṇakicco, okāsaṃ labhitvāva kiccāni karotīti attho. Dhammoti catusaccadhammo. Opasamikoti kilesūpasamāvaho. Parinibbāyikoti kilesaparinibbānakaro. Catumaggañāṇasaṅkhātaṃ sambodhiṃ gacchati sampāpuṇātīti sambodhagāmī. Dīghāyukaṃ devanikāyanti idaṃ asaññaṃ devanikāyaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Aviññātāresūti ativiya aviññūsu.

29. In the ninth discourse, one who 'performs functions at the opportune moment' (khaṇe kiccāni karoti) is called 'one with timely function' (khaṇakicco); the meaning is that one performs functions only after obtaining the opportunity. 'Dhamma' (dhammo) refers to the Dhamma of the Four Noble Truths. 'Pacifying' (opasamiko) means bringing about the pacification of defilements. 'Leading to complete Nibbāna' (parinibbāyiko) means causing the complete extinguishing of defilements. One 'reaches or attains enlightenment' (sambodhiṃ gacchati sampāpuṇāti), which is designated as the four path-knowledges, thus one is called 'going to enlightenment' (sambodhagāmī). 'Long-lived divine realm' (dīghāyukaṃ devanikāyaṃ): this is said with reference to the realm of non-percipient beings. 'Among the unknowing' (aviññātāresūti) means among those who are extremely unknowing.

Suppavediteti sukathite. Antarāyikāti antarāyakarā. Khaṇo ve mā upaccagāti ayaṃ laddho khaṇo mā atikkami. Idha [P 4.0117] ceva naṃ virādhetīti sace koci pamattacārī idha imaṃ khaṇaṃ labhitvāpi saddhammassa niyāmataṃ ariyamaggaṃ virādheti na sampādeti. Atītatthoti hāpitattho. Cirattaṃ anutapissatīti cirarattaṃ socissati. Yathā hi “asukaṭṭhāne bhaṇḍaṃ samuppanna”nti sutvā eko vāṇijo na gaccheyya, aññe gantvā gaṇheyyuṃ, tesaṃ taṃ aṭṭhaguṇampi dasaguṇampi bhaveyya. Atha itaro “mama attho atikkanto”ti anutapeyya, evaṃ yo idha khaṇaṃ labhitvā appaṭipajjanto saddhammassa niyāmataṃ virādheti, so ayaṃ vāṇijova atītattho [M 3.0225] ciraṃ anutapissati socissati. Kiñca bhiyyo avijjānivutoti tathā. Paccavidunti paṭivijjhiṃsu. Saṃvarāti sīlasaṃvarā. Māradheyyaparānugeti māradheyyasaṅkhātaṃ saṃsāraṃ anugate. Pāraṅgatāti nibbānaṃ gatā. Ye pattā āsavakkhayanti ye arahattaṃ pattā. Evamidha gāthāsu vaṭṭavivaṭṭaṃ kathitaṃ.

'Well-proclaimed' (suppavedite) means well-spoken. 'Obstructive' (antarāyikā) means causing obstacles. 'Do not let the moment pass you by' (khaṇo ve mā upaccagā) means may this obtained moment not pass by. 'If one fails here' (idha ceva naṃ virādheti) means if some heedless person, here in this Dispensation, even after obtaining this moment, fails in the noble path, which is the fixed nature of the good Dhamma, and does not accomplish it. 'Lost opportunity' (atītattho) means the benefit is lost. 'Will long lament' (cirattaṃ anutapissatīti) means will grieve for a long time. Just as when hearing, 'Goods have appeared in such and such a place,' one merchant might not go, while others go and take them, and for them the gain might be eightfold or tenfold. Then the other would lament, thinking, 'My opportunity has passed.' So too, one who, having obtained this moment here, does not practice and fails in the fixed nature of the good Dhamma, like that merchant, having lost the opportunity, will long lament and grieve. Furthermore, regarding the verse beginning 'Shrouded in ignorance': 'They understood' (paccaviduṃ) means they penetrated. 'Restraint' (saṃvarā) means restraint by way of morality. 'Following Māra’s sway' (māradheyyaparānugeti) means following after saṃsāra, which is called the domain of Māra. 'Gone beyond' (pāraṅgatā) means gone to Nibbāna. 'Those who have reached the destruction of the taints' (ye pattā āsavakkhayaṃ) means those who have attained Arahantship. Thus, here in the verses, the round of existence and that which is free from the round are taught.

10. Anuruddhamahāvitakkasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Discourse on the Great Man's Thoughts of Anuruddha.

30. Dasame cetīsūti cetināmakānaṃ rājūnaṃ nivāsaṭṭhānattā evaṃladdhavohāre raṭṭhe. Pācīnavaṃsadāyeti dasabalassa vasanaṭṭhānato pācīnadisāya [P 4.0118] ṭhite vaṃsadāye nīlobhāsehi veḷūhi sañchanne [V 3.0224] araññe. Evaṃ cetaso parivitakko udapādīti thero kira pabbajitvā paṭhamaantovassamhiyeva samāpattilābhī hutvā sahassalokadhātudassanasamatthaṃ dibbacakkhuñāṇaṃ uppādesi. So sāriputtattherassa santikaṃ gantvā evamāha – “idhāhaṃ, āvuso sāriputta, dibbena cakkhunā visuddhena atikkantamānusakena sahassalokaṃ olokemi. Āraddhaṃ kho pana me vīriyaṃ asallīnaṃ, upaṭṭhitā sati asammuṭṭhā, passaddho kāyo asāraddho, samāhitaṃ cittaṃ ekaggaṃ. Atha ca pana me anupādāya āsavehi cittaṃ na vimuccatī”ti. Atha naṃ thero āha – “yaṃ kho te, āvuso anuruddha, evaṃ hoti ‘ahaṃ dibbena cakkhunā…pe… olokemī’ti, idaṃ te mānasmiṃ. Yampi te, āvuso, anuruddha evaṃ hoti ‘āraddhaṃ kho pana me vīriyaṃ…pe… ekagga’nti, idaṃ te uddhaccasmiṃ. Yampi te, āvuso anuruddha, evaṃ hoti ‘atha ca pana me anupādāya āsavehi cittaṃ na vimuccatī’ti, idaṃ te kukkuccasmiṃ. Sādhu vatāyasmā anuruddho ime tayo dhamme pahāya ime tayo dhamme amanasikaritvā amatāya dhātuyā cittaṃ upasaṃharatū”ti evamassa thero kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathesi. So kammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvā satthāraṃ āpucchitvā cetiraṭṭhaṃ gantvā samaṇadhammaṃ karonto aṭṭhamāsaṃ caṅkamena vītināmesi. So padhānaveganimmathitattā kilantakāyo ekassa veḷugumbassa heṭṭhā nisīdi. Athassāyaṃ evaṃ cetaso parivitakko udapādi, esa mahāpurisavitakko uppajjīti attho.

30. In the tenth discourse. 'In the land of the Cetīs' (cetīsu) means: in the country that had received this designation because it was the dwelling place of kings named Ceti. 'In the Eastern Bamboo Grove' (pācīnavaṃsadāye) means: in a bamboo grove situated to the east of the Ten-Powered One's dwelling place, in a forest covered with dark green bamboos. 'Thus this reflection arose in his mind' (evaṃ cetaso parivitakko udapādi) means: The elder, it is said, after going forth, became an attainer of absorption in the very first rains-residence and developed the divine eye knowledge capable of seeing the thousandfold world-system. He approached the Venerable Sāriputta and said: 'Friend Sāriputta, here I survey the thousandfold world-system with the divine eye, purified and surpassing the human. My energy is aroused and unflagging, my mindfulness is established and unconfused, my body is tranquil and unagitated, my mind is concentrated and one-pointed. Yet, my mind is not liberated from the taints without clinging.' Then the elder said to him: 'Friend Anuruddha, that you think, “I survey the thousandfold world-system with the divine eye…,” this is conceit in you. That you think, “My energy is aroused… my mind is one-pointed,” this is restlessness in you. And that you think, “Yet, my mind is not liberated from the taints without clinging,” this is worry in you. It would be good if the Venerable Anuruddha, having abandoned these three states and not attending to these three states, would direct his mind to the deathless element.' Thus, the elder instructed him in the meditation subject. Having received the meditation subject, he took leave of the Teacher and went to the Cetī country. Practicing the ascetic’s duty, he spent half a month in walking meditation. His body being weary and exhausted by the force of his striving, he sat down at the base of a bamboo clump. Then this reflection arose in his mind in this way; the meaning is: this thought of a great man arose.

Appicchassāti [M 3.0226] ettha paccayappiccho, adhigamappiccho, pariyattiappiccho, dhutaṅgappicchoti cattāro appicchā. Tattha [P 4.0119] paccayappiccho bahuṃ dente appaṃ gaṇhāti, appaṃ dente appataraṃ gaṇhāti, na anavasesaggāhī hoti. Adhigamappiccho majjhantikatthero viya attano adhigamaṃ aññesaṃ jānituṃ na deti. Pariyattiappiccho tepiṭakopi samāno na bahussutabhāvaṃ jānāpetukāmo hoti sāketatissatthero viya. Dhutaṅgappiccho dhutaṅgapariharaṇabhāvaṃ aññesaṃ jānituṃ na deti dvebhātikattheresu jeṭṭhatthero viya. Vatthu visuddhimagge kathitaṃ. Ayaṃ dhammoti evaṃ santaguṇaniguhanena ca paṭiggahaṇe mattaññutāya ca appicchassa puggalassa ayaṃ navalokuttaradhammo sampajjati, no mahicchassa. Evaṃ sabbattha yojetabbaṃ.

`Appicchassa` (of one with few wishes) here refers to four types of those with few wishes: one with few wishes regarding requisites, one with few wishes regarding attainments, one with few wishes regarding learning, and one with few wishes regarding ascetic practices. Among these, one with few wishes regarding requisites takes little when much is given, takes even less when little is given, and is not one who takes everything without remainder. One with few wishes regarding attainments does not let others know of their attainments, like the Elder Majjhantika. One with few wishes regarding learning, even if a master of the Tipiṭaka, does not wish to make known their great learning, like the Elder Tissa of Sāketa. One with few wishes regarding ascetic practices does not let others know of their observance of ascetic practices, like the elder of the two brother-theras. The story is told in the Visuddhimagga. `Ayaṃ dhammo` (This Dhamma) means: thus, by concealing one's existing virtues and by knowing the measure in accepting, this ninefold supramundane Dhamma is accomplished for a person of few wishes, not for one of many wishes. This should be applied in all cases.

Santuṭṭhassāti catūsu paccayesu tīhi santosehi santuṭṭhassa. Pavivittassāti kāyacittaupadhivivekehi vivittassa. Tattha kāyaviveko nāma gaṇasaṅgaṇikaṃ vinodetvā ārambhavatthuvasena ekībhāvo. Ekībhāvamatteneva kammaṃ na nipphajjatīti kasiṇaparikammaṃ katvā aṭṭha samāpattiyo [V 3.0225] nibbatteti, ayaṃ cittaviveko nāma. Samāpattimatteneva kammaṃ na nipphajjatīti jhānaṃ pādakaṃ katvā saṅkhāre sammasitvā saha paṭisambhidāhi arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti, ayaṃ sabbākārato upadhiviveko nāma. Tenāha bhagavā – “kāyaviveko ca vivekaṭṭhakāyānaṃ nekkhammābhiratānaṃ, cittaviveko ca parisuddhacittānaṃ paramavodānappattānaṃ, upadhiviveko ca nirupadhīnaṃ puggalānaṃ visaṅkhāragatāna”nti (mahāni. 7, 49).

`Santuṭṭhassa` means: of one who is content with the four requisites through the three kinds of contentment. `Pavivittassa` means: of one who is secluded through bodily, mental, and substratum seclusion. Herein, bodily seclusion is the state of being alone by means of the grounds for arousing energy, after having dispelled association with a group. Thinking, 'The task is not accomplished by the mere state of being alone,' one develops the eight attainments after doing the preliminary work on the kasiṇas; this is called mental seclusion. Thinking, 'The task is not accomplished by mere attainment,' one makes jhāna the basis, and after contemplating the formations, one attains arahantship together with the analytical knowledges; this, in all aspects, is called substratum seclusion. Therefore the Blessed One said: 'There is bodily seclusion for those whose bodies are established in seclusion, who delight in renunciation; and mental seclusion for those with purified minds who have reached supreme purification; and substratum seclusion for persons without substrata who have reached the unconditioned.'

Saṅgaṇikārāmassāti [P 4.0120] gaṇasaṅgaṇikāya ceva kilesasaṅgaṇikāya ca ratassa. Āraddhavīriyassāti kāyikacetasikavīriyavasena āraddhavīriyassa. Upaṭṭhitassatissāti catusatipaṭṭhānavasena upaṭṭhitassatissa. Samāhitassāti ekaggacittassa. Paññavatoti kammassakatapaññāya paññavato.

`Saṅgaṇikārāmassa` means: of one who delights in association with a group and in association with defilements. `Āraddhavīriyassa` means: of one who has aroused energy by way of bodily and mental energy. `Upaṭṭhitassatissa` means: of one whose mindfulness is established by way of the four foundations of mindfulness. `Samāhitassa` means: of one whose mind is concentrated. `Paññavato` means: of one who is wise with the wisdom that kamma is one's own property.

Sādhu sādhūti therassa vitakkaṃ sampahaṃsento evamāha. Imaṃ aṭṭhamanti satta nidhī laddhapurisassa aṭṭhamaṃ dento viya, satta maṇiratanāni, satta hatthiratanāni, satta assaratanāni laddhapurisassa aṭṭhamaṃ dento viya satta mahāpurisavitakke vitakketvā ṭhitassa aṭṭhamaṃ ācikkhanto evamāha. Nippapañcārāmassāti [M 3.0227] taṇhāmānadiṭṭhipapañcarahitattā nippapañcasaṅkhāte nibbānapade abhiratassa. Itaraṃ tasseva vevacanaṃ. Papañcārāmassāti yathāvuttesu papañcesu abhiratassa. Itaraṃ tasseva vevacanaṃ.

Saying, 'Good, good,' he spoke thus, gladdening the elder's thought. He spoke thus, explaining this eighth thought: just as one might give an eighth pot of treasure to a person who has found seven, or as one might give an eighth to a person who has obtained the seven gem-treasures, the seven elephant-treasures, and the seven horse-treasures, so to one who stood having thought the seven thoughts of a great man, he taught the eighth thought of a great man. `Nippapañcārāmassa` (of one who delights in non-proliferation) means: of one who delights in the state of Nibbāna, which is called 'non-proliferation' because it is free from the proliferation of craving, conceit, and views. The other term (`nippapañcaratino`) is a synonym for this. `Papañcārāmassa` (of one who delights in proliferation) means: of one who delights in the aforementioned proliferations. The other term (`papañcaratino`) is a synonym for this.

Yatoti yadā. Tatoti tadā. Nānārattānanti nilapītalohitodātavaṇṇehi nānārajanehi rattānaṃ. Paṃsukūlanti tevīsatiyā khettesu ṭhitapaṃsukūlacīvaraṃ. Khāyissatīti yathā tassa pubbaṇhasamayādīsu yasmiṃ samaye yaṃ icchati, tasmiṃ samaye taṃ pārupantassa so dussakaraṇḍako manāpo hutvā khāyati, evaṃ tuyhampi cīvarasantosamahāariyavaṃsena tuṭṭhassa viharato paṃsukūlacīvaraṃ khāyissati upaṭṭhahissati. Ratiyāti ratiatthāya. Aparitassāyāti taṇhādiṭṭhiparitassanāhi aparitassanatthāya. Phāsuvihārāyāti [P 4.0121] sukhavihāratthāya. Okkamanāya nibbānassāti amataṃ nibbānaṃ otaraṇatthāya.

"When" means at whatever time. "Then" means at that time. "Of various colors" means dyed with various dyes of blue, yellow, red, and white colors. "Rag-robe" means a rag-robe situated in the twenty-three fields of disposal. "Will appear" means: just as for one wearing whatever he wishes at any time, such as in the early morning, that chest of clothes becomes pleasing and appears, so too for you, dwelling pleased with the great noble lineage of contentment with robes, the rag-robe will appear and be present. "For delight" means for the purpose of delight. "For non-agitation" means for the purpose of not being agitated by the agitations of craving and views. "For comfortable living" means for the purpose of dwelling comfortably. "For entering into Nibbāna" means for the purpose of entering into Nibbāna, the deathless.

Piṇḍiyālopabhojananti gāmanigamarājadhānīsu jaṅghābalaṃ nissāya gharapaṭipāṭiyā carantena laddhapiṇḍiyālopabhojanaṃ. Khāyissatīti tassa gahapatino nānaggarasabhojanaṃ viya upaṭṭhahissati[V 3.0226]. Santuṭṭhassa viharatoti piṇḍapātasantosamahāariyavaṃsena santuṭṭhassa viharato. Rukkhamūlasenāsanaṃ khāyissatīti tassa gahapatino tebhūmakapāsāde gandhakusumavāsasugandhaṃ kūṭāgāraṃ viya rukkhamūlaṃ upaṭṭhahissati. Santuṭṭhassāti senāsanasantosamahāariyavaṃsena santuṭṭhassa. Tiṇasanthārakoti tiṇehi vā paṇṇehi vā bhūmiyaṃ vā phalakapāsāṇatalāni vā aññatarasmiṃ santhatasanthato. Pūtimuttanti yaṃkiñci muttaṃ. Taṅkhaṇe gahitampi pūtimuttameva vuccati duggandhattā. Santuṭṭhassa viharatoti gilānapaccayabhesajjaparikkhārasantosena santuṭṭhassa viharato.

"Almsfood" means almsfood obtained by a monk wandering from house to house in villages, towns, and royal cities, relying on the strength of his legs. "Will appear" means it will appear like the meal with various excellent flavors of that householder. "For one dwelling content" means for a monk dwelling, content with the great noble lineage of contentment with almsfood. "A lodging at the root of a tree will appear" means: the root of a tree will appear like that householder's gabled mansion in a three-storied palace, fragrant with the scent of perfumes and flowers. "For one who is content" means for one who is content with the great noble lineage of contentment with lodging. "A grass spread" means a spread made of grass or leaves, on the ground, or on one of another kind such as a wooden plank or a stone slab. "Putrid urine" means any kind of urine. Because of its foul smell, even if taken at that moment, it is called putrid urine. "For one dwelling content" means for a monk dwelling, content with the requisite of medicine as a support for the sick.

Iti bhagavā catūsu ṭhānesu arahattaṃ pakkhipanto kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathetvā “katarasenāsane nu kho vasantassa kammaṭṭhānaṃ sappāyaṃ bhavissatī”ti āvajjento “tasmiññeva vasantassā”ti ñatvā tena hi tvaṃ, anuruddhātiādimāha. Pavivittassa viharatoti tīhi vivekehi vivittassa viharantassa. Uyyojanikapaṭisaṃyuttanti [P 4.0122] uyyojanikeheva vacanehi paṭisaṃyuttaṃ, tesaṃ upaṭṭhānagamanakaṃyevāti attho. Papañcanirodheti nibbānapade[M 3.0228]. Pakkhandatīti ārammaṇakaraṇavasena pakkhandati. Pasīdatītiādīsupi ārammaṇavaseneva pasīdanasantiṭṭhanamuccanā veditabbā. Iti bhagavā cetiraṭṭhe pācīnavaṃsadāye āyasmato anuruddhassa kathite aṭṭha mahāpurisavitakke puna bhesakaḷāvanamahāvihāre nisīditvā bhikkhusaṅghassa vitthārena kathesi.
