Thus, the Blessed One, having inserted Arahantship in four places and taught the meditation subject, reflected, “In what kind of lodging will the meditation subject be suitable for the Venerable Anuruddha who is dwelling?” Knowing, “For Anuruddha dwelling in that very Pācīnavaṃsa bamboo grove, the meditation subject will be suitable,” he spoke the words beginning with, “Well then, Anuruddha….” “For one dwelling secluded” means for one dwelling secluded with the three kinds of seclusion. “Connected with urging” means connected with words of urging and reproaching; the meaning is just the action of going to attend to them. “In the cessation of proliferation” means in the state of Nibbāna. “It runs into” means it runs into it by way of making it an object. In “becomes serene,” and so on, becoming serene, being well-established, and inclining should be understood as being by way of the object. Thus, the Blessed One, having taught the eight thoughts of a great man to the Venerable Anuruddha in the Eastern Bamboo Grove in the Ceti country, again sat down in the Bhesakaḷāvana Mahāvihāra and taught them in detail to the Saṅgha of monks.

Manomayenāti manena nibbattitakāyopi manomayoti vuccati manena gatakāyopi, idha manena gatakāyaṃ sandhāyevamāha. Yathā me ahu saṅkappoti yathā mayhaṃ vitakko ahosi, tato uttari aṭṭhamaṃ mahāpurisavitakkaṃ dassento tato uttariṃ desayi. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

“By the mind-made”: a body produced by the mind is called mind-made, and a body that goes by means of the mind is also called mind-made. Here, it is said with reference to a body that goes by means of the mind. “As the thought occurred to me” means: as a thought occurred to me. Wishing to show the eighth thought of a great man beyond that, he taught what is further than that. The rest is clear everywhere.

Gahapativaggo tatiyo.

The Third, the Householder Chapter.

4. Dānavaggo

4. The Chapter on Giving

1. Paṭhamadānasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the First Discourse on Giving

31. Catutthassa [V 3.0227] paṭhame āsajja dānaṃ detīti patvā dānaṃ deti. Āgataṃ disvā taṃ muhuttaṃyeva nisīdāpetvā sakkāraṃ katvā dānaṃ deti, dassāmīti na kilameti. Bhayāti “ayaṃ adāyako akārako”ti garahabhayā, apāyabhayā vā. Adāsi [P 4.0123] meti mayhaṃ pubbe esa idaṃ nāma adāsīti deti. Dassati meti anāgate idaṃ nāma dassatīti deti. Sāhu dānanti dānaṃ nāma sādhu sundaraṃ buddhādīhi paṇḍitehi pasatthanti deti. Cittālaṅkāracittaparikkhāratthaṃ dānaṃ detīti samathavipassanācittassa alaṅkāratthañceva parikkhāratthañca deti. Dānañhi cittaṃ muduṃ karoti. Yena laddho, so “laddhaṃ me”ti muducitto hoti. Yena dinnaṃ, sopi “dinnaṃ mayā”ti muducitto hoti. Iti ubhinnaṃ cittaṃ muduṃ karoti. Teneva “adantadamana”nti vuccati. Yathāha –

31. In the first [discourse] of the fourth [chapter], ‘one gives a gift after being approached’ (āsajja dānaṃ deti) means one gives a gift upon the recipient's arrival. Having seen the one who has come, one makes them sit at that very moment, honors them, and then gives the gift, without causing them weariness by saying, ‘I will give.’ ‘Out of fear’ (bhayā) means out of fear of blame—thinking, ‘This person is a non-giver, a non-doer’—or out of fear of the plane of misery. ‘He gave to me’ (adāsi me) means one gives, thinking, ‘In the past, this person gave me such-and-such.’ ‘He will give to me’ (dassati me) means one gives, thinking, ‘In the future, this person will give me such-and-such.’ ‘Giving is good’ (sāhu dānaṃ) means one gives, thinking, ‘Giving is indeed good and excellent, praised by the wise such as the Buddhas.’ ‘One gives a gift for the adornment and equipment of the mind’ (cittālaṅkāracittaparikkhāratthaṃ dānaṃ deti) means one gives for the sake of both the adornment and the equipment for the mind of serenity and insight. Indeed, a gift softens the mind. The one who has received it becomes soft-minded, thinking, ‘I have received.’ The one who has given it also becomes soft-minded, thinking, ‘I have given.’ Thus, it softens the minds of both. Therefore, it is called ‘the taming of the untamed.’ As it is said:

“Adantadamanaṃ [M 3.0229] dānaṃ, adānaṃ dantadūsakaṃ;

Dānena piyavācāya, unnamanti namanti cā”ti.

“Giving is the taming of the untamed; not giving corrupts the tamed. Through giving and kind speech, they rise up and they bow down.”

Imesu pana aṭṭhasu dānesu cittālaṅkāradānameva uttamanti.

Now, among these eight kinds of giving, the gift for the adornment of the mind is the supreme.

2. Dutiyadānasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Second Discourse on Giving

32. Dutiye saddhāti yāya saddhāya dānaṃ deti, sā saddhā. Hiriyanti yāya hiriyā dānaṃ deti, sāva adhippetā. Kusalañca dānanti anavajjañca dānaṃ. Diviyanti divaṅgamaṃ.

32. In the second [discourse], ‘faith’ (saddhā) means the faith with which one gives a gift; that faith is intended. ‘Conscience’ (hiriyā) means the conscience with which one gives a gift; that very [conscience] is intended. ‘And wholesome giving’ (kusalañca dānaṃ) means blameless giving. ‘Celestial’ (diviyaṃ) means leading to a celestial realm.

3. Dānavatthusuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Discourse on the Bases for Giving

33. Tatiye dānavatthūnīti dānakāraṇāni. Chandā dānaṃ detīti pemena dānaṃ deti. Dosāti [V 3.0228] dosena kuddho hutvā yaṃ atthi, taṃ vegena gaṇhitvā deti. Mohāti mohena mūḷho deti. Bhayāti garahabhayena vā apāyabhayena vā, tassa tasseva vā pana bhayena deti. Kulavaṃsanti kulapaveṇiṃ.

33. In the third [discourse], ‘bases for giving’ (dānavatthūni) means the reasons for giving. ‘One gives a gift out of preference’ (chandā dānaṃ deti) means one gives a gift out of affection. ‘Out of aversion’ (dosā) means, having become angry out of aversion, one takes whatever there is and gives it quickly. ‘Out of delusion’ (mohā) means one gives, being deluded by delusion. ‘Out of fear’ (bhayā) means one gives out of fear of blame, or fear of the plane of misery, or out of fear of that very thing. ‘Family lineage’ (kulavaṃsaṃ) means the family tradition.

4. Khettasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Discourse on the Field

34. Catutthe [P 4.0124] na mahapphalaṃ hotīti dhaññaphalena mahapphalaṃ na hoti. Na mahassādanti yampissa phalaṃ hoti, tassa assādo na mahā hoti mandassādaṃ na madhuraṃ. Na phātiseyyanti seyyāpissa na hoti vuḍḍhi, tassa mahantaṃ vīhithambhasannivesaṃ na hotīti attho. Unnāmaninnāmīti thalaninnavasena visamatalaṃ. Tattha thale udakaṃ na saṇṭhāti, ninne atibahu tiṭṭhati. Pāsāṇasakkharikanti pattharitvā ṭhitapiṭṭhipāsāṇehi ca khuddakapāsāṇehi ca sakkharāhi ca samannāgataṃ. Ūsaranti ubbhinnaloṇaṃ. Na ca gambhīrasitanti thaddhabhūmitāya gambhīrānugataṃ, naṅgalamaggaṃ katvā kasituṃ na sakkā hoti, uttānanaṅgalamaggameva hoti. Na āyasampannanti na udakāgamanasampannaṃ. Na apāyasampannanti pacchābhāge udakaniggamanamaggasampannaṃ na hoti. Na mātikāsampannanti na khuddakamahantīhi udakamātikāhi sampannaṃ hoti[M 3.0230]. Na mariyādasampannanti na kedāramariyādāhi sampannaṃ. Na mahapphalantiādīni sabbāni vipākaphalavaseneva veditabbāni.

34. In the fourth [discourse], ‘it is not of great fruit’ means it is not of great fruit in terms of grain. ‘Not of great flavor’ means whatever fruit it has, its flavor is not great; it is of little flavor and not sweet. ‘Not thriving’ means its excellence is not great, nor is its growth; it does not have a great stand of rice stalks—this is the meaning. ‘With high and low areas’ means it has an uneven surface due to high and low ground. There, water does not stay on the high ground; in the low-lying area, a great deal of water remains. ‘Stony and gravelly’ means it is endowed with spread-out flat rocks, small stones, and gravel. ‘Saline’ means it has salt that has come up. ‘And not deeply plowed’ means because the ground is hard, it is not possible to plow making a deep furrow; only a shallow furrow is made. ‘Not endowed with an inlet’ means not endowed with an entrance for water. ‘Not endowed with an outlet’ means not endowed with small and large water channels. ‘Not endowed with boundaries’ means not endowed with paddy-field boundaries. All the terms beginning with ‘not of great fruit’ should be understood by way of the resultant fruit.

Sampanneti paripuṇṇe sampattiyutte. Pavuttā bījasampadāti sampannaṃ bījaṃ ropitaṃ. Deve [P 4.0125] sampādayantamhīti deve sammā vassante. Anītisampadā hotīti kīṭakimiādipāṇakaītiyā abhāvo ekā sampadā hoti. Virūḷhīti vaḍḍhi dutiyā sampadā hoti. Vepullanti vipulabhāvo tatiyā sampadā hoti. Phalanti paripuṇṇaphalaṃ catutthī sampadā hoti. Sampannasīlesūti paripuṇṇasīlesu. Bhojanasampadāti sampannaṃ vividhabhojanaṃ. Sampadānanti tividhaṃ kusalasampadaṃ. Upanetīti sā bhojanasampadā upanayati. Kasmā? Sampannañhissa taṃ kataṃ, yasmāssa taṃ katakammaṃ sampannaṃ paripuṇṇanti attho. Sampannatthūdhāti sampanno atthu idha. Vijjācaraṇasampannoti tīhi vijjāhi ca pañcadasahi caraṇadhammehi ca samannāgato. Laddhāti evarūpo puggalo cittassa sampadaṃ avekallaparipuṇṇabhāvaṃ labhitvā. Karoti kammasampadanti paripuṇṇakammaṃ karoti. Labhati catthasampadanti atthañca paripuṇṇaṃ labhati. Diṭṭhisampadanti vipassanādiṭṭhiṃ. Maggasampadanti sotāpattimaggaṃ. Yāti sampannamānasoti paripuṇṇacitto hutvā arahattaṃ [V 3.0229] yāti. Sā hoti sabbasampadāti sā sabbadukkhehi vimutti sabbasampadā nāma hotīti.

“Accomplished” (sampanne) means complete, endowed with success. “The accomplishment of seed is declared” (pavuttā bījasampadā) means a complete seed is planted. “When the sky provides” (deve sampādayantamhi) means when the rain falls properly. “There is the accomplishment of freedom from calamity” (anītisampadā hoti) means the absence of the calamity of pests like insects and worms—this is one accomplishment. “Growth” (virūḷhi) means increase—this is the second accomplishment. “Abundance” (vepullaṃ) means the state of being extensive—this is the third accomplishment. “Fruit” (phalaṃ) means the complete fruit—this is the fourth accomplishment. “In those of accomplished virtue” (sampannasīlesu) means in those of fully perfected virtue. “The accomplishment of food” (bhojanasampadā) means complete and varied food. “Accomplishments” (sampadānaṃ) refers to the threefold wholesome accomplishment. “It leads to” (upaneti) means that accomplishment of food leads to it. Why? For what is done by him is accomplished; because that action done by him is accomplished and complete—this is the meaning. “May it be accomplished here” (sampannatthu idha) means may there be accomplishment in this teaching. “Endowed with knowledge and conduct” (vijjācaraṇasampanno) means endowed with the three knowledges and the fifteen kinds of conduct. “Having obtained” (laddhā) means such a person, having obtained the accomplishment of mind—the state of being without deficiency and complete. “He performs the accomplishment of action” (karoti kammasampadaṃ) means he performs a complete action. “And he obtains the accomplishment of the goal” (labhati catthasampadaṃ) means he also obtains the complete goal. “The accomplishment of view” (diṭṭhisampadaṃ) means the view of insight. “The accomplishment of the path” (maggasampadaṃ) means the path of stream-entry. “He goes with an accomplished mind” (yāti sampannamānaso) means having a complete mind, he reaches Arahantship. “That is the supreme accomplishment” (sā hoti sabbasampadā) means that liberation from all suffering is called the supreme accomplishment.

5. Dānūpapattisuttavaṇṇanā

5. Exposition of the Discourse on Rebirths Due to Giving

35. Pañcame [P 4.0126] dānūpapattiyoti dānapaccayā upapattiyo. Dahatīti ṭhapeti. Adhiṭṭhātīti tasseva vevacanaṃ. Bhāvetīti vaḍḍheti. Hīne vimuttanti hīnesu pañcasu kāmaguṇesu vimuttaṃ. Uttari abhāvitanti tato uttarimaggaphalatthāya abhāvitaṃ. Tatrūpapattiyā saṃvattatīti yaṃ ṭhānaṃ patthetvā kusalaṃ kataṃ, tattha nibbattanatthāya saṃvattati. Vītarāgassāti maggena vā samucchinnarāgassa samāpattiyā vā vikkhambhitarāgassa. Dānamatteneva hi brahmaloke nibbattituṃ na sakkā, dānaṃ pana samādhivipassanācittassa alaṅkāraparivāraṃ hoti. Tato dānena muducitto brahmavihāre bhāvetvā brahmaloke nibbattati. Tena vuttaṃ – “vītarāgassa no sarāgassā”ti.

35. In the fifth discourse, ‘rebirths due to giving’ (dānūpapattiyo) means rebirths conditioned by giving. ‘He places’ (dahati) means he establishes. ‘He determines’ (adhiṭṭhāti) is a synonym for that very word. ‘He develops’ (bhāveti) means he cultivates. ‘Inclined towards the inferior’ (hīne vimuttaṃ) means inclined towards the inferior five strands of sensual pleasure. ‘Not further developed’ (uttari abhāvitaṃ) means not developed further beyond that for the sake of the path and fruit. ‘It conduces to rebirth there’ (tatrūpapattiyā saṃvattati) means that the wholesome deed done aspiring for a certain place conduces to arising there. ‘Of one free from lust’ (vītarāgassa) refers to one whose lust has been cut off by the path or suppressed by an attainment. For it is not possible to be reborn in the Brahma-world by mere giving alone, but giving serves as an adornment and an accessory for the mind of concentration and insight. Therefore, a person with a mind softened by giving, having developed the divine abodes, is reborn in the Brahma-world. Hence it is said: “For one free from lust, not for one with lust.”

6. Puññakiriyavatthusuttavaṇṇanā

6. Exposition of the Discourse on the Bases of Meritorious Action

36. Chaṭṭhe [M 3.0231] puññakiriyāni ca tāni tesaṃ tesaṃ ānisaṃsānaṃ vatthūni cāti puññakiriyavatthūni. Dānādīnañhi lakkhaṇe cittaṃ ṭhapetvā “evarūpaṃ nāma amhehi dānaṃ dātabbaṃ, sīlaṃ rakkhitabbaṃ, bhāvanā bhāvetabbā”ti sattā puññāni karonti. Dānameva dānamayaṃ, dānacetanāsu vā purimacetanāto nipphannā sanniṭṭhāpakacetanā dānamayaṃ sīlādīhi sīlamayādīni viya. Sesadvayesupi eseva nayo. Parittaṃ kataṃ hotīti thokaṃ mandaṃ kataṃ hoti. Nābhisambhotīti na nipphajjati. Akataṃ hotīti bhāvanāyayogoyeva anāraddho hotīti attho. Manussadobhagyanti [P 4.0127] manussesu sampattirahitaṃ pañcavidhaṃ nīcakulaṃ. Upapajjatīti paṭisandhivasena upagacchati, tattha nibbattatīti attho. Mattaso katanti pamāṇena kataṃ, thokaṃ na bahu. Manussasobhagyanti manussesu subhagabhāvaṃ tividhakulasampattiṃ. Adhimattanti adhikappamāṇaṃ balavaṃ vā. Adhigaṇhantīti abhibhavitvā gaṇhanti, visiṭṭhatarā jeṭṭhakā hontīti attho.

36. In the sixth discourse, ‘bases of meritorious action’ (puññakiriyavatthūni) is so called because they are meritorious deeds and they are also the bases for their respective benefits. For beings, having established their minds on the characteristics of giving and so on, perform meritorious deeds, thinking: “Such and such a gift should be given by us, virtue should be observed, and development should be undertaken.” Giving itself is ‘made of giving’ (dānamayaṃ). Alternatively, among the volitions of giving, the determining volition that is accomplished from the prior volition is ‘made of giving,’ just as there are things made of stone from stone. This same method applies to the remaining two as well. ‘It is done to a small extent’ (parittaṃ kataṃ hoti) means it is done a little, weakly. ‘It does not come to be’ (nābhisambhoti) means it is not accomplished. ‘It is not done’ (akataṃ hoti) means the effort in development has not been undertaken; this is the meaning. ‘An unfortunate human existence’ (manussadobhagyaṃ) means the five kinds of low family, devoid of accomplishment, among humans. ‘He is reborn’ (upapajjati) means one approaches by means of rebirth-linking; one is born there, this is the meaning. ‘Done in moderation’ (mattaso kataṃ) means done with measure; a little, not much. ‘A fortunate human existence’ (manussasobhagyaṃ) means the state of being fortunate among humans, the threefold accomplishment of family. ‘To an excellent degree’ (adhimattaṃ) means exceeding measure, or strong. ‘They acquire’ (adhigaṇhanti) means they take by overpowering; they become superior and foremost, this is the meaning.

7. Sappurisadānasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Exposition of the Discourse on the Gift of a Good Person

37. Sattame [V 3.0230] sucinti parisuddhaṃ vaṇṇasampannaṃ deti. Paṇītanti rasūpapannaṃ. Kālenāti yuttapattakālena. Kappiyanti yaṃ kappiyaṃ, taṃ deti. Viceyya detīti “imassa dinnaṃ mahapphalaṃ bhavissati, imassa na mahapphala”nti evaṃ paṭiggāhakapariyesanavasena dānaṃ vā paṇidhāyavasena dānaṃ vā vicinitvā deti.

37. In the seventh discourse, ‘pure’ (suciṃ) means one gives what is pure, what is endowed with beauty. ‘Excellent’ (paṇītaṃ) means what is endowed with taste. ‘At the proper time’ (kālena) means at a suitable and proper time. ‘Allowable’ (kappiyaṃ) means one gives that which is allowable. ‘He gives after considering’ (viceyya deti) means: having selected and examined either the recipient—thinking thus, “A gift to this person will be of great fruit; a gift to this person will not be of great fruit”—or the gift itself by way of aspiration, one gives.

8. Sappurisasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Exposition of the Discourse on the True Person

38. Aṭṭhame atthāyāti atthatthāya. Hitāya sukhāyāti hitatthāya sukhatthāya. Pubbapetānanti paralokagatānaṃ ñātīnaṃ. Imasmiṃ sutte anuppanne buddhe cakkavattirājāno bodhisattā paccekabuddhā labbhanti, buddhakāle buddhā ceva buddhasāvakā ca. Yathāvuttānañhi etesaṃ atthāya hitāya sukhāya saṃvattanti. Bahunnaṃ [P 4.0128] vata atthāya, sappañño gharamāvasanti sappañño ghare vasanto bahūnaṃ vata atthāya hoti. Pubbeti paṭhameva. Pubbekatamanussaranti [M 3.0232] mātāpitūnaṃ pubbakāraguṇe anussaranto. Sahadhammenāti sakāraṇena paccayapūjanena pūjeti. Apace brahmacārayoti brahmacārino apacayati, nīcavuttitaṃ nesaṃ āpajjati. Pesaloti piyasīlo.

38. In the eighth discourse, ‘for the benefit’ (atthāya) means for the sake of the goal. ‘For the welfare and for the happiness’ (hitāya sukhāya) means for the sake of welfare and for the sake of happiness. ‘Of departed ancestors’ (pubbapetānaṃ) means of relatives who have passed on to the next world. In this discourse, when a Buddha has not arisen, wheel-turning monarchs, Bodhisattas, and Paccekabuddhas are to be found; in the time of a Buddha, Buddhas and the Buddha’s disciples are. For indeed, these aforementioned ones conduce to their benefit, welfare, and happiness. ‘A wise person dwelling at home is indeed for the benefit of many’ (bahūnaṃ vata atthāya, sappañño gharamāvasati) means: a wise person dwelling at home is indeed for the benefit of many. ‘Formerly’ (pubbe) means at the very first. ‘Remembering what was done before’ (pubbekatamanussaranti) means recollecting the previously done favors of one’s mother and father. ‘In accordance with the Dhamma’ (sahadhammena) means with reason, one honors by means of offering requisites. ‘He reveres those who lead the holy life’ (apace brahmacārino) means one pays respect to those who practice the holy life; one behaves humbly towards them. ‘Amiable’ (pesalo) means one of lovable virtue.

9. Abhisandasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Exposition of the Discourse on the Outflows

39. Navame dānānīti cetanādānāni. Aggaññānītiādīnaṃ attho heṭṭhā vuttoyeva.

39. In the ninth discourse, ‘gifts’ (dānāni) means gifts of volition. The meaning of ‘foremost’ (aggaññāni) and so forth has already been stated below.

10. Duccaritavipākasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Exposition of the Discourse on the Results of Misconduct

40. Dasame pāṇātipātoti pāṇātipātacetanā. Sabbalahusoti sabbalahuko. Appāyukasaṃvattanikoti tena parittakena kammavipākena appāyuko hoti, dinnamattāya vā paṭisandhiyā vilīyati mātukucchito nikkhantamatte vā. Evarūpo hi na aññassa kassaci nissando, pāṇātipātasseva gatamaggo esoti. Bhogabyasanasaṃvattanikoti yathā kākaṇikāmattampi [V 3.0231] hatthe na tiṭṭhati, evaṃ bhogabyasanaṃ saṃvatteti. Sapattaverasaṃvattaniko hotīti saha sapattehi veraṃ saṃvatteti. Tassa hi sapattā ca bahukā honti. Yo ca naṃ passati, tasmiṃ verameva uppādeti na nibbāyati. Evarūpo hi parassa rakkhitagopitabhaṇḍe aparādhassa nissando.

40. In the tenth, 'killing living beings' means the volition of killing. 'The very lightest' means the lightest of all. 'Leading to a short life' means that by the small result of that kamma, one has a short life; or the rebirth-linking perishes just as it is given, or one perishes just as one exits the mother’s womb. Indeed, such a thing is not the consequence of any other kamma; this is the very path followed by the kamma of killing living beings. 'Leading to the destruction of wealth' means it brings about the destruction of wealth, just as not even a tiny coin stays in one’s hand. 'Leading to enmity with rivals' means it brings about enmity together with rivals. For that person, rivals are numerous. And whoever sees him generates only enmity towards him that is not extinguished. Indeed, such a thing is the consequence of the offense against another's guarded and protected property.

Abhūtabbhakkhānasaṃvattaniko hotīti abhūtena abbhakkhānaṃ saṃvatteti, yena kenaci kataṃ tasseva upari patati. Mittehi [P 4.0129] bhedanasaṃvattanikoti mittehi bhedaṃ saṃvatteti. Yaṃ yaṃ mittaṃ karoti, so so bhijjatiyeva. Amanāpasaddasaṃvattanikoti amanāpasaddaṃ saṃvatteti. Yā sā vācā kaṇṭakā kakkasā kaṭukā abhisajjanī mammacchedikā, gatagataṭṭhāne tameva suṇāti, manāpasaddasavanaṃ nāma na labhati. Evarūpo pharusavācāya gatamaggo nāma. Anādeyyavācāsaṃvattanikoti aggahetabbavacanataṃ saṃvatteti, “tvaṃ kasmā kathesi, ko hi tava vacanaṃ gahessatī”ti vattabbataṃ āpajjati. Ayaṃ samphappalāpassa gatamaggo. Ummattakasaṃvattaniko hotīti ummattakabhāvaṃ [M 3.0233] saṃvatteti. Tena hi manusso ummatto vā khittacitto vā eḷamūgo vā hoti. Ayaṃ surāpānassa nissando. Imasmiṃ sutte vaṭṭameva kathitanti.

Leading to false accusation means it brings about false accusation; whatever is done by anyone at all falls upon that very person. Leading to division among friends means it leads to the breaking of friendships. Whatever friend one makes, that one inevitably breaks away. Leading to unpleasant sounds means it leads to unpleasant sounds. One hears only thorny, harsh, bitter, clinging, and heart-cutting words wherever one goes; one does not obtain what is called the hearing of pleasant sounds. Such is the path followed by harsh speech. Leading to speech that is not to be heeded means it brings about the state of having unaccepted speech, bringing one to the state where one is to be told, 'Why do you speak? Who indeed would accept your word?' This is the path followed by idle chatter. Leading to madness means it leads to a state of madness. For by this, a person becomes mad, or distracted in mind, or a drooling mute. This is the consequence of drinking intoxicants. In this discourse, only the round of existence is discussed.

Dānavaggo catuttho.

The Chapter on Giving is the Fourth.

5. Uposathavaggo

5. The Uposatha Chapter

4. Vāseṭṭhasuttavaṇṇanā

4. The Exposition of the Vāseṭṭha Sutta

44. Pañcamassa catutthe ime cepi, vāseṭṭha, mahāsālāti purato ṭhite dve sālarukkhe dassento parikappopamaṃ āha. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – ime tāva mahāsālā acetanā. Sace etepi sacetanā hutvā aṭṭhaṅgasamannāgataṃ uposathaṃ upavaseyyuṃ, etesampi so uposathavāso dīgharattaṃ hitāya sukhāya assa. Bhūte pana vattabbameva natthīti.

44. In the fourth discourse of the fifth chapter, regarding the passage beginning, 'Vāseṭṭha, even if these great sāl trees...': Here, pointing to two sāl trees standing before him, the Buddha gives a simile of conjecture. This is the meaning: These great sāl trees are insentient. If even they were sentient and were to observe the eight-factored Uposatha, that observance of the Uposatha would lead to their long-term welfare and happiness. In the case of an actual being, what need is there to speak of it?

6. Anuruddhasuttavaṇṇanā

6. The Exposition of the Anuruddha Sutta

46. Chaṭṭhe [V 3.0232] yenāyasmā anuruddhoti tā kira devatā attano sampattiṃ oloketvā “kiṃ nu kho nissāya ayaṃ sampatti amhehi laddhā”ti āvajjamānā theraṃ disvā “mayaṃ [P 4.0130] amhākaṃ ayyassa pubbe cakkavattirajjaṃ karontassa pādaparicārikā hutvā tena dinnovāde ṭhatvā imaṃ sampattiṃ labhimha, gacchāma theraṃ ānetvā imaṃ sampattiṃ anubhavissāmā”ti divā yenāyasmā anuruddho tenupasaṅkamiṃsu. Tīsu ṭhānesūti tīsu kāraṇesu. Ṭhānaso paṭilabhāmāti khaṇeneva labhāma. Saranti vacanasaddaṃ vā gītasaddaṃ vā ābharaṇasaddaṃ vā. Pītā assūtiādīni nīlā tāva jātā, pītā bhavituṃ na sakkhissantītiādinā nayena cintetvā vitakketi. Tāpi “idāni ayyo amhākaṃ pītabhāvaṃ icchati, idāni lohitabhāva”nti tādisāva ahesuṃ.

46. In the sixth discourse, regarding the passage beginning, '...where the Venerable Anuruddha was...': It is said that those deities, reflecting on their own prosperity, wondered, “Depending on what have we attained this prosperity?” Seeing the Elder, they thought, “We were foot attendants of our lord when he was a wheel-turning king in the past, and by adhering to the instructions he gave, we gained this prosperity. Let us go, bring the Elder, and enjoy this prosperity.” Thus, they approached the Venerable Anuruddha during the day. 'In three instances' means in three causes. 'We obtain it according to the circumstances' means we obtain it instantly. The sound of speech, or the sound of song, or the sound of ornaments issues forth. He thought and reflected in this way: “Regarding such things as ‘May they be yellow,’ having been born as blue, they will not be able to become yellow,” and so on. And they, thinking, “Now the noble one desires us to be yellow, now red,” became just so.

Accharaṃ vādesīti pāṇitalaṃ vādesi. Pañcaṅgikassāti ātataṃ, vitataṃ, ātatavitataṃ, ghanaṃ, susiranti imehi pañcahi aṅgehi samannāgatassa. Tattha ātataṃ nāma cammapariyonaddhesu bheriādīsu ekatalatūriyaṃ, vitataṃ nāma ubhayatalaṃ, ātatavitataṃ nāma sabbaso [M 3.0234] pariyonaddhaṃ, susiraṃ vaṃsādi, ghanaṃ sammādi. Suvinītassāti ākaḍḍhanasithilakaraṇādīhi samucchitassa. Suppaṭipatāḷitassāti pamāṇe ṭhitabhāvajānanatthaṃ suṭṭhu paṭipatāḷitassa. Kusalehi susamannāhatassāti ye vādetuṃ kusalā chekā, tehi vāditassa. Vaggūti cheko sundaro. Rajanīyoti rañjetuṃ samattho. Kamanīyoti kāmetabbayutto. Khamanīyoti [P 4.0131] vā pāṭho, divasampi suyyamāno khamateva, na nibbindatīti attho. Madanīyoti mānamadapurisamadajanano. Indriyāni okkhipīti “asāruppaṃ imā devatā karontī”ti indriyāni heṭṭhā khipi, na akkhīni ummīletvā olokesi. Na khvayyo anuruddho sādiyatīti “mayaṃ naccāma gāyāma, ayyo pana anuruddho na kho sādiyati, akkhīni ummīletvā na oloketi, kiṃ mayaṃ naccitvā vā gāyitvā vā karissāmā”ti tattheva antaradhāyiṃsu. Yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkamīti tāsaṃ devatānaṃ ānubhāvaṃ disvā “katihi nu kho dhammehi samannāgato mātugāmo manāpakāyike devaloke nibbattatī”ti imamatthaṃ pucchituṃ upasaṅkami.

'She sounded a snap' means she struck her palm. 'Of the five-factored' means endowed with these five factors: ātata (stretched), vitata (spread), ātatavitata (stretched and spread), ghana (solid), and susira (hollow). Therein, 'ātata' refers to one-sided instruments like drums covered with hide; 'vitata' refers to double-sided instruments; 'ātatavitata' refers to those completely covered; 'susira' refers to wind instruments like the flute; and 'ghana' refers to solid instruments like cymbals. 'Well-adjusted' means made to sound well by means of tightening, loosening, and so on. 'Well-tuned' means properly struck to ascertain its state of being established in the proper measure. 'Well-played by the skilled' means played by those who are clever and skilled in playing. 'Melodious' means excellent, beautiful. 'Enchanting' means capable of delighting. 'Desirable' means worthy of being desired. 'Pleasing' is another reading, meaning it is pleasing even if listened to for a whole day and does not cause weariness. 'Intoxicating' means causing the intoxication of pride and masculine pride. 'He cast down his faculties' means, thinking, 'These deities are behaving unsuitably,' he cast his faculties downward; he did not open his eyes to look. 'The noble Anuruddha does not approve' means, thinking, 'We dance and sing, but the noble Anuruddha does not approve; he does not open his eyes to look. What can we achieve by dancing or singing?' they vanished right there. 'He approached the Blessed One' means, having seen the power of those deities, he approached to ask this question: 'Endowed with how many qualities, indeed, is a woman reborn in the deva world of those with pleasing bodies?'

9-10. Idhalokikasuttadvayavaṇṇanā

9-10. Exposition of the Two Discourses on This World

49-50. Navame [V 3.0233] ayaṃ’sa loko āraddho hotīti ayamassa loko idhaloke karaṇamattāya āraddhattā paripuṇṇattā āraddho hoti paripuṇṇo. Soḷasākārasampannāti sutte vuttehi aṭṭhahi, gāthāsu aṭṭhahīti soḷasahi ākārehi samannāgatā, yāni vā aṭṭhaṅgāni parampi tesu samādapetīti evampi soḷasākārasampannāti eke. Saddhāsīlapaññā panettha missikā kathitā. Dasamaṃ bhikkhusaṅghassa kathitaṃ. Sabbasuttesu pana yaṃ na vuttaṃ, taṃ heṭṭhā āgatanayattā uttānatthamevāti.

In the ninth, 'this world is undertaken for her' means that for this woman, this world is undertaken and complete, because it has been undertaken and completed with respect to what is to be done in this world. 'Endowed with sixteen aspects' means she is endowed with the sixteen aspects spoken of in the sutta—eight in the prose and eight in the verses. Or, as some say, because she also establishes others in those eight factors, she is thus endowed with sixteen aspects. Herein, faith, virtue, and wisdom are spoken of as intermingled. The tenth was spoken to the Saṅgha of bhikkhus. As for whatever is not stated in all the suttas, its meaning is indeed clear due to the method that has come before.

Uposathavaggo pañcamo.

The Uposatha Chapter is the fifth.

Paṭhamapaṇṇāsakaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.

The First Fifty is concluded.

2. Dutiyapaṇṇāsakaṃ

2. The Second Fifty.

(6) 1. Gotamīvaggo

(6) 1. The Gotamī Chapter.

1. Gotamīsuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Gotamī Sutta.

51. Chaṭṭhassa [V 3.0234][M 3.0235][P 4.0132] paṭhame sakkesu viharatīti paṭhamagamanena gantvā viharati. Mahāpajāpatīti puttapajāya ceva dhītupajāya ca mahantattā evaṃladdhanāmā. Yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkamīti bhagavā kapilapuraṃ gantvā paṭhamameva nandaṃ pabbājesi, sattame divase rāhulakumāraṃ. Cumbaṭakakalahe (dī. ni. aṭṭha. 2.331; saṃ. ni. aṭṭha. 1.1.37) pana ubhayanagaravāsikesu yuddhatthāya nikkhantesu satthā gantvā te rājāno saññāpetvā attadaṇḍasuttaṃ (su. ni. 941 ādayo; mahāni. 170 ādayo) kathesi. Rājāno pasīditvā aḍḍhatiyasate aḍḍhatiyasate kumāre adaṃsu, tāni pañca kumārasatāni satthu santike pabbajiṃsu, atha nesaṃ pajāpatiyo sāsanaṃ pesetvā anabhiratiṃ uppādayiṃsu. Satthā tesaṃ anabhiratiyā uppannabhāvaṃ ñatvā te pañcasate daharabhikkhū kuṇāladahaṃ netvā attano kuṇālakāle nisinnapubbe pāsāṇatale nisīditvā kuṇālajātakakathāya (jā. 2.21.kuṇālajātaka) tesaṃ anabhiratiṃ vinodetvā sabbepi te sotāpattiphale patiṭṭhāpesi, puna mahāvanaṃ ānetvā arahattaphaleti. Tesaṃ cittajānanatthaṃ punapi pajāpatiyo sāsanaṃ pahiṇiṃsu. Te “abhabbā mayaṃ gharāvāsassā”ti paṭisāsanaṃ pahiṇiṃsu. Tā “na dāni amhākaṃ gharaṃ gantuṃ yuttaṃ, mahāpajāpatiyā santikaṃ gantvā pabbajjaṃ anujānāpetvā pabbajissāmā”ti pañcasatāpi mahāpajāpatiṃ upasaṅkamitvā “ayye, amhākaṃ pabbajjaṃ anujānāpethā”ti āhaṃsu. Mahāpajāpatī tā itthiyo gahetvā yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami. Setacchattassa heṭṭhā rañño parinibbutakāle upasaṅkamītipi vadantiyeva.

51. In the first discourse of the sixth chapter, 'he dwelt among the Sakyans' means he went and dwelt there on his first visit. Mahāpajāpatī—she received this name because of the greatness of her progeny, both of sons and of daughters. 'She approached the Blessed One'—the Blessed One went to Kapilapura and first gave the going-forth to Nanda, and on the seventh day to Prince Rāhula. Then, during the Cumbaṭaka quarrel, when the residents of both cities had gone out for battle, the Teacher went, instructed those kings, and taught the Attadaṇḍa Sutta. The kings were pleased and each gave two hundred and fifty princes; those five hundred princes went forth in the Teacher's presence. Then their former wives sent a message and aroused discontent in them. The Teacher, knowing that their discontent had arisen, led those five hundred young bhikkhus to Lake Kuṇāla. Having sat on the stone slab where he had formerly sat in his time as Kuṇāla, he dispelled their discontent with the story of the Kuṇāla Jātaka and established all of them in the fruit of stream-entry. Then, having brought them to the Great Forest, he established them in the fruit of Arahantship. To know their state of mind, their former wives again sent a message. They sent a reply: 'We are unfit for the household life.' Those women thought, 'It is not proper for us to go to another's home now. We will go to Mahāpajāpatī, have her get permission for the going-forth, and we will go forth.' All five hundred approached Mahāpajāpatī and said, 'Noble lady, have permission granted for our going-forth.' Mahāpajāpatī took those women and approached the Blessed One. It is also said that she approached at the time of the king's parinibbāna, under the white parasol.

Alaṃ gotami, mā te ruccīti kasmā paṭikkhipi, nanu sabbesampi buddhānaṃ catasso parisā [V 3.0235] hontīti? Kāmaṃ honti, kilametvā pana anekavāraṃ yācite anuññātaṃ pabbajjaṃ [P 4.0133] “dukkhena laddhā”ti sammā paripālessantīti garuṃ katvā anuññātukāmo paṭikkhipi. Pakkāmīti puna kapilapurameva pāvisi. Yathābhirantaṃ [M 3.0236] viharitvāti bodhaneyyasattānaṃ upanissayaṃ olokento yathājjhāsayane viharitvā. Cārikaṃ pakkāmīti mahājanasaṅgahaṃ karonto uttamāya buddhasiriyā anopamena buddhavilāsena aturitacārikaṃ pakkāmi.

'Enough, Gotamī, let this not be your wish.' Why did he refuse? Is it not so that all Buddhas have four assemblies? Indeed, they do. However, wishing to grant permission only after making it a weighty matter, he refused, thinking: 'When the going-forth, permitted after I have been wearied by being asked many times, is obtained with difficulty, they will guard it well.' 'He departed' means he re-entered Kapilapura itself. 'Having dwelt as he pleased' means having dwelt according to his inclination while observing the supporting conditions for beings who could be enlightened. 'He set out on a wandering tour' means, while benefiting the great multitude, he set out on an unhurried tour with the supreme splendor of a Buddha and with incomparable Buddha-grace.

Sambahulāhi sākiyānīhi saddhinti antonivesanamhiyeva dasabalaṃ uddissa pabbajjāvesaṃ gahetvā pañcasatā sākiyāniyo pabbajjāvesaṃyeva gāhāpetvā sabbāhipi tāhi sambahulāhi sākiyānīhi saddhiṃ. Cārikaṃ pakkāmīti gamanaṃ abhinīhari. Gamanābhinīharaṇakāle pana tā sukhumālā rājitthiyo padasā gantuṃ na sakkhissantīti sākiyakoliyarājāno sovaṇṇasivikāyo upaṭṭhāpayiṃsu. Tā pana “yāne āruyha gacchantīti satthari agāravo kato hotī”ti ekapaṇṇāsayojanikaṃ padasāva paṭipajjiṃsu. Rājānopi purato ca pacchato ca ārakkhaṃ saṃvidahāpetvā taṇḍulasappitelādīnaṃ sakaṭāni pūrāpetvā “gataṭṭhāne gataṭṭhāne āhāraṃ paṭiyādethā”ti purise pesayiṃsu. Sūnehi pādehīti tāsañhi sukhumālattā pādesu eko phoṭo uṭṭheti, eko bhijjati. Ubho pādā katakaṭṭhisamparikiṇṇā viya hutvā uddhumātā jātā. Tena vuttaṃ – “sūnehi pādehī”ti. Bahidvārakoṭṭhaketi dvārakoṭṭhakato bahi. Kasmā panevaṃ [P 4.0134] ṭhitāti? Evaṃ kirassā ahosi – “ahaṃ tathāgatena ananuññātā sayameva pabbajjāvesaṃ aggahesiṃ, evaṃ gahitabhāvo ca pana me sakalajambudīpe pākaṭo jāto. Sace satthā pabbajjaṃ anujānāti, iccetaṃ kusalaṃ. Sace pana nānujānissati, mahatī garahā bhavissatī”ti vihāraṃ pavisituṃ asakkontī rodamānāva aṭṭhāsi.

'With many Sakyan women' means: with all those many Sakyan women, after she herself, right within the palace, had taken on the guise of one gone forth with the Ten-Powered One in mind, and had caused the five hundred Sakyan women to take on that same guise. 'She set out on a wandering tour' means she undertook the journey. At the time of undertaking the journey, however, thinking that those delicate royal ladies would be unable to go on foot, the Sakyan and Koliyan kings provided golden palanquins. But those ladies, thinking, 'If we go having mounted a vehicle, it would be an act of disrespect toward the Teacher,' undertook the fifty-one yojana journey on foot. The kings also had guards arranged in front and behind, had carts filled with rice, ghee, oil, and so on, and sent men with the instruction, 'Prepare food at every place they go.' 'With swollen feet': because of their delicateness, one blister would arise on their feet, and another would break. Both feet became swollen, as if covered all over with blisters. Therefore it was said, 'with swollen feet.' 'Outside the gatehouse' means outside of the gatehouse. Why did she stand there like that? It is said that this thought occurred to her: 'Without being permitted by the Tathāgata, I took on the guise of one gone forth by myself, and the fact that I have done so has become well-known throughout all of Jambudīpa. If the Teacher permits the going-forth, that is good. But if he does not permit it, there will be great blame.' Unable to enter the monastery, she just stood there weeping.

Kiṃ nu tvaṃ gotamīti kiṃ nu rājakulānaṃ vipatti uppannā, kena tvaṃ kāraṇena evaṃ vivaṇṇabhāvaṃ pattā, sūnehi pādehi…pe… ṭhitāti. Aññenapi pariyāyenāti aññenapi kāraṇena. Bahukārā, bhantetiādinā tassā guṇaṃ kathetvā puna pabbajjaṃ yācanto evamāha. Satthāpi “itthiyo nāma parittapaññā, ekayācitamattena pabbajjāya anuññātāya na mama sāsanaṃ garuṃ katvā gaṇhissantī”ti tikkhattuṃ paṭikkhipitvā idāni garuṃ katvā gāhāpetukāmatāya sace, ānanda, mahāpajāpatī gotamī [M 3.0237] aṭṭha garudhamme paṭiggaṇhāti, sāva’ssā hotu [V 3.0236] upasampadātiādimāha. Tattha sāvassāti sā eva assā pabbajjāpi upasampadāpi hotu.

"What is it, Gotamī? Has some misfortune befallen the royal families? For what reason have you attained such a discolored state, standing with swollen feet and a body covered in dust?" Thus he spoke. 'By another means' means by another reason. Having spoken of her virtues, beginning with, "Venerable sir, she was of great help," and desiring to request ordination again, he spoke thus. The Teacher also, thinking, "Women are of little wisdom. If ordination is granted to these Sakyan women merely by being asked, they will not take my teaching seriously," refused three times. Now, due to his desire to make her accept it seriously, he said, "Ānanda, if Mahāpajāpatī Gotamī accepts the eight weighty principles, let that be her full ordination," and so on. Therein, 'sāvassā' means: for her, let that very thing be both the going forth and the full ordination.

Tadahūpasampannassāti taṃdivasaṃ upasampannassa. Abhivādanaṃ paccuṭṭhānaṃ añjalikammaṃ sāmīcikammaṃ kattabbanti omānātimāne akatvā pañcapatiṭṭhitena abhivādanaṃ, āsanā paccuṭṭhāya paccuggamanavasena paccuṭṭhānaṃ, dasanakhe samodhānetvā añjalikammaṃ, āsanapaññāpanabījanādikaṃ anucchavikakammasaṅkhātaṃ [P 4.0135] sāmīcikammañca katabbaṃ. Abhikkhuke āvāseti yattha vasantiyā anantarāyena ovādatthāya upasaṅkamanaṭṭhāne ovādadāyako ācariyo natthi, ayaṃ abhikkhuko āvāso nāma. Evarūpe āvāse vassaṃ na upagantabbaṃ. Anvaḍḍhamāsanti anuposathikaṃ. Ovādūpasaṅkamananti ovādatthāya upasaṅkamanaṃ. Diṭṭhenāti cakkhunā diṭṭhena. Sutenāti sotena sutena. Parisaṅkāyāti diṭṭhasutavasena parisaṅkitena. Garudhammanti garukaṃ saṅghādisesāpattiṃ. Pakkhamānattanti anūnāni pannarasa divasāni mānattaṃ. Chasu dhammesūti vikālabhojanacchaṭṭhesu sikkhāpadesu. Sikkhitasikkhāyāti ekasikkhampi akhaṇḍaṃ katvā pūritasikkhāya. Akkositabbo paribhāsitabboti dasannaṃ akkosavatthūnaṃ aññatarena akkosavatthunā na akkositabbo, bhayūpadaṃsanāya yāya kāyaci paribhāsāya na paribhāsitabbo.

'Of one ordained that day' means of one ordained on that very day. 'Homage, rising, raising joined palms, and proper conduct should be done' means: without making a display of inferiority or superiority, homage should be done with the five points of contact; rising means rising from one's seat to go and meet them; raising joined palms means bringing the ten fingernails together; and proper conduct, known as suitable actions such as preparing a seat, fanning, and so on, should be done. 'In an abode without a bhikkhu' means a residence where, for a bhikkhunī dwelling there, there is no teacher to give admonition at a place of approach for the purpose of receiving admonition without obstruction; this is called an abode without a bhikkhu. In such an abode, the rains-residence should not be undertaken. 'Every half-month' means on every Uposatha day. 'Approaching for admonition' means approaching for the purpose of admonition. 'By what is seen' means seen with the eye. 'By what is heard' means heard with the ear. 'On suspicion' means suspected on the basis of what is seen and heard. 'A grave rule' means a grave Saṅghādisesa offense. 'Probation for a fortnight' means mānatta for no less than fifteen days. 'In the six rules' means in the training rules of which eating at the wrong time is the sixth. 'Trained in the training' means for one who has fulfilled the training by keeping even a single training rule unbroken. 'Should not be reviled or abused' means one should not be reviled with any one of the ten grounds for reviling, nor should one be abused with any kind of abuse that shows a succession of dangers.

Ovaṭo bhikkhunīnaṃ bhikkhūsu vacanapathoti ovādānusāsanadhammakathāsaṅkhāto vacanapatho bhikkhunīnaṃ bhikkhūsu ovarito pihito, na bhikkhuniyā koci bhikkhu ovaditabbo anusāsitabbo vā “bhante, porāṇakattherā idaṃ cīvaravattaṃ pūrayiṃsū”ti evaṃ pana paveṇivasena kathetuṃ vaṭṭati. Anovaṭo [P 4.0136] bhikkhūnaṃ bhikkhunīsu vacanapathoti bhikkhūnaṃ pana bhikkhunīsu vacanapatho anivārito, yathāruci ovadituṃ anusāsituṃ dhammakathaṃ kathetunti ayamettha saṅkhepo, vitthārato panesā garudhammakathā samantapāsādikāya vinayasaṃvaṇṇanāya (pāci. aṭṭha. 148) vuttanayeneva veditabbā.

'The path of speech for bhikkhunīs towards bhikkhus is closed' means: the path of speech for bhikkhunīs towards bhikkhus, which is known as admonition, instruction, and Dhamma talk, is closed and blocked. A bhikkhunī should not admonish or instruct any bhikkhu. However, it is permissible to speak according to tradition, thus: "Venerable sir, the ancient elders fulfilled this duty concerning robes." 'The path of speech for bhikkhus towards bhikkhunīs is not closed' means: for bhikkhus, the path of speech towards bhikkhunīs—to admonish, instruct, and give Dhamma talks according to their wish—is not forbidden. This is the summary here. But in detail, this talk on the grave rules should be understood according to the method already explained in the Vinaya commentary called the Samantapāsādikā.

Ime pana aṭṭha garudhamme satthu santike uggahetvā therena attano ārociyamāne sutvāva mahāpajāpatiyā tāva mahantaṃ domanassaṃ khaṇena [M 3.0238] paṭippassambhi, anotattadahato ābhatena sītudakassa ghaṭasatena matthake parisittā viya vigatapariḷāhā attamanā hutvā garudhammapaṭiggahaṇena [V 3.0237] uppannapītipāmojjaṃ āvikarontī seyyathāpi, bhantetiādikaṃ udānaṃ udānesi.

Having learned these eight grave rules in the presence of the Teacher, as soon as Mahāpajāpatī heard them being declared to her by the elder, her great displeasure immediately subsided. As if sprinkled on the head with a hundred pots of cool water brought from Lake Anotatta, she became free from burning distress and glad at heart. Making manifest the joy and gladness that arose from accepting the grave rules, she uttered an inspired utterance beginning with, "Just as, venerable sir..." and so on.

Kumbhatthenakehīti kumbhe dīpaṃ jāletvā tena ālokena paraghare bhaṇḍaṃ vicinitvā thenakacorehi. Setaṭṭhikā nāma rogajātīti eko pāṇako nāḷamajjhagataṃ kaṇḍaṃ vijjhati, yena viddhā kaṇḍā nikkhantampi sālisīsaṃ khīraṃ gahetuṃ na sakkoti. Mañjiṭṭhikā nāma rogajātīti ucchūnaṃ antorattabhāvo.

'By pot-thieves' means by thieves who, having lit a lamp in a pot, search for goods in another's house by that light to steal. 'The disease called white-stalk' means a type of disease where a certain insect pierces the stalk inside the stem; because it has been pierced, the ear of rice, even though it has emerged, is unable to take up the milky sap. 'The disease called madder-stalk' is the state of being red inside of sugarcanes.

Mahato taḷākassa paṭikacceva āḷinti iminā pana etamatthaṃ dasseti – yathā mahato taḷākassa pāḷiyā abaddhāyapi kiñci udakaṃ tiṭṭhateva, paṭhamameva baddhāya pana yaṃ abaddhapaccayā na tiṭṭheyya, tampi tiṭṭheyya, evameva ye ime anuppanne vatthusmiṃ paṭikacceva anatikkamanatthāya garudhammā paññattā, tesu apaññattesu mātugāmassa pabbajitattā pañca vassasatāni saddhammo [P 4.0137] tiṭṭheyya. Paṭikacceva paññattattā pana aparānipi pañca vassasatāni ṭhassatīti evaṃ paṭhamaṃ vuttavassasahassameva ṭhassati. Vassasahassanti cetaṃ paṭisambhidāpabhedappattakhīṇāsavānaṃ vaseneva vuttaṃ, tato pana uttaripi sukkhavipassakakhīṇāsavavasena vassasahassaṃ, anāgāmivasena vassasahassaṃ, sakadāgāmivasena vassasahassaṃ, sotāpannavasena vassasahassanti evaṃ pañcavassasahassāni paṭivedhasaddhammo ṭhassati. Pariyattidhammopi tāniyeva. Na hi pariyattiyā asati paṭivedho atthi, nāpi pariyattiyā sati paṭivedho na hoti. Liṅgaṃ pana pariyattiyā antarahitāyapi ciraṃ pavattissatīti.

By the text beginning, 'Building an embankment for a great reservoir in advance,' he shows this meaning: Just as for a great reservoir, even if the embankment is not built, some water will still remain; but if it is built beforehand, even that water which would not have remained due to the lack of an embankment would remain. In the very same way, these grave rules were laid down in advance before the matter arose, for the sake of non-transgression. If these had not been laid down, because of women having gone forth, the good Dhamma would have lasted for five hundred years. But because they were laid down in advance, the good Dhamma will last for another five hundred years. Thus, the thousand years spoken of at first will stand. And this 'thousand years' is spoken of with respect to the arahants who have attained the analytical knowledges. Beyond that, for a thousand years with respect to the dry-insight arahants, for a thousand years with respect to non-returners, for a thousand years with respect to once-returners, and for a thousand years with respect to stream-enterers—thus, for five thousand years the good Dhamma of penetration will last. The Dhamma of learning will also last for that same five thousand years. For when the learning does not exist, penetration does not exist; and when the learning exists, it is not that penetration does not happen—it surely happens. But the outward sign of a monk will continue for a long time even when the learning has disappeared.

2. Ovādasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Discourse on Exhortation

52. Dutiye bahussutoti idha sakalassapi buddhavacanassa vasena bahussutabhāvo veditabbo. Garudhammanti kāyasaṃsaggaṃ. Ayamettha saṅkhepo. Bhikkhunovādakavinicchayo pana samantapāsādikāya (pāci. aṭṭha. 144 ādayo) vuttanayeneva veditabbo.

52. In the second discourse, 'one of much learning' here should be understood as the state of being one of much learning by way of the entire word of the Buddha. By 'a grave rule' is meant bodily contact. This is the summary here. However, the decision regarding the exhortation of bhikkhunīs should be understood according to the method spoken of in the Samantapāsādikā.

3. Saṃkhittasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Concise Discourse

53. Tatiye [M 3.0239][V 3.0238] sarāgāyāti sarāgatthāya. Virāgāyāti virajjanatthāya. Saṃyogāyāti vaṭṭe saṃyogatthāya. Visaṃyogāyāti vaṭṭe visaṃyogabhāvatthāya. Ācayāyāti vaṭṭassa vaḍḍhanatthāya. No apacayāyāti na vaṭṭaviddhaṃsanatthāya. Dubbharatāyāti dupposanatthāya. No subharatāyāti na sukhaposanatthāya. Imasmiṃ sutte paṭhamavārena vaṭṭaṃ kathitaṃ, dutiyavārena vivaṭṭaṃ kathitaṃ. Iminā ca pana ovādena gotamī arahattaṃ pattāti.

53. In the third discourse: 'For passion' (sarāgāya) means for the purpose of passion. 'For dispassion' (virāgāya) means for the purpose of becoming dispassionate. 'For bondage' (saṃyogāya) means for the purpose of bondage in the round of existence. 'For release' (visaṃyogāya) means for the purpose of the state of being without bondage in the round of existence. 'For accumulation' (ācayāya) means for the purpose of the increase of the round. 'Not for decline' (no apacayāya) means not for the purpose of destroying the round. 'For being difficult to support' (dubbharatāya) means for the purpose of being difficult to support. 'Not for being easy to support' (no subharatāya) means not for the purpose of being easy to support. In this discourse, in the first section the round of existence is taught; in the second section, that which is free from the round is taught. And by this exhortation, Gotamī attained Arahantship.

4. Dīghajāṇusuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Discourse to Dīghajāṇu

54. Catutthe byagghapajjāti idamassa paveṇi nāma vasena [P 4.0138] ālapanaṃ. Tassa hi pubbapurisā byagghapathe jātāti tasmiṃ kule manussā byagghapajjāti vuccanti. Issatthenāti issāsakammena. Tatrupāyāyāti “imasmiṃ kāle idaṃ nāma kātuṃ vaṭṭatī”ti jānane upāyabhūtāya. Vuddhasīlinoti vaḍḍhitasīlā vuddhasamācārā. Āyanti āgamanaṃ. Nāccogāḷhanti nātimahantaṃ. Nātihīnanti nātikasiraṃ. Pariyādāyāti gahetvā khepetvā. Tattha yassa vayato diguṇo āyo, tassa vayo āyaṃ pariyādātuṃ na sakkoti.

54. In the fourth discourse: 'Byagghapajja' is his term of address by way of his family name. For his forefathers were born on a tiger's path; therefore, in that family, people are called 'Byagghapajja.' 'By archery' (issatthena) means by the work of archery. 'By a suitable means for that' (tatrupāyāya) means by being a means for knowing, 'At this time, it is suitable to do such and such a thing.' 'Of mature virtue' (vuddhasīlino) means those of developed virtue, of mature conduct. 'Income' (āyaṃ) means that which comes in. 'Not too much' (nāccogāḷhaṃ) means not too great. 'Not too little' (nātihīnaṃ) means not too miserable. 'Having exhausted' (pariyādāya) means having taken and spent. In that context, for one whose income is double his expenditure, his expenditure is not able to exhaust his income.

“Catudhā vibhaje bhoge, paṇḍito gharamāvasaṃ;

Ekena bhoge bhuñjeyya, dvīhi kammaṃ payojaye;

Catutthañca nidhāpeyya, āpadāsu bhavissatī”ti. (dī. ni. 3.265) –

A wise person, living at home, should divide their wealth into four portions: with one portion, they should enjoy their wealth; with two, they should apply it to their work; and the fourth they should set aside, thinking, 'It will be for me in times of misfortune.'

Evaṃ paṭipajjato pana vayo āyaṃ pariyādātuṃ na sakkotiyeva.

However, for one who practices thus, his expenditure is indeed unable to exhaust his income.

Udumbarakhādīvāti yathā udumbarāni khāditukāmena pakke udumbararukkhe cālite ekappahāreneva bahūni phalāni patanti, so khāditabbayuttakāni khāditvā itarāni bahutarāni pahāya gacchati, evamevaṃ yo āyato vayaṃ bahutaraṃ katvā vippakiranto bhoge paribhuñjati, so “udumbarakhādikaṃvāyaṃ kulaputto bhoge khādatī”ti vuccati. Ajeṭṭhamaraṇanti [M 3.0240] anāyakamaraṇaṃ[V 3.0239]. Samaṃ jīvikaṃ kappetīti sammā jīvikaṃ kappeti. Samajīvitāti samajīvitāya jīvitā. Apāyamukhānīti [P 4.0139] vināsassa ṭhānāni.

'Like one who eats udumbara fruits' (udumbarakhādīva) is as follows: just as when a person wishing to eat udumbara fruits shakes a ripe udumbara tree, many fruits fall with a single shake; he eats those that are fit to be eaten and leaves, abandoning the others which are more numerous. In the same way, one who, making his expenditure much greater than his income, enjoys his wealth while scattering it about, is said to be a clansman who 'consumes wealth like one who eats udumbara fruits.' 'Dying without a senior' (ajeṭṭhamaraṇaṃ) means dying without a leader. 'He manages his livelihood evenly' (samaṃ jīvikaṃ kappeti) means he manages his livelihood rightly. 'A balanced livelihood' (samajīvitā) means living by means of a balanced livelihood. 'The mouths of ruin' (apāyamukhāni) means the causes of destruction.

Uṭṭhātā kammadheyyesūti kammakaraṇaṭṭhānesu uṭṭhānavīriyasampanno. Vidhānavāti vidahanasampanno. Sotthānaṃ samparāyikanti sotthibhūtaṃ samparāyikaṃ. Saccanāmenāti buddhattāyeva buddhoti evaṃ avitathanāmena. Cāgo puññaṃ pavaḍḍhatīti cāgo ca sesapuññañca pavaḍḍhati. Imasmiṃ sutte saddhādayo missakā kathitā. Pañcamaṃ uttānameva.

'Energetic in undertakings' (uṭṭhātā kammadheyyesu) means endowed with rising energy in the spheres of work. 'One who arranges' (vidhānavā) means endowed with management. 'Well-being in the future life' (sotthānaṃ samparāyikaṃ) means that which pertains to the future life and is a state of well-being. 'By a true name' (saccanāmena) means by an unerring name, thus: he is called 'Buddha' precisely because of having awakened. 'Giving and merit increase' (cāgo puññaṃ pavaḍḍhati) means that both giving and the remaining merit increase. In this discourse, faith and the other qualities are taught in combination. The fifth is self-evident.

6. Bhayasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Bhaya Sutta

56. Chaṭṭhe gabbhoti gabbhavāso. Diṭṭhadhammikāpīti sandiṭṭhikā gabbhavāsasadisā punapi manussagabbhā. Samparāyikāpīti ṭhapetvā manussagabbhe sesagabbhā. Ubhayaṃ ete kāmā pavuccantīti bhayañca dukkhañca, bhayañca rogo ca, bhayañca gaṇḍo ca, bhayañca sallañca, bhayañca saṅgo ca, bhayañca paṅko ca, bhayañca gabbho cāti evaṃ ubhayaṃ ete kāmā pavuccanti. Sātarūpenāti kāmasukhena. Palipathanti vaṭṭapalipathaṃ. Atikkammāti imasmiṃ ṭhāne vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā assa bhikkhuno arahattappattabhāvo gahito. Evarūpaṃ pajaṃ jātijarūpetaṃ tīsu bhavesu phandamānaṃ avekkhatīti sutte vaṭṭaṃ kathetvā gāthāsu vivaṭṭaṃ kathitanti. Sattamaṭṭhamāni [P 4.0140] uttānatthāneva.

56. In the sixth discourse: ‘The womb’ (gabbha) means conception in the womb. ‘Even in this life’ (diṭṭhadhammikāpi) means that which is visible, similar to conception in the womb, and again, human conception. ‘In the hereafter’ (samparāyikāpi) means, having set aside human conception, the remaining conceptions. ‘Both these are called sensual pleasures’ (ubhayaṃ ete kāmā pavuccanti) means fear and suffering, fear and disease, fear and boils, fear and darts, fear and attachment, fear and mire, and fear and conception in the womb—thus both these are called sensual pleasures. ‘With delightful form’ (sātarūpena) means with the pleasure of sensual desires. ‘A difficult path’ (palipathaṃ) means the difficult path of the cycle of existence. ‘Having transcended’ (atikkamma): in this instance, it should be understood that the state of Arahantship has been attained by that monk who has developed insight. ‘He observes such a being, endowed with birth and old age, trembling in the three states of existence’ means that having taught about the cycle of existence in the discourse, freedom from the cycle is taught in the verses. The seventh and eighth have only an obvious meaning.

9-10. Puggalasuttadvayavaṇṇanā

9-10. Commentary on the Two Puggala Suttas

59-60. Navame ujubhūtoti kāyavaṅkādīnaṃ abhāvena ujuko. Paññāsīlasamāhitoti paññāya ca sīlena ca samannāgato. Yajamānānanti dānaṃ dadantānaṃ. Puññapekkhānanti puññaṃ olokentānaṃ gavesantānaṃ[M 3.0241]. Opadhikanti upadhivipākaṃ, opadhibhūtaṃ ṭhānaṃ appamāṇaṃ. Dasame samukkaṭṭhoti ukkaṭṭho uttamo. Sattānanti sabbasattānaṃ. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

In the ninth discourse: ‘Upright’ (ujubhūto) means straight due to the absence of bodily crookedness and so on. ‘Endowed with wisdom and virtue’ (paññāsīlasamāhito) means endowed with wisdom and with virtue. ‘Of donors’ (yajamānānaṃ) means of those who give gifts. ‘Of those who seek merit’ (puññapekkhānaṃ) means of those who look for and search for merit. ‘Pertaining to the substrata’ (opadhikaṃ) means having the aggregates as its result, an immeasurable state that has become a substratum. In the tenth discourse: ‘Most excellent’ (samukkaṭṭho) means excellent, supreme. ‘Of beings’ (sattānaṃ) means of all beings. The rest is obvious everywhere.

Gotamīvaggo chaṭṭho.

The Gotamī Chapter, the Sixth.

(7) 2. Bhūmicālavaggo

(7) 2. The Bhūmicāla Chapter

1. Icchāsuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Icchā Sutta

61. Sattamassa [V 3.0240] paṭhame pavivittassāti kāyavivekena vivittassa. Nirāyattavuttinoti katthaci anāyattavuttino vipassanākammikassa. Lābhāyāti catupaccayalābhāya. Socī ca paridevī cāti sokī ca paridevī ca. Socicca parideviccātipi pāṭho. Cuto ca saddhammāti taṃkhaṇaṃyeva vipassanāsaddhammā cuto. Imasmiṃ sutte vaṭṭavivaṭṭaṃ kathitaṃ.

61. In the first discourse of the seventh chapter: ‘Secluded’ (pavivittassa) means secluded by the seclusion of the body. ‘Of one whose living is not dependent’ (nirāyattavuttino) means of one whose living is not dependent on anything, one who is engaged in the work of insight meditation. ‘For gain’ (lābhāya) means for the gain of the four requisites. ‘Grieving and lamenting’ (socī ca paridevī ca) means with grief and with lamentation. There is also the reading ‘socicca paridevicca’. ‘Fallen from the good Dhamma’ (cuto saddhammā) means at that very moment, one has fallen from the good Dhamma which is insight. In this discourse, the cycle of existence and freedom from the cycle are taught.

2. Alaṃsuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Alaṃ Sutta

62. Dutiye alaṃ attano alaṃ paresanti attano ca paresañca hitapaṭipattiyaṃ samattho pariyatto anucchaviko. Khippanisantīti [P 4.0141] khippaṃ upadhāreti, khandhadhātuāyatanādīsu kathiyamānesu te dhamme khippaṃ jānātīti attho. Imasmiṃ sutte samathavipassanā kathitā. Puggalajjhāsayena pana desanāvilāsena cetaṃ matthakato paṭṭhāya heṭṭhā otarantaṃ kathitanti.

62. In the second discourse: ‘Sufficient for oneself, sufficient for others’ (alaṃ attano alaṃ paresaṃ) means one is capable, sufficient, and suitable for the practice for the welfare of both oneself and others. ‘Quick to penetrate’ (khippanisanti) means one quickly comprehends; when the aggregates, elements, sense bases, and so on are being taught, one quickly knows those principles—this is the meaning. In this discourse, serenity and insight are taught. However, by means of the individual’s disposition and the grace of the teaching, this is taught descending from the top downwards.

3. Saṃkhittasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Concise Discourse

63. Tatiye evamevāti nikkāraṇeneva. Yathā vā ayaṃ yācati, evameva. Moghapurisāti mūḷhapurisā tucchapurisā. Ajjhesantīti yācanti. Anubandhitabbanti iriyāpathānugamanena anubandhitabbaṃ maṃ na vijahitabbaṃ maññanti. Ājānanatthaṃ apasādento evamāha. Esa kira bhikkhu ovāde dinnepi pamādameva anuyuñjati, dhammaṃ sutvā tattheva vasati, samaṇadhammaṃ kātuṃ na icchati. Tasmā bhagavā evaṃ apasādetvā puna yasmā so arahattassa upanissayasampanno[M 3.0242], tasmā taṃ ovadanto tasmātiha te bhikkhu evaṃ sikkhitabbantiādimāha. Tattha ajjhattaṃ me cittaṃ ṭhitaṃ bhavissati susaṇṭhitaṃ, na ca uppannā pāpakā akusalā dhammā cittaṃ pariyādāya ṭhassantīti iminā tāvassa ovādena niyakajjhattavasena cittekaggatāmatto mūlasamādhi vutto.

63. In the third sutta, 'just so' means without any reason. Just as this monk requests, so it is. 'Foolish persons' means deluded persons, worthless persons. 'They beg' means they request. 'Should be followed' means they think, 'I should be followed by following my posture and should not be abandoned.' Wishing to make it known, he speaks thus, disparagingly. It seems this monk, even when given advice, strives only in heedlessness; having heard the Dhamma, he remains just as he was and does not wish to practice the ascetic’s duty. Therefore, the Blessed One, having disparaged him thus, then—because that monk was endowed with the strong supporting condition for Arahantship—wishing to advise him, spoke the words beginning with, 'Therefore, monk, you should train thus…' Therein, by this instruction, 'Internally my mind will be steady, well-composed, and arisen evil, unwholesome states will not stand overwhelming my mind,' firstly, for that monk, by way of his own internal state, the root concentration, which is the cause of one-pointedness of mind, is spoken of.

Tato [V 3.0241] “ettakeneva santuṭṭhiṃ anāpajjitvā evaṃ so samādhi vaḍḍhetabbo”ti dassetuṃ yato kho te bhikkhu ajjhattaṃ cittaṃ ṭhitaṃ hoti susaṇṭhitaṃ, na ca uppannā pāpakā akusalā dhammā cittaṃ pariyādāya tiṭṭhanti, tato te bhikkhu evaṃ sikkhitabbaṃ [P 4.0142] “mettā me cetovimutti bhāvitā bhavissati…pe… susamāraddhā”ti evamassa mettāvasena bhāvanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā puna yato kho te bhikkhu ayaṃ samādhi evaṃ bhāvito hoti bahulīkato, tato tvaṃ bhikkhu imaṃ samādhiṃ savitakkasavicārampi bhāveyyāsītiādi vuttaṃ. Tassattho – yadā te bhikkhu ayaṃ mūlasamādhi evaṃ mettāvasena bhāvito hoti, tadā tvaṃ tāvatakenapi tuṭṭhiṃ anāpajjitvāva imaṃ mūlasamādhiṃ aññesupi ārammaṇesu catukkapañcakajjhānāni pāpayamāno “savitakkasavicārampī”tiādinā nayena bhāveyyāsīti.

Then, to show that 'without being content with just this, that concentration should be developed,' it is said: 'When, monk, your mind is internally steady and well-composed, and arisen evil, unwholesome states do not stand overwhelming the mind, then, monk, you should train thus: “My liberation of mind through loving-kindness will be developed… thoroughly undertaken.”' Thus, for that monk, having developed the meditation by way of loving-kindness, it is further said: 'When, monk, this concentration is thus developed and frequently practiced, then you, monk, should develop this concentration with applied and sustained thought,' and so on. The meaning is: when, monk, this root concentration is thus developed by way of loving-kindness, then you, without being content with just that, should develop this root concentration, while causing it to attain the fourfold and fivefold jhānas in other objects as well, by the method beginning with 'with applied and sustained thought.'

Evaṃ vatvā ca pana avasesabrahmavihārapubbaṅgamampissa aññesu ārammaṇesu catukkapañcakajjhānabhāvanaṃ kareyyāsīti dassento yato kho te bhikkhu ayaṃ samādhi evaṃ bhāvito hoti subhāvito, tato te bhikkhu evaṃ sikkhitabbaṃ “karuṇā me cetovimuttī”tiādimāha. Evaṃ mettāpubbaṅgamaṃ catukkapañcakajjhānabhāvanaṃ dassetvā puna kāyānupassanādipubbaṅgamaṃ dassetuṃ yato kho te bhikkhu ayaṃ samādhi evaṃ bhāvito hoti subhāvito, tato te bhikkhu evaṃ sikkhitabbaṃ “kāye kāyānupassī”tiādiṃ vatvā yato kho te bhikkhu ayaṃ samādhi evaṃ bhāvito hoti subhāvito, tato tvaṃ bhikkhu yena yeneva gagghasītiādimāha. Tattha gagghasīti gamissasi. Phāsuṃyevāti iminā arahattaṃ dasseti. Arahattappatto hi sabbiriyāpathesu phāsu viharati nāma.

And having said this, to show that for that monk one should also practice the development of the fourfold and fivefold jhānas in other objects, preceded by the remaining divine abidings, he said, 'When, monk, this concentration is thus developed and well-developed, then you, monk, should train thus: “My liberation of mind through compassion…”' and so on. After showing the development of the fourfold and fivefold jhānas preceded by loving-kindness, to then show the development preceded by the contemplation of the body and so on, after saying, 'When, monk, this concentration is thus developed and well-developed, then you, monk, should train thus: “Contemplating the body in the body…”' and so on, he then said, 'When, monk, this concentration is thus developed and well-developed, then you, monk, wherever you go…' and so on. Therein, `gagghasi` means 'you will go.' By 'at ease indeed,' he shows Arahantship. For one who has attained Arahantship is indeed said to dwell at ease in all postures.

4. Gayāsīsasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Gayāsīsa Sutta

64. Catutthe [M 3.0243] etadavocāti attano padhānabhūmiyaṃ uppannaṃ vitakkaṃ bhikkhusaṅghassa ārocetuṃ – “pubbāhaṃ, bhikkhave”tiādivacanaṃ avoca. Obhāsanti [P 4.0143] dibbacakkhuñāṇobhāsaṃ. Ñāṇadassananti dibbacakkhubhūtaṃ ñāṇasaṅkhātaṃ dassanaṃ. Sannivutthapubbanti ekato vasitapubbaṃ. Imasmiṃ pana sutte dibbacakkhuñāṇaṃ, iddhividhañāṇaṃ, cetopariyañāṇaṃ, yathākammupagañāṇaṃ, anāgataṃsañāṇaṃ, paccuppannaṃsañāṇaṃ, atītaṃsañāṇaṃ, pubbenivāsañāṇanti imāni tāva aṭṭha ñāṇāni pāḷiyaṃyeva āgatāni, tehi pana saddhiṃ vipassanāñāṇāni cattāri maggañāṇāni, cattāri [V 3.0242] phalañāṇāni, cattāri paccavekkhaṇañāṇāni, cattāri paṭisambhidāñāṇāni cha asādhāraṇañāṇānīti etāni ñāṇāni samodhānetvā kathentena evaṃ idaṃ suttaṃ kathitaṃ nāma hoti.

64. In the fourth sutta, 'he spoke thus' means: to announce to the Saṅgha of monks the thought that arose in His own place of striving, He spoke the words beginning, 'Formerly, I, monks...'. 'Radiance' means the radiance of the knowledge of the divine eye. 'Knowledge and vision' means the vision, which is the divine eye, called knowledge. 'Previously dwelt together' means having lived together. However, in this sutta, these eight knowledges have come in the Pāḷi itself: the knowledge of the divine eye, the knowledge of psychic powers, the knowledge of others' minds, the knowledge of the passing away and rebirth of beings according to their kamma, the knowledge of the future, the knowledge of the present, the knowledge of the past, and the knowledge of previous lives. Furthermore, together with these, by one who explains having collected the nine insight knowledges, the four path knowledges, the four fruition knowledges, the four reviewing knowledges, the four analytical knowledges, and the six uncommon knowledges—by explaining thus, this sutta comes to be well explained.

5. Abhibhāyatanasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Abhibhāyatana Sutta

65. Pañcame abhibhāyatanānīti abhibhavanakāraṇāni. Kiṃ abhibhavanti? Paccanīkadhammepi ārammaṇānipi. Tāni hi paṭipakkhabhāvena paccanīkadhamme abhibhavanti, puggalassa ñāṇuttariyatāya ārammaṇāni. Ajjhattaṃ rūpasaññītiādīsu pana ajjhattarūpe parikammavasena ajjhattaṃ rūpasaññī nāma hoti. Ajjhattañhi nīlaparikammaṃ karonto kese vā pitte vā akkhitārakāya vā karoti. Pītaparikammaṃ karonto mede vā chaviyā vā hatthatalapādatalesu vā akkhīnaṃ pītaṭṭhāne vā karoti. Lohitaparikammaṃ karonto maṃse vā lohite vā jivhāya vā akkhīnaṃ rattaṭṭhāne vā karoti. Odātaparikammaṃ karonto aṭṭhimhi vā dante vā nakhe vā akkhīnaṃ setaṭṭhāne vā karoti. Taṃ pana sunīlakaṃ supītakaṃ sulohitakaṃ suodātaṃ na hoti, avisuddhameva hoti.

65. In the fifth sutta, 'bases of mastery' means the causes for overcoming. What do they overcome? Both opposing states and objects. For they overcome opposing states by being their counterpart, and they overcome objects due to the superiority of a person's knowledge. Now, in phrases such as 'one who perceives form internally,' one is called 'one who perceives form internally' by means of preliminary practice on an internal form. Indeed, one practicing the blue preliminary meditation internally does so on the hair, or the bile, or the pupils of the eyes. One practicing the yellow preliminary meditation does so on the fat, or the skin, or the palms of the hands and soles of the feet, or the yellow parts of the eyes. One practicing the red preliminary meditation does so on the flesh, or the blood, or the tongue, or the red parts of the eyes. One practicing the white preliminary meditation does so on a bone, or the teeth, or the nails, or the white parts of the eyes. But that form is not perfectly blue, perfectly yellow, perfectly red, or perfectly white; it is only impure.

Eko bahiddhā rūpāni passatīti yassevaṃ parikammaṃ ajjhattaṃ uppannaṃ hoti, nimittaṃ pana bahiddhā, so evaṃ ajjhattaṃ [P 4.0144] parikammassa bahiddhā ca appanāya vasena “ajjhattaṃ rūpasaññī eko bahiddhā rūpāni passatī”ti vuccati. Parittānīti avaḍḍhitāni. Suvaṇṇadubbaṇṇānīti suvaṇṇāni vā hontu dubbaṇṇāni vā, parittavaseneva idaṃ abhibhāyatanaṃ vuttanti veditabbaṃ. Tāni abhibhuyyāti yathā nāma sampannaggahaṇiko kaṭacchumattaṃ bhattaṃ labhitvā “kiṃ ettha bhuñjitabbaṃ [M 3.0244] atthī”ti saṃkaḍḍhitvā ekakabaḷameva karoti, evameva ñāṇuttariko puggalo visadañāṇo “kimettha parittake ārammaṇe samāpajjitabbaṃ atthi, nāyaṃ mama bhāro”ti tāni rūpāni abhibhavitvā samāpajjati, saha nimittuppādenevettha appanaṃ pāpetīti attho. Jānāmi passāmīti iminā panassa ābhogo kathito. So ca kho samāpattito vuṭṭhitassa, na antosamāpattiyaṃ. Evaṃsaññī hotīti ābhogasaññāyapi jhānasaññāyapi evaṃsaññī hoti. Abhibhavanasaññā hissa antosamāpattiyampi atthi, ābhogasaññā pana samāpattito vuṭṭhitasseva.

One sees forms externally means: for a person whose preliminary practice has arisen internally, but the counterpart sign has arisen externally, that person is called 'one who, perceiving form internally, sees forms externally' by means of internal preliminary practice and external absorption. Limited means undeveloped. Of good color and bad color means: whether they are of good color or bad color, it should be known that this base of mastery is spoken of by way of the limited. Having mastered them: just as a person with a good appetite, upon receiving a mere ladleful of rice, thinks, 'What is there to eat here?' and, gathering it up, makes just a single mouthful, in the same way a person with superior, clear knowledge thinks, 'What is there to attain in this limited object? This is not difficult for me,' and masters those forms and enters absorption. The meaning is that, in this case, he attains absorption simultaneously with the very arising of the counterpart sign. By I know, I see, his advertence is described. And that occurs for one who has emerged from the attainment, not while within the attainment. He is one who perceives thus means he perceives thus with both the perception related to advertence and the perception related to jhāna. Indeed, for him the perception of mastery exists even within the attainment, but the perception related to advertence exists only for one who has emerged from the attainment.

Appamāṇānīti [V 3.0243] vaḍḍhitappamāṇāni, mahantānīti attho. Abhibhuyyāti ettha ca pana yathā mahagghaso puriso ekaṃ bhattavaḍḍhitakaṃ labhitvā “aññāpi hotu, aññāpi hotu, kiṃ esā mayhaṃ karissatī”ti na taṃ mahantato passati, evameva ñāṇuttaro puggalo visadañāṇo “kiṃ ettha samāpajjitabbaṃ, nayidaṃ appamāṇaṃ, na mayhaṃ cittekaggatākaraṇe [P 4.0145] bhāro atthī”ti abhibhavitvā samāpajjati, saha nimittuppādanevettha appanaṃ pāpetīti attho.

Immeasurable means of increased measure; the meaning is 'great.' And here, regarding having mastered: just as a gluttonous person, upon receiving a single large serving of rice, thinks, 'Let there be another, and another! What can this do for me?' and does not regard it as much, in the same way, a person with superior, clear knowledge thinks, 'What is there to attain here? This is not immeasurable; there is no burden for me in achieving one-pointedness of mind,' and having mastered it, enters absorption. The meaning is that, in this case, he attains absorption simultaneously with the very arising of the counterpart sign.

Ajjhattaṃ arūpasaññīti alābhitāya vā anatthikatāya vā ajjhattarūpe parikammasaññāvirahito.

One who is not percipient of form internally means: one who is devoid of the perception of preliminary practice regarding internal form, either due to not having obtained it or due to not desiring it.

Eko bahiddhā rūpāni passatīti yassa parikammampi nimittampi bahiddhā uppannaṃ, so evaṃ bahiddhā parikammassa ceva appanāya ca vasena “ajjhattaṃ arūpasaññī eko bahiddhā rūpāni passatī”ti vuccati. Sesamettha catutthābhibhāyatane ca vuttanayameva. Imesu pana catūsu parittaṃ vitakkacaritavasena āgataṃ, appamāṇaṃ mohacaritavasena, suvaṇṇaṃ dosacaritavasena, dubbaṇṇaṃ rāgacaritavasena. Etesañhi etāni sappāyāni. Sā ca nesaṃ sappāyatā visuddhimagge (visuddhi. 1.43) cariyaniddese vuttā.

One sees forms externally means: a person for whom both the preliminary practice and the counterpart sign have arisen externally is, by means of external preliminary practice and absorption, called 'one who, not percipient of form internally, sees forms externally.' The remainder here is in the same way as was stated for the fourth base of mastery. But among these four, the limited object is for one of a speculative temperament, the immeasurable for one of a deluded temperament, the object of good color for one of a hateful temperament, and the object of bad color for one of a lustful temperament. Indeed, for these persons, these objects are suitable. And their suitability is stated in the Visuddhimagga in the Exposition on Temperaments.

Pañcamaabhibhāyatanādīsu nīlānīti sabbasaṅgāhikavasena vuttaṃ. Nīlavaṇṇānīti vaṇṇavasena. Nīlanidassanānīti nidassanavasena. Apaññāyamānavivarāni asambhinnavaṇṇāni ekanīlāneva hutvā dissantīti vuttaṃ hoti. Nīlanibhāsānīti [M 3.0245] idaṃ pana obhāsavasena vuttaṃ, nīlobhāsāni nīlappabhāyuttānīti attho. Etena nesaṃ suvisuddhataṃ dasseti. Visuddhavaṇṇavaseneva hi imāni abhibhāyatanāni vuttāni. “Nīlakasiṇaṃ uggaṇhanto nīlasmiṃ nimittaṃ gaṇhāti pupphasmiṃ vā vatthasmiṃ vā vaṇṇadhātuyā vā”tiādikaṃ panettha kasiṇakaraṇañca [P 4.0146] parikammañca appanāvidhānañca sabbaṃ visuddhimagge (visuddhi. 1.55) vitthārato vuttamevāti.

In the fifth mastery sphere and so on, 'blue' is said by way of encompassing all. 'Blue-colored' is said by way of color. 'Of blue appearance' is said by way of appearance. This is what is meant: having indiscernible gaps and unmixed color, they appear as being of a solid blue. As for 'of blue radiance', this is said by way of radiance, meaning they have a blue radiance, are endowed with a blue radiance. By this, their special purity is shown. For indeed, these mastery spheres are spoken of only by way of pure color. Herein, that which begins, “One who is taking up the blue kasiṇa grasps the sign in a blue object, whether in a flower, or in a cloth, or in a color-element,” and also the making of the kasiṇa, the preliminary work, and the method of absorption—all this is already explained in detail in the Visuddhimagga.

6. Vimokkhasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Vimokkha Sutta

66. Vimokkhāti kenaṭṭhena vimokkhā? Adhimuccanaṭṭhena. Ko panāyaṃ adhimuccanaṭṭho nāma? Paccanīkadhammehi ca suṭṭhu muccanaṭṭho, ārammaṇe ca abhirativasena suṭṭhu muccanaṭṭho, pituaṅke [V 3.0244] vissaṭṭhaṅgapaccaṅgassa dārakassa sayanaṃ viya aniggahitabhāvena nirāsaṅkatāya ārammaṇe pavattīti vuttaṃ hoti. Ayaṃ panattho pacchime vimokkhe natthi, purimesu vimokkhesu atthi.

6. ‘Liberations’—in what sense are they liberations? In the sense of being resolved upon. And what is this sense of being resolved upon? It is the sense of being well released from opposing states, and the sense of being well released by way of delight in the object. This is what is meant: it proceeds towards the object without being restrained and with a lack of hesitation, like the lying down of a child with relaxed limbs on its father’s lap. This meaning, however, is not present in the final liberation, but it is present in the earlier liberations.

Rūpī rūpāni passatīti ettha ajjhattaṃ kesādīsu nīlakasiṇādivasena uppāditaṃ rūpajjhānaṃ rūpaṃ, tadassatthīti rūpī. Bahiddhāpi nīlakasiṇādīni rūpāni jhānacakkhunā passati. Iminā ajjhattabahiddhavatthukesu kasiṇesu uppāditajjhānassa puggalassa cattāri rūpāvacarajjhānāni dassitāni. Ajjhattaṃ arūpasaññīti ajjhattaṃ na rūpasaññī, attano kesādīsu anuppāditarūpāvacarajjhānoti attho. Iminā bahiddhā parikammaṃ katvā bahiddhāva uppāditajjhānassa rūpāvacarajjhānāni dassitāni.

66. Regarding ‘One with form perceives forms’: here, the form-jhāna produced internally by way of the blue kasiṇa, etc., in relation to one’s own hair and so on, is ‘form’; one who possesses that is ‘one with form’. Externally, one perceives forms such as the blue kasiṇa with the eye of jhāna. By this, the four form-sphere jhānas of a person who has produced jhāna in kasiṇas based on internal and external objects are shown. Regarding ‘Not percipient of form internally’: this means one is not percipient of form internally; the meaning is that one has not produced a form-sphere jhāna in relation to one’s own hair and so on. By this, the form-sphere jhānas of one who, having done the preliminary work externally, has produced jhāna only externally, are shown.

Subhanteva adhimutto hotīti iminā suvisuddhesu nīlādīsu vaṇṇakasiṇesu jhānāni dassitāni. Tattha kiñcāpi antoappanāya “subha”nti ābhogo natthi, yo pana suvisuddhaṃ subhaṃ kasiṇaṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā viharati, so yasmā “subhanti adhimutto hotī”ti vattabbataṃ āpajjati, tasmā evaṃ desanā katā. Paṭisambhidāmagge pana –

By ‘One is resolved only upon the beautiful,’ the jhānas in relation to the especially pure color kasiṇas, such as blue, are shown. Therein, although within absorption there is no reflection of ‘beautiful,’ one who dwells having made an especially pure and beautiful kasiṇa the object, since they attain a state that can be described as ‘resolved upon the beautiful,’ hence it is taught thus. In the Paṭisambhidāmagga, however—

“Kathaṃ subhanteva adhimutto hotīti vimokkho? Idha bhikkhu mettāsahagatena cetasā ekaṃ disaṃ [P 4.0147]…pe… viharati. Mettāya bhāvitattā sattā appaṭikūlā honti. Karuṇāsahagatena…pe… muditāsahagatena [M 3.0246] …pe… upekkhāsahagatena cetasā ekaṃ disaṃ…pe… viharati. Upekkhāya bhāvitattā sattā appaṭikūlā honti. Evaṃ subhanteva adhimutto hotīti vimokkho”ti (paṭi. ma. 1.212) vuttaṃ.

“How is the liberation one of being resolved only upon the beautiful? Here, a bhikkhu dwells pervading one direction with a mind accompanied by loving-kindness… Because loving-kindness has been developed, beings become non-repugnant. With a mind accompanied by compassion… with appreciative joy… with equanimity, one dwells pervading one direction… Because equanimity has been developed, beings become non-repugnant. Thus is the liberation one of being resolved only upon the beautiful,” it is said.

Sabbaso rūpasaññānantiādīsu yaṃ vattabbaṃ, taṃ visuddhimagge (visuddhi. 1.276-277) vuttameva. Ayaṃ aṭṭhamo vimokkhoti ayaṃ catunnaṃ khandhānaṃ sabbaso vissaṭṭhattā vimuttattā aṭṭhamo uttamo vimokkho nāma.

Regarding ‘the complete transcendence of perceptions of form’ and so on, whatever should be said has already been said in the Visuddhimagga. As for ‘This is the eighth liberation’: this is called the eighth, supreme liberation because of complete detachment from and liberation from the four aggregates.

7-8. Anariyavohārasuttavaṇṇanā

7–8. Explanation of the Discourse on Ignoble Speech

67-68. Sattame [V 3.0245] anariyavohārāti na ariyakathā sadosakathā. Yāhi cetanāhi te vohāre voharanti, tāsaṃ etaṃ nāmaṃ. Aṭṭhame vuttapaṭipakkhanayena attho veditabbo.

67–68. In the seventh, ‘ignoble speech’ means speech that is not noble, culpable speech. This is the name for the intentions by which they utter those expressions. In the eighth, the meaning should be understood in the manner that is the opposite of what has been said.

9. Parisāsuttavaṇṇanā

9. Explanation of the Discourse on Assemblies

69. Navame khattiyaparisāti khattiyānaṃ parisānaṃ sannipāto samāgamo. Esa nayo sabbattha. Anekasataṃ khattiyaparisanti bimbisārasamāgama-ñātisamāgama-licchavisamāgamādisadisaṃ, aññesu cakkavāḷesupi labbhateva. Sallapitapubbanti ālāpasallāpo katapubbo. Sākacchāti dhammasākacchāpi samāpajjitapubbā. Yādisako tesaṃ vaṇṇoti te odātāpi honti kāḷāpi maṅguracchavīpi, satthā suvaṇṇavaṇṇo. Idaṃ pana saṇṭhānaṃ paṭicca [P 4.0148] kathitaṃ. Saṇṭhānampi ca kevalaṃ tesaṃ paññāyatiyeva. Na pana bhagavā milakkhasadiso hoti, nāpi āmuttamaṇikuṇḍalo, buddhaveseneva nisīdati. Tepi attano samānasaṇṭhānameva passanti. Yādisako tesaṃ saroti te chinnassarāpi honti gaggassarāpi kākassarāpi, satthā brahmassarova. Idaṃ pana bhāsantaraṃ sandhāya kathitaṃ. Sacepi hi satthā rājāsane nisinno katheti, “ajja rājā madhurena kathetī”ti nesaṃ hoti. Kathetvā pakkante pana bhagavati puna rājānaṃ āgataṃ disvā “ko nu kho aya”nti vīmaṃsā uppajjati. Tattha ko nu kho ayanti “imasmiṃ ṭhāne idāneva māgadhabhāsāya sīhaḷabhāsāya madhurena ākārena kathento ko nu kho ayaṃ antarahito, kiṃ devo udāhu [M 3.0247] manusso”ti evaṃ vīmaṃsantāpi na jānantīti attho. Kimatthaṃ panevaṃ ajānantānaṃ dhammaṃ desetīti? Vāsanatthāya. Evaṃ sutopi hi dhammo anāgate paccayo hotīti anāgataṃ paṭicca deseti. Anekasataṃ brāhmaṇaparisantiādinaṃ soṇadaṇḍasamāgamādivasena ceva aññacakkavāḷavasena ca sambhavo veditabbo.
