Iminā [P 4.0176] cattāro arūpakkhandhā bhūtupādāyarūpañca vitakkānaṃ paccayoti dasseti. Kva nānattaṃ gacchantīti kasmiṃ ṭhāne nānāsabhāvataṃ vemattaṃ gacchanti. Dhātusūti rūpadhātuādīsu. Aññoyeva hi rūpavitakko, aññe saddavitakkādayoti. Phassasamudayāti sampayuttaphassapaccayā. Vedanāsamosaraṇāti tisso vedanā samosaraṇā. Ettakena kusalākusalamissakā kathitā. Samādhippamukhātiādayo pana apacayapakkhikāti veditabbā. Tattha [V 3.0264] pubbaṅgamaṭṭhena jeṭṭhakaṭṭhena vā samādhi pamukhaṃ etesanti samādhippamukhā. Jeṭṭhakakāraṇaṭṭhena sati adhipateyyā etesanti satādhipateyyā. Maggapaññā uttarā etesanti paññuttarā. Phalavimuttiṃ patvā sārappattā hontīti vimuttisārā. Ārammaṇavasena amataṃ nibbānaṃ ogāhitvā [M 3.0266] tattha patiṭṭhitāti amatogadhā. Tena ca mā maññīti tena vissajjanena “ahaṃ aggasāvakena pucchite pañhe vissajjesi”nti mā mānaṃ vā dappaṃ vā akāsi.

14. In the fourth sutta, Samiddhi: this is the name given to an elder, a co-resident of the Elder Sāriputta, because of the perfection of his person. 'With what as object?' means with what as condition. 'Thoughts of intention' means thoughts that have become intentions. 'With name-and-form as object' means conditioned by name-and-form. By this, it is shown that the four immaterial aggregates and primary and derived material form are the condition for thoughts. 'Where do they become diverse?' means in what place do they attain a diverse nature, a difference. 'In the elements' means in the form element, etc. For indeed, thought concerning form is one thing; thoughts concerning sound, etc., are other things. 'Arising from contact' means they are conditioned by associated contact. 'Converging in feeling' means the three feelings are their convergence point. By this much, thoughts that are a mixture of wholesome and unwholesome are spoken of. But 'with concentration as their chief,' etc., should be understood as belonging to the side of the unconditioned. Herein, because concentration is their chief in the sense of being a forerunner or in the sense of being the principal, they are 'those with concentration as their chief'. Because mindfulness is their authority in the sense of making it the principal, they are 'those with mindfulness as their authority'. Because the wisdom of the path is superior to them, they are 'those for whom wisdom is superior'. Having attained the liberation that is the fruit, they have reached the essence; therefore, they are 'those with liberation as their essence'. Having plunged into the Deathless, Nibbāna, by way of object, they are established therein; therefore, they are 'those who have plunged into the Deathless'. 'And by that, do not think' means: by that answer, do not give rise to conceit or pride, thinking, 'I have answered the questions asked by the chief disciple'.

5-6. Gaṇḍasuttādivaṇṇanā

5-6. Commentary on the Gaṇḍa Sutta and others

15-16. Pañcame tīṇi cattāri vassāni vassagaṇā, aneke vassagaṇā uppannā assāti anekavassagaṇiko. Tassassūti tassa bhaveyyuṃ. Abhedanamukhānīti [P 4.0177] na kenaci bhinditvā katāni, kevalaṃ kammasamuṭṭhitāneva vaṇamukhāni. Jegucchiyaṃyevāti jigucchitabbameva paṭikūlameva. Cātumahābhūtikassāti catumahābhūtamayassa. odanakummāsūpacayassāti odanena ceva kummāsena ca upacitassa vaḍḍhitassa. Aniccucchādanaparimaddanabhedanaviddhaṃsanadhammassāti hutvā abhāvaṭṭhena aniccadhammassa, duggandhavighātatthāya tanuvilepanena ucchādanadhammassa, aṅgapaccaṅgābādhavinodanatthāya khuddakasambāhanena parimaddanadhammassa, daharakāle vā ūrūsu sayāpetvā gabbhavāsena dussaṇṭhitānaṃ tesaṃ tesaṃ aṅgapaccaṅgānaṃ saṇṭhānasampādanatthaṃ añchanapīḷanādivasena parimaddanadhammassa, evaṃ pariharitassāpi ca bhedanaviddhaṃsanadhammassa, bhijjanavikiraṇasabhāvassevāti attho. Ettha ca aniccapadena ceva bhedanaviddhaṃsanapadehi cassa atthaṅgamo kathito, sesehi samudayo. Nibbindathāti ukkaṇṭhatha pajahatha imaṃ kāyanti dasseti. Evamimasmiṃ sutte balavavipassanā kathitā. Chaṭṭhaṃ vuttanayameva. Saññāsīsena panettha ñāṇameva kathitaṃ.

In the fifth sutta: Because many counts of years—three years, four years, many counts of years—have arisen for it, it is called 'of many years' count'. 'For it there would be' means they would be for it. 'With un-cut openings' means they are not openings made by anyone by cutting, but are simply sore-openings arisen from kamma. 'It is simply loathsome' means it is simply to be detested, simply repulsive. 'Of the four great elements' means made of the four great elements. 'Accumulated by rice and barley-meal' means accumulated and nourished by rice and by barley-meal. 'Having the nature of impermanence, of being scrubbed, massaged, breaking up, and being destroyed' means: having the nature of impermanence in the sense of non-existence after having been; having the nature of being scrubbed with a thin unguent to destroy a bad smell; having the nature of being massaged with a little rubbing to dispel pain in the major and minor limbs; or, having the nature of being massaged by means of stretching, pressing, and so forth, in order to perfect the shape of those various major and minor limbs which are ill-formed due to dwelling in the womb, after having been made to lie on the thighs in youth; and even when cared for thus, it has the nature of breaking up and being destroyed—the meaning is that it has the very nature of breaking up and scattering. And here, its passing away is spoken of by the word 'impermanent' and by the words 'breaking up' and 'being destroyed'; its arising is spoken of by the remaining terms. 'You should be disenchanted' shows: 'Be weary, abandon this body.' Thus, in this sutta, powerful insight is taught. The sixth is in the same way as has been stated. Here, however, it is just knowledge that is taught under the heading of perception.

7-8. Kulasuttādivaṇṇanā

7-8. Commentary on the Kula Sutta and others.

17-18. Sattame na manāpena paccuṭṭhentīti manavaḍḍhanena [P 4.0178] manaṃ allīyanākārena āsanā vuṭṭhāya paccuggamanaṃ na karonti. Na manāpena abhivādentīti na pañcapatiṭṭhitena vandanti. Asakkaccaṃ dentīti acittīkārena denti. No sakkaccanti sahatthā na denti. Na upanisīdanti dhammasavanāyāti “dhammaṃ suṇissāmā”ti na samīpe nisīdanti. Na sussūsantīti ghaṭapiṭṭhe [V 3.0265] āsittaudakaṃ viya vivaṭṭetvā gacchati. Aṭṭhame veneyyajjhāsayavasena mettābhāvanaṃ pakkhipitvā navaṅgasamannāgatoti vuttaṃ.

In the seventh sutta, 'they do not rise up in a pleasing manner' means they do not rise from their seats and go forward to meet them in a manner that increases the mind and causes it to cling. 'They do not pay homage in a pleasing manner' means they do not pay homage with the five-point prostration. 'They give carelessly' means they give disrespectfully. 'Not carefully' means they do not give with their own hands. 'They do not sit nearby to listen to the Dhamma' means they do not sit nearby thinking, 'We will listen to the Dhamma.' 'They do not wish to listen' means it rolls away like water poured on the outer surface of a pot. In the eighth sutta, by including the cultivation of loving-kindness in accordance with the disposition of those to be trained, it is said to be 'endowed with nine factors'.

9. Devatāsuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Devatā Sutta

19. Navame [M 3.0267] vippaṭisāriniyoti vippaṭisāritaṃ maṅkubhāvaṃ āpajjimha. Hīnaṃ kāyanti uparidevalokaṃ upādāya heṭṭhimo hīnoti vuccati. No ca kho yathāsatti yathābalaṃ saṃvibhajimhāti attano sattiyā ca balassa ca anurūpena sīlavantānaṃ saṃvibhāgaṃ katvā na bhuñjimhā.

19. In the ninth sutta, 'being regretful' means 'we fell into a state of regret and dismay'. Regarding 'an inferior body', with reference to the upper deva world, the one that is lower is called 'inferior'. 'And we did not share according to our ability and strength' means we did not make a sharing with the virtuous in accordance with our own ability and strength.

10. Velāmasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Velāma Sutta

20. Dasame api nu te, gahapati, kule dānaṃ dīyatīti nayidaṃ bhagavā bhikkhusaṅghassa dānaṃ sandhāya pucchati. Seṭṭhissa hi ghare bhikkhusaṅghassa niccaṃ paṇītadānaṃ dīyati, na taṃ satthā na jānāti. Lokiyamahājanassa pana diyyamānadānaṃ atthi, taṃ lūkhaṃ hoti, seṭṭhissa cittaṃ na pīṇeti. Taṃ pucchāmīti pucchati. Kaṇājakanti [P 4.0179] sakuṇḍakabhattaṃ, sakuṇḍakehipi kaṇikataṇḍuleheva pakkaṃ. Biḷaṅgadutiyanti kañjiyadutiyaṃ. Asakkaccaṃ detīti asakkaritvā deti. Acittīkatvāti acittīkārena dakkhiṇeyya agāravena deti. Asahatthā detīti sahatthena adatvā parahatthena deti, āṇattimattameva karotīti attho. Apaviddhaṃ detīti na nirantaraṃ deti, saṃvaccharikaṃ soṇḍabali viya hoti. Anāgamanadiṭṭhiko detīti na kammañca phalañca saddahitvā deti.

20. In the tenth sutta, regarding the phrase 'But, householder, is giving offered in your family?', the Blessed One does not ask this with reference to giving to the Saṅgha of bhikkhus. Indeed, in the treasurer's house, an excellent gift is always given to the Saṅgha of bhikkhus; it is not that the Teacher does not know this. However, there is a gift that is given to the worldly multitude. That gift is coarse; it does not delight the treasurer's mind. Thinking, 'I will ask about that,' he asks. 'A meal of broken rice' means a coarse meal cooked with broken rice. 'With sour gruel as the second item' means having a dish of sour gruel as the second item. 'One gives carelessly' means one gives without respect. 'Without respect' means one gives in a disrespectful manner, with irreverence for the recipients of the offering. 'Not with one's own hand' means not giving with one's own hand, one gives through the hand of another; the meaning is that one only gives the command. 'One gives what is thrown away' means one does not give continuously; making it a yearly event, it is like a drunkards' offering. 'One gives with the view that there is no future result' means one gives without faith in kamma and its fruit.

Yattha yatthāti tīsu kulasampadāsu yasmiṃ yasmiṃ kule. Na uḷārāya bhattabhogāyātiādīsu nānaggarasasugandhasālibhojane upanīte cittaṃ na namati, “harathetaṃ rogavaḍḍhana”nti vatvā yena vā tena vā ḍākena saddhiṃ sakuṇḍakabhattaṃ amataṃ viya sampiyāyamāno bhuñjati. Kāsikādīsu varavatthesu upanītesu “harathetāni nivāsentassa paṭicchādetumpi na sakkonti, gattesupi na saṇṭhahantī”ti vatvā nāḷikerasāṭakamūlatacasadisāni pana thūlavatthāni “imāni nivāsento nivatthabhāvampi jānāti, paṭicchādetabbampi [V 3.0266] paṭicchādentī”ti sampiyāyamāno nivāseti. Hatthiyānaassayānarathayānasuvaṇṇasivikādīsu [P 4.0180] upanītesu “harathetāni calācalāni, na sakkā ettha nisīditu”nti vatvā jajjararathake upanīte “ayaṃ niccalo, ettha sukhaṃ nisīditu”nti taṃ sādiyati. Na uḷāresu pañcasu kāmaguṇesūti alaṅkatapaṭiyattā rūpavatiyo itthiyo [M 3.0268] disvā “yakkhiniyo maññe, etā khāditukāmā, kiṃ etāhī”ti yathāphāsukeneva vītināmeti. Na sussūsantīti sotuṃ na icchanti, na saddahantīti attho. Na sotaṃ odahantīti kathitassa savanatthaṃ na sotapasādaṃ odahanti. Sakkaccantiādīni vuttavipariyāyena veditabbāni.

'In whichever, whichever' means in whichever family among the three kinds of family prosperity. Regarding 'not for excellent enjoyment of food' and so forth: when food—that is, fragrant sāli rice with various excellent flavors—is brought, his mind does not incline toward it. Saying, 'Take this away—it increases illness,' he eats a coarse meal of broken rice together with some simple vegetable stew, greatly delighting in it as if it were ambrosia. When excellent cloths from places like the Kāsi kingdom are brought, he says, 'Take these away—they are not able to cover one who wears them, nor do they stay on the body.' Instead, greatly delighting, he wears coarse cloths similar to coconut-fiber cloth or root-bark, thinking, 'Wearing these, one knows one is clothed, and what needs to be covered is covered.' When elephant-vehicles, horse-vehicles, chariot-vehicles, golden palanquins, and so forth are brought, he says, 'Take these away—they are unsteady; one cannot sit in them.' But when a dilapidated chariot is brought, he delights in it, saying, 'This is steady; one can sit comfortably here.' Regarding 'not in the five excellent strands of sensual pleasure': seeing women who are adorned, prepared, and beautiful, he thinks, 'I suppose they are yakkhiṇīs; they want to devour me. What is the use of them?' and he passes his time with whatever is simple and comfortable. 'They do not wish to listen' means they do not wish to hear; the meaning is also 'they do not have faith'. 'They do not lend an ear' means they do not apply their faculty of hearing for the purpose of hearing what is said. 'Attentively' and so forth should be understood in the opposite way to what has been stated.

Velāmoti jātigottarūpabhogasaddhāpaññādīhi mariyādavelaṃ atikkantehi uḷārehi guṇehi samannāgatattā evaṃladdhanāmo. So evarūpaṃ dānaṃ adāsi mahādānanti ettha ayaṃ anupubbīkathā – so kira atīte bārāṇasiyaṃ purohitagehe paṭisandhiṃ gaṇhi, velāmakumārotissa nāmaṃ akaṃsu. So soḷasavassakāle bārāṇasirājakumārena saddhiṃ sippuggahaṇatthaṃ takkasilaṃ agamāsi. Te ubhopi disāpāmokkhassa ācariyassa santike sippaṃ paṭṭhapayiṃsu. Yathā ca te, evaṃ aññepi jambudīpe caturāsītisahassarājakumārā. Bodhisatto attanā gahitaṭṭhāne piṭṭhiācariyo hutvā caturāsīti rājakumārasahassāni sikkhāpeti, sayampi soḷasavassehi gahetabbasippaṃ tīhi vassehi uggaṇhi. Ācariyo “velāmakumārassa sippaṃ paguṇa”nti ñatvā, “tātā, velāmo [P 4.0181] mayā ñātaṃ sabbaṃ jānāti, tumhe sabbepi samaggā gantvā etassa santike sippaṃ uggaṇhathā”ti caturāsīti kumārasahassāni bodhisattassa niyyādesi.

Velāma was so named because he was endowed with excellent qualities that surpassed the boundary of birth, lineage, form, wealth, faith, wisdom, and so on. Regarding 'He gave such a gift, a great gift,' here is the sequential story. It is said that in the past, he took conception in the household of a chaplain in Bārāṇasī, and they gave him the name Velāmakumāra. At the age of sixteen, he went to Takkasilā with the prince of Bārāṇasī to learn the arts. Both of them began their studies in the presence of the teacher who was the chief of the directions. Just as they did, so too did eighty-four thousand other princes in Jambudīpa. The Bodhisatta, in the subjects he himself had mastered, became the assistant teacher and taught the eighty-four thousand princes. He himself learned in three years the art that was to be learned in sixteen years. The teacher, knowing that Velāmakumāra was proficient in the art, entrusted the eighty-four thousand princes to the Bodhisatta, saying, 'Sirs, Velāma knows everything that is known by me. All of you, go together and learn the art from him.'

Bodhisatto ācariyaṃ vanditvā caturāsīti kumārasahassaparivāro nikkhamitvā ekaṃ āsannanagaraṃ patvā nagarasāmikaṃ rājakumāraṃ uggaṇhāpetvā tassa sippe paguṇe jāte taṃ tattheva nivattesi. Etenupāyena caturāsīti nagarasahassāni gantvā caturāsītiyā rājakumārānaṃ sippaṃ paguṇaṃ kāretvā tasmiṃ tasmiṃ nagare taṃ taṃ nivattetvā bārāṇasirājakumāraṃ ādāya bārāṇasiṃ paccāgañchi. Manussā kumāraṃ pariyositasippaṃ rajje abhisiñciṃsu, velāmassa purohitaṭṭhānaṃ adaṃsu. Tepi caturāsītisahassarājakumārā sakesu sakesu rajjesu abhisekaṃ patvā anusaṃvaccharaṃ bārāṇasirañño upaṭṭhānaṃ āgacchanti. Te rājānaṃ disvā velāmassa santikaṃ gantvā, “ācariya, amhe rajjesu patiṭṭhitā, vadeyyātha yenattho”ti vatvā gacchanti. Tesaṃ gamanāgamanakāle [V 3.0267] sakaṭasandamānikagāvigoṇakukkuṭasūkarādayo gaṇhantānaṃ janapado ativiya [M 3.0269] upadduto hoti, mahājano sannipatitvā rājaṅgaṇe kandati.

The Bodhisatta, having paid homage to his teacher, departed accompanied by eighty-four thousand princes. He arrived at a nearby city and had the local prince instructed. When that prince became proficient in his skill, he left him there. By this method, he went to eighty-four thousand cities, caused the skill of the eighty-four thousand princes to become proficient, and having left each one in his respective city, he took the prince of Bārāṇasī and returned to Bārāṇasī. The people consecrated the prince, whose training in the arts was completed, in the kingship and gave Velāma the position of chaplain. Those eighty-four thousand princes, having attained consecration in their own kingdoms, would come every year to attend upon the king of Bārāṇasī. Having seen the king, they would go to Velāma and say, 'Teacher, we are established in our kingdoms; please tell us what you need,' and then depart. During their comings and goings, the countryside was greatly oppressed by them seizing carts, palanquins, cows, oxen, chickens, pigs, and the like. The great populace gathered and wept in the royal courtyard.

Rājā velāmaṃ pakkositvā, “ācariya, upadduto janapado, rājāno gamanāgamanakāle mahāvilopaṃ karonti, manussā sandhāretuṃ na sakkonti, janapadapīḷāya upasamaṃ ekaṃ upāyaṃ karothā”ti[P 4.0182]. Sādhu mahārāja, upāyaṃ karissāmi, tumhākaṃ yattakena janapadena attho, taṃ paricchinditvā gaṇhathāti. Rājā tathā akāsi. Velāmo caturāsītiyā rājasahassānaṃ janapade vicāretvā cakkanābhiyaṃ are viya rañño janapadasmiṃ oropesi. Tato paṭṭhāya te rājāno āgacchantāpi gacchantāpi attano attano janapadeneva sañcaranti, amhākaṃ janapadoti vilopaṃ na karonti. Rājagāravena rañño janapadampi na pīḷenti. Janapadā sannisinnā nissaddā niravā ahesuṃ. Sabbe rājāno haṭṭhatuṭṭhā “yena vo, ācariya, attho, taṃ amhākaṃ vadethā”ti pavārayiṃsu.

The king summoned Velāma and said, 'Teacher, the countryside is oppressed. The kings cause great destruction when they come and go, and the people cannot endure it. Please devise a means to appease the affliction of the countryside.' 'Very well, great king, I will devise a means. Demarcate and take however much of the countryside you need.' The king did so. Velāma organized the countrysides of the eighty-four thousand kings and established them around the king's countryside like spokes in the hub of a wheel. From then on, those kings, whether coming or going, traveled only through their own countrysides. Thinking, 'This is our countryside,' they did not plunder. Out of respect for the king, they did not oppress the king's countryside either. The countrysides became settled, quiet, and free from lamentation. All the kings, joyful and pleased, invited him, saying, 'Teacher, whatever you need, please tell us.'

Velāmo sīsaṃnhāto attano antonivesane sattaratanaparipūrānaṃ gabbhānaṃ dvārāni vivarāpetvā yāva sattamā kulaparivaṭṭā ṭhapitaṃ dhanaṃ oloketvā āyavayaṃ upadhāretvā “mayā sakalajambudīpaṃ khobhentena dānaṃ dātuṃ vaṭṭatī”ti rañño ārocetvā gaṅgātīre dvādasayojanikā uddhanapantiyo kāretvā tasmiṃ tasmiṃ ṭhāne sappimadhuphāṇitatelatilataṇḍulādīnaṃ ṭhapanatthāya mahākoṭṭhāgārāni patiṭṭhāpetvā “ekekasmiṃ ṭhāne ettakā ettakā janā saṃvidahatha, yaṃkiñci manussānaṃ laddhabbaṃ nāma atthi, tato ekasmimpi asati mayhaṃ āroceyyāthā”ti manusse saṃvidhāya “asukadivasato paṭṭhāya velāmabrāhmaṇassa dānaṃ bhuñjantū”ti nagare bheriṃ carāpetvā “dānaggaṃ pariniṭṭhita”nti dānayuttehi ārocite sahassagghanakaṃ vatthaṃ nivāsetvā [P 4.0183] pañcasatagghanakaṃ ekaṃsaṃ katvā sabbālaṅkārabhūsito dānavīmaṃsanatthāya phalikavaṇṇassa udakassa suvaṇṇabhiṅgāraṃ pūretvā “imasmiṃ loke sace imaṃ dānaṃ paṭiggahetuṃ yuttarūpā dakkhiṇeyyapuggalā atthi, idaṃ udakaṃ nikkhamitvā pathaviṃ gaṇhātu. Sace natthi, evameva tiṭṭhatū”ti saccakiriyaṃ katvā bhiṅgāraṃ adhomukhaṃ akāsi. Udakaṃ dhamakaraṇena gahitaṃ viya ahosi. Bodhisatto “suñño vata, bho, jambudīpo, ekapuggalopi dakkhiṇaṃ paṭiggahetuṃ yuttarūpo natthī”ti vippaṭisāraṃ akatvā “sace dāyakassa [M 3.0270] vasenāyaṃ dakkhiṇā visujjhissati, udakaṃ nikkhamitvā pathaviṃ gaṇhātū”ti cintesi. Phalikavaṇṇasadisaṃ udakaṃ nikkhamitvā pathaviṃ gaṇhi[V 3.0268]. “Idāni dānaṃ dassāmī”ti dānaggaṃ patvā dānaṃ oloketvā yāguvelāya yāguṃ, khajjakavelāya khajjakaṃ, bhojanavelāya bhojanaṃ dāpesi. Eteneva nīhārena divase divase dānaṃ dīyati.

Velāma, having bathed his head, had the doors of the inner chambers in his own residence opened. These chambers were filled with the seven kinds of treasures. He surveyed the wealth that had been stored for up to seven generations of his family, and having considered his income and expenditure, he thought, 'It is fitting for me to give a gift that will stir up the whole of Jambudīpa.' Having informed the king, he had twelve-yojana-long rows of hearths made on the bank of the Ganges. In each of those places, he established great storehouses for storing ghee, honey, molasses, oil, sesame, rice, and other such things. He organized the people, saying, 'In each and every place, let this many people make arrangements. Whatever things are to be received by people, if even one of them is missing, you must inform me.' He had a drum beaten in the city, proclaiming, 'From such-and-such a day, let people partake of the gift of Velāma the brahmin.' When it was announced by those engaged in the almsgiving that the alms hall was completed, he put on a garment worth a thousand, placed a garment worth five hundred over one shoulder, and, adorned with all ornaments, he filled a golden water-pot with water the color of crystal in order to investigate the gift. He made an act of truth, saying, 'If in this world there are worthy persons, individuals deserving of offerings, fit to receive this gift, let this water come forth and take the earth. If there are not, let it remain just so.' Having made this act of truth, he turned the water-pot upside down. The water was as if held by a water-strainer. The Bodhisatta, without feeling remorse, thought, 'Alas, Jambudīpa is empty! There is not even one person fit to receive my offering.' Then he reflected, 'If this offering is to be purified by the power of the giver, let the water come forth and take the earth.' The water, like the color of crystal, came forth and took the earth. Thinking, 'Now I will give the gift,' he went to the alms hall. Having surveyed the gift, at the time for gruel, he had gruel given; at the time for hard food, he had hard food given; and at the time for the main meal, he had the main meal given. In this very manner, day after day, the gift was given.

Tasmiṃ kho pana dānagge “idaṃ nāma atthi, idaṃ nāma natthī”ti vattabbaṃ natthi. Idāni taṃ dānaṃ ettakamatteneva na niṭṭhaṃ gamissatīti rattasuvaṇṇaṃ nīharāpetvā suvaṇṇapātiyo kāretvā caturāsītisuvaṇṇapātisahassādīnaṃ atthāya caturāsītirājasahassānaṃ sāsanaṃ pahiṇi. Rājāno “cirassaṃ vata mayaṃ ācariyena anuggahitā”ti sabbaṃ sampādetvā pesesuṃ. Dāne diyyamāneyeva satta vassāni satta māsā atikkantā. Atha brāhmaṇo “hiraññaṃ bhājetvā dānaṃ dassāmī”ti mahante okāse dānaṃ sajjāpesi. Sajjāpetvā caturāsīti suvaṇṇapātisahassāni ādiṃ katvā koṭito paṭṭhāya adāsi.

Now, at that place of giving, there was no occasion to say, 'This is available,' or 'This is not available.' Thinking, 'Now this gift will not be completed with just this much,' he had red gold brought out and golden bowls made. For the sake of eighty-four thousand golden bowls and so on, he sent a message to eighty-four thousand kings. The kings, thinking, 'Indeed, after a long time we have been favored by our teacher,' prepared everything and sent it. While the gift was still being given, seven years and seven months passed. Then the brahmin, thinking, 'I will distribute the wealth and give the gift,' had the gift prepared in a vast space. Having had it prepared, beginning with the eighty-four thousand golden bowls, he gave, starting from one end.

Tattha [P 4.0184] rūpiyapūrānīti rajatataṭṭirajataphālarajatamāsakehi pūrāni. Pātiyo pana khuddikāti na sallakkhetabbā, ekakarīsappamāṇe bhūmibhāge catassova pātiyo ṭhapayiṃsu. Pātimakuḷaṃ navaratanaṃ hoti, mukhavaṭṭito paṭṭhāya aṭṭharatanaṃ, pātimukhavaṭṭiyā chayutto ājaññaratho anupariyāyati, dadamāno pātiyā bāhirantena vaggavagge paṭiggāhake ṭhapetvā paṭhamaṃ pātiyā pakkhittaṃ datvā pacchā sandhisandhito viyojetvā pātinti evaṃ caturāsīti pātisahassāni adāsi. Rūpiyapātiādīsupi eseva nayo. Etthapi ca suvaṇṇapūrānīti suvaṇṇataṭṭisuvaṇṇaphālasuvaṇṇamāsakehi pūrāni. Hiraññapūrānīti sattavidharatanapūrāni. Sovaṇṇālaṅkārānīti suvaṇṇālaṅkārāni. Kaṃsūpadhāraṇānīti rajatamayakhīrapaṭicchakāni. Tāsaṃ pana dhenūnaṃ siṅgāni suvaṇṇakosakapariyonaddhāni ahesuṃ, gīvāya sumanadāmaṃ piḷandhiṃsu, catūsu pādesu nupūrāni, piṭṭhiyaṃ varadukūlaṃ pārutaṃ, kaṇṭhe suvaṇṇaghaṇṭaṃ bandhiṃsu. Vatthakoṭisahassānīti lokavohārato vīsativatthayugāni ekā koṭi[M 3.0271], idha pana dasa sāṭakāti vuttaṃ. Khomasukhumānantiādimhi [P 4.0185] khomādīsu yaṃ yaṃ sukhumaṃ, taṃ tadeva adāsi. Yāni panetāni itthidānaṃ usabhadānaṃ majjadānaṃ samajjādānanti adānasammatāni, tānipi esa “velāmassa dānamukhe idaṃ nāma natthī”ti vacanapathaṃ pacchindituṃ parivāratthāya adāsi. Najjo maññe vissandantīti nadiyo viya vissandanti.

Therein, 'filled with silver' means filled with silver bowls, silver ingots, and silver coins. The bowls, however, should not be considered small; on a plot of land the size of one karīsa, they placed only four bowls. The mouth of the bowl is nine cubits; from the rim, it is eight cubits. A yoked chariot drawn by thoroughbreds circles around the rim of the bowl. When giving, having placed recipients in groups at the outer edge of the bowl, and having first given what was placed in the bowl, he afterwards gives, having separated it from the joint. In this way, he gave eighty-four thousand bowls. In the case of the silver bowls and so on, this is the same method. Here too, 'filled with gold' means filled with gold bowls, gold ingots, and gold coins. 'Filled with bullion' means filled with the seven kinds of gems. 'Golden ornaments' means having golden ornaments. 'Bronze vessels for holding' means silver containers for holding milk. The horns of those cows were covered with golden sheaths; on their necks, they were adorned with excellent flower garlands; on their four feet, golden hooves were bound; and on their backs, excellent white cloth was bound. Regarding 'a thousand koṭis of cloth': according to worldly convention, twenty pairs of cloths are one koṭi, but here it is said that ten cloths are one koṭi. Regarding 'fine linen, etc.': among linen and other cloths, whichever was fine, that very one he gave. As for those gifts considered to be non-gifts—namely, the gift of women, the gift of bulls, the gift of intoxicants, and the gift of festivals—this Velāma gave those too, for the sake of being a complete retinue and to cut off the course of speech that 'At Velāma's place of giving, such-and-such a thing is not available.' 'Rivers, I think, are flowing' means they flow like rivers.

Iminā [V 3.0269] satthā velāmassa dānaṃ kathetvā, “gahapati, etaṃ mahādānaṃ nāñño adāsi, ahaṃ adāsiṃ. Evarūpaṃ pana dānaṃ dadantopi ahaṃ paṭiggahetuṃ yuttarūpaṃ puggalaṃ nālatthaṃ, tvaṃ mādise buddhe lokasmiṃ diṭṭhamāne dānaṃ dadamāno kasmā cintesī”ti seṭṭhissa desanaṃ vaḍḍhento siyā kho pana tetiādimāha. Nanu ca yāni tadā ahesuṃ rūpavedanāsaññāsaṅkhāraviññāṇāni, tāni niruddhāni? Kasmā “ahaṃ tena samayena velāmo brāhmaṇo”ti āhāti? Paveṇiyā avicchinnattā. Tāni hi rūpādīni nirujjhamānāni imesaṃ paccaye datvā niruddhāni aparāparaṃ avicchinnaṃ paveṇiṃ gahetvā evamāha. Na taṃ koci dakkhiṇaṃ sodhetīti koci samaṇo vā brāhmaṇo vā devo vā māro vā uṭṭhāya taṃ dakkhiṇaṃ sodhetīti vattabbo nāhosi. Tañhi dakkhiṇaṃ sodhento uttamakoṭiyā buddho, heṭṭhimakoṭiyā dhammasenāpatisāriputtattherasadiso sāvako sodheyya.

By this, the Teacher, having spoken of Velāma's gift, and wishing to augment the teaching for the wealthy merchant, spoke the words beginning with 'siyā kho pana te', saying: 'Householder, no other person gave that great gift; I gave it. But even while giving such a gift, I did not get a suitable person to receive it. You, while a Buddha like me is seen in the world, why do you hesitate when giving a gift?' Is it not the case that the form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness that existed at that time have ceased? Why did he say, 'At that time, I was the brahmin Velāma'? He said it because of the unbrokenness of the continuity. To elaborate: those aggregates of form and so on, while ceasing, ceased having given conditions for these present aggregates. Taking up the successive, unbroken continuity, he spoke thus. 'No one purifies that offering' means it is not to be said that any ascetic, brahmin, god, or Māra, having arisen, purifies that offering. Indeed, one who could purify that offering would be, at the highest level, a Buddha; at the lowest level, a disciple like the Elder Sāriputta, the General of the Dhamma, could purify it.

Diṭṭhisampannanti dassanasampannaṃ sotāpannaṃ. Idaṃ tato mahapphalataranti idaṃ sotāpannassa dinnadānaṃ lokiyamahājanassa sattamāsādhikāni satta saṃvaccharāni [P 4.0186] ettakaṃ hiraññasuvaṇṇaṃ pariccajantena dinnadānato mahapphalaṃ.

'One accomplished in view' means a stream-enterer, one accomplished in vision. 'This is more fruitful than that' means this gift given to a stream-enterer is of greater fruit than the gift given to a multitude of worldlings by one who renounces so much gold and silver for seven years and seven months.

Yo ca sataṃ diṭṭhisampannānanti ettha ekassa sakadāgāmissa vasena ekuttarasataṃ sotāpanne katvā sotāpannagaṇanā veditabbā. Iminā upāyena sabbavāresu heṭṭhā heṭṭhā āgate anantarena sataguṇaṃ katvā puggalagaṇanā veditabbā.

Regarding 'And he who feeds a hundred accomplished in view': here, the enumeration of stream-enterers should be understood by making it one hundred and one by means of one once-returner. By this method, in all cases, the enumeration of persons should be understood by considering the person immediately following in the hierarchy to be one hundred times more valuable than those below.

Buddhappamukhanti ettha sammāsambuddhaṃ saṅghattheraṃ katvā nisinno saṅgho buddhappamukho saṅghoti veditabbo. Cātuddisaṃ saṅghaṃ uddissāti ettha cātuddisaṃ saṅghaṃ uddissa katavihāro nāma yattha cetiyaṃ patiṭṭhitaṃ hoti, dhammassavanaṃ karīyati, catūhi disāhi anudisāhi ca bhikkhū āgantvā appaṭipucchitvāyeva pāde dhovitvā kuñcikāya dvāraṃ vivaritvā senāsanaṃ paṭijaggitvā [M 3.0272] vasitvā yathāphāsukaṃ gacchanti. So antamaso caturataniyā paṇṇasālāpi hotu, cātuddisaṃ saṅghaṃ uddissa katavihārotveva vuccati.

Here, 'the Sangha headed by the Buddha' means that the Sangha, with the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One made its elder, is to be understood as the Sangha headed by the Buddha. Here, 'dedicated to the Sangha of the four quarters' means that a monastery made dedicated to the Sangha of the four quarters is one where a shrine is established, the Dhamma is heard, and bhikkhus come from the four directions and intermediate directions, and without asking anyone, they wash their feet, open the door with a key, prepare their lodging, stay, and leave at their ease. Even if it is merely a leaf hut four cubits in size, it is still called a monastery made dedicated to the Sangha of the four quarters.

Saraṇaṃ gaccheyyāti ettha maggenāgataṃ anivattanasaraṇaṃ adhippetaṃ. Apare panāhu – attānaṃ niyyādetvā dinnattā saraṇāgamanaṃ tato mahapphalataranti vuttaṃ. Sikkhāpadāni samādiyeyyāti pañca [V 3.0270] sīlāni gaṇheyya. Sīlampi maggena āgataṃ anivattanasīlameva kathitaṃ. Apare panāhu – sabbasattānaṃ abhayadānassa dinnattā sīlaṃ tato mahapphalataranti vuttaṃ. Gandhohanamattanti [P 4.0187] gandhaūhanamattaṃ, dvīhaṅgulīhi gaṇḍapiṇḍaṃ gahetvā upasiṅghanamattaṃ. Apare pana “gaddohanamatta”nti pāḷiṃ vatvā gāviyā ekavāraṃ thanaañchanamattanti atthaṃ vadanti. Mettacittanti sabbasattānaṃ hitānupharaṇacittaṃ. Taṃ pana appanāvaseneva gahitaṃ. Aniccasaññanti maggassa anantarapaccayabhāvena sikhāpattabalavavipassanaṃ.

Here, ‘he would go for refuge’ intends the irreversible refuge that comes by way of the path. But other teachers say that going for refuge is said to be more fruitful than that because of having given oneself over. ‘He would undertake the training rules’ means he would take up the five precepts. The virtue, too, spoken of is only the irreversible virtue that comes by way of the path. But other teachers say that virtue is said to be more fruitful than that because the gift of fearlessness is given to all beings. ‘Just a moment for fragrance’ means for the duration of smearing on perfume, or for the duration of taking a lump of perfume with two fingers and sniffing it. But others, stating the Pāḷi reading as ‘gaddohanamattaṃ,’ say the meaning is ‘for the duration of pulling a cow's teat once.’ ‘A mind of loving-kindness’ means a mind that pervades all beings with their welfare, and that is taken only by way of absorption. ‘Perception of impermanence’ means powerful insight that has reached its peak, being the proximate condition for the path.

Upamāto pana imāni dānādīni puññāni evaṃ veditabbāni – sacepi hi jambudīpaṃ bheritalasadisaṃ samatalaṃ katvā koṭito paṭṭhāya pallaṅke attharitvā ariyapuggale nisīdāpeyya, tattha sotāpannānaṃ dasa pantiyo assu, sakadāgāmīnaṃ pañca, anāgāmīnaṃ aḍḍhateyyā, khiṇāsavānaṃ diyaḍḍhā, paccekabuddhānaṃ ekā panti bhaveyya, sammāsambuddho ekakova. Ettakassa janassa dinnadānato sammāsambuddhassa dinnameva mahapphalaṃ. Itaraṃ pana –

By way of comparison, these meritorious deeds such as giving should be understood thus: if one were to make Jambudīpa a level surface like that of a drum, and, beginning from the edge, spread couches and cause noble persons to be seated there, there would be ten rows of stream-enterers, five of once-returners, two and a half of non-returners, one and a half of Arahants, and one row of Paccekabuddhas; the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One would be alone. Compared to the gift given to such an assembly of persons, the gift given to the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One is indeed of great fruit. As for the other—

“Vihāradānaṃ paṇipāto, sikkhā mettāya bhāvanā;

Khayato sammasantassa, kalaṃ nāgghati soḷasiṃ”.

The gift of a monastery, reverence, training, the development of loving-kindness—for one who contemplates from the standpoint of cessation, these are not worth a sixteenth part.

Teneva bhagavā parinibbānasamaye “dhammānudhammappaṭipatti anuttarā pūjā”ti āha. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

For that very reason, at the time of His final passing away, the Blessed One said, ‘The practice in accordance with the Dhamma is the unsurpassed homage.’ The rest has a clear meaning everywhere.

Sīhanādavaggo dutiyo.

The Chapter of the Lion's Roar, the second.

3. Sattāvāsavaggo

3. The Chapter on the Abodes of Beings.

1. Tiṭhānasuttavaṇṇanā

1. In the first [sutta] of the third [chapter].

21. Tatiyassa [M 3.0273][V 3.0271][P 4.0188] paṭhame uttarakurukāti uttarakuruvāsino. Adhiggaṇhantīti adhibhavanti, adhikā visiṭṭhā jeṭṭhakā honti. Amamāti nittaṇhā. Aṭṭhakathāyaṃ pana niddukkhāti vuttaṃ. Apariggahāti “idaṃ mayha”nti pariggaharahitā. Niyatāyukāti tesañhi nibaddhaṃ āyu vassasahassameva, gatipi nibaddhā, tato cavitvā saggeyeva nibbattanti. Satimantoti devatānañhi ekantasukhitāya sati thirā na hoti, nerayikānaṃ ekantadukkhitāya. Imesaṃ pana vokiṇṇasukhadukkhattā sati thirā hoti. Idha brahmacariyavāsoti jambudīpe buddhapaccekabuddhānaṃ uppajjanato aṭṭhaṅgikamaggabrahmacariyavāsopi idheva hoti.

21. ‘Uttarakurukā’ means the inhabitants of Uttarakuru. ‘Adhiggaṇhanti’ means they overcome; they are superior, distinguished, and foremost. ‘Amamā’ means without craving. In the commentary, however, it is said to mean ‘without suffering.’ ‘Apariggahā’ means free from possession, from thinking, ‘This is mine.’ ‘Niyatāyukā’ means they have a fixed lifespan, for their lifespan is fixed at one thousand years indeed, and their destiny is also fixed; having passed away from there, they are reborn only in a heavenly realm. ‘Satimanto’ means mindful—for the devas, because of their exclusive happiness, mindfulness is not firm, and for the beings in hell, because of their exclusive suffering. But for these inhabitants of Jambudīpa, because of the mixture of happiness and suffering, mindfulness is firm. ‘Here there is the living of the holy life’ means that because of the arising of Buddhas and Paccekabuddhas in Jambudīpa, the living of the holy life of the eight-factored path also occurs only here.

2. Assakhaḷuṅkasuttavaṇṇanā

2. The Explanation of the Assakhaḷuṅka Sutta.

22. Dutiye javasampannoti padajavena sampanno. Na vaṇṇasampannoti na sarīravaṇṇena sampanno. Purisakhaḷuṅkesu javasampannoti ñāṇajavena sampanno. Na vaṇṇasampannoti na guṇavaṇṇena sampanno. Sesaṃ pāḷinayeneva veditabbaṃ. Yañhettha vattabbaṃ siyā, taṃ tikanipātavaṇṇanāyaṃ vuttameva.

22. In the second sutta, ‘endowed with speed’ means endowed with speed of foot. ‘Not endowed with beauty’ means not endowed with beauty of body. Among untamed men, ‘endowed with speed’ means endowed with swiftness of wisdom. ‘Not endowed with beauty’ means not endowed with the beauty of virtues. The rest should be understood according to the method of the Pāḷi text. Whatever should be said here has already been said in the explanation of the Tikanipāta.

3. Taṇhāmūlakasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Sutta Rooted in Craving.

23. Tatiye taṇhaṃ paṭiccāti dve taṇhā esanataṇhā esitataṇhā ca. Yāya taṇhāya ajapathasaṅkupathādīni paṭipajjitvā [P 4.0189] bhoge esati gavesati, ayaṃ esanataṇhā nāma. Yā tesu esitesu gavesitesu paṭiladdhesu taṇhā, ayaṃ esitataṇhā nāma. Idha pana esanataṇhā daṭṭhabbā. Pariyesanāti rūpādiārammaṇapariyesanā. Sā hi esanataṇhāya sati hoti. Lābhoti rūpādiārammaṇapaṭilābho. So hi pariyesanāya sati hoti.

23. In the third sutta, ‘dependent on craving’ refers to two kinds of craving: craving in seeking and craving for what is sought. The craving by which, having gone along goat-paths, stake-paths, and so on, one seeks and searches for possessions, is called ‘craving in seeking.’ The craving for those possessions that have been sought, searched for, and obtained, is called ‘craving for what is sought.’ Here, however, ‘craving in seeking’ should be understood. ‘Searching’ is the search for objects such as forms. For that occurs when there is craving in seeking. ‘Gain’ is the acquisition of objects such as forms. For that occurs when there is searching.

Vinicchayo [V 3.0272] pana ñāṇataṇhādiṭṭhivitakkavasena catubbidho. Tattha “sukhavinicchayaṃ jaññā, sukhavinicchayaṃ ñatvā ajjhattaṃ sukhamanuyuñjeyyā”ti (ma. ni. 3.323) ayaṃ ñāṇavinicchayo[M 3.0274]. “Vinicchayāti dve vinicchayā taṇhāvinicchayo ca diṭṭhivinicchayo cā”ti (mahāni. 102) evaṃ āgatāni aṭṭhasatataṇhāvicaritāni taṇhāvinicchayo. Dvāsaṭṭhi diṭṭhiyo diṭṭhivinicchayo. “Chando kho, devānaminda, vitakkanidāno”ti (dī. ni. 2.358) imasmiṃ pana sutte idha vinicchayoti vutto vitakkoyeva āgato. Lābhaṃ labhitvā hi iṭṭhāniṭṭhaṃ sundarāsundaraṃ vitakkeneva vinicchinanti “ettakaṃ me rūpārammaṇatthāya bhavissati, ettakaṃ saddhārammaṇatthāya, ettakaṃ mayhaṃ bhavissati, ettakaṃ parassa, ettakaṃ paribhuñjissāmi, ettakaṃ nidahissāmī”ti. Tena vuttaṃ – lābhaṃ paṭicca vinicchayoti.

Decision, however, is fourfold, by way of knowledge, craving, view, and thought. Therein, “One should know the decision of happiness; having known the decision of happiness, one should pursue inner happiness”—this is decision by knowledge. “‘Decision’—there are two decisions: decision by craving and decision by view”—thus, the one hundred and eight courses of craving that have been mentioned are decision by craving. The sixty-two views are decision by view. “Desire, O lord of the devas, has thought as its source”—in this sutta, however, what is spoken of as ‘decision’ is simply thought. For having obtained a gain, they decide what is desirable and undesirable, beautiful and ugly, by thought alone: “This much will be for my purpose of form-objects, this much for the purpose of sound-objects, this much will be for me, this much for another, this much I will enjoy, this much I will store away.” Therefore it is said: ‘Dependent on gain, there is decision.’

Chandarāgoti [P 4.0190] evaṃ akusalavitakkena vitakkite vatthusmiṃ dubbalarāgo ca balavarāgo ca uppajjati. Idañhi idha chandoti dubbalarāgassādhivacanaṃ. Ajjhosānanti ahaṃ mamanti balavasanniṭṭhānaṃ. Pariggahoti taṇhādiṭṭhivasena pariggahakaraṇaṃ. Macchariyanti parehi sādhāraṇabhāvassa asahanatā. Tenevassa porāṇā evaṃ vacanatthaṃ vadanti – “idaṃ acchariyaṃ mayhameva hotu, mā aññassa acchariyaṃ hotūti pavattattā macchariyanti vuccatī”ti. Ārakkhoti dvārapidahanamañjūsāgopanādivasena suṭṭhu rakkhanaṃ. Adhikarotīti adhikaraṇaṃ, kāraṇassetaṃ nāmaṃ. Ārakkhādhikaraṇanti bhāvanapuṃsakaṃ, ārakkhāhetūti attho. Daṇḍādānādīsu paranisedhanatthaṃ daṇḍassa ādānaṃ daṇḍādānaṃ. Ekato dhārādino satthassa ādānaṃ satthādānaṃ. Kalahoti kāyakalahopi vācākalahopi. Purimo viggaho, pacchimo vivādo (dī. ni. aṭṭha. 2.103). Tuvaṃtuvanti agāravavasena tuvaṃtuvaṃvacanaṃ.

Regarding 'desire-lust' (chandarāga): in an object thus thought of with unwholesome thought, both weak lust and strong lust arise. For here, 'desire' (chanda) is a designation for weak lust. 'Clinging' (ajjhosāna) is the strong determination, 'I,' 'mine.' 'Possessiveness' (pariggaha) is the act of taking possession by way of craving and view. 'Stinginess' (macchariya) is the inability to bear sharing with others. Therefore, the ancients state the meaning of this word thus: 'Because it proceeds as, "May this wonder be for me alone; may this wonder not be for another," it is called stinginess.' 'Guarding' (ārakkha) is protecting well by means of closing doors, securing chests, and so on. Because it brings about (adhikaroti) a result, it is called a cause (adhikaraṇa); this is a name for a cause. 'Guarding as a cause' (ārakkhādhikaraṇa) is an abstract neuter noun; the meaning is 'due to guarding.' In 'taking up sticks' (daṇḍādāna), etc., the taking up of a stick to prevent others is daṇḍādāna. The taking up of a weapon such as a single-edged blade is 'taking up a weapon' (satthādāna). 'Conflict' (kalaha) is both bodily conflict and verbal conflict. The former is 'dispute' (viggaha), the latter is 'contention' (vivāda). 'You-you' (tuvaṃtuvaṃ) is the saying of 'you, you' by way of disrespect.

4. Sattāvāsasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Sattāvāsa Sutta

24. Catutthe sattāvāsāti sattānaṃ āvāsā, vasanaṭṭhānānīti attho. Tattha suddhāvāsāpi sattāvāsova, asabbakālikattā pana na gahitā. Suddhāvāsā hi buddhānaṃ khandhāvāraṭṭhānasadisā, asaṅkheyyakappe buddhesu anibbattesu taṃ ṭhānaṃ suññaṃ hoti. Iti asabbakālikattā na gahitā. Sesamettha [P 4.0191] viññāṇaṭṭhitīsu vuttanayeneva veditabbaṃ.

24. In the fourth sutta, 'abodes of beings' (sattāvāsa) means 'abodes of beings,' that is, 'places of dwelling.' Herein, the Pure Abodes are also an abode of beings, but they are not included because they do not exist at all times. Indeed, the Pure Abodes are like the encampments of Buddhas; for an incalculable aeon when Buddhas do not arise, that place is empty. Thus, because they do not exist at all times, they are not included. The remainder here should be understood in the same way as has been stated regarding the stations of consciousness.

5. Paññāsuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Paññā Sutta

25. Pañcame [M 3.0275][V 3.0273] yatoti yasmiṃ kāle. Suparicitaṃ hotīti suṭṭhu upacitaṃ suvaḍḍhitaṃ hoti. Kallaṃ vacanāyāti yuttaṃ vattuṃ. Vītarāganti vigatarāgaṃ. Asarāgadhammanti na sarajjanasabhāvaṃ. Anāvattidhammanti anāvattanasabhāvaṃ anibbattārahaṃ, appaṭisandhikabhāveneva nirujjhanasabhāvanti attho. Imasmiṃ sutte khīṇāsavova kathito.

25. In the fifth sutta, 'when' (yato) means 'at which time.' 'Well-practiced' (suparicitaṃ hoti) means it is well accumulated and well developed. 'Fit for speech' (kallaṃ vacanāya) means suitable to say. 'Devoid of passion' (vītarāgaṃ) means having departed from passion. 'A nature of non-passion' (asarāgadhammaṃ) means a nature of not being impassioned. 'Not subject to return' (anāvattidhammaṃ) means having a nature of not returning, not fit to be reborn, having a nature of ceasing precisely because of not taking rebirth—this is the meaning. In this sutta, only one whose taints are destroyed is spoken of.

6. Silāyūpasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Silāyūpa Sutta

26. Chaṭṭhe candikāputtoti mātu nāmavasena paññāto candikāputtatthero. Cetasā cittaṃ hotīti cittavārapariyāyena cittavārapariyāyo cito vaḍḍhito hoti. Cetasā cittaṃ suparicitanti cittavārapariyāyena cittavārapariyāyo uparūpari sucito suvaḍḍhito hoti. Nevassa cittaṃ pariyādiyantīti tāni ārammaṇāni tassa khīṇāsavassa cittuppādaṃ gahetvā khepetvā ṭhātuṃ na sakkonti. Amissīkatanti tāni ārammaṇāni anallīnattā tehi amissīkataṃ. Āneñjappattanti [P 4.0192] aniñjanabhāvaṃ nipphandanabhāvaṃ pattaṃ.

26. In the sixth sutta, 'Candikāputta' refers to the Elder Candikāputta, who was known by his mother’s name. 'Accumulated by mind' (cetasācitaṃ hoti) means that the cycle of mind is accumulated and developed by means of the cycle of mind. 'Well-practiced with the mind' (cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ) means that the cycle of mind is successively well accumulated and well developed by means of the cycle of mind. 'They do not overpower his mind' (nevassa cittaṃ pariyādiyanti) means those sense-objects are not able to remain by seizing and exhausting the mind-moment of that one whose taints are destroyed. 'Unmixed' (amissīkataṃ) means unmixed with those sense-objects due to non-adherence. 'Attained to the imperturbable' (āneñjappattaṃ) means attained to the state of non-agitation, the state of stillness.

Silāyūpoti silāthambho. Soḷasakukkukoti dīghato soḷasahattho. Heṭṭhānemaṅgamāti āvāṭassa heṭṭhāgatā. Upari nemassāti upari āvāṭassa. Sunikhātattāti ayamusalehi koṭṭetvā koṭṭetvā suṭṭhu nikhātattā. Evameva khoti ettha silāyūpo viya khīṇāsavo daṭṭhabbo, mahāvātā viya chasu dvāresu uppajjanakā kilesā, catūhi disāhi āgantvā vātānaṃ silāyūpaṃ cāletuṃ asamatthabhāvo viya chasu dvāresu uppajjanakakilesānaṃ khīṇāsavassa cittaṃ cāletuṃ asamatthabhāvo veditabbo. Imasmimpi sutte khīṇāsavova kathito.

'A stone pillar' (silāyūpa) means 'a stone post.' 'Sixteen cubits' (soḷasakukkuka) means 'sixteen cubits in length.' 'Gone below the foundation' (heṭṭhānemaṅgamā) means 'gone below the pit.' 'Above its foundation' (upari nemassa) means 'above the pit.' 'Because of being well-planted' (sunikhātattā) means 'because of being well-planted by repeatedly pounding with iron pestles.' Similarly, here the one whose taints are destroyed should be seen as like the stone pillar, the defilements arising at the six sense doors as like the great winds, and it should be understood that just as the winds coming from the four directions are unable to shake the stone pillar, so the defilements arising at the six sense doors are unable to shake the mind of one whose taints are destroyed. In this sutta too, only one whose taints are destroyed is spoken of.

7-8. Verasuttadvayavaṇṇanā

Commentary on the Two Vera Suttas

27-28. Sattame bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavatīti cittutrāsabhayañca puggalaverañca paṭilabhati. Cetasikanti cittanissitaṃ. Dukkhanti kāyavatthukaṃ. Domanassanti [M 3.0276] paṭighasampayuttadukkhaṃ. Imasmiṃ [V 3.0274] sutte sotāpattimaggo kathito. Aṭṭhamaṃ bhikkhusaṅghassa kathitaṃ, imasmiṃ pana sotāpannova kathitoti vuttaṃ.

In the seventh sutta, 'fear generates enmity' (bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati) means 'one obtains both mental terror and personal enmity.' 'Mental' (cetasika) means 'dependent on the mind.' 'Suffering' (dukkha) refers to 'that based on the body.' 'Mental distress' (domanassa) refers to 'suffering associated with repugnance.' In this sutta, the path of stream-entry is taught. The eighth sutta is taught to the Saṅgha of bhikkhus, but in this one, it is said that only the stream-enterer is taught.

9. Āghātavatthusuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Āghātavatthu Sutta

29. Navame āghātavatthūnīti āghātakāraṇāni. Āghātaṃ bandhatīti kopaṃ bandhati uppādeti.

29. In the ninth sutta, 'grounds for resentment' (āghātavatthūni) means 'causes of resentment.' 'One binds resentment' (āghātaṃ bandhati) means 'one binds or generates anger.'

10-11. Āghātapaṭivinayasuttādivaṇṇanā

Commentary on the Āghātapaṭivinaya Sutta and so forth

30-31. Dasame āghātapaṭivinayāti āghātassa paṭivinayakāraṇāni. Taṃ [P 4.0193] kutettha labbhāti “taṃ anatthacaraṇaṃ mā ahosī”ti etasmiṃ puggale kuto labbhā, kena kāraṇena sakkā laddhuṃ, “paro nāma parassa attano cittaruciyā anatthaṃ karotī”ti evaṃ cintetvā āghātaṃ paṭivineti. Atha vā sacāhaṃ kopaṃ kareyyaṃ, taṃ kopakaraṇaṃ ettha puggale kuto labbhā, kena kāraṇena laddhabbanti attho. Kuto lābhātipi pāṭho. Sacāhaṃ ettha kopaṃ kareyyaṃ, tasmiṃ me kopakaraṇe kuto lābhā lābhā, nāma ke siyunti attho. Imasmiñca atthe tanti nipātamattameva hoti. Ekādasame anupubbanirodhāti anupaṭipāṭinirodhā. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

In the tenth sutta, 'methods for removing resentment' (āghātapaṭivinayā) means the causes for dispelling resentment. The phrase 'How could that be obtained here?' (taṃ kutettha labbhā) is explained thus: How could one obtain from this person the fulfillment of the wish, 'May that harmful conduct not have occurred!'? By what reason could it be obtained? One reflects, 'Another person acts harmfully toward another according to the inclination of their own mind.' Thinking thus, one dispels resentment. Or alternatively: If I were to get angry, how could that act of anger be obtained with respect to this person? For what reason should it be obtained? This is the meaning. There is also the reading 'From where are gains?' (kuto lābhā). This means: If I were to get angry at this person, what gains, what benefits would there be for me in that act of anger? This is the meaning. And in this sense, the word 'taṃ' is merely a particle. In the eleventh sutta, 'cessation in sequence' (anupubbanirodhā) means cessation in due order. The rest is everywhere clear in meaning.

Sattāvāsavaggo tatiyo.

The Section on Abodes of Beings, the Third.

4. Mahāvaggo

4. The Great Chapter

1-2. Anupubbavihārasuttādivaṇṇanā

Commentary on the Anupubbavihāra Sutta and so forth

32-33. Catutthassa [V 3.0275] paṭhame anupubbavihārāti anupaṭipāṭiyā samāpajjitabbavihārā. Dutiye yattha kāmā nirujjhantīti yasmiṃ ṭhāne kāmā vūpasammanti. Nirodhetvāti appaṭivatte katvā. Nicchātāti taṇhādiṭṭhicchātānaṃ abhāvena nicchātā. Nibbutāti attaparitāpanakilesānaṃ abhāvena nibbutā. Tiṇṇāti kāmato tiṇṇā. Pāraṃgatāti kāme pāraṃ gatā. Tadaṅgenāti tena jhānaṅgena. Ettha [P 4.0194] kāmā nirujjhantīti ettha paṭhamajjhāne [M 3.0277] kāmā nirujjhanti. Te cāti ye paṭhamajjhānaṃ samāpajjanti, te kāme nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti nāma. Pañjalikoti paggahitaañjaliko hutvā. Payirupāseyyāti upaṭṭhāpeyya. Iminā upāyena sabbattha attho veditabbo.

In the first sutta of the fourth chapter, 'successive dwellings' (anupubbavihārā) means dwellings to be attained in due order. In the second sutta, 'where sensual pleasures cease' (yattha kāmā nirujjhanti) means the place where sensual pleasures are stilled. 'Having caused them to cease' (nirodhetvā) means having made them non-returning. 'Without thirst' (nicchātā) means free from thirst by the absence of the thirst of craving and views. 'Quenched' (nibbutā) means quenched by the absence of self-tormenting defilements. 'Crossed over' (tiṇṇā) means crossed over from sensual pleasures. 'Gone to the far shore' (pāraṃgatā) means gone to the far shore regarding sensual pleasures. 'By that factor' (tadaṅgena) means by that factor of jhāna. 'Here sensual pleasures cease' (ettha kāmā nirujjhanti) means that in the first jhāna, sensual pleasures cease. 'And they' (te ca) means: those who attain the first jhāna, they are said to dwell having caused sensual pleasures to cease again and again. 'With palms joined' (pañjaliko) means having become one with raised joined palms. 'Should attend' (payirupāseyya) means should wait upon. By this method, the meaning should be understood everywhere.

3. Nibbānasukhasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Explanation of the Discourse on the Happiness of Nibbāna

34. Tatiye udāyīti lāḷudāyitthero. Etadeva khvetthāti etadeva kho ettha. Kāmasahagatāti kāmanissitā. Samudācarantīti manodvāre sañcaranti. Ābādhāyāti ābādhanāya pīḷanāya. Pariyāyenāti kāraṇena. Evaṃ sabbavāresu attho veditabbo. Imasmiṃ sutte avedayitasukhaṃ nāma kathitaṃ.

34. In the third discourse, Udāyī is the Elder Lāḷudāyī. 'Etadeva khvettha' is to be parsed as 'etadeva kho ettha'. 'Accompanied by sensual pleasure' (kāmasahagatā) means dependent on sensual pleasures. 'They occur' (samudācaranti) means they roam about at the mind-door. 'For affliction' (ābādhāya) means for causing affliction, for oppression. 'By way of' (pariyāyena) means by reason. In this way, the meaning should be understood in all instances. In this discourse, what is called happiness not accompanied by feeling is taught.

4. Gāvīupamāsuttavaṇṇanā

4. Explanation of the Discourse with the Simile of the Cow

35. Catutthe pabbateyyāti pabbatacārinī. Na suppatiṭṭhitaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvāti yathā suppatiṭṭhitā hoti, evaṃ na patiṭṭhāpetvā. Taṃ nimittanti taṃ paṭhamajjhānasaṅkhātaṃ nimittaṃ. Na svādhiṭṭhitaṃ adhiṭṭhātīti yathā suṭṭhu adhiṭṭhitaṃ hoti, na evaṃ adhiṭṭhāti. Anabhihiṃsamānoti apothento aviheṭhento. Mudu cittaṃ hoti kammaññanti yathā vipassanācittaṃ lokuttaramaggakkhaṇe mudu kammakkhamaṃ kammayoggaṃ [P 4.0195] hoti, evamassa abhiññāpādakaṃ catutthajjhānacittaṃ mudu hoti kammaññaṃ[V 3.0276]. Appamāṇo samādhīti catubrahmavihārasamādhipi maggaphalasamādhipi appamāṇo samādhi nāma, idha pana “appamāṇaṃ appamāṇārammaṇa”nti iminā pariyāyena suppaguṇasamādhi appamāṇasamādhīti daṭṭhabbo. So appamāṇena samādhinā subhāvitenāti imasmiṃ ṭhāne ayaṃ bhikkhu vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā arahattaṃ patto. Idāni khīṇāsavassa abhiññāpaṭipāṭiṃ dassento yassa yassa cātiādimāha.

35. In the fourth discourse, 'of the mountain' (pabbateyyā) means one who wanders on the mountains. 'Not having established it well' (na suppatiṭṭhitaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvā) means not having established it in such a way that it becomes well-established. 'That sign' (taṃ nimittaṃ) refers to that sign known as the first jhāna. 'Does not resolve it well' (na svādhiṭṭhitaṃ adhiṭṭhāti) means does not resolve it in such a way that it becomes well-resolved. 'Not harming' (anabhihiṃsamāno) means not striking, not tormenting. 'The mind becomes pliant and workable' (mudu cittaṃ hoti kammaññaṃ) means: just as the insight-mind at the moment of the supramundane path becomes pliant, fit for work, and suitable for work, so too the fourth-jhāna mind of this yogi, which is the basis for direct knowledge, becomes pliant and workable. 'Measureless concentration' (appamāṇo samādhi) refers to both the concentration of the four divine abidings and the concentration of the path and fruition, both of which are called 'measureless concentration.' Here, however, 'measureless concentration' should be understood by way of the phrase 'measureless, with a measureless object,' as a well-practiced concentration. 'By that measureless concentration, well-developed' (so appamāṇena samādhinā subhāvitenā) means that at this point, this bhikkhu has developed insight and attained Arahantship. Now, showing the sequence of direct knowledges for one whose taints are destroyed, he spoke the words beginning with 'of whatever' (yassa yassa cātiādi).

5. Jhānasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Explanation of the Discourse on Jhāna

36. Pañcame āsavānaṃ khayanti arahattaṃ. Yadeva tattha hoti rūpagatanti tasmiṃ paṭhamajjhānakkhaṇe vatthuvasena vā cittasamuṭṭhānikādivasena vā yaṃ rūpaṃ nāma pavattati. Vedanāgatādīni sampayuttavedanādīnaṃ vasena veditabbāni[M 3.0278]. Te dhammeti te rūpādayo pañcakkhandhadhamme. Aniccatotiādīsu hutvā abhāvākārena aniccato, paṭipīḷanākārena dukkhato, rujjanākārena rogato, antodussanaṭṭhena gaṇḍato, anupaviṭṭhaṭṭhena anukantanaṭṭhena ca sallato, dukkhaṭṭhena aghato, ābādhanaṭṭhena ābādhato, asakaṭṭhena parato, palujjanaṭṭhena palokato, assāmikaṭṭhena suññato, avasavattanaṭṭhena anattato. Samanupassatīti balavavipassanāpaññāya passati.

36. In the fifth sutta, 'the destruction of the taints' (āsavānaṃ khayaṃ) means Arahantship. 'Whatever material form is included there' (yadeva tattha hoti rūpagataṃ) refers to whatever material form arises at that moment of the first jhāna, whether by way of the physical basis or by way of mind-originated matter, and so forth. 'Included in feeling, and so forth' (vedanāgatādīni) should be understood by way of associated feelings and so forth. 'Those phenomena' (te dhamme) refers to those phenomena of the five aggregates, such as material form. In 'as impermanent,' and so forth: as impermanent in the manner of non-existence after having been; as suffering in the manner of oppression; as a disease in the manner of affliction; as a boil in the sense of internal corruption; as a dart in the sense of penetration and cutting; as misery in the sense of suffering; as an affliction in the sense of torment; as other in the sense of not being one's own; as crumbling in the sense of perishing; as empty in the sense of having no master; and as non-self in the sense of not being subject to one's will. 'He contemplates' (samanupassati) means he sees with powerful insight-wisdom.

Tehi dhammehīti tehi pañcakkhandhadhammehi. Paṭivāpetīti nibbānavasena nivatteti. Amatāya [P 4.0196] dhātuyāti nibbānadhātuyā. Cittaṃ upasaṃharatīti ñāṇena ānisaṃsaṃ disvā otāreti. Santanti paccanīkasantatāya santaṃ. Paṇītanti atappakaṃ. So tattha ṭhito āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇātīti so tasmiṃ paṭhamajjhāne ṭhito taṃ balavavipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti. Aparo nayo – so tehi dhammehīti yasmā aniccatotiādīsu aniccato palokatoti dvīhi padehi aniccalakkhaṇaṃ kathitaṃ, dukkhatotiādīhi chahi dukkhalakkhaṇaṃ, parato, suññato, anattatoti tīhi anattalakkhaṇaṃ. Tasmā so tehi evaṃ tilakkhaṇaṃ āropetvā diṭṭhehi antosamāpattiyaṃ pañcakkhandhadhammehi. Cittaṃ paṭivāpetīti cittaṃ paṭisaṃharati moceti apaneti. Upasaṃharatīti vipassanācittaṃ tāva savanavasena thutivasena pariyattivasena paññattivasena ca santaṃ nibbānanti evaṃ asaṅkhatāya amatāya dhātuyā upasaṃharati. Maggacittaṃ nibbānaṃ [V 3.0277] ārammaṇakaraṇavaseneva “etaṃ santaṃ etaṃ paṇīta”nti na evaṃ vadati. Iminā panākārena taṃ paṭivijjhanto tattha cittaṃ upasaṃharatīti attho.

'By those phenomena' (tehi dhammehi) means by those phenomena of the five aggregates. 'He turns it away' (paṭivāpeti) means he makes it cease by way of Nibbāna. 'To the deathless element' (amatāya dhātuyā) means to the Nibbāna element. 'He directs the mind' (cittaṃ upasaṃharati) means he inclines it, having seen the benefit with wisdom. 'Peaceful' (santaṃ) means peaceful due to the pacification of opposing forces. 'Sublime' (paṇītaṃ) means unagitated. 'Established therein, he reaches the destruction of the taints' (so tattha ṭhito āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti) means that established in that first jhāna, he develops that powerful insight and attains Arahantship. Another method: 'by those phenomena' (so tehi dhammehi)—because in 'as impermanent,' and so forth, the characteristic of impermanence is stated by two terms ('as impermanent' and 'as crumbling'); the characteristic of suffering by six terms ('as suffering,' and so forth); and the characteristic of non-self by three terms ('as other,' 'as empty,' and 'as non-self'). Therefore, by those phenomena of the five aggregates, which he has thus seen by applying the three characteristics within the attainment. 'He turns the mind away' (cittaṃ paṭivāpeti) means he withdraws, releases, and removes the mind. 'He directs' (upasaṃharati) means he first directs the insight-mind toward peaceful Nibbāna, the unconditioned, deathless element, through the ways of hearing, praise, learning, and designation. The path-mind, however, does not operate by saying, 'This is peaceful, this is sublime,' merely by making Nibbāna its object. Rather, by penetrating it in this way, he directs the mind there—this is the meaning.

So tattha ṭhitoti tassā tilakkhaṇārammaṇāya vipassanāya ṭhito. Āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇātīti anukkamena cattāro magge bhāvetvā arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti. Teneva dhammarāgenāti samathavipassanādhamme chandarāgena. Dhammanandiyāti [P 4.0197] tasseva vevacanaṃ. Samathavipassanāsu hi sabbaso chandarāgaṃ pariyādātuṃ sakkonto arahattaṃ pāpuṇāti, asakkonto anāgāmī hoti.

'Established therein' (so tattha ṭhito) means he is established in that insight which has the three characteristics as its object. 'He reaches the destruction of the taints' (āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti) means he gradually develops the four paths and attains Arahantship. 'By that very passion for the Dhamma' (teneva dhammarāgena) means with passionate desire for the Dhamma of serenity and insight. 'By delight in the Dhamma' (dhammanandiyā) is a synonym for that. For one who is able to completely eliminate all passionate desire for serenity and insight attains Arahantship; one who is unable becomes a non-returner.

Tiṇapurisarūpake [M 3.0279] vāti tiṇapotthakarūpe vā. Dūre kaṇḍe pātetīti dūrepātī. Avirādhitaṃ vijjhatīti akkhaṇavedhī. Yadeva tattha hoti vedanāgatanti idha rūpaṃ na gahitaṃ. Kasmā? Samatikkantattā. Ayañhi heṭṭhā rūpāvacarajjhānaṃ samāpajjitvā rūpaṃ atikkamitvā arūpāvacarasamāpattiṃ samāpannoti samathavasenāpi anena rūpaṃ samatikkantaṃ, heṭṭhā rūpaṃ sammasitvā taṃ atikkamma idāni arūpaṃ sammasatīti vipassanāvasenāpi anena rūpaṃ atikkantaṃ. Āruppe pana sabbasopi rūpaṃ natthīti taṃ sandhāyapi rūpaṃ na gahitaṃ. Atha nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ kasmā na gahitanti? Sukhumattā. Tasmiñhi cattāropi arūpakkhandhā sukhumā na sammasanūpagā. Tenevāha – “iti kho, bhikkhave, yāvatā saññāsamāpatti tāvatā aññāpaṭivedho”ti. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – yāvatā sacittakasamāpatti nāma atthi, tāvatā oḷārike dhamme sammasato aññāpaṭivedho hoti, arahattaṃ [P 4.0198] sampajjati. Nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ pana sukhumattā saññāsamāpattīti na vuccati. Jhāyīheteti jhāyīhi jhānābhiratehi etāni. Vuṭṭhahitvāti tato samāpattito vuṭṭhāya. Samakkhātabbānīti sammā akkhātabbāni, “santāni paṇītānī”ti evaṃ kevalaṃ ācikkhitabbāni thometabbāni vaṇṇetabbānīti.

'In the form of a grass man' means in the form of a grass doll. 'Dūre kaṇḍe pātetīti' (he causes an arrow to fall from afar) means 'distant-striker.' 'Avirādhitaṃ vijjhatīti' (he pierces without missing) means 'unerring-piercer.' 'Yadeva tattha hoti vedanāgataṃ' (whatever feeling exists there): here, material form is not taken. Why? Because it has been completely transcended. For this yogī, having attained the form-sphere jhāna below and transcended material form, has attained a formless-sphere attainment; thus, by way of serenity, material form has been completely transcended by him. Having contemplated material form below and transcended it, he now contemplates the formless; thus, by way of insight, material form has been transcended by him. Furthermore, in the formless realm, there is no material form at all; therefore, with reference to that, material form is not taken. But why is the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception not taken? Due to its subtlety. For in that state, the four formless aggregates are subtle and not suitable for contemplation. Therefore, He said: 'Monks, as far as there are attainments with perception, so far is there penetration to final knowledge.' This is what is meant: as far as there are attainments with consciousness, for one who contemplates gross phenomena, there is penetration to final knowledge, and Arahantship is accomplished. The base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, however, due to its subtlety, is not called an 'attainment with perception.' 'Jhāyīhi etāni' (these are for meditators) means these are for meditators, for those who delight in jhāna. 'Vuṭṭhahitvā' (having emerged) means having emerged from that attainment. 'Samakkhātabbāni' (should be well declared) means they should be rightly declared, simply to be stated, praised, and extolled as 'peaceful and sublime.'

6. Ānandasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Ānanda Sutta

37. Chaṭṭhe sambādheti pañcakāmaguṇasambādhe. Okāsādhigamoti okāsassa adhigamo. Sattānaṃ visuddhiyāti sattānaṃ visuddhiṃ pāpanatthāya. Samatikkamāyāti samatikkamanatthāya. Atthaṅgamāyāti atthaṃ gamanatthāya. Ñāyassa adhigamāyāti sahavipassanakassa maggassa adhigamanatthāya[V 3.0278]. Nibbānassa sacchikiriyāyāti apaccayanibbānassa paccakkhakaraṇatthāya. Tadeva nāma cakkhuṃ bhavissatīti taññeva pasādacakkhu asambhinnaṃ bhavissati. Te rūpāti tadeva rūpārammaṇaṃ āpāthaṃ āgamissati. Tañcāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedissatīti tañca rūpāyatanaṃ na jānissati. Sesesupi [P 4.0199] eseva nayo.

6. In the sixth sutta, 'in a constricted place' means in the place constricted by the five strands of sensual pleasure. 'Okāsādhigamo' means the attainment of an opportunity. 'Sattānaṃ visuddhiyā' means for the purpose of leading beings to purity. 'Samatikkamāya' means for the purpose of completely transcending. 'Atthaṅgamāya' means for the purpose of reaching cessation. 'Ñāyassa adhigamāya' means for the purpose of attaining the path accompanied by insight. 'Nibbānassa sacchikiriyāya' means for the purpose of directly realizing unconditioned Nibbāna. 'Tadeva nāma cakkhuṃ bhavissati' means that very sensitive eye will remain unimpaired. 'Te rūpā' means that very form-object will come into range. 'Tañcāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedissatīti' means one will not cognize that form-base. The same method applies to the remaining phrases.

Udāyīti kāḷudāyitthero. Saññīmeva nu khoti sacittakoyeva nu kho. Makāro padasandhimattaṃ. Kiṃsaññīti katarasaññāya saññī hutvā. Sabbaso [M 3.0280] rūpasaññānanti idaṃ kasmā gaṇhi, kiṃ paṭhamajjhānādisamaṅgino rūpādipaṭisaṃvedanā hotīti? Na hoti, yāva pana kasiṇarūpaṃ ārammaṇaṃ hoti, tāva rūpaṃ samatikkantaṃ nāma na hoti. Asamatikkantattā paccayo bhavituṃ sakkhissati. Samatikkantattā pana taṃ natthi nāma hoti, natthitāya paccayo bhavituṃ na sakkotīti dassetuṃ idameva gaṇhi.

37. 'Udāyī' refers to the Elder Kāḷudāyī. 'Saññīmeva nu kho' means 'is it only one with consciousness?' The letter 'm' is merely for euphonic conjunction. 'Kiṃsaññī' means being percipient with which perception? Regarding 'Sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ' (the complete transcendence of perceptions of form): why was this taken up? For one endowed with the first jhāna and so on, is there not an experience of form, etc.? There is not. However, as long as a kasiṇa-form is the object, material form is not said to be completely transcended. Because it is not completely transcended, it can be a condition. But because it is completely transcended, it is said to be non-existent; and due to its non-existence, it cannot be a condition. To show this meaning, this very phrase was taken up.

Jaṭilavāsikāti jaṭilanagaravāsinī. Na cābhinatotiādīsu rāgavasena na abhinato, dosavasena na apanato. Sasaṅkhārena sappayogena kilese niggaṇhitvā vāretvā ṭhito, kilesānaṃ pana chinnante uppannoti na sasaṅkhāraniggayhavāritagato. Vimuttattā ṭhitoti kilesehi vimuttattāyeva ṭhito. Ṭhitattā santusitoti ṭhitattāyeva santuṭṭho nāma jāto. Santusitattā [P 4.0200] no paritassatīti santuṭṭhattāyeva paritāsaṃ nāpajjati. Ayaṃ, bhante ānanda, samādhi kiṃ phaloti iminā ayaṃ therī tālaphalaññeva gahetvā “idaṃ phalaṃ kiṃ phalaṃ nāmā”ti pucchamānā viya arahattaphalasamādhiṃ gahetvā “ayaṃ, bhante ānanda, samādhi kiṃ phalo vutto bhagavatā”ti pucchati. Aññāphalo vuttoti aññā vuccati arahattaṃ, arahattaphalasamādhi nāmeso vutto bhagavatāti attho. Evaṃsaññīpīti imāya arahattaphalasaññāya saññīpi tadāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedetīti evaṃ imasmiṃ sutte arahattaphalasamādhi kathitoti.

'Jaṭilavāsikā' means a female resident of the city of Jaṭila. In phrases like 'Na cābhinato' and so forth: 'na abhinato' means not inclined towards due to lust, 'na apanato' means not turned away from due to hatred. One does not stand by suppressing and warding off defilements with effort and exertion; rather, that state arose at the end of cutting off the defilements, and so one is not in a state of having to suppress and ward off with effort. 'Vimuttattā ṭhito' means one stands firm precisely because of being liberated from defilements. 'Ṭhitattā santusito' means one is called content simply because of standing firm. 'Santusitattā no paritassati' means because one is content, one does not fall into agitation. By the phrase 'Venerable Ānanda, what is the fruit of this concentration?', this elder nun, as if taking a palm fruit and asking, 'This fruit, what is its name?', takes hold of the concentration of the fruit of Arahantship and asks, 'Venerable Ānanda, what fruit has this concentration been declared to be by the Blessed One?' 'Aññāphalo vutto' means 'final knowledge' (aññā) refers to Arahantship; this concentration is declared by the Blessed One to be the concentration of the fruit of Arahantship — this is the meaning. 'Evaṃsaññīpi' means even being percipient with this perception of the fruit of Arahantship, one does not experience that sense-sphere. Thus, in this sutta, the concentration of the fruit of Arahantship is taught.

7. Lokāyatikasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Lokāyatika Sutta

38. Sattame lokāyatikāti lokāyatavādakā. Satatanti sadā. Samitanti nirantaraṃ. Tiṭṭhatetanti tiṭṭhatu etaṃ, mā etaṃ paṭṭhapetha, ko vo etena attho. Dhammaṃ vo brāhmaṇā desessāmīti ahaṃ vo catusaccadhammaṃ desessāmi.

In the seventh sutta, 'Lokāyatikā' means those who recite the Lokāyata treatises. 'Satataṃ' means always. 'Samitaṃ' means without interruption. The word 'tiṭṭhatetaṃ' should be divided as 'tiṭṭhatu etaṃ'. Let that statement stand. Do not put that forward. What benefit is there for you in that statement? 'Dhammaṃ vo brāhmaṇā desessāmi' means: I will teach you the Dhamma of the Four Truths.

Daḷhadhammoti [V 3.0279] daḷhadhanuṃ gahetvā ṭhito. Dhanuggahoti issāso. Daḷhadhanu nāma dvisahassathāmaṃ vuccati. Dvisahassathāmaṃ nāma yassa āropitassa [P 4.0201] jiyābaddho [M 3.0281] lohasīsādīnaṃ bhāro daṇḍe gahetvā yāva kaṇḍappamāṇā ukkhittassa pathavito muccati. Sikkhitoti dasa dvādasa vassāni ācariyakule uggahitasippo. Katahatthoti eko sippameva uggaṇhāti, katahattho na hoti ayaṃ pana katahattho ciṇṇavasibhāvo. Katūpāsanoti rājakulādīsu dassitasippo. Lahukena asanenāti anto susiraṃ katvā tūlādīhi pūretvā katalakkhaparikammena sallahukakaṇḍena. Evaṃ katañhi ekausabhagāmī dve usabhānipi gacchati…pe… aṭṭhusabhagāmī soḷasa usabhānipi gacchati. Appakasirenāti niddukkhena. Atipāteyyāti atikkameyya. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – yathā so dhanuggaho taṃ vidatthicaturaṅgulaṃ chāyaṃ sīghameva atikkāmeti, evaṃ sakalacakkavāḷaṃ sīghaṃ sīghaṃ atikkamanasamatthena javena samannāgato. Sandhāvanikāyāti padasā dhāvanena. Evamāhaṃsūti evaṃ vadanti.

'Daḷhadhammo' means one who stands holding a strong bow. 'Dhanuggaho' means an archer. A 'strong bow' is said to be one of two-thousand strength. A bow of two-thousand strength is defined as one for which, when it is strung and drawn to the length of an arrow while a `bhāra` weight of bronze, lead, and so forth is held on the bow-stave, its bowstring is freed from the ground. 'Sikkhito' means having learned the skill in a teacher's household for ten or twelve years. 'Katahattho' means that while some only learn the skill and do not become skilled-handed, this one is skilled-handed, having practiced to mastery. 'Katūpāsano' means having demonstrated the skill in royal courts and so forth. 'Lahukena asanena' means with a very light arrow, made hollow inside, filled with cotton and so forth, and crafted with workmanship for the target. Indeed, an arrow made thus, which goes one `usabha`, can go even two `usabhas`... and one that goes eight `usabhas` can go even sixteen `usabhas`. 'Appakasirena' means without difficulty. 'Atipāteyya' means it would surpass. This is the intended meaning: just as that archer can swiftly make an arrow surpass a shadow of one span and four finger-breadths, so too is he endowed with a speed capable of swiftly surpassing the entire Cakkavāḷa. 'Sandhāvanikāya' means by running on foot. 'Evamāhaṃsu' means thus they say.

8. Devāsurasaṅgāmasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Devāsurasaṅgāma Sutta

39. Aṭṭhame samupabyūḷho ahosīti paccupaṭṭhito ahosi. Saṅgāmeyyāmāti saṅgāmaṃ kareyyāma yujjheyyāma. Apayiṃsuyevāti palāyiṃsuyeva. Uttarenābhimukhāti uttarāmukhā hutvā. Abhiyante vāti [P 4.0202] anubandhantiyeva. Bhīruttānagatenāti bhīruttānaṃ bhayanivāraṇaṃ patiṭṭhānaṃ gatena. Akaraṇīyāti yuddhena kiñci akattabbā. Kasmā pana nesaṃ saṅgāmo hotīti? Asurā hi pubbe tāvatiṃsavāsino, te cittapāṭaliyā pupphanakāle dibbapāricchattakapupphaṃ anussaranti. Tato uppannakodhā “gaṇhatha deve”ti sammukhasammukhaṭṭhāneneva sineruṃ abhiruhanti, devāpi nikkhamanti. Tesaṃ gopālakadārakānaṃ aññamaññaṃ daṇḍakehi paharaṇasadisaṃ yuddhaṃ hoti. Sakko devarājā heṭṭhā pañcasu ṭhānesu ārakkhaṃ ṭhapetvā upari devapuraṃ parivāretvā attasadisā vajirahatthā paṭimā ṭhapāpesi. Asurā heṭṭhā pañca ṭhānāni paṭibāhitvā abhiruḷhā indapaṭimāyo disvā nivattitvā asurapurameva gacchanti.

39. In the eighth sutta, 'he was arrayed' means 'he was present'. 'Let us fight' means 'let us make battle, let us wage war'. 'They fled indeed' means 'they fled indeed'. 'Facing north' means 'having become north-faced'. 'They pursue' means 'they pursue indeed'. 'Gone to a refuge for the fearful' means 'gone to a support that is a protection from fear, a warding off of fear'. 'Not to be done' means 'nothing is to be done by war'. Why, then, does a battle occur for them? The asuras were formerly dwellers in Tāvatiṃsa. At the time of the flowering of the Cittapāṭali tree, they remember the divine Pāricchattaka flower. Then, with anger having arisen, saying, 'Seize the devas!', they ascend Mount Sineru from a position directly opposite. The devas also come forth. Their battle is like cowherd boys striking each other with sticks. Sakka, the king of the devas, having placed guards in five places below and having surrounded the deva city above, had images resembling himself and holding vajras set up. The asuras, having repelled the five places below and ascended, see the images of Indra, turn back, and go to the asura city itself.

Dakkhiṇenābhimukhāti dakkhiṇāmukhā hutvā. Apadaṃ vadhitvāti nippadaṃ niravasesaṃ vadhitvā. Adassanaṃ gatoti māropi vaṭṭapādakaṃ katvā rūpāvacaracatutthajjhānaṃ [M 3.0282] samāpannassa cittaṃ jānāti, tadeva vipassanāpādakaṃ katvā samāpannassa cittaṃ jānāti. Arūpāvacarasamāpatti pana [V 3.0280] vaṭṭapādā vā hotu vipassanāpādā [P 4.0203] vā, taṃ samāpannassa māro cittaṃ na jānāti. Tena vuttaṃ – “adassanaṃ gato pāpimato”ti.

‘Facing south’ means ‘having become south-faced’. ‘Having destroyed without a foothold’ means ‘having destroyed without a foothold, without remainder’. As for ‘he has gone out of sight’: Māra, having made the round of existence the basis, knows the mind of one who has attained the fourth jhāna of the form realm; and having made that same jhāna the basis for insight, he knows the mind of one who has attained it. But as for a formless attainment, whether it has the round as its basis or insight as its basis, Māra does not know the mind of one who has attained it. Therefore it was said: ‘He has gone out of sight of the Evil One’.

9. Nāgasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Nāga Sutta

40. Navame āraññakassāti araññavāsino. Gocarapasutassāti gocaraggahaṇatthāya gacchantassa. Hatthikalabhāti mahantā mahantā nāgā. Hatthicchāpāti taruṇapotakā. Obhaggobhagganti nāmetvā nāmetvā ṭhapitaṃ. Ogāhaṃ otiṇṇassāti ogāhitabbattā ogāhanti laddhanāmaṃ udakatitthaṃ otiṇṇassa. Ogāhā uttiṇṇassāti udakatitthato uttiṇṇassa. Vūpakaṭṭhoti vūpakaṭṭho hutvā. Idāni yasmā dasabalassa hatthināgena kiccaṃ natthi, sāsane pana taṃsarikkhakaṃ puggalaṃ dassetuṃ idamāhaṭaṃ, tasmā taṃ puggalaṃ dassento evameva khotiādimāha.

40. In the ninth sutta, 'of the forest-dweller' means 'of one who lives in the forest'. 'Intent on the range' means 'of one going for the purpose of seeking food'. 'Elephant calves' means 'great, great elephants'. 'Elephant young' means 'very young elephants'. 'Bent and broken' means 'bent down and placed'. 'Of one who has entered the ford' means 'of one who has entered a watering place which has received the name 'ford' because it is to be entered'. 'Of one who has emerged from the ford' means 'of one who has emerged from the watering place'. 'Secluded' means 'having become secluded'. Now, because the Ten-Powered One has no business with a great elephant, but this was brought forth to show a person comparable to it in the Dispensation, therefore, showing that person, he said, 'Even so, indeed,' and so forth.

10. Tapussasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Tapussa Sutta

41. Dasame mallesūti mallaraṭṭhe. Idheva tāva tvaṃ, ānanda, hotīti idha bhagavā “tapussagahapatino idha ṭhitena ānandena saddhiṃ kathāsallāpo bhavissati, tatonidānaṃ ahaṃ mahantaṃ dhammapariyāyaṃ desessāmī”ti ñatvā āha. Upasaṅkamīti so kira bhuttapātarāso “dasabalassa upaṭṭhānaṃ [P 4.0204] gamissāmī”ti nikkhamanto dūratova theraṃ disvā yenāyasmā ānando tenupasaṅkami. Papāto viya khāyati, yadidaṃ nekkhammanti yamidaṃ pabbajjāsaṅkhātaṃ nekkhammaṃ, taṃ amhākaṃ mahāpapāto viya ogāhitvā upaṭṭhāti. Nekkhamme cittaṃ pakkhandatīti pabbajjāya cittaṃ ārammaṇavasena pakkhandati, tadeva ārammaṇaṃ katvā pasīdati, tadeva patiṭṭhāti, paccanīkadhammehi ca vimuccati. ‘Etaṃ santa’nti passatoti etaṃ nekkhammaṃ santaṃ vigatadarathapariḷāhanti evaṃ passantānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ. Bahunā janena visabhāgoti tayidaṃ bahunā mahājanena saddhiṃ bhikkhūnaṃ visabhāgaṃ, asadisanti attho.

41. In the tenth sutta, 'among the Mallians' means 'in the country of the Mallians'. 'Ānanda, you stay right here for now'—here the Blessed One said this, knowing that a conversation between the householder Tapussa and the Venerable Ānanda, who was standing here, would arise, and that from that cause he would teach a great discourse on the Dhamma. 'Approached'—it seems that he, having eaten his morning meal, thinking, 'I will go to attend upon the Ten-Powered One,' departed, saw the elder from afar and approached the Venerable Ānanda. 'Appears like a precipice, that is, renunciation' means 'this renunciation, which is called going forth, appears to us like a great precipice'. 'The mind leaps forward to renunciation' means 'the mind leaps forward to going forth by way of object; it becomes clear by making that same renunciation the object, it is established in that same renunciation, and it is freed from opposing qualities'. 'Seeing this as peaceful' means 'for bhikkhus seeing this renunciation as peaceful, free from agitation and fever'. 'At odds with many people' means 'this is at odds with the great multitude of people for bhikkhus; the meaning is 'unlike'.

Kathāpābhatanti kathāmūlaṃ. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, nekkhamme cittaṃ na pakkhandatīti tassa evaṃ vitakkentassāpi mayhaṃ pabbajjāya cittaṃ na otarati. “Etaṃ [M 3.0283] santa”nti passatoti “sādhu [V 3.0281] nekkhamma”nti parivitakkanavasena “etaṃ nekkhammaṃ santa”nti passantassapi. Anāsevitoti na āsevito na phassito na sacchikato. Adhigammāti adhigantvā patvā sacchikatvā. Tamāseveyyanti taṃ ānisaṃsaṃ seveyyaṃ bhajeyyaṃ. Yaṃ [P 4.0205] meti yena kāraṇena mayhaṃ. Adhigammāti adhigantvā. Svāssa me hoti ābādhoti so mayhaṃ ābādhanaṭṭhena ābādho hoti. Avitakke cittaṃ na pakkhandatīti avitakkavicāre dutiyajjhāne ārammaṇavasena cittaṃ na pakkhandati. Vitakkesūti vitakkavicāresu. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

'The root of the discussion' means 'the root of the discussion'. 'For me, Ānanda, the mind does not leap forward to renunciation' means 'even for me, reflecting thus, the mind does not descend to going forth'. 'Seeing this as peaceful' means 'even for one seeing this renunciation as peaceful, by way of reflecting, “renunciation is good.”' 'Not cultivated' means 'not cultivated, not touched, not realized'. 'Having attained' means 'having attained, having reached, having realized'. 'Should cultivate that' means 'I should cultivate, I should resort to that benefit'. 'That which for me' means 'by which reason for me'. 'Having attained' means 'having attained'. 'That would be an affliction for me' means 'that is an affliction for me in the sense of afflicting'. 'The mind does not leap forward to the thought-free' means 'the mind does not leap forward to the second jhāna, which is free from thought and examination, by way of object'. 'In thoughts' means 'in thought and examination'. The rest is clear in meaning everywhere.

Mahāvaggo catuttho.
