The Great Chapter, the Fourth.

5. Sāmaññavaggo

5. The Chapter on Sameness

1. Sambādhasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Sutta on Constriction

42. Pañcamassa paṭhame udāyīti kāḷudāyitthero. Avidvāti aññāsi. Bhūrimedhasoti mahāpañño. Yo jhānamabujjhīti yo jhānaṃ abujjhi. Paṭilīnanisabhoti ekībhāvavasena paṭilīno ceva uttamaṭṭhena ca nisabho. Munīti buddhamuni. Pariyāyenāti ekena kāraṇena. Kāmasambādhassa hi abhāvamatteneva paṭhamajjhānaṃ okāsādhigamo nāma, na sabbathā sabbaṃ. Tatrāpatthi sambādhoti tasmimpi paṭhamajjhāne sambādho [P 4.0206] paṭipīḷanaṃ atthiyeva. Tatrāpitthītipi pāṭho. Kiñca tattha sambādhoti tasmiṃ pana jhāne kiṃ sambādho nāma. Ayamettha sambādhoti ayaṃ vitakkavicārānaṃ aniruddhabhāvo sambādho saṃpīḷā nāma. Iminā upāyena sabbavāresu attho veditabbo. Nippariyāyenāti na ekena kāraṇena, atha kho āsavakkhayo nāma sabbasambādhānaṃ pahīnattā sabbena sabbaṃ okāsādhigamo nāmāti.

42. In the first sutta of the fifth chapter: “Udāyī” is the Elder Kāḷudāyī. “Unwise” (avidvā) means he did not know. “Of vast wisdom” (bhūrimedhaso) means of great wisdom. “Who understood the jhāna” (yo jhānamabujjhi) means he who understood the jhāna. “Secluded and a leader” (paṭilīnanisabho) means he is both secluded (paṭilīna) by way of solitude (ekībhāvavasena) and a leader (nisabha) in the supreme sense (uttamaṭṭhena). “Sage” (muni) means the sage who is the Buddha. “Relatively” (pariyāyena) means for one reason. The first jhāna is called the attainment of an opening (okāsādhigamo) merely through the absence of the constriction of sensual pleasures, but this is not the attainment of an opening in every respect. “There is constriction even there” (tatrāpatthi sambādho) means that even in that first jhāna, there is indeed constriction and oppression. There is also the reading “tatrāpitthi.” “And what is the constriction there?” (kiñca tattha sambādho) means what is called constriction in that jhāna? “This is the constriction there” (ayamettha sambādho) means this unsuppressed state of applied and sustained thought is called constriction and oppression. The meaning should be understood in all cases by this method. “Absolutely” (nippariyāyena) means not for just one reason; rather, the destruction of the cankers is called the attainment of an opening in every respect because all constrictions have been abandoned.

2. Kāyasakkhisuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Sutta on the Bodily Witness

43. Dutiye [V 3.0282][M 3.0284] yathā yathā ca tadāyatananti yena yena kāraṇena yena yenākārena taṃ paṭhamajjhānasaṅkhātaṃ āyatanaṃ hoti. Tathā tathā naṃ kāyena phusitvā viharatīti tena tena kāraṇena tena tenākārena taṃ samāpattiṃ sahajātanāmakāyena phusitvā viharati, samāpajjatīti attho. Kāyasakkhi vutto bhagavatā pariyāyenāti yasmā tena nāmākāyena paṭhamajjhānaṃ sacchikataṃ, tasmā iminā pariyāyena kāyasakkhi vutto. Nippariyāyenāti yattakaṃ kāyena sacchikātabbaṃ, sabbassa katattā ayaṃ nippariyāyena kāyasakkhi nāma.

43. In the second sutta: “In whatever way that base exists” (yathā yathā ca tadāyatanaṃ) means for whatever reason, in whatever manner, that base known as the first jhāna exists. “He dwells having touched it with the body in that same way” (tathā tathā naṃ kāyena phusitvā viharati) means for that reason, in that manner, he dwells having contacted that attainment with the co-arisen mental body; the meaning is that he enters into it. “The Blessed One has spoken of the bodily witness relatively” (kāyasakkhi vutto bhagavatā pariyāyena) means because the first jhāna is realized by that mental body, he is called a bodily witness in this relative sense. “Absolutely” (nippariyāyena) means that because everything that can be realized by the body has been accomplished, this person is called a bodily witness in the absolute sense.

3. Paññāvimuttasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Sutta on One Liberated by Wisdom

44. Tatiye paññāya ca naṃ pajānātīti taṃ paṭhamajjhānavipassanāpaññāya jānāti. Idhāpi pariyāyanippariyāyā purimanayeneva veditabbā. Yathā ca idha, evaṃ ito paresupi.

44. In the third sutta: “And he understands it with wisdom” (paññāya ca naṃ pajānāti) means he knows that first jhāna with the wisdom of insight. Here too, the relative and absolute senses (pariyāyanippariyāyā) should be understood in the same way as before. And as here, so too in the subsequent suttas.

4. Ubhatobhāgavimuttasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Ubhatobhāgavimutta Sutta

45. Catutthaṃ [P 4.0207] ubhayena veditabbaṃ. Ettha ca ubhatobhāgavimuttoti ubhatobhāgehi samathavipassanānaṃ paccanīkakilesehi vimutto. Pariyosāne pana samāpattiyā rūpakāyato, ariyamaggena nāmakāyato vimuttoyeva ubhatobhāgavimuttoti veditabbo.

45. The fourth [sutta] should be understood in both ways. And here, 'one freed in both ways' (ubhatobhāgavimutta) is one who is freed from both parts, that is, from the defilements that are the opponents of serenity and insight. At the conclusion, however, he is freed from the form-body by attainment and from the mental body by the noble path; thus he should be understood as 'one freed in both ways'.

5-10. Sandiṭṭhikadhammasuttādivaṇṇanā

5-10. Commentary on the Sandiṭṭhikadhamma Sutta and Others

46-51. Pañcamādīsu sandiṭṭhikoti sayaṃ passitabbako. Nibbānanti kilesanibbānaṃ. Parinibbānanti tasseva vevacanaṃ. Tadaṅganibbānanti paṭhamajjhānādinā tena tena aṅgena nibbānaṃ. Diṭṭhadhammanibbānanti ismiṃyeva attabhāve nibbānaṃ. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

In the fifth [sutta] and those that follow, 'sandiṭṭhika' means 'to be seen by oneself'. 'Nibbāna' means the extinguishing of defilements. 'Parinibbāna' is a synonym for that very thing. 'Tadaṅganibbāna' means nibbāna by means of those respective factors, beginning with the first jhāna. 'Diṭṭhadhammanibbāna' means nibbāna in this very existence. The remainder has an obvious meaning everywhere.

Sāmaññavaggo pañcamo.

The Chapter on Sameness, the fifth.

Paṭhamapaṇṇāsakaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.

The First Fifty is concluded.

2. Dutiyapaṇṇāsakavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Second Fifty

52. Ito [M 3.0285][V 3.0283] paresu khemanti nirupaddavaṃ. Khemappattoti khemabhāvaṃ patto. Sikkhādubbalyānīti sikkhāya dubbalabhāvakaraṇāni. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

52. In the [suttas] that follow this, 'khema' (safe) means 'without affliction'. 'Khemappatta' (one who has reached safety) means 'one who has reached the state of safety'. 'Sikkhādubbalyāni' (weaknesses in the training) means 'causes for the state of weakness in the training'. The remainder has an obvious meaning everywhere.

Manorathapūraṇiyā aṅguttaranikāya-aṭṭhakathāya

In the Manorathapūraṇī, the commentary on the Aṅguttara Nikāya,

Navakanipātassa saṃvaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The explanation of the Navaka Nipāta is concluded.

. Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa.

Homage to that Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Enlightened One.

Aṅguttaranikāye

In the Aṅguttara Nikāya

Dasakanipāta-aṭṭhakathā

Commentary on the Book of the Tens

1. Paṭhamapaṇṇāsakaṃ

1. The First Fifty

1. Ānisaṃsavaggo

1. The Chapter on Benefits

1. Kimatthiyasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Kimatthiya Sutta

1. Dasakanipātassa [V 3.0285][M 3.0287][P 5.0001] paṭhame kusalāni sīlānīti anavajjasīlāni. Amaṅkubhāvassa avippaṭisārassa atthāya saṃvattantīti avippaṭisāratthāni. So nesaṃ ānisaṃsoti avippaṭisārānisaṃsāni. Yathābhūtañāṇadassanatthotiādīsu yathābhūtañāṇadassanaṃ nāma taruṇavipassanā, nibbidā nāma balavavipassanā, virāgo nāma maggo, vimutti nāma arahattaphalaṃ, ñāṇadassanaṃ nāma paccavekkhaṇañāṇaṃ. Aggāya parentīti arahattatthāya gacchanti.

1. In the first [sutta] of the Dasaka Nipāta, 'wholesome virtues' means blameless virtues. They conduce to the benefit of non-remorse; therefore, they are for the purpose of non-remorse. That [non-remorse] is their benefit; therefore, they have non-remorse as their benefit. In [the phrase] 'for the purpose of knowledge and vision of things as they are' and so on, 'knowledge and vision of things as they are' is called nascent insight; 'disenchantment' is called powerful insight; 'dispassion' is called the path; 'liberation' is called the fruit of arahantship; and 'knowledge and vision' is called reviewing knowledge. 'They lead to the highest' means they lead for the sake of arahantship.

2. Cetanākaraṇīyasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Cetanākaraṇīya Sutta

2. Dutiye [V 3.0286] na cetanāya karaṇīyanti na cetetvā kappetvā pakappetvā kātabbaṃ. Dhammatā esāti dhammasabhāvo eso kāraṇaniyamo ayaṃ. Abhisandentīti pavattenti. Paripūrentīti [P 5.0002] paripuṇṇaṃ karonti. Apārā pāraṃ gamanāyāti orimatīrabhūtā tebhūmakavaṭṭā nibbānapāraṃ gamanatthāya.

2. In the second [sutta], 'not to be done by volition' means it is not to be done after intending, planning, or scheming. 'This is the nature of things' means this is the inherent nature of phenomena, this is the fixed order of causation. 'They flow on' means 'they cause to proceed'. 'They fulfill' means 'they make complete'. 'For going from the near shore to the far shore' means for the purpose of going from the near shore, which is the round of existence in the three realms, to the far shore of Nibbāna.

3-5. Upanisasuttattayavaṇṇanā

3-5. Explanation of the Triad of Upanisa Suttas

3-5. Tatiye hatūpanisoti hatakāraṇo. Catutthapañcamesu dvīhi therehi kathitabhāvova viseso.

3-5. In the third [sutta], hatūpaniso means 'one whose cause is destroyed'. In the fourth and fifth [suttas], the only distinction is the fact of their having been spoken by two elders.

6. Samādhisuttavaṇṇanā

6. Explanation of the Samādhi Sutta

6. Chaṭṭhe neva pathaviyaṃ pathavīsaññī assāti pathaviṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā pathavīti evaṃ uppannāya saññāya saññī na bhaveyya. Āpādīsupi eseva [M 3.0288] nayo. Na idhaloketi idhaloke uppajjanakacatukkapañcakajjhānasaññāya na saññī bhaveyya. Na paraloketi paraloke uppajjanakacatukkapañcakajjhānasaññāya na saññī bhaveyya. Saññī ca pana assāti atha ca panassa samāpatti savitakkasamāpattiyeva assāti vuccati. Etaṃ santaṃ etaṃ paṇītanti santaṃ santanti appetvā nisinnassa divasampi cittuppādo “santaṃ santa”nteva pavattati, paṇītaṃ paṇītanti appetvā nisinnassa divasampi cittuppādo “paṇītaṃ paṇīta”nteva pavattati. Yadidaṃ sabbasaṅkhārasamathoti nibbānaṃ nibbānanti appetvā nisinnassa divasampi cittuppādo “nibbānaṃ nibbāna”nteva pavattatīti sabbampetaṃ phalasamāpattisamādhiṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ.

6. In the sixth [sutta], neva pathaviyaṃ pathavīsaññī assā means one should not be percipient with a perception that has arisen thus, 'earth', by making the earth element an object. In regard to the water element and so on, this is the method. Na idhaloke means one should not be percipient with the perception of the fourfold and fivefold jhānas that arise in this world. Na paraloke means one should not be percipient with the perception of the fourfold and fivefold jhānas that arise in the next world. Saññī ca pana assā means: however, for that person, that attainment, it is said, should be only an attainment accompanied by initial thought. Etaṃ santaṃ etaṃ paṇītaṃ: for one who is seated, having entered upon [the attainment thinking] 'peaceful, peaceful', for a whole day the arising of consciousness proceeds only as 'peaceful, peaceful'; for one who is seated, having entered upon [the attainment thinking] 'sublime, sublime', for a whole day the arising of consciousness proceeds only as 'sublime, sublime'. Regarding yadidaṃ sabbasaṅkhārasamatho: for one who is seated, having entered upon [the attainment thinking] 'Nibbāna, Nibbāna', for a whole day the arising of consciousness proceeds only as 'Nibbāna, Nibbāna'. Therefore, all this was spoken in reference to the concentration of the attainment of fruition.

7. Sāriputtasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Explanation of the Sāriputta Sutta

7. Sattame saññī ca panāhaṃ, āvuso, tasmiṃ samaye ahosinti, āvuso, tasmiṃ samaye [V 3.0287] ahaṃ “bhavanirodho nibbāna”nti imāya [P 5.0003] phalasamāpattisaññāya saññī ahosiṃ. Sacittakā me sā samāpatti ahosīti paccavekkhaṇā kathitā.

7. In the seventh [sutta], saññī ca panāhaṃ, āvuso, tasmiṃ samaye ahosinti means: 'Friend, at that time I was percipient with this perception of the attainment of fruition, thus: “The cessation of existence is Nibbāna.”' The phrase Sacittakā me sā samāpatti ahosi speaks of the reviewing, meaning, 'That attainment of mine was accompanied by consciousness.'

8. Jhānasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Explanation of the Jhāna Sutta

8. Aṭṭhame samantapāsādikoti pasādāvahānaṃyeva kāyakammādīnaṃ sabbhāvato samanto pāsādiko. Sabbākāraparipūroti sabbehi kāraṇehi paripuṇṇo.

8. In the eighth [sutta], samantapāsādiko means inspiring confidence all around due to the existence of bodily actions and so on which bring about confidence. Sabbākāraparipūro means complete in all respects.

9. Santavimokkhasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Explanation of the Santavimokkha Sutta

9. Navame santāti ārammaṇasantatāyapi aṅgasantatāyapi santā. Vimokkhāti paccanīkadhammehi vimuttattā ārammaṇe ca nirāsaṅkabhāvena suṭṭhu muttattā evaṃladdhanāmā. Atikkamma rūpeti rūpajjhānāni atikkamitvā pavattā. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

9. In the ninth [sutta], santā means peaceful because of the tranquility of the object and also because of the tranquility of the factors. Vimokkho has obtained this name because of being liberated from opposing states and because of being well-freed in the object without attachment. Atikkamma rūpe means it occurs having transcended the form jhānas. The rest has an obvious meaning everywhere.

Ānisaṃsavaggo paṭhamo.

The First Chapter on Benefits.

2. Nāthavaggo

2. The Nātha Chapter

1. Senāsanasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Senāsana Sutta

11. Dutiyassa [M 3.0289] paṭhame pañcaṅgasamannāgatoti pañcahi guṇaṅgehi samannāgato. Nātidūraṃ hoti nāccāsannanti yañhi atidūre hoti, piṇḍāya caritvā tattha gacchantassa kāyacittadarathā hoti, tato anuppannaṃ vā samādhiṃ uppādetuṃ uppannaṃ vā thiraṃ kātuṃ na sakkoti. Accāsannaṃ bahujanākiṇṇaṃ hoti. Cattālīsausabhamatte pana padese vasataṃ dūrāsannadosavimuttañca gamanāgamanasampannaṃ nāma hoti. Divāappākiṇṇanti divasabhāge mahājanena anākiṇṇaṃ.

11. In the first [sutta] of the second [chapter], pañcaṅgasamannāgato means endowed with five factors which are qualities. Nātidūraṃ hoti nāccāsannaṃ: for if [a lodging] is too far, after going for alms, distress of body and mind arises for one going there, and because of that one is not able to arouse unarisen concentration or to make arisen concentration firm. If it is too near, it is crowded with many people. But for one dwelling in a place about forty usabhas in extent, being free from the faults of being too far and too near, it is called 'endowed with coming and going'. Divāappākiṇṇaṃ means not crowded with many people during the daytime.

2. Pañcaṅgasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Pañcaṅga Sutta

12. Dutiye [V 3.0288] kevalīti kevalehi sakalehi guṇehi samannāgato. Vusitavāti vutthabrahmacariyavāso. Asekhenāti [P 5.0004] asekhadhammapariyāpannena lokuttarena. Sīlakkhandhenāti sīlarāsinā. Vimuttikkhandhenāti ettha ṭhapetvā sīlādayo tayo sesā phaladhammā vimutti nāma, vimuttiñāṇadassanaṃ paccavekkhaṇañāṇaṃ, taṃ lokiyameva.

12. In the second [sutta], kevalī means one endowed with all, complete qualities. Vusitavā means one who has lived the holy life. Asekhena means by the supramundane state included in the dhammas of one beyond training. Sīlakkhandhena means by the aggregate of virtue. As for vimuttikkhandhena, here, setting aside the three beginning with virtue, the remaining fruition states are called 'liberation'; the knowledge and vision of liberation is the reviewing knowledge, and that is just mundane.

3-4. Saṃyojanasuttādivaṇṇanā

3-4. Commentary on the Saṃyojana Sutta and others

13-14. Tatiye orambhāgiyānīti heṭṭhābhāgiyāni. Uddhambhāgiyānīti uparibhāgiyāni. Imasmiṃ sutte vaṭṭameva kathitaṃ. Catutthe khilavinibandhā pañcakanipāte vitthāritāyeva. Ārohapariṇāhenāti dīghaputhulantena.

In the third [sutta], orambhāgiyāni means those pertaining to the lower part. Uddhambhāgiyāni means those pertaining to the upper part. In this sutta, only the round of existence is taught. In the fourth, the mental barrennesses and bondages have already been explained in detail in the Pañcakanipāta. Ārohapariṇāhena means by height and circumference.

5. Appamādasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Appamāda Sutta

15. Pañcame evameva khoti yathā sabbasattānaṃ sammāsambuddho aggo, evaṃ sabbesaṃ kusaladhammānaṃ kārāpakaappamādo aggoti daṭṭhabbo. Nanu cesa lokiyova, kusaladhammā pana lokuttarāpi. Ayañca kāmāvacarova, kusaladhammā pana catubhūmakā. Kathamesa tesaṃ aggoti? Paṭilābhakattena. Appamādena hi te paṭilabhanti, tasmā so tesaṃ aggo. Teneva vuttaṃ – sabbe te appamādamūlakāti.

15. In the fifth [sutta], it should be understood thus: just as the Perfectly Enlightened One is supreme among all beings, so too, heedfulness, which causes them to arise, is supreme among all wholesome states. Now, is this [heedfulness] not just mundane, whereas wholesome states are also supramundane? And this [heedfulness] belongs to the sense-sphere, whereas wholesome states belong to the four planes. How then is it supreme among them? Because it is the cause of their attainment. For through heedfulness they are attained; therefore, it is supreme among them. For that reason it is said: 'All of them have heedfulness as their root.'

Jaṅgalānanti [M 3.0290] pathavitalacārīnaṃ. Pāṇānanti sapādakapāṇānaṃ. Padajātānīti padāni. Samodhānaṃ gacchantīti odhānaṃ pakkhepaṃ gacchanti. Aggamakkhāyatīti seṭṭhamakkhāyati. Yadidaṃ [P 5.0005] mahantattenāti mahantabhāvena aggamakkhāyati, na guṇaggenāti attho. Vassikanti sumanapupphaṃ. Idaṃ kira suttaṃ sutvā bhātiyamahārājā vīmaṃsitukāmatāya ekasmiṃ gabbhe catujātigandhehi paribhaṇḍaṃ katvā sugandhapupphāni āharāpetvā ekassa samuggassa majjhe sumanapupphamuṭṭhiṃ ṭhapetvā sesāni tassa samantato muṭṭhiṃ katvā ṭhapetvā dvāraṃ pidhāya bahi nikkhanto. Athassa muhuttaṃ bahi vītināmetvā dvāraṃ vivaritvā pavisantassa sabbapaṭhamaṃ sumanapupphagandho ghānaṃ pahari. So mahātalasmiṃyeva [V 3.0289] mahācetiyābhimukho nipajjitvā “vassikaṃ tesaṃ agganti kathentena sukathitaṃ sammāsambuddhenā”ti cetiyaṃ vandi. Khuddarājānoti khuddakarājāno. Kūṭarājānotipi pāṭho.

Jaṅgalānaṃ ('of jungle creatures') means of those that move on the earth's surface. Pāṇānaṃ ('of living beings') means of beings with feet. Padajātāni ('kinds of footprints') means footprints. Samodhānaṃ gacchanti ('they converge') means they come to be included. Aggamakkhāyati ('it is called the chief') means it is called supreme. Yadidaṃ mahantattena ('in that it is of great size') means it is called supreme because of its great size, not because of the supremacy of its quality—this is the meaning. Vassikaṃ ('jasmine') means the sumanā flower. It is said that, having heard this sutta, King Bhātiya, wishing to investigate, had a chamber prepared with four kinds of perfumes, had fragrant flowers brought, placed a handful of jasmine flowers in the middle of a casket, placed the remaining flowers in handfuls around it, closed the door, and went outside. Then, having spent a moment outside, he opened the door and entered, and the scent of the jasmine flowers was the very first to strike his sense of smell. Lying down on the great terrace itself, facing the Great Cetiya, he paid homage to the cetiya, saying: “It was well said by the Perfectly Enlightened One when he stated, ‘The jasmine is supreme among them.’” Khuddarājāno means petty kings. There is also the reading kūṭarājāno ('treacherous kings').

6. Āhuneyyasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Āhuneyya Sutta

16. Chaṭṭhe gotrabhūti sikhāpattavipassanābhūtena nibbānārammaṇena gotrabhuñāṇena samannāgato.

16. In the sixth [sutta], gotrabhū is a person endowed with gotrabhū-knowledge, which has Nibbāna as its object and has become insight that has reached its peak.

7. Paṭhamanāthasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the First Nātha Sutta

17. Sattame sanāthāti sañātakā bahuñātivaggā hutvā viharatha. Nāthaṃ karontīti nāthakaraṇā, attano sanāthabhāvakarā patiṭṭhākarāti attho. Kalyāṇamittotiādīsu sīlādiguṇasampannā kalyāṇā mittā assāti kalyāṇamitto. Tevassa ṭhānanisajjādīsu saha ayanato sahāyāti kalyāṇasahāyo. Cittena ceva kāyena ca kalyāṇamittesuyeva sampavaṅko oṇatoti kalyāṇasampavaṅko. Suvaco [P 5.0006] hotīti sukhena vattabbo hoti, sukhena anusāsitabbo. Khamoti gāḷhena pharusena kakkhaḷena vutto khamati na kuppati. Padakkhiṇaggāhī anusāsaninti yathā ekacco ovadiyamāno vāmato gaṇhāti, paṭippharati vā, assuṇanto vā gacchati, evaṃ akatvā “ovadatha[M 3.0291], bhante, anusāsatha, tumhesu anovadantesu ko añño ovadissatī”ti padakkhiṇaṃ gaṇhāti.

17. In the seventh, as for 'sanāthā': Dwell having relatives, having a large group of kin. 'They make a refuge' (nāthaṃ karonti), thus they are 'nāthakaraṇā' (refuge-makers); the meaning is that they are makers of a state of having a refuge for oneself, makers of a foundation. In such phrases as 'kalyāṇamitto': 'He has good friends endowed with virtues such as morality,' thus he is a 'kalyāṇamitto' (one with good friends). Those very friends, because of being together with him in standing, sitting, and so forth, are companions (sahāyā), thus he is a 'kalyāṇasahāyo' (one with good companions). 'He is inclined and bent in mind and body only towards good friends,' thus he is a 'kalyāṇasampavaṅko' (one inclined towards the good). As for 'suvaco hoti' (he is easy to speak to): he is one who is spoken to with ease, one who is admonished with ease. As for 'khamo' (he is patient): when spoken to with harsh, rough, and severe words, he endures and does not get angry. As for 'padakkhiṇaggāhī anusāsaniṃ' (one who accepts instruction receptively): just as a certain person, when being advised, takes it wrongly, or retorts, or goes away as if not hearing, not acting in such a way, but saying, 'Venerable sirs, advise me, instruct me. If you do not advise, who else will advise?', he accepts it receptively.

Uccāvacānīti uccanīcāni. Kiṃkaraṇīyānīti “kiṃ karomī”ti evaṃ vatvā kattabbakammāni. Tattha uccakammaṃ nāma cīvarassa karaṇaṃ rajanaṃ, cetiye sudhākammaṃ, uposathāgāracetiyagharabodhigharesu kattabbakammanti evamādi. Avacakammaṃ nāma pādadhovanamakkhanādikhuddakakammaṃ. Tatrūpāyāyāti tatrupagamaniyāya. Alaṃ kātunti kātuṃ samattho hoti. Alaṃ saṃvidhātunti vicāretuṃ samattho hoti.

As for 'uccāvacāni': high and low. As for 'kiṃkaraṇīyāni': tasks to be done after saying, 'What shall I do?'. Therein, a 'high task' refers to the making and dyeing of a robe, plasterwork on a cetiya, work to be done in Uposatha halls, cetiya-houses, and Bodhi-tree houses, and so forth. A 'low task' refers to a minor task such as washing the feet, anointing, and the like. As for 'tatrūpāyāya': by means of a method for accomplishing that. As for 'alaṃ kātuṃ': he is able to do. As for 'alaṃ saṃvidhātuṃ': he is able to arrange.

Dhamme assa kāmo sinehoti dhammakāmo, tepiṭakaṃ buddhavacanaṃ piyāyatīti attho. Piyasamudāhāroti parasmiṃ kathente sakkaccaṃ suṇāti, sayañca paresaṃ desetukāmo hotīti attho[V 3.0290]. Abhidhamme abhivinayeti ettha dhammo abhidhammo, vinayo abhivinayoti catukkaṃ veditabbaṃ. Tattha [P 5.0007] dhammoti suttantapiṭakaṃ. Abhidhammoti satta pakaraṇāni. Vinayoti ubhatovibhaṅgo. Abhivinayoti khandhakaparivārā. Atha vā suttantapiṭakampi abhidhammapiṭakampi dhammo eva, maggaphalāni abhidhammo. Sakalavinayapiṭakaṃ vinayo, kilesavūpasamakaraṇaṃ abhivinayo. Iti sabbasmimpi ettha dhamme ca abhidhamme ca vinaye ca abhivinaye ca uḷārapāmojjo hotīti attho. Kusalesu dhammesūti kāraṇatthe bhummaṃ, cātubhūmakakusaladhammakāraṇā tesaṃ adhigamatthāya anikkhittadhuro hotīti attho.

'He has desire and affection for the Dhamma' (dhamme assa kāmo sineho), thus he is 'dhammakāmo' (a lover of the Dhamma); the meaning is that he cherishes the word of the Buddha, the Tipiṭaka. As for 'piyasamudāhāro' (one whose speech is dear): when another is speaking, he listens respectfully, and he himself also desires to teach others respectfully; this is the meaning. In the phrase 'in the Abhidhamma, in the Abhivinaya' (abhidhamme abhivinaye), a fourfold set should be known: Dhamma, Abhidhamma, Vinaya, and Abhivinaya. Therein, 'Dhamma' is the Suttanta Piṭaka. 'Abhidhamma' is the seven treatises. 'Vinaya' is the Ubhatovibhaṅga. 'Abhivinaya' is the Khandhakas and the Parivāra. Alternatively, both the Suttanta Piṭaka and the Abhidhamma Piṭaka are simply 'Dhamma'; the paths and fruits are 'Abhidhamma'; the entire Vinaya Piṭaka is 'Vinaya'; and the teaching that causes the pacification of defilements is 'Abhivinaya'. The meaning is that thus, in all of this—in the Dhamma, in the Abhidhamma, in the Vinaya, and in the Abhivinaya—he has great and abundant joy. As for 'in wholesome states' (kusalesu dhammesu), this is a locative case in the sense of reason; the meaning is that because of the wholesome states belonging to the four planes, he is one who has not laid down the yoke for the sake of their attainment.

8. Dutiyanāthasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Second Nātha Sutta

18. Aṭṭhame therānukampitassāti therehi ovādānusāsanidānasamussāhitāya hitapharaṇāya anukampitassa.

18. In the eighth [sutta], 'therānukampitassa' means: one who is compassionated upon by the elders with a compassion that brings about welfare and is prompted by [their] admonition and instruction.

9. Paṭhamaariyāvāsasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the First Ariyāvāsa Sutta

19. Navame ariyavāsāti ariyānaṃ āvāso, te āvasiṃsu āvasanti āvasissantīti ariyāvāsā. Yadariyāti ye vāse ariyā.

19. In the ninth [sutta], 'ariyāvāsā' means: the abodes of the noble ones. The noble ones have dwelt, are dwelling, and will dwell in them; therefore, they are called 'ariyāvāsā' (noble abodes).

10. Dutiyaariyāvāsasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Second Ariyāvāsa Sutta

20. Dasamaṃ [M 3.0292] yasmā kururaṭṭhavāsino bhikkhū gambhīrapaññākārakā yuttappayuttā, tasmā yathā tesaṃ dīghanikāyādīsu mahānidānādīni kathitāni, evamidampi gambhīraṃ sukhumaṃ tilakkhaṇāhataṃ suttaṃ tattheva avoca. Tattha [P 5.0008] pañcaṅgavippahīnoti pañcahi aṅgehi vippayutto hutvā khīṇāsavo avasi vasati vasissati. Tasmā ayaṃ pañcaṅgavippahīnatā ariyāvāsoti vutto. Esa nayo sabbattha. Evaṃ kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu chaḷaṅgasamannāgato hotīti chaḷaṅgupekkhāya samannāgato hoti. Chaḷaṅgupekkhā dhammā nāma keti? Ñāṇādayo. “Ñāṇa”nti vutte kiriyato cattāri ñāṇasampayuttacittāni labbhanti, “satatavihāro”ti vutte aṭṭha mahācittāni, “rajjanadussanaṃ natthī”ti vutte dasa cittāni labbhanti. Somanassaṃ āsevanavasena [V 3.0291] labbhati. Satārakkhena cetasāti khīṇāsavassa hi tīsu dvāresu sabbakāle sati ārakkhakiccaṃ sādheti. Tenevassa carato ca tiṭṭhato ca suttassa ca jāgarassa ca satataṃ samitaṃ ñāṇadassanaṃ paccupaṭṭhitaṃ hotīti vuccati.

20. The tenth [sutta]. Because the monks residing in the Kuru country were accustomed to asking profound questions and were diligently engaged, therefore, just as the Mahānidāna Sutta and others in the Dīgha Nikāya and so on were taught to them, so too was this profound, subtle sutta that bears the three characteristics spoken right there. Therein, 'pañcaṅgavippahīno' means: the one whose taints are destroyed, having become dissociated from the five factors, dwelt, dwells, and will dwell. Therefore, this state of having abandoned the five factors is called 'ariyāvāsa' (a noble one's dwelling). This method is to be understood in all cases. As for 'Thus, monks, a monk is endowed with six factors,' it means he is endowed with equanimity concerning the six factors. What are the things called 'six-factored equanimity'? Knowledge and so on. When 'knowledge' is said, from the functional [consciousness], four consciousnesses associated with knowledge are obtained. When 'constant abiding' is said, the eight great functional consciousnesses are obtained. When 'there is no lusting or aversion' is said, ten consciousnesses are obtained. Gladness is obtained by way of cultivation. As for 'with a mind guarded by mindfulness': for one whose taints are destroyed, mindfulness indeed accomplishes the task of guarding at all times in the three doors. For that very reason, it is said that for him, whether walking, standing, sleeping, or waking, knowledge and vision is constantly and uninterruptedly present.

Puthusamaṇabrāhmaṇānanti bahūnaṃ samaṇabrāhmaṇānaṃ. Ettha samaṇāti pabbajjūpagatā, brāhmaṇāti bhovādino. Puthupaccekasaccānīti bahūni pāṭekkasaccāni. “Idameva dassanaṃ saccaṃ, idameva sacca”nti evaṃ pāṭiyekkaṃ gahitāni bahūni saccānīti attho. Nuṇṇānīti nīhaṭāni. Panuṇṇānīti suṭṭhu nīhaṭāni. Cattānīti vissaṭṭhāni. Vantānīti vamitāni. Muttānīti chinnabandhanāni katāni. Pahīnānīti [P 5.0009] pajahitāni. Paṭinissaṭṭhānīti yathā na puna cittaṃ ārohanti, evaṃ paṭinissajjitāni. Sabbāneva tāni gahitaggahaṇassa vissaṭṭhabhāvavevacanāni.

'Puthusamaṇabrāhmaṇānaṃ' means: of many ascetics and brahmins. Herein, 'samaṇā' are those who have gone forth, and 'brāhmaṇā' are those who are accustomed to saying 'bho.' 'Puthupaccekasaccāni' means: many individual truths. The meaning is: many truths individually held thus, 'This view alone is true; this alone is true.' 'Nuṇṇāni' means: expelled. 'Panuṇṇāni' means: well expelled. 'Cattāni' means: relinquished. 'Vantāni' means: vomited. 'Muttāni' means: made to have their bonds cut. 'Pahīnāni' means: abandoned. 'Paṭinissaṭṭhāni' means: relinquished in such a way that they do not rise to the mind again. All of these are synonyms for the state of having relinquished the grasping of what was grasped.

Samavayasaṭṭhesanoti ettha avayāti anūnā, saṭṭhāti vissaṭṭhā. Sammā avayā saṭṭhā esanā assāti samavayasaṭṭhesano, suṭṭhuvissaṭṭhasabbaesanoti attho. Rāgā cittaṃ vimuttantiādīhi maggassa kiccanipphatti kathitā. Rāgo me pahīnotiādīhi paccavekkhaṇaphalaṃ kathitaṃ. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

In the phrase 'samavayasaṭṭhesano': herein, 'avayā' means not deficient; 'saṭṭhā' means relinquished. 'Samavayasaṭṭhesano' is one for whom there are quests that are rightly not deficient and are relinquished; the meaning is one who has completely relinquished all quests. By such phrases as 'the mind is liberated from lust,' the accomplishment of the path's function is taught. By such phrases as 'lust has been abandoned by me,' the fruit of reviewing is taught. The remainder everywhere has a plain meaning.

Nāthavaggo dutiyo.

The Chapter on Protectors, the Second.

3. Mahāvaggo

3. The Great Chapter

1. Sīhanādasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Sīhanāda Sutta

21. Tatiyassa [M 3.0293] paṭhame visamagateti visamaṭṭhānesu gocaresu gate. Saṅghātaṃ āpādesinti ghātaṃ vadhaṃ pāpesiṃ. Tassa hi ussannatejatāya khuddakesu pāṇesu anukampā hoti. Tasmā ye paṭisattubhāvena [V 3.0292] saṇṭhātuṃ sakkhissanti, ye dubbalā palāyitukāmā bhavissanti, te palāyissantīti sīhanādaṃ naditvāva gocarāya pakkamati. Tathāgatassetaṃ adhivacananti yadi hi sahanatāya hananatāya ca sīho, tathāgato [P 5.0010] hi sabbāni ca iṭṭhāniṭṭhāni sahati, sabbaparappavādino ca vādānaṃ nimmathanena hanati. Idamassa hoti sīhanādasminti ayamassa sīhanādo.

21. In the first sutta of the third chapter: Visamagata means gone to grazing grounds in uneven places. Saṅghātaṃ āpādesi means caused killing, slaughter. Indeed, due to his abundant majesty, he has compassion for small creatures. Therefore, thinking, 'Those who will not be able to stand as adversaries, and those who are weak and will wish to flee—they will flee,' he first roars the lion's roar and then departs for his feeding ground. Regarding This is an epithet for the Tathāgata: if a lion is so called because of its nature of enduring and its nature of destroying, the Tathāgata is indeed so, for he endures all things agreeable and disagreeable, and he destroys the views of all who hold other doctrines by crushing them. Regarding This is his lion's roar, it means: this is his lion's roar.

Tathāgatabalānīti aññehi asādhāraṇāni tathāgatasseva balāni. Yathā vā pubbabuddhānaṃ balāni puññasampattiyā āgatāni, tathā āgatabalānītipi attho. Tattha duvidhaṃ tathāgatassa balaṃ kāyabalaṃ ñāṇabalaṃ. Tesu kāyabalaṃ hatthikulānusārena veditabbaṃ. Vuttañhetaṃ porāṇehi –

The powers of the Tathāgata means the powers of the Tathāgata alone, not shared with others. Or, the meaning may also be understood thus: just as the powers of former Buddhas arose through the accomplishment of merit, so too are these powers that have arisen. Therein, the Tathāgata's power is twofold: physical power and knowledge-power. Among these, physical power is to be understood according to the families of elephants. For this was said by the ancients:

“Kālāvakañca gaṅgeyyaṃ, paṇḍaraṃ tambapiṅgalaṃ;

Gandhamaṅgalahemañca, uposathachaddantime dasā”ti.

“Kālāvaka and Gaṅgeyya, Paṇḍara, Tamba, and Piṅgala; Gandha, Maṅgala, and Hema, Uposatha, and Chaddanta—these are the ten.”

Imāni dasa hatthikulāni. Tattha kālāvakanti pakatihatthikulaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Yaṃ dasannaṃ purisānaṃ kāyabalaṃ, taṃ ekassa kālāvakassa hatthino. Yaṃ dasannaṃ kālāvakānaṃ balaṃ, taṃ ekassa gaṅgeyyassa. Yaṃ dasannaṃ gaṅgeyyānaṃ, taṃ ekassa paṇḍarassa. Yaṃ dasannaṃ paṇḍarānaṃ, taṃ ekassa tambassa. Yaṃ dasannaṃ tambānaṃ, taṃ ekassa piṅgalassa. Yaṃ dasannaṃ piṅgalānaṃ, taṃ ekassa gandhahatthino. Yaṃ dasannaṃ gandhahatthīnaṃ, taṃ ekassa maṅgalassa. Yaṃ dasannaṃ maṅgalānaṃ, taṃ ekassa hemassa. Yaṃ dasannaṃ hemānaṃ, taṃ ekassa uposathassa. Yaṃ dasannaṃ uposathānaṃ, taṃ ekassa chaddantassa. Yaṃ dasannaṃ chaddantānaṃ, taṃ ekassa tathāgatassa. Nārāyanasaṅghātabalantipi idameva [M 3.0294] vuccati[P 5.0011]. Tadetaṃ pakatihatthigaṇanāya hatthīnaṃ koṭisahassānaṃ, purisagaṇanāya dasannaṃ purisakoṭisahassānaṃ balaṃ hoti. Idaṃ tāva tathāgatassa kāyabalaṃ.

These are the ten families of elephants. Among them, the Kālāvaka should be understood as the ordinary family of elephants. The physical strength of ten men is that of one Kālāvaka elephant. The strength of ten Kālāvaka elephants is that of one Gaṅgeyya. The strength of ten Gaṅgeyyas is that of one Paṇḍara. The strength of ten Paṇḍaras is that of one Tamba. The strength of ten Tambas is that of one Piṅgala. The strength of ten Piṅgalas is that of one Gandha elephant. The strength of ten Gandha elephants is that of one Maṅgala. The strength of ten Maṅgalas is that of one Hema. The strength of ten Hemas is that of one Uposatha. The strength of ten Uposathas is that of one Chaddanta. The strength of ten Chaddantas is that of one Tathāgata. This is also called the Nārāyanasaṅghāṭa power. This power, by the count of ordinary elephants, is the strength of one thousand koṭis of elephants; by the count of men, it is the strength of ten thousand koṭis of men. This, to begin with, is the physical power of the Tathāgata.

Ñāṇabalaṃ pana pāḷiyaṃ tāva āgatameva. Dasabalañāṇaṃ, majjhime āgataṃ catuvesārajjañāṇaṃ, aṭṭhasu parisāsu akampanañāṇaṃ, catuyoniparicchedañāṇaṃ, pañcagatiparicchedañāṇaṃ, saṃyuttake (saṃ. ni. 2.33) āgatāni tesattati ñāṇāni sattasattati ñāṇānīti, evaṃ aññānipi anekāni ñāṇabalaṃ nāma. Idhāpi ñāṇabalameva adhippetaṃ. Ñāṇañhi akampiyaṭṭhena upatthambhanaṭṭhena ca balanti vuttaṃ.

The knowledge-power, on the other hand, has already been mentioned in the Pāḷi. The ten powers of knowledge; the knowledge of the four grounds of confidence that comes in the Majjhima Nikāya; the unshakable knowledge in the eight assemblies; the knowledge that discerns the four kinds of birth; the knowledge that discerns the five destinations; the seventy-three knowledges and the seventy-seven knowledges that come in the Saṃyutta Nikāya—thus many other knowledges are called knowledge-power. Here, too, it is the knowledge-power that is intended. For knowledge is called 'power' because of its unshakeable nature and its supporting nature.

Āsabhaṃ [V 3.0293] ṭhānanti seṭṭhaṭṭhānaṃ uttamaṭṭhānaṃ. Āsabhā vā pubbabuddhā, tesaṃ ṭhānanti attho. Apica gavasatajeṭṭhako usabho, gavasahassajeṭṭhako vasabho. Vajasatajeṭṭhako vā usabho, vajasahassajeṭṭhako vasabho. Sabbagavaseṭṭho sabbaparissayasaho seto pāsādiko mahābhāravaho asanisatasaddehipi asampakampiyo nisabho, so idha usabhoti adhippeto. Idampi hi tassa pariyāyavacanaṃ. Usabhassa idanti āsabhaṃ. Ṭhānanti catūhi pādehi pathaviṃ uppīḷetvā acalaṭṭhānaṃ. Idaṃ pana āsabhaṃ viyāti āsabhaṃ. Yatheva hi nisabhasaṅkhāto usabho usabhabalena samannāgato catūhi pādehi pathaviṃ uppīḷetvā acalaṭṭhānena tiṭṭhati, evaṃ tathāgatopi dasahi tathāgatabalehi samannāgato catūhi vesārajjapādehi aṭṭhaparisapathaviṃ uppīḷetvā sadevake loke kenaci paccatthikena paccāmittena akampiyo acalaṭṭhānena tiṭṭhati. Evaṃ tiṭṭhamāno ca taṃ āsabhaṃ ṭhānaṃ paṭijānāti upagacchati na paccakkhāti attani āropeti. Tena vuttaṃ – “āsabhaṃ ṭhānaṃ paṭijānātī”ti.

Āsabhaṃ ṭhānaṃ means the best stance, the supreme stance. Or, the former Buddhas are bulls (āsabhā); the meaning is 'their stance.' Furthermore, the chief of a hundred cattle is a usabha; the chief of a thousand cattle is a vasabha. Or, the chief of a hundred cattle pens is a usabha; the chief of a thousand cattle pens is a vasabha. The nisabha is the best of all cattle, endures all hardships, is white, pleasing, a great bearer of burdens, and is unshaken even by the sound of a hundred thunderbolts; it is this one that is intended here by usabha. For this is a synonym for it. Āsabhaṃ means 'of a bull.' Ṭhānaṃ means an unmoving stance, having pressed down on the earth with four feet. This stance, however, is like that of a bull (āsabhaṃ viya), thus it is called āsabhaṃ. For just as the bull known as nisabha, endowed with a bull's strength, stands in an unmoving stance, having pressed down on the earth with its four feet, so too the Tathāgata, endowed with the ten powers of a Tathāgata, stands in an unmoving stance, having pressed down on the earth of the eight assemblies with the four feet of the grounds of confidence, unshaken by any adversary or enemy in the world with its gods. And standing thus, he professes that bull's stance, attains it, does not reject it, and applies it to himself. Therefore it is said: “He professes a bull's stance.”

Parisāsūti [P 5.0012] aṭṭhasu parisāsu. Sīhanādaṃ nadatīti seṭṭhanādaṃ nadati, abhītanādaṃ nadati, sīhanādasadisaṃ vā nādaṃ nadati. Tatrāyaṃ upamā – yathā sīho sīhabalena samannāgato sabbattha visārado vigatalomahaṃso sīhanādaṃ nadati, evaṃ tathāgatasīhopi tathāgatabalehi samannāgato aṭṭhasu parisāsu visārado vigatalomahaṃso “iti [M 3.0295] sakkāyo”tiādinā nayena nānāvidhadesanāvilāsasampannaṃ sīhanādaṃ nadati. Tena vuttaṃ – “parisāsu sīhanādaṃ nadatī”ti.

In assemblies means in the eight assemblies. He utters a lion's roar means he utters the supreme roar, a fearless roar, or a roar similar to a lion's roar. Herein is a simile: just as a lion, endowed with a lion's strength, confident everywhere, with no bristling of its hairs, utters a lion's roar, so too the Tathāgata-lion, endowed with the Tathāgata's powers, confident in the eight assemblies, with no bristling of his hairs, utters a lion's roar endowed with the grace of manifold teachings, in the manner beginning, “Thus is personality…” Therefore it is said: “He utters a lion's roar in assemblies.”

Brahmacakkaṃ pavattetīti ettha brahmanti seṭṭhaṃ uttamaṃ visiṭṭhaṃ. Cakkanti dhammacakkaṃ. Taṃ panetaṃ duvidhaṃ hoti paṭivedhañāṇañceva desanāñāṇañca. Tattha paññāpabhāvitaṃ attano ariyaphalāvahaṃ paṭivedhañāṇaṃ, karuṇāpabhāvitaṃ sāvakānaṃ ariyaphalāvahaṃ desanāñāṇaṃ. Tattha paṭivedhañāṇaṃ uppajjamānaṃ uppannanti duvidhaṃ. Tañhi abhinikkhamanato yāva arahattamaggā uppajjamānaṃ, phalakkhaṇe uppannaṃ nāma. Tusitabhavanato vā yāva mahābodhipallaṅke arahattamaggā uppajjamānaṃ, phalakkhaṇe uppannaṃ nāma. Dīpaṅkarato vā paṭṭhāya yāva arahattamaggā uppajjamānaṃ, phalakkhaṇe uppannaṃ nāma. Desanāñāṇampi pavattamānaṃ pavattanti duvidhaṃ. Tañhi yāva aññāsikoṇḍaññassa sotāpattimaggā pavattamānaṃ, phalakkhaṇe pavattaṃ nāma. Tesu paṭivedhañāṇaṃ lokuttaraṃ, desanāñāṇaṃ lokiyaṃ. Ubhayampi panetaṃ aññehi asādhāraṇaṃ, buddhānaṃyeva orasañāṇaṃ.

In the phrase 'He sets rolling the Brahma-wheel,' 'Brahma' means supreme, excellent, distinguished. 'The wheel' means the Dhamma-wheel. This, moreover, is of two kinds: the knowledge of penetration and the knowledge of teaching. Among these, the knowledge of penetration, imbued with wisdom, brings the noble fruit to oneself; the knowledge of teaching, imbued with compassion, brings the noble fruit to disciples. Among these, the knowledge of penetration is twofold: 'arising' and 'arisen.' Indeed, it is called 'arising' from the time of the great renunciation up to the path of Arahantship, and at the moment of fruition, it is called 'arisen.' Or, it is called 'arising' from the Tusita realm up to the path of Arahantship at the great Bodhi-seat, and at the moment of fruition, it is called 'arisen.' Or, starting from the time of the Buddha Dīpaṅkara up to the path of Arahantship, it is called 'arising,' and at the moment of fruition, it is called 'arisen.' The knowledge of teaching is also twofold: 'proceeding' and 'proceeded.' Indeed, it is called 'proceeding' up to the stream-entry path of Aññāsikoṇḍañña, and at the moment of fruition, it is called 'proceeded.' Among these, the knowledge of penetration is supramundane, and the knowledge of teaching is mundane. Moreover, both of these are not shared with others; they are the innate knowledge of the Buddhas alone.

Idāni [V 3.0294] yehi dasahi balehi samannāgato tathāgato āsabhaṃ ṭhānaṃ paṭijānāti, tāni vitthārato dassetuṃ katamāni dasa? Idha, bhikkhave, tathāgato ṭhānañca ṭhānatotiādimāha. Tattha ṭhānañca ṭhānatoti kāraṇañca kāraṇato. Kāraṇañhi [P 5.0013] yasmā tattha phalaṃ tiṭṭhati, tadāyattavuttitāya uppajjati ceva pavattati ca, tasmā ṭhānanti vuccati. Taṃ bhagavā “ye ye dhammā yesaṃ yesaṃ dhammānaṃ hetū paccayā uppādāya, taṃ taṃ ṭhānaṃ. Ye ye dhammā yesaṃ yesaṃ dhammānaṃ na hetū na paccayā uppādāya, taṃ taṃ aṭṭhāna”nti pajānanto ṭhānañca ṭhānato aṭṭhānañca aṭṭhānato yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti. Abhidhamme panetaṃ “tattha katamaṃ tathāgatassa ṭhānañca ṭhānato aṭṭhānañca aṭṭhānato yathābhūtaṃ ñāṇa”ntiādinā (vibha. 809) nayena vitthāritameva. Yampīti yena ñāṇena. Idampi, bhikkhave, tathāgatassāti idampi ṭhānāṭṭhānañāṇaṃ tathāgatassa tathāgatabalaṃ nāma hotīti attho. Evaṃ sabbapadesu yojanā veditabbā.

Now, to explain in detail the ten powers with which the Tathāgata is endowed, by which he claims the noble position, he spoke the words beginning with: 'What are the ten? Here, monks, the Tathāgata knows cause as cause...' Herein, 'cause as cause' means cause as cause. Indeed, because the effect stands in that cause, and because it arises and proceeds in dependence on it, it is therefore called 'cause' (ṭhāna). The Blessed One, knowing thus: 'Whatever phenomena are the generative causes and supporting conditions for the arising of whatever other phenomena, that is a cause. Whatever phenomena are not the generative causes and not the supporting conditions for the arising of whatever other phenomena, that is a non-cause,' knows as it truly is cause as cause and non-cause as non-cause. Moreover, in the Abhidhamma, this is elaborated upon in the manner beginning with, 'Therein, what is the Tathāgata’s knowledge of cause as cause and non-cause as non-cause, as it truly is?' 'By which' means by which knowledge. 'This too, monks, is the Tathāgata’s'—the meaning is that this knowledge of cause and non-cause is called a power of the Tathāgata. The application should be understood in this way in all passages.

Kammasamādānānanti samādiyitvā katānaṃ kusalākusalakammānaṃ, kammameva vā kammasamādānaṃ. Ṭhānaso hetusoti paccayato ceva [M 3.0296] hetuto ca. Tattha gatiupadhikālapayogā vipākassa ṭhānaṃ, kammaṃ hetu. Imassa pana ñāṇassa vitthārakathā “atthekaccāni pāpakāni kammasamādānāni gatisampattipaṭibāḷhāni na vipaccantī”tiādinā (vibha. 810) nayena abhidhamme āgatāyeva.

'Of actions undertaken' means of wholesome and unwholesome kammas that have been undertaken and performed; or, kamma itself is 'action undertaken.' 'By way of place and by way of cause' means by way of supporting condition and by way of generative cause. Therein, destination, substratum of existence, time, and endeavor are the 'place' (ṭhāna) for the result; kamma is the 'cause' (hetu). Moreover, the detailed explanation of this knowledge has indeed come in the Abhidhamma in the manner beginning with, 'There are certain evil actions undertaken that are obstructed by a fortunate destination and do not ripen...'

Sabbatthagāmininti sabbagatigāminiñca agatigāminiñca. Paṭipadanti maggaṃ. Yathābhūtaṃ pajānātīti bahūsupi manussesu ekameva pāṇaṃ [P 5.0014] ghātentesu “imassa cetanā nirayagāminī bhavissati, imassa tiracchānayonigāminī”ti iminā nayena ekavatthusmimpi kusalākusalacetanāsaṅkhātānaṃ paṭipattīnaṃ aviparītato sabhāvaṃ jānāti. Imassapi ca ñāṇassa vitthārakathā “tattha katamaṃ tathāgatassa sabbatthagāminiṃ paṭipadaṃ yathābhūtaṃ ñāṇaṃ? Idha tathāgato ayaṃ maggo ayaṃ paṭipadā nirayagāminīti pajānātī”tiādinā (vibha. 811) nayena abhidhamme āgatāyeva.

'Leading everywhere' means leading to all destinations and leading to the destination-less. 'The path' means the way. 'He knows as it truly is' means that even when many people kill a single living being, he knows without distortion the nature of the practices, which are called wholesome and unwholesome volitions, even in a single instance, in this way: 'This person’s volition will lead to hell; this person’s volition will lead to the animal realm.' The detailed explanation of this knowledge has also indeed come in the Abhidhamma in the manner beginning with, 'Therein, what is the Tathāgata's knowledge of the path leading everywhere, as it truly is? Here, the Tathāgata understands: "This way, this path leads to hell"...'

Anekadhātunti cakkhudhātuādīhi kāmadhātuādīhi vā dhātūhi bahudhātuṃ. Nānādhātunti tāsaṃyeva dhātūnaṃ vilakkhaṇatāya nānappakāradhātuṃ. Lokanti khandhāyatanadhātulokaṃ. Yathābhūtaṃ pajānātīti tāsaṃ dhātūnaṃ aviparītato sabhāvaṃ paṭivijjhati. Idampi ñāṇaṃ “tattha katamaṃ tathāgatassa [V 3.0295] anekadhātunānādhātulokaṃ yathābhūtaṃ ñāṇaṃ? Idha tathāgato khandhanānattaṃ pajānātī”tiādinā nayena abhidhamme vitthāritameva.

'Manifold elements' means the many elements, by way of the elements beginning with the eye-element, or the elements beginning with the sensual realm element. 'Diverse elements' means the various kinds of elements, due to the dissimilar characteristics of those very elements. 'The world' means the world of aggregates, sense bases, and elements. 'He knows as it truly is' means he penetrates the nature of those elements without distortion. This knowledge, too, is elaborated upon in the Abhidhamma in the manner beginning with, 'Therein, what is the Tathāgata’s knowledge of the world of manifold and diverse elements, as it truly is? Here, the Tathāgata understands the diversity of aggregates...'

Nānādhimuttikatanti hīnādīhi adhimuttīhi nānādhimuttikabhāvaṃ. Idampi ñāṇaṃ “tattha katamaṃ tathāgatassa sattānaṃ nānādhimuttikataṃ yathābhūtaṃ ñāṇaṃ? Idha tathāgato pajānāti santi sattā hīnādhimuttikā”tiādinā nayena abhidhamme vitthāritameva.

'The diverse inclinations' means the state of having various inclinations, by way of inclinations that are inferior and so forth. This knowledge, too, is elaborated upon in the Abhidhamma in the manner beginning with, 'Therein, what is the Tathāgata’s knowledge of the diverse inclinations of beings, as it truly is? Here, the Tathāgata understands: "There are beings with inferior inclinations"...'

Parasattānanti padhānasattānaṃ. Parapuggalānanti tato aññesaṃ hīnasattānaṃ. Ekatthameva vā etaṃ padadvayaṃ, veneyyavasena dvidhā vuttaṃ. Indriyaparopariyattanti [P 5.0015] saddhādīnaṃ indriyānaṃ parabhāvañca aparabhāvañca, vuddhiñca hāniñcāti attho. Imassāpi ñāṇassa vitthārakathā “tattha katamaṃ tathāgatassa parasattānaṃ parapuggalānaṃ indriyaparopariyattaṃ yathābhūtaṃ ñāṇaṃ[M 3.0297]? Idha tathāgato sattānaṃ āsayaṃ pajānātī”ti (vibha. 814) ādinā nayena abhidhamme āgatāyeva.

'Of other beings' means of principal beings. 'Of other individuals' means of other, inferior beings. Or, these two terms have a single meaning but are spoken of in two ways according to those who are to be trained. 'The maturity and immaturity of the faculties' means the state of being mature and the state of being immature, that is, the growth and decline, of the faculties beginning with faith. The detailed explanation of this knowledge has also indeed come in the Abhidhamma in the manner beginning with, 'Therein, what is the Tathāgata’s knowledge of the maturity and immaturity of the faculties of other beings and other individuals, as it truly is? Here, the Tathāgata understands the underlying disposition of beings...'

Jhānavimokkhasamādhisamāpattīnanti paṭhamādīnaṃ catunnaṃ jhānānaṃ, “rūpī rūpāni passatī”tiādīnaṃ aṭṭhannaṃ vimokkhānaṃ, savitakkasavicārādīnaṃ tiṇṇaṃ samādhīnaṃ, paṭhamajjhānasamāpattiādīnañca navannaṃ anupubbasamāpattīnaṃ. Saṃkilesanti hānabhāgiyadhammaṃ. Vodānanti visesabhāgiyadhammaṃ. Vuṭṭhānanti “vodānampi vuṭṭhānaṃ, tamhā tamhā samādhimhā vuṭṭhānampi vuṭṭhāna”nti (vibha. 828) evaṃ vuttaṃ paguṇajjhānañceva bhavaṅgaphalasamāpattiyo ca. Heṭṭhimaṃ heṭṭhimañhi paguṇajjhānaṃ uparimassa uparimassa padaṭṭhānaṃ hoti, tasmā “vodānampi vuṭṭhāna”nti vuttaṃ. Bhavaṅgena pana sabbajjhānehi vuṭṭhānaṃ hoti, phalasamāpattiyā nirodhasamāpattito vuṭṭhānaṃ hoti. Taṃ sandhāya ca “tamhā tamhā samādhimhā vuṭṭhānampi vuṭṭhāna”nti vuttaṃ. Idampi ñāṇaṃ “tattha katamaṃ tathāgatassa jhānavimokkhasamādhisamāpattīnaṃ saṃkilesaṃ vodānaṃ vuṭṭhānaṃ yathābhūtaṃ ñāṇaṃ? Jhāyīti cattāro jhāyī, atthekacco jhāyī sampattiṃyeva samānaṃ vipattīti paccetī”tiādinā (vibha. 828) nayena abhidhamme vitthāritameva. Sabbañāṇānaṃ [P 5.0016] vitthārakathāya vinicchayo sammohavinodaniyā vibhaṅgaṭṭhakathāya vutto, pubbenivāsānussatidibbacakkhuñāṇakathā visuddhimagge vitthāritā, āsavakkhayakathā heṭṭhā vuttāyevāti.

The jhānas, liberations, concentrations, and attainments refer to the four jhānas beginning with the first; the eight liberations such as “one with form sees forms;” the three concentrations including those with initial and sustained thought; and the nine successive attainments beginning with the first jhāna attainment. Defilement refers to states associated with decline. Purification refers to states associated with distinction. Emergence refers to what is stated as follows: “Purification is also emergence, and emergence is also the rising from each respective concentration” (Vibh. 828)—namely, both practiced jhāna, and the life-continuum and fruition attainments. For each lower practiced jhāna serves as the proximate cause for the next higher one, which is why it is said, “Purification is also emergence.” Furthermore, emergence from all jhānas occurs through the life-continuum, while emergence from the attainment of cessation occurs through the attainment of fruition. Referring to this, it is stated, “Emergence is also the rising from each respective concentration.” This knowledge is elaborated in the Abhidhamma in this way: “Here, what is the Tathāgata’s knowledge of the defilement, purification, and emergence regarding the jhānas, liberations, concentrations, and attainments, as they actually are? ‘A meditator’ means four kinds of meditators. In this case, one meditator regards success itself as failure...” (Vibh. 828). The detailed explanation and analysis of all knowledges is given in the Vibhaṅga commentary, the Sammohavinodanī. The discussion on the recollection of past lives and the divine eye is elaborated in the Visuddhimagga, while the discussion on the destruction of the taints has already been stated below.

Tattha [V 3.0296] paravādīkathā hoti “dasabalañāṇaṃ nāma pāṭiyekkaṃ ñāṇaṃ natthi, sabbaññutaññāṇassevāyaṃ pabhedo”ti. Taṃ na tathā daṭṭhabbaṃ. Aññameva hi dasabalañāṇaṃ, aññaṃ sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ. Dasabalañāṇañhi sakasakakiccameva jānāti, sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ tampi tato avasesampi jānāti. Dasabalañāṇesu hi paṭhamaṃ kāraṇākāraṇameva jānāti, dutiyaṃ kammavipākantarameva, tatiyaṃ kammaparicchedameva, catutthaṃ dhātunānattakāraṇameva, pañcamaṃ sattānaṃ ajjhāsayādhimuttimeva, chaṭṭhaṃ indriyānaṃ tikkhamudubhāvameva, sattamaṃ jhānādīhi saddhiṃ tesaṃ saṃkilesādimeva, aṭṭhamaṃ pubbenivutthakkhandhasantatimeva, navamaṃ sattānaṃ cutipaṭisandhimeva, dasamaṃ saccaparicchedameva. Sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ pana etehi jānitabbañca tato uttariñca pajānāti, etesaṃ pana kiccaṃ na sabbaṃ karoti. Tañhi jhānaṃ hutvā appetuṃ [M 3.0298] na sakkoti, iddhi hutvā vikubbituṃ na sakkoti, maggo hutvā kilese khepetuṃ na sakkoti.

In this regard, there is the talk of an opponent: “There is no such thing as the knowledge of the ten powers as a distinct knowledge; this is merely a division of omniscient knowledge.” That should not be regarded as such. For the knowledge of the ten powers is one thing, and omniscient knowledge is another. The knowledge of the ten powers knows only its own respective function, whereas omniscient knowledge knows that and what remains beyond it. For among the knowledges of the ten powers: the first knows only cause and non-cause; the second, only the specific results of kamma; the third, only the demarcation of kamma; the fourth, only the cause of the diversity of elements; the fifth, only the inclinations and dispositions of beings; the sixth, only the keenness or dullness of the faculties; the seventh, only their defilement and so on, together with the jhānas and so on; the eighth, only the continuity of aggregates in past lives; the ninth, only the passing away and rebirth of beings; the tenth, only the demarcation of the truths. Omniscient knowledge, however, understands what is to be known by these and what is beyond that, yet it does not perform all their functions. For it cannot, having become a jhāna, apply the mind; having become a psychic power, perform transformations; or having become the path, eradicate the defilements.

Apica paravādī evaṃ pucchitabbo “dasabalañāṇaṃ nāmetaṃ savitakkasavicāraṃ avitakkavicāramattaṃ avitakkaavicāraṃ, kāmāvacaraṃ rūpāvacaraṃ arūpāvacaraṃ, lokiyaṃ lokuttara”nti. Jānanto “paṭipāṭiyā satta ñāṇāni savitakkasavicārānī”ti vakkhati, “tato parāni dve avitakkaavicārānī”ti vakkhati. “Āsavakkhayañāṇaṃ siyā savitakkasavicāraṃ siyā avitakkavicāramattaṃ, siyā avitakkaavicāra”nti vakkhati[P 5.0017]. Tathā “paṭipāṭiyā satta kāmāvacarāni, tato dve rūpāvacarāni, avasāne ekaṃ lokuttara”nti vakkhati. “Sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ pana savitakkasavicārameva lokiyamevā”ti vakkhati.

Furthermore, an opponent should be asked thus: “Is this knowledge of the Ten Powers with initial and sustained thought, with only sustained thought, or without initial and sustained thought? Is it of the sense-sphere, of the form-sphere, of the formless-sphere, mundane, or supramundane?” One who knows will say: “In sequence, the first seven knowledges are with initial and sustained thought.” They will say: “The two after that are without initial and sustained thought.” They will say: “The knowledge of the destruction of the taints may be with initial and sustained thought, may be with only sustained thought, or may be without initial and sustained thought.” Similarly, they will say: “In sequence, the first seven are of the sense-sphere, then two are of the form-sphere, and the last one is supramundane.” They will say: “But the knowledge of omniscience is only with initial and sustained thought, only of the sense-sphere, and only mundane.”

Evamettha anupadavaṇṇanaṃ ñatvā idāni yasmā tathāgato paṭhamaṃyeva ṭhānāṭṭhānañāṇena veneyyasattānaṃ āsavakkhayādhigamassa ceva anadhigamassa ca ṭhānāṭṭhānabhūtaṃ kilesāvaraṇābhāvaṃ passati lokiyasammādiṭṭhiṭṭhānādidassanato niyatamicchādiṭṭhiṭṭhānābhāvadassanato ca. Atha nesaṃ kammavipākañāṇena vipākāvaraṇābhāvaṃ passati tihetukappaṭisandhidassanato, sabbatthagāminipaṭipadāñāṇena kammāvaraṇābhāvaṃ passati ānantariyakammābhāvadassanato. Evamanāvaraṇānaṃ anekadhātunānādhātuñāṇena anukūladhammadesanatthaṃ cariyāvisesaṃ passati dhātuvemattadassanato. Atha nesaṃ nānādhimuttikatañāṇena adhimuttiṃ passati payogaṃ anādiyitvāpi adhimuttivasena dhammadesanatthaṃ. Athevaṃ diṭṭhādhimuttīnaṃ yathāsatti yathābalaṃ dhammaṃ desetuṃ indriyaparopariyattiñāṇena indriyaparopariyattaṃ passati saddhādīnaṃ tikkhamudubhāvadassanato. Evaṃ pariññātindriyaparopariyattā [V 3.0297] pana te sace dūre honti, atha jhānādiñāṇena jhānādīsu vasībhūtattā iddhivisesena te khippaṃ upagacchati. Upagantvā ca nesaṃ pubbenivāsānussatiñāṇena pubbajātibhavaṃ, dibbacakkhānubhāvato pattabbena cetopariyañāṇena sampati cittavisesaṃ [P 5.0018] passanto āsavakkhayañāṇānubhāvena āsavakkhayagāminiyā paṭipadāya vigatasammohattā āsavakkhayāya dhammaṃ deseti. Tasmā iminānukkamena imāni balāni vuttānīti veditabbāni.

Having thus understood the word-by-word explanation here, now, because the Tathāgata first, with the knowledge of what is possible and impossible, sees for beings to be trained the absence of the defilement-hindrance—which constitutes the possible and impossible ground for the attainment and non-attainment of the destruction of the taints—by seeing the basis for mundane right view and the absence of a basis for fixed wrong view. Then, for them, with the knowledge of the result of kamma, he sees the absence of the result-hindrance, by seeing rebirth-linking with three roots. With the knowledge of the way leading to all destinations, he sees the absence of the kamma-hindrance, by seeing the absence of kamma with immediate result. Thus, for those without hindrances, with the knowledge of the many and various elements, he sees their specific conduct in order to teach the Dhamma appropriately. Then, for them, with the knowledge of their various dispositions, he sees their disposition, in order to teach the Dhamma according to the power of that disposition, even if they have not yet made an effort. Then, for those whose dispositions have been seen, in order to teach the Dhamma according to their capacity and strength, he sees the superiority and inferiority of their faculties with the knowledge of the superiority and inferiority of the faculties, by seeing the sharpness or dullness of faith and other faculties. If those whose faculties have been thus fully understood are far away, then, because of his mastery over the jhānas and so on, through the knowledge of the jhānas and so on, he swiftly approaches them by means of special psychic power. Having approached, and seeing their past existences with the knowledge of recollection of past lives, and the specific state of their success and failure with the mind-penetrating knowledge attainable through the power of the divine eye, he then, through the power of the knowledge of the destruction of the taints, being free from delusion by the practice leading to the destruction of the taints, teaches them the Dhamma for the destruction of the taints. Therefore, it should be understood that these powers are stated in this sequence.

2. Adhivuttipadasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Exposition of the Adhivuttipadasutta

22. Dutiye [M 3.0299] ye te dhammāti ye te dasabalañāṇaṃ sabbaññutaññāṇadhammā. Adhivuttipadānanti adhivacanapadānaṃ, khandhāyatanadhātudhammānanti attho. Adhivuttiyoti hi adhivacanāni vuccanti, tesaṃ ye padabhūtā desanāya padaṭṭhānattā. Atītā buddhāpi hi ete dhamme kathayiṃsu, anāgatāpi eteva kathayissanti. Tasmā khandhādayo adhivuttipadāni nāma. Tesaṃ adhivuttipadānaṃ. Atha vā bhūtamatthaṃ abhibhavitvā yathāsabhāvato aggahetvā vattanato adhivuttiyoti diṭṭhiyo vuccanti, adhivuttīnaṃ padāni adhivuttipadāni, diṭṭhidīpakāni vacanānīti attho. Tesaṃ adhivuttipadānaṃ diṭṭhivohārānaṃ. Abhiññā sacchikiriyāyāti jānitvā paccakkhakaraṇatthāya. Visāradoti ñāṇasomanassappatto. Tatthāti tesu dhammesu tesaṃ tesaṃ tathā tathā dhammaṃ desetunti tesaṃ tesaṃ diṭṭhigatikānaṃ vā itaresaṃ vā āsayaṃ ñatvā tathā tathā dhammaṃ desetuṃ. Hīnaṃ vā hīnanti ñassatīti hīnaṃ vā dhammaṃ “hīno dhammo”ti jānissati. Ñāteyyanti ñātabbaṃ. Daṭṭheyyanti daṭṭhabbaṃ. Sacchikareyyanti sacchikātabbaṃ. Tattha [P 5.0019] tattha yathābhūtañāṇanti tesu tesu dhammesu yathāsabhāvañāṇaṃ. Iminā sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ dasseti. Evaṃ sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ dassetvā puna dasabalañāṇaṃ dassento dasayimānītiādimāha. Dasabalañāṇampi hi tattha tattha yathābhūtañāṇamevāti.

22. In the second sutta, 'those dhammas' means those dhammas that are the knowledge of the ten powers and the knowledge of omniscience. 'Adhimuttipadānaṃ' means the bases of designations; that is, the dhammas that are the aggregates, sense-bases, and elements. Indeed, designations are called 'adhimutti', because those aggregates, etc., are the proximate cause for the teaching which is the basis of those designations. Indeed, past Buddhas also taught these dhammas, and future Buddhas will also teach them. Therefore, the aggregates, etc., are called 'adhimuttipadāni'. Of those bases of designations. Or alternatively, because they arise by overwhelming the true meaning and not grasping it according to its real nature, wrong views are called 'adhimutti'. The bases of 'adhimutti' are 'adhimuttipadāni'; the meaning is: words that indicate views. Of those bases of designations, which are verbal expressions of views. 'For direct knowledge and realization' means for the purpose of knowing and realizing. 'Confident' means having attained gladness associated with knowledge. 'Therein' means in those dhammas. 'To teach the Dhamma to each of them accordingly' means in order to teach the Dhamma in just such a way, having known the inclination of those various individuals with wrong views or of others. 'He will know the inferior as inferior' means he will know an inferior dhamma thus: 'it is an inferior dhamma'. 'To be known' means what should be known. 'To be seen' means what should be seen. 'To be realized' means what should be realized. 'Knowledge of things as they really are in each case' means knowledge according to the true nature in those various dhammas. By this, he shows the knowledge of omniscience. Thus, having shown the knowledge of omniscience, again, wishing to show the knowledge of the ten powers, he spoke the words beginning with 'These ten...'. Indeed, the knowledge of the ten powers is also just the knowledge of things as they really are in each case.

3. Kāyasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Kāya Sutta

23. Tatiye āpanno hoti kañcideva desanti kañci āpattikoṭṭhāsaṃ āpanno hoti. Anuviccāti anupavisitvā pariyogāhetvā. Kāyaduccaritanti tividhaṃ kāyaduccaritaṃ. Vacīduccaritanti [V 3.0298] catubbidhaṃ vacīduccaritaṃ. Pāpikā issāti lāmikā usūyā. Paññāya disvāti sahavipassanāya maggapaññāya passitvā passitvā pahātabbā. Ijjhatīti samijjhati. Upavāsassāti nissāya upasaṅkamitvā vasantassa. Abhibhuyyāti ajjhottharitvā [M 3.0300] madditvā. Irīyatīti vattati. Imasmiṃ sutte sahavipassanāya maggo kathito.

3. In the third sutta, 'āpanno hoti kiñcideva desaṃ' means 'he has fallen into some portion'; that is, he has fallen into some category of offense. 'Anuvicca' means having investigated, having thoroughly examined. 'Kāyaduccaritaṃ' means threefold bodily misconduct. 'Vacīduccaritaṃ' means fourfold verbal misconduct. 'Pāpikā issā' means base envy. 'Paññāya disvā' means having seen with wisdom—that is, with the path-wisdom accompanied by insight; having seen thus, it should be abandoned. 'Ijjhati' means it succeeds. 'Upavāsassa' means for one who lives in association, having approached and relied upon. 'Abhibhuyya' means having overwhelmed and subdued. 'Irīyati' means it proceeds. In this sutta, the path accompanied by insight is taught.

4. Mahācundasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Mahācunda Sutta

24. Catutthe jānāmimaṃ dhammanti iminā ñāṇavādassa vadanākāro vutto. Bhāvitakāyomhītiādīhi bhāvanāvādassa. Tatiyavāre dvepi vādā ekato vuttā, tayopi cete arahattameva paṭijānanti. Aḍḍhavādaṃ vadeyyāti aḍḍhohamasmīti vādaṃ vadeyya. Upanīhātunti nīharitvā dātuṃ.

23. In the fourth discourse, by 'I know this Dhamma,' the manner of speaking of one who claims knowledge is stated. By the phrases beginning with 'My body is developed,' the manner of speaking of one who claims cultivation is stated. In the third instance, both claims are stated together, and all three of these profess only Arahantship. 'He would make a claim of wealth' means he would make the claim, 'I am wealthy.' 'To bring forth' means to take out and give.

5. Kasiṇasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Kasiṇa Sutta

25. Pañcame sakalaṭṭhena kasiṇāni, tadārammaṇānaṃ dhammānaṃ khettaṭṭhena adhiṭṭhānaṭṭhena vā āyatanānīti kasiṇāyatanāni. Uddhanti [P 5.0020] upari gagaṇatalābhimukhaṃ. Adhoti heṭṭhā bhūmitalābhimukhaṃ. Tiriyanti khettamaṇḍalaṃ viya samantā paricchinditvā. Ekacco hi uddhameva kasiṇaṃ vaḍḍheti, ekacco adho, ekacco samantato. Tena tena vā kāraṇena evaṃ pasāreti ālokamiva rūpadassanakāmo. Tena vuttaṃ – “pathavīkasiṇameko sañjānāti uddhaṃ adho tiriya”nti. Advayanti idaṃ pana ekassa aññabhāvānupagamanatthaṃ vuttaṃ. Yathā hi udakaṃ paviṭṭhassa sabbadisāsu udakameva hoti na aññaṃ, evameva pathavīkasiṇaṃ pathavīkasiṇameva hoti. Natthi tassa aññakasiṇasambhedoti. Eseva nayo sabbattha. Appamāṇanti idaṃ tassa tassa pharaṇaappamāṇavasena vuttaṃ. Tañhi cetasā pharanto sakalameva pharati, “ayamassa ādi, idaṃ majjha”nti pamāṇaṃ na gaṇhāti. Viññāṇakasiṇanti cettha kasiṇugghāṭimākāse pavattaviññāṇaṃ. Tattha kasiṇavasena kasiṇugghāṭimākāse, kasiṇugghāṭimākāsavasena tattha pavattaviññāṇe uddhaṃadhotiriyatā veditabbā. Ayamettha saṅkhepo, kammaṭṭhānabhāvanānayena panetāni pathavīkasiṇādīni vitthārato visuddhimagge (visuddhi. 1.51 ādayo) vuttāneva.

4. In the fifth discourse, they are called 'kasiṇas' in the sense of 'whole'; they are called 'kasiṇa-bases' (kasiṇāyatanāni) because they are bases (āyatana) for the jhānic states that have them as their object, in the sense of being a sphere of arising or in the sense of being a foundation. 'Uddhaṃ' (upward) means facing the surface of the sky above. 'Adho' (downward) means facing the surface of the ground below. 'Tiriyaṃ' (across) means having demarcated all around like a circular field. Indeed, a certain yogī develops the kasiṇa only upwards; another, only downwards; another, from all around. Or, for this or that reason, he spreads it thus, just as one who wishes to see a visual form spreads light. Therefore it is said: 'One perceives the earth kasiṇa above, below, across.' This word 'advayaṃ' (non-dual), however, is said to show that one kasiṇa does not arrive at the state of another. For just as for a person who has entered water, in all directions there is only water and nothing else, in the same way, the earth kasiṇa is only the earth kasiṇa. There is no mixing with another kasiṇa. This same method should be understood in all cases. This word 'appamāṇaṃ' (immeasurable) is said on account of the immeasurability of the pervasion of each respective kasiṇa. Indeed, pervading it with the mind, one pervades the whole of it; one does not take a measure, thinking, 'This is its beginning, this is its middle.' Here, 'viññāṇakasiṇa' (consciousness kasiṇa) is the consciousness that arises in the space-concept obtained by removing a kasiṇa. Therein, the state of being above, below, and across of the consciousness that has arisen there should be understood by way of the removed kasiṇa and by way of the space from which the kasiṇa was removed. This is the summary here. However, these, the earth kasiṇa and so on, have been explained in detail in the Visuddhimagga according to the method of developing a meditation subject.

6. Kāḷīsuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Kāḷī Sutta

26. Chaṭṭhe [V 3.0299][M 3.0301] kumāripañhesūti kumārīnaṃ māradhītānaṃ pucchāsu. Atthassa [P 5.0021] pattiṃ hadayassa santinti dvīhipi padehi arahattameva kathitaṃ. Senanti rāgādikilesasenaṃ. Piyasātarūpanti piyajātikesu ca sātajātikesu ca vatthūsu uppajjanato evaṃladdhanāmaṃ. Ekohaṃ jhāyaṃ sukhamanubodhinti evaṃ kilesasenaṃ jinitvā ahaṃ ekakova jhāyanto sukhaṃ anubujjhiṃ sacchiakāsiṃ. Sakkhinti sakkhibhāvappattaṃ dhammasakkhiṃ. Na sampajjati kenaci meti mayhaṃ kenaci saddhiṃ mittadhammo nāma natthi. Pathavīkasiṇasamāpattiparamā kho, bhagini, eke samaṇabrāhmaṇā atthoti abhinibbattesunti pathavīkasiṇasamāpattiparamo uttamo atthoti gahetvā abhinibbattesuṃ. Yāvatā kho, bhagini, pathavīkasiṇasamāpattiparamatāti yattakā pathavīkasiṇasamāpattiyā uttamakoṭi. Tadabhiññāsi bhagavāti taṃ bhagavā abhiññāpaññāya abhiññāsi. Assādamaddasāti samudayasaccaṃ addasa. Ādīnavamaddasāti dukkhasaccaṃ addasa. Nissaraṇamaddasāti nirodhasaccaṃ addasa. Maggāmaggañāṇadassanamaddasāti maggasaccaṃ addasa. Atthassa pattīti etesaṃ catunnaṃ saccānaṃ diṭṭhattā arahattasaṅkhātassa atthassa patti, sabbadarathapariḷāhavūpasantatāya hadayassa santīti.

26. In the sixth discourse, 'in the questions of the maidens' means 'in the questions of Māra's daughters, who were maidens.' By the two phrases 'the attainment of the goal, the peace of the heart,' only Arahantship is taught. 'Army' means the army of defilements beginning with lust. 'Dear and pleasing forms' is so named because it arises in things that are dear by nature and agreeable by nature. 'Alone, absorbed in jhāna, I understood bliss' means: thus, having conquered the army of defilements, I, all alone, while absorbed in jhāna, understood and realized bliss. 'Friendship' means the Dhamma-friendship, having attained the state of a friend. 'No friendship is found with anyone for me' means: for me, there is no such thing as friendship with anyone. 'Some ascetics and brahmins, sister, brought into being the attainment of the earth-kasiṇa as the highest goal' means: having taken it that 'the attainment with the earth kasiṇa as its object is the supreme, highest goal', they brought it into being. 'As far, sister, as the supreme state of the earth-kasiṇa attainment' means: whatever is the supreme limit of the attainment with the earth kasiṇa as its object. 'The Blessed One directly knew that' means: the Blessed One directly knew that with his special wisdom. 'He saw the gratification' means: He saw the truth of origination. 'He saw the danger' means: He saw the truth of suffering. 'He saw the escape' means: He saw the truth of cessation. 'He saw the knowledge and vision of the path and not-path' means: He saw the truth of the path. 'The attainment of the goal' means that because of having seen these four truths, there is the attainment of the goal called Arahantship. 'The peace of the heart' is because of the pacification of all anxiety and fever.

7. Paṭhamamahāpañhasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the First Great Question Discourse

27. Sattame abhijānāthāti abhijānitvā paccakkhaṃ katvā viharatha. Abhiññāyāti [P 5.0022] abhijānitvā. Idhāti imāya. Dhammadesanāya vā dhammadesananti yadidaṃ samaṇassa gotamassa dhammadesanāya saddhiṃ amhākaṃ dhammadesanaṃ, amhākaṃ vā dhammadesanāya saddhiṃ samaṇassa gotamassa dhammadesanaṃ ārabbha nānākaraṇaṃ vuccetha, taṃ kiṃ nāmāti vadanti. Dutiyapadepi eseva nayo. Iti te majjhe bhinnasuvaṇṇaṃ viya sāsanena saddhiṃ attano laddhiṃ vacanamattena samadhuraṃ ṭhapayiṃsu. Neva abhinandiṃsūti “evameta”nti na sampaṭicchiṃsu. Nappaṭikkosiṃsūti “na idaṃ eva”nti nappaṭisedhesuṃ. Kasmā? Te kira “titthiyā nāma andhasadisā jānitvā vā ajānitvā vā katheyyu”nti nābhinandiṃsu.

27. In the seventh discourse, `abhijānātha` means: you should dwell having known with higher knowledge and having made it directly evident. `Abhiññāya` means: having known. `Idha` means: in this teaching of the Dhamma. `Dhammadesanāya vā dhammadesanaṃ` means: if a differentiation were to be spoken of concerning the teaching of the ascetic Gotama together with our teaching, or our teaching together with the teaching of the ascetic Gotama, they ask, 'What is that differentiation called?' The same method applies to the second phrase. Thus, those sectarians, in the middle, like broken gold, established their own doctrine—a mere verbal expression—as having equal prominence with the Teaching. `Neva abhinandiṃsu` means: they did not accept it, saying, 'So it is.' `Nappaṭikkosiṃsu` means: they did not refuse it, saying, 'This is not so.' Why? It is said that those sectarians are like blind people; they might speak whether knowing or not knowing. Therefore, they did not approve.

Na [M 3.0302] sampāyissantīti sampādetvā kathetuṃ na sakkhissanti. Uttari ca vighātanti asampādanato [V 3.0300] uttarimpi dukkhaṃ āpajjissanti. Sampādetvā kathetuṃ asakkontānañhi dukkhaṃ uppajjati. Yathā taṃ, bhikkhave, avisayasminti ettha ca tanti nipātamattaṃ. Yathāti kāraṇavacanaṃ, yasmā avisaye pañhaṃ pucchitā hontīti attho. Ito vā pana sutvāti ito vā pana mama sāsanato sutvā. Itoti tathāgatatopi tathāgatasāvakatopi. Ārādheyyāti paritoseyya, aññathā ārādhanaṃ nāma natthīti dasseti.

`Na sampāyissanti` means: they will not be able to speak having made it complete. `Uttari ca vighātaṃ` means: because of not making it complete, they will fall into further suffering. Indeed, for those who are unable to speak having made it complete, suffering arises. In the phrase `yathā taṃ, bhikkhave, avisayasmiṃ`, the word `taṃ` is merely a particle. `Yathā` is a word indicating cause; the meaning is: because they have asked a question in what is not their domain. `Ito vā pana sutvā` means: or having heard from this Teaching of mine. `Ito` means from the Tathāgata or from a disciple of the Tathāgata. `Ārādheyya` means one could please or satisfy; this shows that otherwise, there is no such thing as satisfying.

Ekadhammeti [P 5.0023] ekasmiṃ dhamme. Iminā uddeso dassito. Parato katamasmiṃ ekadhammeti iminā pañho dassito. Sabbe sattā āhāraṭṭhitikāti idaṃ panettha veyyākaraṇaṃ. Sesesupi eseva nayo. Sammā nibbindamānotiādīsu pana sammā hetunā nayena nibbidānupassanāya nibbindanto ukkaṇṭhanto, virāgānupassanāya virajjanto, paṭisaṅkhānupassanāya muccanassa upāyaṃ katvā vimuccamāno, adhimokkhavasena vā vimuccamāno sanniṭṭhānaṃ kurumānoti attho. Udayabbayehi paricchinditvā pubbantāparantadassanena sammā pariyantadassāvī. Sammadatthaṃ abhisameccāti sammā sabhāgatthaṃ ñāṇena abhisamāgantvā. Dukkhassantakaro hotīti sakalavaṭṭadukkhassa pariyantaṃ parivaṭumaṃ karo hoti.

`Ekadhamme` means: in one thing. By this, the summary statement is shown. Further, by 'in what one thing?', the question is shown. `Sabbe sattā āhāraṭṭhitikā` (All beings are sustained by nutriment) is the explanation here. In the remaining cases, this is the method. In phrases such as `sammā nibbindamāno` (rightly becoming disenchanted), `sammā` (rightly) means by way of cause and method; `nibbindamāno` (becoming disenchanted) means becoming disenchanted and weary through the contemplation of disenchantment; `virajjanto` (becoming dispassionate) means becoming dispassionate through the contemplation of dispassion; `vimuccamāno` (becoming liberated) means becoming liberated by making the means for liberation through the contemplation of reflection, or becoming liberated by way of resolution; this is the meaning of making a conclusion. Having delimited by arising and passing away, and by seeing the prior and posterior ends, one rightly sees the limit. `Sammadatthaṃ abhisamecca` means: having rightly and directly arrived with wisdom at the meaning which is the intrinsic nature. `Dukkhassantakaro hoti` means: one becomes a maker of the end, the termination, of all the suffering of the round of existence.

Sabbe sattāti kāmabhavādīsu ekavokārabhavādīsu ca sabbabhavesu sabbe sattā. Āhāraṭṭhitikāti āhārato ṭhiti etesanti āhāraṭṭhitikā. Iti sabbasattānampi ṭhitihetu āhāro nāma eko dhammo, tasmiṃ ekadhamme. Nanu ca evaṃ sante yaṃ vuttaṃ – “asaññasattā devā ahetukā anāhārā aphassakā”tiādi (vibha. 1017), taṃ virujjhatīti. Na virujjhati. Tesañhi jhānaṃ āhāro hoti. Evaṃ santepi “cattārome, bhikkhave, āhārā”ti (saṃ. ni. 2.11) idaṃ virujjhatīti. Idampi na virujjhati. Etasmiñhi sutte nippariyāyena āhāralakkhaṇā dhammā āhārāti vuttā, idha pana pariyāyena paccayo [P 5.0024] āhāroti vutto. Sabbadhammānañhi paccayo laddhuṃ vaṭṭati. So ca yaṃ yaṃ phalaṃ janeti, taṃ taṃ āharati nāma. Tasmā āhāroti vuccati. Tenevāha – “avijjampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, sāhāraṃ vadāmi, no anāhāraṃ. Ko [M 3.0303] ca, bhikkhave, avijjāya āhāro? Pañca nīvaraṇātissa vacanīya”nti (a. ni. 10.61). Ayaṃ idha adhippeto. Etasmiñhi paccayāhāre gahite pariyāyāhāropi nippariyāyāhāropi sabbo gahitova hoti.

`Sabbe sattā` means: all beings in all existences, such as the desire-existence and the one-constituent existence. `Āhāraṭṭhitikā` means: for them, persistence is from nutriment, hence they are `āhāraṭṭhitikā` (sustained by nutriment). Thus, for all beings, the cause of their persistence is the one thing called nutriment; in this one thing... But if this is so, does it not contradict what was said: 'The non-percipient devas are without cause, without nutriment, without contact,' etc.? It does not contradict. For them, jhāna is nutriment. Even so, does this not contradict the statement: 'There are, O bhikkhus, these four nutriments'? This too does not contradict. For in that sutta, things with the characteristic of nutriment are called 'nutriments' in a direct sense (nippariyāyena), but here a condition (paccayo) is called 'nutriment' in an indirect sense (pariyāyena). Indeed, it is fitting to take the condition for all things. And whatever fruit that condition produces, it is said to 'bring' (āharati) that fruit. Therefore, it is called 'nutriment' (āhāra). That is why it was said: 'Bhikkhus, I say that ignorance has a nutriment, it is not without a nutriment. And what, bhikkhus, is the nutriment for ignorance? The five hindrances, it should be said.' This is what is intended here. For when this nutriment-as-condition is taken, then all nutriment—both indirect and direct—is included.

Tattha [V 3.0301] asaññībhave paccayāhāro labbhati. Anuppanne hi buddhe titthāyatane pabbajitā vāyokasiṇe parikammaṃ katvā catutthajjhānaṃ nibbattetvā tato vuṭṭhāya “dhi cittaṃ, dhi vatetaṃ cittaṃ, cittassa nāma abhāvoyeva sādhu. Cittañhi nissāya vadhabandhādipaccayaṃ dukkhaṃ uppajjati. Citte asati nattheta”nti khantiṃ ruciṃ uppādetvā aparihīnajjhānā kālaṃ katvā asaññībhave nibbattanti. Yo yassa iriyāpatho manussaloke paṇihito ahosi, so tena iriyāpathena nibbattitvā cittarūpasadiso hutvā pañca kappasatāni tiṭṭhati. Ettakaṃ addhānaṃ sayito viya hoti. Evarūpānampi sattānaṃ paccayāhāro labbhati. Te hi yaṃ jhānaṃ bhāvetvā nibbattā, tadeva nesaṃ paccayo hoti. Yathā jiyāvegena khittasaro yāva jiyāvego atthi, tāva gacchati. Evaṃ yāva jhānapaccayo atthi, tāva tiṭṭhanti. Tasmiṃ niṭṭhite khīṇavego viya saro patanti. Cavanakāle ca tesaṃ so rūpakāyo antaradhāyati, kāmāvacarasaññā uppajjati, tena saññuppādena te devā tamhā kāyā cutāti paññāyanti.

There, in the non-percipient existence, nutriment-as-condition is obtained. For when a Buddha has not yet arisen, those who have gone forth in the domain of the sectarians, having performed the preliminary work on the air kasiṇa, having produced the fourth jhāna, and having arisen from it, give rise to the acceptance and inclination, thinking: 'Shame on the mind! Shame indeed on this mind! Truly, the very non-existence of mind is good. For dependent on the mind, suffering with causes such as killing and binding arises. When the mind does not exist, this suffering does not exist.' Not having fallen away from that jhāna, they pass away and are reborn in the non-percipient existence. In whatever posture one had established in the human world, one is reborn in that same posture and remains for five hundred great aeons. For that entire period, one is as if asleep. Even for such beings, nutriment-as-condition is obtained. For that very jhāna which they developed and by which they were reborn, that itself is their condition. Just as an arrow shot with the force of the bowstring travels as long as the force of the bowstring lasts, so too they remain as long as the jhāna-condition lasts. When that is exhausted, they fall like an arrow whose force is spent.

Ye pana te nerayikā neva vuṭṭhānaphalūpajīvī, na puññaphalūpajīvīti vuttā, tesaṃ ko āhāroti? Tesaṃ kammameva āhāro[P 5.0025]. Kiṃ pañca āhārā atthīti?
