Pañca, na pañcāti idaṃ na vattabbaṃ, nanu “paccayo āhāro”ti vuttametaṃ. Tasmā yena kammena niraye nibbattanti, tadeva tesaṃ ṭhitipaccayattā āhāro hoti. Yaṃ sandhāya idaṃ vuttaṃ – “na ca tāva kālaṃ karoti, yāva na taṃ pāpakammaṃ byantī hotī”ti (ma. ni. 3.250, 268; a. ni. 3.36).

Furthermore, regarding those beings in hell who are said to subsist neither on the fruit of exertion nor on the fruit of merit—what is their nutriment? For them, kamma itself is the nutriment. Are there five nutriments? One should not speak in terms of 'five' or 'not five.' Was it not stated, 'A condition is nutriment'? Therefore, the very kamma by which they are reborn in hell becomes their nutriment, because it is the condition for their continued existence. It is in reference to this that it was said: 'They do not die so long as that evil kamma is not exhausted.'

Kabaḷīkārāhāraṃ ārabbhāpi cettha vivādo na kātabbo. Mukhe uppajjanakheḷopi hi tesaṃ āhārakiccaṃ sādheti. Kheḷo hi niraye dukkhavedanīyo hutvā paccayo hoti, sagge sukhavedaniyo. Iti kāmabhave nippariyāyena cattāro āhārā, rūpārūpabhavesu ṭhapetvā asaññe [M 3.0304] sesānaṃ tayo, asaññānañceva avasesānañca paccayāhāroti iminā ākārena sabbe sattā āhāraṭṭhitikāti veditabbā. Tattha cattāro āhāro yo vā pana koci paccayāhāro dukkhasaccaṃ, āhārasamuṭṭhāpikā purimataṇhā samudayasaccaṃ, ubhinnaṃ appavatti nirodhasaccaṃ, nirodhappajānanā paññā maggasaccanti evaṃ catusaccavasena sabbavāresu yojanā kātabbā.

And here, there should be no dispute even concerning physical nutriment. For indeed, even the saliva that arises in the mouth accomplishes the function of nutriment for them. Indeed, saliva in hell, being a cause of painful feeling, becomes a condition; in heaven, being a cause of pleasant feeling, it becomes a condition. Thus, in the sense-sphere existence, there are invariably four nutriments; in the form and formless existences, excepting the non-percipient beings, there are three for the remaining beings; and for the non-percipient beings as well as for the other remaining beings, there is nutriment as a condition. In this way, it should be understood that all beings are sustained by nutriment. Therein, the four nutriments, or any nutriment that is a condition, is the truth of suffering; the preceding craving that gives rise to nutriment is the truth of the origin; the non-occurrence of both is the truth of cessation; and the wisdom that fully understands cessation is the truth of the path. Thus, in all sections, the application should be made in accordance with the Four Noble Truths.

8. Dutiyamahāpañhasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Second Sutta on the Great Questions

28. Aṭṭhame [V 3.0302] kajaṅgalāyanti evaṃnāmake nagare. Kajaṅgalāti kajaṅgalāvāsino. Mahāpañhesūti mahantaatthapariggāhakesu pañhesu. Yathā mettha khāyatīti yathā me ettha upaṭṭhāti. Sammā subhāvitacittoti hetunā nayena suṭṭhu bhāvitacitto. Eso ceva tassa atthoti kiñcāpi bhagavatā “cattāro dhammā”tiādayo pañhā “cattāro āhārā”tiādinā nayena vissajjitā, yasmā pana catūsu āhāresu pariññātesu cattāro satipaṭṭhānā bhāvitā honti, tesu ca bhāvitesu cattāro āhārā pariññātāva honti. Tasmā desanāvilāsena byañjanamevettha nānaṃ, attho pana ekoyeva. Indriyādīsupi eseva nayo. Tena vuttaṃ – “eso [P 5.0026] ceva tassa attho”ti. Atthato hi ubhayampetaṃ majjhe bhinnasuvaṇṇamiva hoti.

28. In the eighth sutta: `Kajaṅgalāyaṃ` means in the city so named. `Kajaṅgalā` means the inhabitants of Kajaṅgala. `Mahāpañhesu` means in questions that grasp great meaning. `Yathā mettha khāyati` means as it appears to me here. `Sammā subhāvitacitto` means a mind well-developed by means of reason and method. As to `Eso ceva tassa attho` ('This is indeed its meaning'): Although the Blessed One answered questions such as 'the four dhammas' by the method of 'the four nutriments,' and so on, yet because when the four nutriments are fully understood, the four foundations of mindfulness are developed, and when these are developed, the four nutriments are fully understood. Therefore, through the elegance of the teaching, only the letter is different here, but the meaning is one and the same. This is the same method for the faculties and so on. For that reason it was said, 'This is indeed its meaning.' For in terms of meaning, both of these are like a piece of gold broken in the middle.

9. Paṭhamakosalasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the First Kosala Sutta

29. Navame yāvatāti yattakā. Kāsikosalāti kāsikosalajanapadā. Attheva aññathattanti ṭhitassa aññathattaṃ atthiyeva. Atthi vipariṇāmoti maraṇampi atthiyeva. Tasmimpi nibbindatīti tasmimpi sampattijāte ukkaṇṭhati. Agge virajjatīti sampattiyā agge kosalarājabhāve virajjati. Pageva hīnasminti paṭhamataraṃyeva hīne ittaramanussānaṃ pañca kāmaguṇajāte.

29. In the ninth sutta: 'as far as' (yāvatā) means to whatever extent. 'Kāsikosalā' refers to the Kāsikosala regions. 'There is indeed alteration' (attheva aññathattaṃ) means that even for what is stable, alteration exists. 'There is transformation' (atthi vipariṇāmo) means that death indeed exists. 'He becomes disenchanted with that too' (tasmimpi nibbindati) means he becomes weary even with that attainment. 'He becomes dispassionate toward the highest' (agge virajjati) means he becomes dispassionate toward the peak of attainment, the Kosala kingship. 'How much more so toward the inferior' (pageva hīnasmiṃ) means all the more so toward the inferior, the five strands of sensual pleasure among people of little merit.

Manomayāti jhānamanena nibbattā. Bārāṇaseyyakanti bārāṇasiyaṃ uppannaṃ. Tattha kira kappāsopi mudu, suttakantikāyopi tantavāyāpi chekā, udakampi suci siniddhaṃ. Ubhatobhāgavimaṭṭhanti dvīsupi passesu maṭṭhaṃ mudu [M 3.0305] siniddhaṃ khāyati. Catasso paṭipadā lokiyalokuttaramissikā kathitā. Saññāsu paṭhamā kāmāvacarasaññā, dutiyā rūpāvacarasaññā, tatiyā lokuttarasaññā, catutthā ākiñcaññāyatanasaññā. Yasmā pana sā saññā aggāti āgatā, tato paraṃ saññāpaññatti nāma natthi, tasmā agganti vuttā.

'Mind-made' (manomayā) means produced by the jhāna-mind. 'Bārāṇaseyyaka' means originated in Bārāṇasī. It is said that there even the cotton is soft, and both the female spinners and female weavers are skillful, and the water is pure and smooth. 'Polished on both sides' (ubhatobhāgavimaṭṭhaṃ) means it appears polished, soft, and smooth on both sides. Regarding perceptions: the first is perception pertaining to the sense-sphere, the second is perception pertaining to the form-sphere, the third is supramundane perception, and the fourth is perception of the base of nothingness. But since that perception is stated as the highest, beyond that there is no such thing as a designation of perception, therefore it is called 'the highest.'

Bāhirakānanti sāsanato bahiddhā pavattānaṃ. No [P 5.0027] cassaṃ no ca me siyāti sace ahaṃ atīte na bhavissaṃ, etarahipi me ayaṃ attabhāvo na siyā. Na bhavissāmi na me bhavissatīti [V 3.0303] sacepi anāgate na bhavissāmi, na ca me kiñci palibodhajātaṃ bhavissati. Agge virajjatīti ucchedadiṭṭhiyaṃ virajjati. Ucchedadiṭṭhi hi idha nibbānassa santatāya agganti jātā.

'Of outsiders' (bāhirakānaṃ) refers to those who conduct themselves outside the Teaching. 'I would not be, nor would it be mine' (no cassaṃ no ca me siyā) means: if I had not been in the past, this present existence of mine would not be. 'I will not be, nor will it be mine' (na bhavissāmi na me bhavissati) means: even if I will not be in the future, no kind of encumbrance will arise for me. 'He becomes dispassionate toward the highest' (agge virajjati) means he becomes dispassionate toward the annihilation view. For the annihilation view here has become 'the highest' because of its proximity to Nibbāna.

Paramatthavisuddhinti uttamatthavisuddhiṃ. Nevasaññānāsaññāyatanasamāpattiyā etaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Ākiñcaññāyatanañhi vipassanāpadaṭṭhānattā aggaṃ nāma jātaṃ, nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ dīghāyukattā. Paramadiṭṭhadhammanibbānanti imasmiññeva attabhāve paramanibbānaṃ. Anupādā vimokkhoti catūhi upādānehi aggahetvā cittassa vimokkho. Arahattassetaṃ nāmaṃ. Pariññanti samatikkamaṃ. Tattha bhagavā paṭhamajjhānena kāmānaṃ pariññaṃ paññāpeti, arūpāvacarehi rūpānaṃ pariññaṃ paññāpeti, anupādānibbānena vedanānaṃ pariññaṃ paññāpeti. Nibbānañhi sabbavedayitappahānattā vedanānaṃ pariññā nāma. Anupādāparinibbānanti apaccayaparinibbānaṃ. Idaṃ pana suttaṃ kathento bhagavā anabhiratipīḷitāni pañca bhikkhusatāni disvā tesaṃ anabhirativinodanatthaṃ kathesi. Tepi anabhiratiṃ vinodetvā desanānusārena ñāṇaṃ pesetvā sotāpannā hutvā aparabhāge vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā arahattaṃ pāpuṇiṃsūti.

‘Ultimate purity’ (paramatthavisuddhi) means the purity of the ultimate meaning. This is a designation for the attainment of the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception. For the base of nothingness is called ‘supreme’ because it is a proximate cause for insight, while the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception is so called due to its long lifespan. ‘The ultimate Nibbāna in this very life’ (paramadiṭṭhadhammanibbāna) means the supreme Nibbāna in this very existence. ‘Liberation without clinging’ (anupādā vimokkho) refers to the liberation of the mind without grasping with the four kinds of clinging. This is a name for the fruit of Arahantship. ‘Full understanding’ (pariññā) means overcoming. Herein, the Blessed One makes known the full understanding of sensual pleasures through the first jhāna, he makes known the full understanding of forms through the formless attainments, and he makes known the full understanding of feelings through Nibbāna without clinging. For Nibbāna is called the full understanding of feelings because it is the abandonment of all that is felt. ‘Final Nibbāna without clinging’ (anupādāparinibbāna) means final Nibbāna without a condition. Moreover, while teaching this sutta, the Blessed One saw five hundred monks afflicted by discontent and spoke it to dispel their discontent. They, having dispelled their discontent, directing their knowledge in accordance with the teaching, and having become stream-enterers, in a later part developed insight and attained Arahantship.

10. Dutiyakosalasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Explanation of the Second Kosala Sutta.

30. Dasame uyyodhikā nivatto hotīti yuddhato nivatto hoti. Laddhādhippāyoti [P 5.0028] mahākosalaraññā kira bimbisārassa dhītaraṃ dentena dvinnaṃ rajjānaṃ antare satasahassuṭṭhāno kāsigāmo nāma dhītu dinno[M 3.0306]. Ajātasattunā pitari mārite mātāpissa rañño viyogasokena nacirasseva matā. Tato rājā pasenadikosalo “ajātasattunā mātāpitaro māritā, mama pitu santako gāmo”ti tassatthāya aṭṭaṃ karoti, ajātasattupi “mama mātu santako”ti tassa gāmassatthāya. Dvepi mātulabhāgineyyā caturaṅginiṃ senaṃ sannayhitvā yujjhiṃsu. Tattha pasenadikosalo dve vāre ajātasattunā parājito nagarameva pāvisi. Tatiyavāre “kathaṃ nu kho me jayo bhaveyyā”ti upassutivasena yujjhitabbākāraṃ ñatvā byūhaṃ racayitvā ubhohi passehi parikkhipitvā ajātasattuṃ gaṇhi. Tāvadeva jayādhippāyassa laddhattā laddhādhippāyo nāma ahosi.

30. In the tenth sutta, uyyodhikā nivatto hoti means ‘he has returned from battle’. Laddhādhippāyo (‘having attained his aim’): It is said that when King Mahākosala gave his daughter to Bimbisāra, the village named Kāsigāma, which yielded a revenue of one hundred thousand and was situated between the two kingdoms, was given to his daughter. When Ajātasattu killed his father, his mother also died not long after from grief over separation from the king. Then King Pasenadi of Kosala, thinking, ‘Ajātasattu has killed his mother and father; the village is my father's property,’ made a legal claim for its sake. Ajātasattu also, thinking, ‘It is my mother's property,’ made a legal claim for the sake of that village. Thus, for the sake of that village, both of them, the maternal uncle and nephew, having assembled a four-limbed army, fought. There, Pasenadi of Kosala, having been defeated twice by Ajātasattu, entered the city. On the third occasion, thinking, ‘How indeed might victory be mine?’, and having learned the manner of fighting by means of secretly listening, he arranged his forces in formation, surrounded Ajātasattu on both flanks, and captured him. At that very moment, because his aim of victory had been attained, he was, it is said, named ‘one who has attained his aim’.

Yena [V 3.0304] ārāmo tena pāyāsīti bahinagare jayakhandhāvāraṃ nivesetvā “yāva nagaraṃ alaṅkaronti, tāva dasabalaṃ vandissāmi. Nagaraṃ paviṭṭhakālato paṭṭhāya hi papañco hotī”ti amaccagaṇaparivuto yenārāmo tena pāyāsi, ārāmaṃ pāvisi. Kasmiṃ kāle pāvisīti? Piṇḍapātappaṭikkantānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ ovādaṃ datvā sammāsambuddhe gandhakuṭiṃ paviṭṭhe bhikkhusaṅghe ca ovādaṃ sampaṭicchitvā attano attano rattiṭṭhānadivāṭṭhānāni gate. Caṅkamantīti kasmiṃ samaye caṅkamanti? Paṇītabhojanapaccayassa thinamiddhassa vinodanatthaṃ, divā padhānikā vā te. Tādisānañhi pacchābhattaṃ caṅkamitvā nhatvā sarīraṃ utuṃ gāhāpetvā [P 5.0029] nisajja samaṇadhammaṃ karontānaṃ cittaṃ ekaggaṃ hoti. Ye te bhikkhūti so kira “kahaṃ satthā kahaṃ sugatoti pariveṇena pariveṇaṃ āgantvā pucchitvāva pavisissāmī”ti vilokento araññahatthī viya mahācaṅkame caṅkamamāne paṃsukūlike bhikkhū disvā tesaṃ santikaṃ agamāsi. Taṃ sandhāyetaṃ vuttaṃ. Dassanakāmāti passitukāmā. Vihāroti gandhakuṭiṃ sandhāya āhaṃsu. Ataramānoti aturito, saṇikaṃ padapamāṇaṭṭhāne padaṃ nikkhipanto vattaṃ katvā susammaṭṭhaṃ muttajālasinduvārasadisaṃ vālukaṃ avināsentoti attho. Ālindanti pamukhaṃ. Aggaḷanti kavāṭaṃ. Ukkāsitvāti ukkāsitasaddaṃ katvā. Ākoṭehīti agganakhena īsakaṃ kuñcikāchiddasamīpe koṭehīti vuttaṃ hoti. Dvāraṃ kira atiupari amanussā, atiheṭṭhā dīghajātikā koṭenti. Tathā akoṭetvā majjhe chiddasamīpe koṭetabbanti idaṃ dvārakoṭanavattanti [M 3.0307] vadanti. Vivari bhagavā dvāranti na bhagavā uṭṭhāya dvāraṃ vivarati, vivaratūti pana hatthaṃ pasāreti. Tato “bhagavā tumhehi anekakappakoṭīsu dānaṃ dadamānehi na sahatthā dvāravivaraṇakammaṃ kata”nti sayameva dvāraṃ vivaṭaṃ. Taṃ pana yasmā bhagavato manena vivaṭaṃ, tasmā “vivari bhagavā dvāra”nti vattuṃ vaṭṭati.

‘He went towards the monastery’ (yena ārāmo tena pāyāsi): Having established a victory camp outside the city, he thought, ‘While they are decorating the city, I will pay homage to the Ten-Powered One. For from the time of entering the city, it becomes complicated.’ Surrounded by a group of ministers, he went towards the monastery and entered the monastery grounds. At what time did he enter? When, after giving instruction to the monks who had returned from the alms-round, the Perfectly Enlightened One had entered the fragrant chamber, and the community of monks, having accepted the instruction, had gone to their respective places for the night and day. Caṅkamanti (‘they are walking’): At what time do they walk? For the purpose of dispelling sloth and torpor caused by fine food, or because they are monks who strive during the day. Indeed, for such monks who, after the meal, walk on the cloister-path, bathe, let the body adjust to the temperature, and sitting, practice the ascetic's duties, the mind becomes one-pointed. Ye te bhikkhū (‘those monks’): It is said that he, thinking, ‘Where is the Teacher? Where is the Sugata? I will enter only after going from one monastic compound to another and asking,’ was looking about. Seeing monks wearing rag-robes walking on the cloister-path like forest elephants, he went to them. This was said with reference to that. Dassanakāmā (‘desirous of seeing’) means wishing to see. Vihāro (‘the dwelling’): They spoke referring to the fragrant chamber. Ataramāno (‘not hurrying’) means not agitated; the meaning is: slowly, placing his steps in the space of a footstep, having performed the proper conduct, not destroying the very smooth sand which was like a net of pearls or Sinduvāra flowers. Ālindaṃ (‘the porch’) means the front porch. Aggaḷaṃ (‘the bolt’) means the door-leaf. Ukkāsitvā (‘having cleared his throat’) means having made the sound of clearing the throat. Ākoṭehi (‘knock’): It is meant to be said, ‘Knock lightly with the tip of a fingernail near the keyhole.’ It is said that non-human beings knock on the door very high up, and long-bodied creatures knock very low down. They say: ‘Not knocking in that way, one should knock in the middle near the keyhole; this is the etiquette of knocking on a door.’ Vivari bhagavā dvāraṃ (‘the Blessed One opened the door’): The Blessed One does not get up and open the door; but, thinking ‘Let it open,’ he extends his hand. Then, as if it were being said, ‘You, Blessed One, who have given gifts for many crores of aeons, have not performed the act of opening a door with your own hand,’ the door opened by itself. But since that door opened by the Blessed One's mind, therefore it is fitting to say, ‘the Blessed One opened the door’.

Mettūpahāranti [P 5.0030] mettāsampayuttaṃ kāyikavācasikaupahāraṃ. Kataññutanti ayañhi rājā pubbe thūlasarīro ahosi, doṇapākaṃ bhuñjati. Athassa bhagavā divase divase thokaṃ thokaṃ hāpanatthāya –

‘An offering of loving-kindness’ (mettūpahāraṃ) means a bodily and verbal offering associated with loving-kindness. ‘Gratitude’ (kataññutaṃ): For this king was formerly of a stout body, eating a doṇa-measure of cooked rice. Then the Blessed One, for the sake of his gradually diminishing his food intake day by day—

“Manujassa sadā satīmato,

Mattaṃ jānato laddhabhojane;

Tanukassa [V 3.0305] bhavanti vedanā,

Saṇikaṃ jīrati āyupālaya”nti. (saṃ. ni. 1.124) –

‘For a person ever mindful, knowing moderation in food received, his pains become slight; slowly he ages, protecting his life.’

Imaṃ ovādaṃ adāsi. So imasmiṃ ovāde ṭhatvā divase divase thokaṃ thokaṃ hāpetvā anukkamena nāḷikodanaparamatāya saṇṭhāsi, gattānipissa tanūni thirāni jātāni. Taṃ bhagavatā kataṃ upakāraṃ sandhāya “kataññutaṃ kho ahaṃ, bhante, kataveditaṃ sampassamāno”ti āha. Ariye ñāyeti sahavipassanake magge. Vuddhasīloti vaḍḍhitasīlo. Ariyasīloti apothujjanikehi sīlehi samannāgato. Kusalasīloti anavajjehi sīlehi samannāgato. Āraññakoti jāyamānopi araññe jāto, abhisambujjhamānopi araññe abhisambuddho, devavimānakappāya gandhakuṭiyā vasantopi araññeyeva vasīti dassento evamāha. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

He gave this advice. Having stood firm in this advice, he gradually reduced his intake little by little each day, until he was established on a maximum of a nāḷika of rice, and his limbs became slender and firm. Referring to the help given by the Blessed One, he said, 'Venerable sir, I indeed see the gratitude and thankfulness.' 'The noble way' (ariye ñāye) means the path together with insight. 'Of developed virtue' (vuddhasīlo) means one whose virtue has been developed. 'Noble virtue' (ariyasīlo) means endowed with virtues not possessed by worldlings. 'Skillful virtue' (kusalasīlo) means endowed with blameless virtues. 'A forest dweller' (āraññako): He spoke thus to show this meaning: even when being born, he was born in the forest; even when fully awakening, he was fully awakened in the forest; and even while dwelling in a fragrant chamber comparable to a celestial mansion, he dwelt only in the forest. The rest everywhere has a plain meaning.

Mahāvaggo tatiyo.

The Third Great Chapter.

4. Upālivaggo

4. The Upāli Chapter

1. Upālisuttavaṇṇanā

1. Explanation of the Upāli Sutta

31. Catutthassa [P 5.0031] paṭhame saṅghasuṭṭhutāyātiādīsu saṅghasuṭṭhutā nāma saṅghassa suṭṭhubhāvo, “suṭṭhu devā”ti āgataṭṭhāne viya “suṭṭhu, bhante”ti [M 3.0308] vacanasampaṭicchanabhāvo. Yo ca tathāgatassa vacanaṃ sampaṭicchati, tassa taṃ dīgharattaṃ hitāya sukhāya saṃvattati. Tasmā saṅghassa “suṭṭhu, bhante”ti vacanasampaṭicchanatthaṃ paññattaṃ, asampaṭicchane ādīnavaṃ, sampaṭicchane ānisaṃsaṃ dassetvā, na balakkārena abhibhavitvāti etamatthaṃ āvikaronto āha – saṅghasuṭṭhutāyāti. Saṅghaphāsutāyāti saṅghassa phāsubhāvāya, sahajīvitāya sukhavihāratthāyāti attho.

31. In the first sutta of the fourth chapter, regarding 'for the Saṅgha's excellence' and so on: 'Saṅgha's excellence' means the Saṅgha's state of excellence; it is the state of accepting the utterance, 'Excellent, venerable sir!', just as in the instance where 'Excellent, deva!' occurs. And for whoever accepts the word of the Tathāgata, that acceptance leads to their welfare and happiness for a long time. Therefore, it was laid down for the purpose of the Saṅgha's acceptance of the utterance, 'Excellent, venerable sir!', having shown the danger in not accepting and the benefit in accepting, and not by overpowering with force. Making this meaning clear, he said: 'for the Saṅgha's excellence.' 'For the Saṅgha's comfort' means for the Saṅgha's state of comfort, for living together, for the purpose of dwelling happily. This is the meaning.

Dummaṅkūnaṃ [V 3.0306] puggalānaṃ niggahāyāti dummaṅkūnāma dussīlapuggalā, ye maṅkutaṃ āpādiyamānāpi dukkhena āpajjanti, vītikkamaṃ karontā vā katvā vā na lajjanti, tesaṃ niggahatthāya. Te hi sikkhāpade asati “kiṃ tumhehi diṭṭhaṃ, kiṃ sutaṃ, kiṃ amhehi kataṃ, katamasmiṃ vatthusmiṃ katamaṃ āpattiṃ ropetvā amhe niggaṇhathā”ti saṅghaṃ viheṭheyyuṃ. Sikkhāpade pana sati te saṅgho sikkhāpadaṃ dassetvā saha dhammena niggahessati. Tena vuttaṃ – “dummaṅkūnaṃ puggalānaṃ niggahāyā”ti.

For the restraint of unruly persons: 'unruly' means immoral persons who, even when being made ashamed, become so with difficulty, and who, while transgressing or having transgressed, feel no shame. This is for their restraint. Indeed, if there were no training rule, they might harass the Saṅgha by saying, 'What was seen by you? What was heard? What was done by us? Having charged us with what offense, on what grounds, do you restrain us?' But when the training rule exists, the Saṅgha, having shown the training rule, will restrain them in accordance with the Dhamma. Therefore it is said: 'for the restraint of unruly persons.'

Pesalānanti piyasīlānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ phāsuvihāratthāya. Piyasīlā hi bhikkhū kattabbākattabbaṃ sāvajjānavajjaṃ velaṃ mariyādañca ajānantā sikkhāttayapāripūriyā ghaṭamānā kilamanti, te pana sāvajjānavajjaṃ velaṃ mariyādañca [P 5.0032] ñatvā sikkhāpāripūriyā ghaṭamānā na kilamanti. Tena tesaṃ sikkhāpadapaññāpanaṃ phāsuvihārāya saṃvattatiyeva. Yo vā dummaṅkūnaṃ puggalānaṃ niggaho, sveva tesaṃ phāsuvihāro. Dussīlapuggale nissāya hi uposathappavāraṇā na tiṭṭhanti, saṅghakammāni nappavattanti, sāmaggī na hoti, bhikkhū anekaggā uddesādīsu anuyuñjituṃ na sakkonti. Dussīlesu pana niggahitesu sabbopi ayaṃ upaddavo na hoti, tato pesalā bhikkhū phāsu viharanti. Evaṃ “pesalānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ phāsuvihārāyā”ti ettha dvidhā attho veditabbo.

For the amiable ones: for the comfortable living of monks of amiable conduct. Indeed, monks of amiable conduct, not knowing what should and should not be done, what is blameworthy and blameless, the boundary and the limit, and striving for the fulfillment of the threefold training, become weary. But those who know what is blameworthy and blameless, the boundary and the limit, and are striving for the fulfillment of the training, do not become weary. Therefore, the laying down of training rules contributes to their comfortable living. Alternatively, the very restraint of unruly individuals is their comfortable living. For indeed, on account of immoral persons, the Uposatha and Pavāraṇā ceremonies are not established, Saṅgha acts do not proceed, there is no harmony, and monks, being of divided minds, are not able to apply themselves to recitation and so on. But when the immoral are restrained, all this trouble does not arise, and thus the amiable monks live comfortably. In this way, the phrase 'for the comfortable living of amiable monks' should be understood in two ways.

Diṭṭhadhammikānaṃ āsavānaṃ saṃvarāyāti diṭṭhadhammikā āsavā nāma asaṃvare ṭhitena tasmiṃyeva attabhāve pattabbā pāṇippahāradaṇḍappahārasatthappahārahatthacchedapādacchedaakittiayasavippaṭisārādayo dukkhaviseso, tesaṃ saṃvarāya pidahanāya āgamanamaggathakanāyāti attho. Samparāyikānanti samparāyikā āsavā nāma asaṃvare ṭhitena katapāpakammamūlakā [M 3.0309] samparāye narakādīsu pattabbā dukkhavisesā, tesaṃ paṭighātatthāya vūpasamatthāya.

For the restraint of taints pertaining to the present life: 'taints pertaining to the present life' means the particular kinds of suffering that can be experienced in this very existence by one who remains unrestrained, such as being struck by the hand, struck by a stick, struck by a weapon, the cutting off of hands, the cutting off of feet, ill-repute, loss of followers, remorse, and so on. The meaning is: for their restraint, for their shutting out, for blocking the path of their arising. For those pertaining to the future life: 'taints pertaining to the future life' means the particular kinds of suffering, rooted in evil kamma that has been done, to be experienced in the future life in hell and so on, by one who remains unrestrained—for the purpose of their destruction, for the purpose of their pacification.

Appasannānanti sikkhāpadapaññattiyā hi sati sikkhāpadapaññattiṃ ñatvā vā, yathāpaññattaṃ paṭipajjamāne bhikkhū disvā vā, yepi appasannā paṇḍitamanussā, te “yāni vata loke [P 5.0033] mahājanassa rajjanadussanamuyhanaṭṭhānāni, tehi ime samaṇā ārakā viratā viharanti, dukkaraṃ vata karontī”ti pasādaṃ āpajjanti vinayapiṭakapotthakaṃ disvā micchādiṭṭhikatavedibrāhmaṇā viya. Tena vuttaṃ – “appasannānaṃ pasādāyā”ti.

For those who lack faith: Indeed, when the laying down of a training rule exists, either by knowing of the laying down of the training rule or by seeing monks practicing as laid down, even those wise people who lack faith are inspired with faith, thinking: 'Indeed, these ascetics dwell far, abstaining from those things in the world that are the basis for the great populace's lust, aversion, and delusion. Indeed, they do what is difficult!'—just like the brahmins of wrong view who are accustomed to recognizing virtue did upon seeing the book of the Vinaya Piṭaka. Therefore it is said: 'for inspiring faith in those who lack it.'

Pasannānanti [V 3.0307] yepi sāsane pasannā kulaputtā, tepi sikkhāpadapaññattiṃ vā ñatvā, yathāpaññattaṃ paṭipajjamāne bhikkhū vā disvā “aho ayyā dukkaraṃ karonti, ye yāvajīvaṃ ekabhattā vinayasaṃvaraṃ pālentī”ti bhiyyo bhiyyo pasīdanti. Tena vuttaṃ – “pasannānaṃ bhiyyobhāvāyā”ti.

For the faithful: even those sons of good families who have faith in the Dispensation, they too, either by knowing of the laying down of the training rules or by seeing monks practicing as laid down, become ever more faithful, thinking, 'Oh, the noble ones do what is difficult, who, eating only one meal a day, maintain the restraint of the Vinaya for as long as life lasts.' Therefore it is said: 'for the increase of the faithful.'

Saddhammaṭṭhitiyāti tividho saddhammo pariyattisaddhammo paṭipattisaddhammo adhigamasaddhammoti. Tattha sakalampi buddhavacanaṃ pariyattisaddhammo nāma. Terasa dhutaguṇā cārittavārittasīlasamādhivipassanāti ayaṃ paṭipattisaddhammo nāma. Navalokuttaradhammo adhigamasaddhammo nāma. So sabbopi yasmā sikkhāpadapaññattiyā sati bhikkhū sikkhāpadañca tassa vibhaṅgañca tadatthajotanatthaṃ aññañca buddhavacanaṃ pariyāpuṇanti, yathāpaññattañca paṭipajjamānā paṭipattiṃ pūretvā paṭipattiyā adhigantabbaṃ lokuttaradhammaṃ adhigacchanti, tasmā sikkhāpadapaññattiyā saddhammo ciraṭṭhitiko hoti. Tena vuttaṃ – “saddhammaṭṭhitiyā”ti.

For the stability of the good Dhamma: the good Dhamma is threefold: the good Dhamma of learning, the good Dhamma of practice, and the good Dhamma of realization. Herein, the entire word of the Buddha is called the good Dhamma of learning. The thirteen ascetic practices, the virtue of conduct and the virtue of avoidance, concentration, and insight—this is called the good Dhamma of practice. The nine supramundane Dhammas are called the good Dhamma of realization. Because, when the laying down of the training rules exists, monks learn the training rule, its analysis, and other words of the Buddha to illuminate its meaning; and, practicing in accordance with what was laid down, they fulfill the practice and through practice realize the supramundane Dhamma that is to be realized. Therefore, with the laying down of the training rules, the good Dhamma is long-lasting. Hence it is said: 'for the stability of the good Dhamma.'

Vinayānuggahāyāti sikkhāpadapaññattiyā sati saṃvaravinayo, pahānavinayo, samathavinayo, paññattivinayoti catubbidho vinayo anuggahito hoti sūpatthambhito. Tena vuttaṃ – “vinayānuggahāyā”ti.

For the support of the Vinaya: when the laying down of a training rule exists, the fourfold Vinaya—the Vinaya of restraint, the Vinaya of abandonment, the Vinaya of calming, and the Vinaya of prescription—is supported and well-established. Hence it is said: 'for the support of the Vinaya.'

2. Pātimokkhaṭṭhapanāsuttavaṇṇanā

2. Explanation of the Discourse on the Suspension of the Pātimokkha

32. Dutiye pārājikoti pārājikāpattiṃ āpanno. Pārājikakathā [P 5.0034] vippakatā hotīti “asukapuggalo pārājikaṃ āpanno nu kho no”ti [M 3.0310] evaṃ kathā ārabhitvā aniṭṭhāpitā hoti. Esa nayo sabbattha.

32. In the second, pārājiko means: one who has committed a pārājika offense. Pārājikakathā vippakatā hoti means: a discussion has been started—'Has such-and-such a person committed a pārājika offense or not?'—but has not been concluded. This method applies everywhere.

3. Ubbāhikāsuttavaṇṇanā

3. Explanation of the Discourse on Inquiry

33. Tatiye ubbāhikāyāti sampattaadhikaraṇaṃ vūpasametuṃ saṅghato ubbāhitvā uddharitvā gahaṇatthāya. Vinaye kho pana ṭhito hotīti vinayalakkhaṇe patiṭṭhito hoti. Asaṃhīroti na aññassa [V 3.0308] vacanamatteneva attano laddhiṃ vissajjeti. Paṭibaloti kāyabalenapi ñāṇabalenapi samannāgato. Saññāpetunti jānāpetuṃ. Paññāpetunti sampajānāpetuṃ. Nijjhāpetunti olokāpetuṃ. Pekkhatunti passāpetuṃ. Pasādetunti sañjātapasādaṃ kātuṃ. Adhikaraṇanti vivādādhikaraṇādicatubbidhaṃ. Adhikaraṇasamudayanti vivādamūlādikaṃ adhikaraṇakārakaṃ. Adhikaraṇanirodhanti adhikaraṇānaṃ vūpasamaṃ. Adhikaraṇanirodhagāminiṃ paṭipadanti sattavidhaadhikaraṇasamathaṃ.

33. In the third, ubbāhikāya means: for the purpose of taking up, having extracted and lifted out from the Saṅgha, in order to settle a legal issue that has arisen. Vinaye kho pana ṭhito hoti means: is established in the characteristic of the Vinaya. Asaṃhīro means: one does not abandon one's own view merely by the word of another. Paṭibalo means: endowed with both physical strength and the strength of wisdom. Saññāpetuṃ means: to cause to know. Paññāpetuṃ means: to cause to know well and in detail. Nijjhāpetuṃ means: to cause to look into. Pekkhetuṃ means: to cause to behold. Pasādetuṃ means: to cause confidence to arise. Adhikaraṇaṃ means: the four kinds of legal issues, beginning with disputes. Adhikaraṇasamudayaṃ means: that which causes a legal issue, such as the root of a dispute. Adhikaraṇanirodhaṃ means: the settling of legal issues. Adhikaraṇanirodhagāminiṃ paṭipadaṃ means: the sevenfold settlement of legal issues.

4. Upasampadāsuttavaṇṇanā

4. Explanation of the Upasampadā Discourse

34. Catutthe anabhiratinti ukkaṇṭhitabhāvaṃ. Vūpakāsetunti vinetuṃ. Adhisīleti uttamasīle. Cittapaññāsupi eseva nayo.

34. In the fourth, anabhiratiṃ means: the state of discontent. Vūpakāsetuṃ means: to remove. Adhisīle means: in supreme virtue. This same method also applies to mind and wisdom.

7. Saṅghabhedasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Explanation of the Discourse on Division in the Saṅgha

37. Sattame vatthūhīti kāraṇehi. Avakassantīti [P 5.0035] parisaṃ ākaḍḍhanti vijaṭenti ekamantaṃ ussārenti. Apakassantīti ativiya ākaḍḍhanti, yathā visaṃsaṭṭhā honti, evaṃ karonti. Āveni kammāni karontīti visuṃ saṅghakammāni karonti.

37. In the seventh, vatthūhi means: by means of causes. Avakassanti means: they pull the assembly, scatter them, and move them to one side. Apakassanti means: they pull excessively; they act in such a way that they stand apart. Āvenikammāni karonti means: they perform separate Saṅgha acts.

9-10. Ānandasuttadvayavaṇṇanā

9-10. Explanation of the Two Ānanda Discourses

39-40. Navame kappaṭṭhikanti āyukappaṃ nirayamhi ṭhitikāraṇaṃ. Kibbisaṃ pasavatīti pāpaṃ paṭilabhati. Āpāyikoti apāyagamanīyo. Nerayikoti niraye nibbattanako. Vaggaratoti bhedarato. Yogakkhemā [M 3.0311] padhaṃsatīti yogehi khemato arahattato dhaṃsati vigacchati. Dasame anuggahoti aññamaññassa saṅgahānuggaho. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

In the ninth, kappaṭṭhiyaṃ means: the cause for remaining in hell for a life-aeon. Kibbisaṃ pasavati means: one acquires evil. Āpāyiko means: one who is bound for the plane of misery. Nerayiko means: one who is reborn in hell. Vaggarato means: delighting in division. Yogakkhemā padhaṃsati means: one falls away, one departs from arahantship, which is security from the yokes. In the tenth, anuggaho means: mutual support and assistance. The rest everywhere has an obvious meaning.

Upālivaggo catuttho.

The fourth chapter is the Upāli Vagga.

5. Akkosavaggo

5. The Akkosavagga (Division on Abuse)

4. Kusinārasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Explanation of the Kusināra Discourse

44. Pañcamassa [V 3.0309] catutthe kusinārāyanti evaṃnāmake nagare. Devatānaṃ atthāya baliṃ haranti etthāti baliharaṇo, tasmiṃ baliharaṇe. Acchiddena appaṭimaṃsenātiādīsu yena kenacideva pahaṭo vā hoti, vejjakammādīni vā katāni, tassa kāyasamācāro upacikādīhi khāyitatālapaṇṇaṃ viya chiddo ca, paṭimasituṃ yattha katthaci gahetvā ākaḍḍhituṃ sakkuṇeyyatāya paṭimaṃso ca hoti, viparīto acchiddo appaṭimaṃso nāma. Vacīsamācāro pana musāvādaomasavādapesuññaamūlakānuddhaṃsanādīhi [P 5.0036] chiddo sappaṭimaṃso ca hoti, viparīto acchiddo appaṭimaṃso. Mettaṃ nu kho me cittanti palibodhaṃ chinditvā kammaṭṭhānabhāvanānuyogena adhigataṃ me mettacittaṃ. Anāghātanti āghātavirahitaṃ, vikkhambhanena vihatāghātanti attho. Kattha vuttanti idaṃ sikkhāpadaṃ kismiṃ nagare vuttaṃ.

44. In the fourth (sutta) of the fifth vagga, `Kusinārāyaṃ` means: in the city with this name, Kusināra. Because here (`ettha`) they bring (`haranti`) offerings (`baliṃ`) for the sake of (`atthāya`) devas (`devatānaṃ`), it is called `baliharaṇa` (an offering ground); `tasmiṃ baliharaṇe` means in that offering ground. In phrases such as `acchiddena appaṭimaṃsena`: if one has been struck by anyone whatsoever, or medical treatments and so on have been performed, that monk's bodily conduct is `chidda` (perforated) like a palm leaf eaten by termites, and `paṭimaṃsa` (graspable) because it is possible to grasp and pull it anywhere. The opposite is called `acchidda` (unperforated) and `appaṭimaṃsa` (ungraspable). Verbal conduct, however, through false speech, harsh speech, slander, baseless accusation, and so on, becomes `chidda` (perforated) and `sappaṭimaṃsa` (graspable). The opposite is `acchidda` (unperforated) and `appaṭimaṃsa` (ungraspable). `Mettaṃ nu kho me cittaṃ` means: having cut off hindrances, this mind of loving-kindness has been attained by me through application to the development of a meditation subject. `Anāghātaṃ` means free from resentment; the meaning is that resentment has been dispelled by suppression. `Kattha vuttaṃ` means: in which city was this training rule spoken?

Kālena vakkhāmītiādīsu eko ekaṃ okāsaṃ kāretvā codento kālena vadati nāma. Saṅghamajjhe vā gaṇamajjhe vā salākaggayāgaggavitakkamāḷakabhikkhācāramaggaāsanasālādīsu vā upaṭṭhākehi parivāritakkhaṇe vā codento akālena vadati nāma. Tacchena vadanto bhūtena vadati nāma. “Daharamahallakaparisāvacarakapaṃsukūlikadhammakathikapatirūpaṃ tava ida”nti vadanto pharusena vadati nāma. Kāraṇanissitaṃ pana katvā, “bhante mahallakattha, parisāvacarakattha, paṃsukūlikattha, dhammakathikatthapatirūpaṃ tumhākamida”nti vadanto [M 3.0312] saṇhena vadati nāma. Kāraṇanissitaṃ katvā vadanto atthasaṃhitena vadati nāma. Mettacitto vakkhāmi no dosantaroti mettacittaṃ paccupaṭṭhapetvā vakkhāmi, na duṭṭhacitto hutvā.

In phrases such as `Kālena vakkhāmi`: when one monk, having created an opportunity, reproves another, this is called speaking at the proper time. Reproving someone in the midst of the Saṅgha or a group; or in places such as a ticket-food distribution point, a gruel distribution point, a hall for discussion, the alms-round path, or a sitting hall; or at a moment when surrounded by attendants, is called speaking at an improper time. One who speaks truthfully (`tacchena`) is said to speak with what is factual (`bhūtena`). Speaking harshly (`pharusena`) is to say, for example: 'Oh, great elder! Oh, you who frequent assemblies! Oh, you rag-robe wearer! Oh, you Dhamma-preacher! Is this conduct suitable for you?' However, having based it on a suitable reason, to speak thus: 'Venerable sir, you are an elder, you frequent assemblies, you are a rag-robe wearer, you are a Dhamma-preacher. Is this suitable for you?'—this is called speaking gently (`saṇhena`). Speaking based on a reason is called speaking what is beneficial (`atthasaṃhitena`). `Mettacitto vakkhāmi no dosantaro` means: having established a mind of loving-kindness, I will speak; I will not speak with a mind corrupted by hatred.

5. Rājantepurappavesanasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Explanation of the Rājantepurappavesana Sutta

45. Pañcame kataṃ vā karissanti vāti methunavītikkamaṃ kariṃsu vā karissanti vā. Ratananti maṇiratanādīsu yaṃkiñci. Patthetīti māretuṃ icchati. Hatthisambādhanti [P 5.0037] hatthīhi sambādhaṃ[V 3.0310]. Hatthisammaddanti vā pāṭho, tassattho – hatthīhi sammaddo etthāti hatthisammaddaṃ. Sesesupi eseva nayo. Rajanīyāni rūpasaddagandharasaphoṭṭhabbānīti etāni rāgajanakāni rūpādīni tattha paripūrāni honti.

45. In the fifth (sutta), “whether they have done or will do” means they have committed or will commit sexual misconduct. “Jewel” means any precious gem such as a ruby. `Pattheti` means wishes to kill. “Elephant-crowded” means crowded with elephants. Alternatively, there is the reading “elephant-trampled,” and its meaning is: here there is trampling by elephants, thus “elephant-trampled.” The same method applies to the remaining cases. “Desirable sights, sounds, smells, tastes, and tactile sensations” means these sense objects that generate lust are abundant there.

6. Sakkasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Sakka Sutta

46. Chaṭṭhe sokasabhayeti sokena sabhaye. Sokabhayeti vā pāṭho, ayamevattho. Dutiyapadepi eseva nayo. Yena kenaci kammaṭṭhānenāti kasivaṇijjādikammesu yena kenaci kammena. Anāpajja akusalanti kiñci akusalaṃ anāpajjitvā. Nibbiseyyāti uppādeyya ācineyya. Dakkhoti cheko. Uṭṭhānasampannoti uṭṭhānavīriyena samannāgato. Alaṃ vacanāyāti yutto vacanāya. Ekantasukhappaṭisaṃvedī vihareyyāti ekantameva kāyikacetasikasukhaṃ ñāṇena paṭisaṃvedento vihareyya. Aniccāti hutvā abhāvato. Tucchāti sārarahitā. Musāti niccasubhasukhā viya khāyamānāpi tathā na hontīti musā. Mosadhammāti nassanasabhāvā. Tasmā te paṭicca dukkhaṃ uppajjatīti sandasseti. Idha [P 5.0038] pana voti ettha vo ti nipātamattaṃ. Apaṇṇakaṃ vā sotāpannoti avirādhitaṃ ekaṃsena sotāpanno vā hoti. Sopi jhānaṃ nibbatteti, brahmalokaṃ vā gantvā chasu vā kāmasaggesu ekantasukhappaṭisaṃvedī hutvā vihareyya. Imasmiṃ sutte satthā aṭṭhaṅguposathassa guṇaṃ kathesi.

46. In the sixth (sutta), “with sorrow and fear” means fearful on account of sorrow. Alternatively, the reading is “fear of sorrow,” and the meaning is the same. The same principle applies to the second word. “By whatever means of livelihood” means by any work such as farming or trading. “Without incurring unwholesomeness” means not having incurred any unwholesomeness. “Would accumulate” means would produce and increase. “Skillful” means clever. “Endowed with diligence” means endowed with energetic effort. “Fit to be spoken of” means suitable to be spoken of. “Would dwell experiencing exclusive happiness” means dwelling experiencing exclusively bodily and mental happiness through wisdom. “Impermanent” means because of non-existence after having been. “Empty” means devoid of substance. “False” means although they appear as permanent, pleasant, and happy, they are not so; thus they are false. “Of a deceptive nature” means having a nature to perish. This shows that suffering arises dependent on them. Here, however, in “idha pana vo,” “vo” is merely a particle. “Or an assured stream-enterer” means one becomes, unfailingly and certainly, a stream-enterer. That person also generates jhāna; or, having gone to the Brahmā world or to the six sensual heavens, would dwell experiencing exclusive happiness. In this sutta, the Teacher spoke of the virtue of the eight-factored uposatha.

7. Mahālisuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Mahāli Sutta

47. Sattame [M 3.0313] micchāpaṇihitanti micchā ṭhapitaṃ. Adhammacariyāvisamacariyāti akusalakammapathavasena adhammacariyasaṅkhātā visamacariyā. Kusalakammapathavasena itarā veditabbā. Evamidha vaṭṭameva kathitaṃ.

47. In the seventh (sutta), “wrongly directed” means wrongly established. “Unrighteous conduct and uneven conduct” refers to uneven conduct, designated as unrighteous conduct, by way of the unwholesome courses of action. The others—righteous conduct and even conduct—should be understood by way of the wholesome courses of action. Thus, here, only this is taught.

8. Pabbajitaabhiṇhasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Pabbajitaabhiṇha Sutta

48. Aṭṭhame pabbajitenāti gharāvāsaṃ pahāya sāsane pabbajjaṃ upagatena. Abhiṇhanti abhikkhaṇaṃ punappunaṃ, paccavekkhitabbā oloketabbā sallakkhetabbā. Vevaṇṇiyanti vivaṇṇabhāvaṃ. Taṃ panetaṃ vevaṇṇiyaṃ duvidhaṃ hoti sarīravevaṇṇiyaṃ parikkhāravevaṇṇiyañca. Tattha kesamassuoropanena [V 3.0311] sarīravevaṇṇiyaṃ veditabbaṃ. Pubbe pana nānāvirāgāni sukhumavatthāni nivāsetvāpi nānaggarasabhojanaṃ suvaṇṇarajatabhājanesu bhuñjitvāpi sirigabbhe varasayanāsanesu nipajjitvāpi nisīditvāpi sappinavanītādīhi bhesajjaṃ katvāpi pabbajitakālato paṭṭhāya chinnasaṅghaṭitakasāvarasapītāni vatthāni nivāsetabbāni, ayapatte vā mattikapatte vā missakodano bhuñjitabbo, rukkhamūlādisenāsane muñjatiṇasantharaṇādīsu nipajjitabbaṃ[P 5.0039], cammakhaṇḍataṭṭikādīsu nisīditabbaṃ, pūtimuttādīhi bhesajjaṃ kattabbaṃ hoti. Evamettha parikkhāravevaṇṇiyaṃ veditabbaṃ. Evaṃ paccavekkhato kopo ca māno ca pahīyati.

48. In the eighth (sutta), `by one who has gone forth` means by one who has abandoned the household life and undertaken the going forth in the teaching. `Frequently` means constantly, again and again. `Should be reflected upon` means should be looked at, should be considered. `Uncomeliness` means the state of having a changed color. Furthermore, this uncomeliness is of two kinds: bodily uncomeliness and uncomeliness of requisites. Therein, bodily uncomeliness should be understood through the removal of hair and beard. Furthermore, previously, though one wore fine garments of various dyes, ate food of various excellent tastes in gold and silver vessels, lay down and sat on excellent beds and seats in splendid chambers, and made medicine with ghee, fresh butter, and so on, from the time of going forth, one must wear robes that are cut, patched, and have absorbed astringent dye; one must eat mixed rice in an iron bowl or a clay bowl; one must lie down in a dwelling such as the root of a tree on a spread of muñja grass and so on; one must sit on a piece of hide, a mat, and so on; and one must make medicine with putrid cow's urine and so on. Thus, herein, the uncomeliness of requisites should be understood. For one who reflects thus, anger and conceit are abandoned.

Parapaṭibaddhā me jīvikāti mayhaṃ paresu paṭibaddhā parāyattā catupaccayajīvikāti. Evaṃ paccavekkhato hi ājīvo parisujjhati, piṇḍapāto ca apacito hoti, catūsu paccayesu apaccavekkhitaparibhogo nāma na hoti. Añño me ākappo karaṇīyoti yo gihīnaṃ uraṃ abhinīharitvā gīvaṃ paggahetvā lalitenākārena aniyatapadavītihārena gamanākappo hoti, tato aññova ākappo mayā karaṇīyo, santindriyena santamānasena yugamattadassinā visamaṭṭhāne udakasakaṭeneva mandamitapadavītihārena hutvā gantabbanti paccavekkhitabbaṃ. Evaṃ paccavekkhato hi iriyāpatho sāruppo hoti, tisso sikkhā paripūrenti. Kaccinukhoti salakkhaṇe nipātasamudāyo. Attāti cittaṃ. Sīlato na upavadatīti [M 3.0314] aparisuddhaṃ te sīlanti sīlapaccayo na upavadati. Evaṃ paccavekkhato hi ajjhattaṃ hirī samuṭṭhāti, sā tīsu dvāresu saṃvaraṃ sādheti, tīsu dvāresu saṃvaro catupārisuddhisīlaṃ hoti, catupārisuddhisīle ṭhito vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā arahattaṃ gaṇhāti. Anuvicca viññū sabrahmacārīti paṇḍitā sabrahmacārino anuvicāretvā. Evaṃ paccavekkhato [P 5.0040] hi bahiddhā ottappaṃ saṇṭhāti, taṃ tīsu dvāresu saṃvaraṃ sādhetīti anantaranayeneva veditabbaṃ.

“My livelihood is dependent on others” means: my livelihood by means of the four requisites is connected to and dependent on others. Indeed, for one who reflects thus, one’s livelihood is purified, almsfood is respected, and there is no such thing as using the four requisites without reflection. “I must adopt a different demeanor” means: whereas the demeanor of householders is to walk with chest thrust out, neck held high, with a playful manner and an unsteady gait, a demeanor different from that must be adopted by me. It should be reflected: ‘One must go with peaceful faculties, a peaceful mind, looking the distance of a yoke, and with a slow, measured gait like a water-cart in an uneven place.’ Indeed, for one who reflects thus, one’s deportment becomes suitable, and the three trainings are fulfilled. `Kaccinu kho` is a collection of particles with its own characteristic. `Self` means the mind. `Does not reproach on account of virtue` means one's self does not reproach one with respect to virtue, saying, 'Your virtue is impure.' Indeed, for one who reflects thus, internal shame arises. That shame accomplishes restraint in the three doors. Restraint in the three doors is the virtue of fourfold purity. Established in the virtue of fourfold purity, having developed insight, one attains arahantship. `Having considered, the wise fellow practitioners` means the wise fellow practitioners, having investigated. For one who reflects thus, external fear of wrongdoing is established. That fear of wrongdoing accomplishes restraint in the three doors. This should be understood in the manner stated immediately before.

Nānābhāvo vinābhāvoti jātiyā nānābhāvo, maraṇena vinābhāvo. Evaṃ paccavekkhato hi tīsu dvāresu asaṃvutākāro nāma na hoti, maraṇassati sūpaṭṭhitā hoti. Kammassakomhītiādīsu kammaṃ mayhaṃ sakaṃ attano santakanti kammassakā. Kammena dātabbaṃ phalaṃ dāyaṃ, kammassa dāyaṃ kammadāyaṃ, taṃ ādīyāmīti kammadāyādo. Kammaṃ mayhaṃ yoni kāraṇanti kammayoni. Kammaṃ mayhaṃ bandhu ñātakoti kammabandhu. Kammaṃ mayhaṃ paṭisaraṇaṃ patiṭṭhāti kammapaṭisaraṇo. Tassa dāyādo bhavissāmīti tassa kammassa dāyādo tena dinnaphalaṃ paṭiggāhako [V 3.0312] bhavissāmi. Evaṃ kammassakataṃ pana paccavekkhato pāpakaraṇaṃ nāma na hoti. Kathaṃbhūtassa me rattindivā vītivattantīti kinnu kho me vattappaṭipattiṃ karontassa, udāhu akarontassa, buddhavacanaṃ sajjhāyantassa, udāhu asajjhāyantassa, yonisomanasikāre kammaṃ karontassa, udāhu akarontassāti kathaṃbhūtassa me rattindivā vītivattanti, parivattantīti attho. Evaṃ paccavekkhato hi appamādo paripūrati.

"Nānābhāvo vinābhāvo" means: nānābhāvo is becoming different through birth; vinābhāvo is separation through death. Truly, for one who reflects thus, there is no such thing as an unrestrained manner at the three doors, and mindfulness of death is well established. In the passage beginning "Kammassakomhī," kammassakā means: kamma is my own, my personal property. Kammadāyādo means: I am an heir to kamma, which is the fruit to be given by kamma; I receive that which is the heritage of kamma. Kammayoni means: kamma is my origin, my cause. Kammabandhu means: kamma is my relative, my kin. Kammapaṭisaraṇo means: kamma is my refuge, my support. "Tassa dāyādo bhavissāmi" means: I will be the heir of that kamma, a recipient of the fruit given by it. For one who reflects on the ownership of kamma in this way, there is no such thing as doing evil. "How do my days and nights pass?" means: For me, being of what sort, do the days and nights pass? Is it for me who is practicing the duties, or not practicing them? Is it for me who is reciting the word of the Buddha, or not reciting it? Is it for me who is doing the work in wise attention, or not doing it? In this manner, how do my days and nights pass by, how do they revolve? This is the meaning. Truly, for one who reflects thus, heedfulness is perfected.

Suññāgāre abhiramāmīti vivittokāse sabbiriyāpathesu ekakova hutvā kacci nu kho abhiramāmīti attho. Evaṃ paccavekkhato kāyaviveko paripūrati. Uttarimanussadhammoti uttarimanussānaṃ ukkaṭṭhamanussabhūtānaṃ jhāyīnañceva ariyānañca jhānādidhammo, dasakusalakammapathasaṅkhātamanussadhammato vā uttaritaro visiṭṭhataro dhammo me mama santāne atthi nu kho, santi nu khoti attho. Alamariyañāṇadassanavisesoti mahaggatalokuttarapaññā pajānanaṭṭhena ñāṇaṃ, cakkhunā diṭṭhamiva dhammaṃ paccakkhakaraṇato dassanaṭṭhena dassananti ñāṇadassanaṃ, ariyaṃ visuddhaṃ uttamaṃ ñāṇadassananti [M 3.0315] ariyañāṇadassanaṃ, alaṃ pariyattakaṃ kilesaviddhaṃsanasamatthaṃ ariyañāṇadassanamettha, assa vāti alamariyañāṇadassano, jhānādibhedo uttarimanussadhammo alamariyañāṇadassano ca so viseso cāti alamariyañāṇadassanaviseso. Atha vā tameva kilesaviddhaṃsanasamatthaṃ visuddhaṃ ñāṇadassanameva visesoti alamariyañāṇadassanaviseso vā. Adhigatoti paṭiladdho me atthi nu kho. Sohanti paṭiladdhaviseso so ahaṃ. Pacchime kāleti maraṇamañce nipannakāle. Puṭṭhoti sabrahmacārīhi adhigataguṇavisesaṃ pucchito. Na maṅku bhavissāmīti patitakkhandho nittejo na hessāmīti. Evaṃ paccavekkhantassa hi moghakālakiriyā nāma na hoti.

"I delight in an empty abode" means: In a secluded place, in all postures, being quite alone, do I indeed delight? This is the meaning. For one who reflects thus, physical seclusion is fulfilled. "A superhuman state" means: the state of jhānas and so on belonging to meditators and noble ones, who are superhuman, exalted human beings; or it is a state higher and more distinguished than the human state which is reckoned as the ten wholesome courses of action. The meaning is: "Is there such a state in my continuity?" "A special distinction in knowledge and vision worthy of the noble ones": knowledge and vision is knowledge in the sense of knowing the great and supramundane wisdom, and vision in the sense of seeing, of making the Dhamma directly perceptible as if seen with the eye. Noble knowledge and vision is knowledge and vision that is noble, pure, and supreme. One who possesses this adequate noble knowledge and vision, which is capable of destroying the defilements, is alamariyañāṇadassano. The superhuman state, which is differentiated by jhāna and so on, is a special distinction, and it is also knowledge and vision worthy of the noble ones; thus it is alamariyañāṇadassanaviseso. Alternatively, that very pure knowledge and vision capable of destroying the defilements is itself the special distinction. "Attained" means: "Have I obtained it?" "I am one who..." means: "I am one who has obtained that distinction." "At the final time" means: at the time of lying on one's deathbed. "When asked" means: when asked by my companions in the holy life about the special qualities I have attained. "I will not be troubled" means: I will not be downcast and dispirited. Indeed, for one who reflects thus, there is no such thing as spending one's time in vain.

9-10. Sarīraṭṭhadhammasuttādivaṇṇanā

9-10. Commentary on the Discourse on the States of the Body, etc.

49-50. Navame [P 5.0041] ponobbhavikoti punabbhavanibbattako. Bhavasaṅkhāroti bhavasaṅkharaṇakammaṃ. Imasmiṃ sutte vaṭṭameva kathitaṃ. Dasame sīlabāhusaccavīriyasatipaññā lokiyalokuttarāmissikā kathitā. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

In the ninth sutta, "ponobbhaviko" means: that which brings about a new existence again. "Bhavasaṅkhāro" means: the kamma that conditions existence. In this sutta, only the round of existence and that which is free from it are taught. In the tenth sutta, morality, great learning, energy, mindfulness, and wisdom—mixed with the mundane and supramundane—are taught. The rest has a clear meaning everywhere.

Akkosavaggo pañcamo.

The Fifth Chapter: On Abuse.

Paṭhamapaṇṇāsakaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.

The First Fifty is Completed.

2. Dutiyapaṇṇāsakaṃ

2. The Second Fifty

(6) 1. Sacittavaggo

(6) 1. The Chapter on One's Own Mind

1-4. Sacittasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-4. Commentary on the Sacittasutta and others

51-54. Dutiyassa [V 3.0313][M 3.0316] paṭhame sacittapariyāyakusaloti attano cittavārakusalo. Rajanti āgantukaupakkilesaṃ. Aṅgaṇanti tatthajātakaaṅgakāḷatilakādiṃ. Āsavānaṃ khayāyāti arahattatthāya. Tatiye paṭibhānenāti vacanasaṇṭhānena. Catutthe [P 5.0042] adhipaññādhammavipassanāyāti saṅkhārapariggāhakavipassanāya.

51-54. In the second, in the first: "skilled in the ways of one's own mind" means skilled regarding the course of one's own mind. "Dust" refers to adventitious defilements. "Stain" refers to things born on the body, such as dark moles, freckles, and so on. "For the destruction of the taints" means for the sake of arahantship. In the third, "by eloquence" means by the elegance of speech. In the fourth, "by the higher wisdom of insight into phenomena" means by insight that comprehends formations.

8. Mūlakasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Mūlaka Sutta

58. Aṭṭhame amatogadhāti ettha saupādisesā nibbānadhātu kathitā, nibbānapariyosānāti ettha anupādisesā. Anupādisesaṃ pattassa hi sabbe dhammā pariyosānappattā nāma honti. Sesapadāni heṭṭhā vuttatthāneva.

58. In the eighth, by "plunged into the deathless" the Nibbāna-element with residue remaining is stated here; by "ending in Nibbāna" the Nibbāna-element without residue remaining is stated here. Indeed, for one who has attained the Nibbāna-element without residue remaining, all phenomena are said to have reached their final end. The remaining terms have the same meaning as stated before.

9. Pabbajjāsuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Pabbajjā Sutta

59. Navame tasmāti yasmā evaṃ aparicitacittassa sāmaññattho na sampajjati, tasmā. Yathāpabbajjāparicitañca no cittaṃ bhavissatīti yathā pabbajjānurūpena paricitaṃ. Ye hi keci pabbajanti nāma, sabbe te arahattaṃ patthetvā. Tasmā yaṃ cittaṃ arahattādhigamatthāya paricitaṃ vaḍḍhitaṃ, taṃ yathāpabbajjāparicitaṃ nāmāti veditabbaṃ. Evarūpaṃ pana cittaṃ bhavissatīti sikkhitabbaṃ. Lokassa samañca visamañcāti sattalokassa sucaritaduccaritāni. Lokassa bhavañca vibhavañcāti tassa vaḍḍhiñca vināsañca, tathā sampattiñca vipattiñca. Lokassa [V 3.0314] samudayañca atthaṅgamañcāti pana saṅkhāralokaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ, khandhānaṃ nibbattiñca bhedañcāti attho.

59. In the ninth, "therefore" means: because for one with an unpracticed mind the goal of recluseship is not accomplished, therefore. "And our mind will be practiced in accordance with the going forth" means practiced in a way suitable to the going forth. For indeed, whoever goes forth, all of them are said to go forth having aspired to arahantship. Therefore, the mind that is practiced and developed for the sake of attaining arahantship should be known as "practiced in accordance with the going forth." One should train thinking, "Such a mind will come to be." "The world's even and uneven" means the good and bad conduct of the world of beings. "The world's becoming and passing away" means its prosperity and adversity. But "the world's arising and ceasing" is said with reference to the world of formations; the meaning is the arising and dissolution of the aggregates.

10. Girimānandasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Girimānanda Sutta

60. Dasame [M 3.0317] anukampaṃ upādāyāti girimānandatthere anukampaṃ paṭicca. Cakkhurogotiādayo vatthuvasena veditabbā. Nibbattitappasādānañhi rogo nāma natthi. Kaṇṇarogoti [P 5.0043] bahikaṇṇe rogo. Pināsoti bahināsikāya rogo. Nakhasāti nakhehi vilekhitaṭṭhāne rogo. Pittasamuṭṭhānāti pittasamuṭṭhitā. Te kira dvattiṃsa honti. Semhasamuṭṭhānādīsupi eseva nayo. Utupariṇāmajāti utupariṇāmena accuṇhātisītena uppajjanakarogā. Visamaparihārajāti aticiraṭṭhānanisajjādinā visamaparihārena jātā. Opakkamikāti vadhabandhanādinā upakkamena jātā. Kammavipākajāti balavakammavipākasambhūtā. Santanti rāgādisantatāya santaṃ. Atappakaṭṭhena paṇītaṃ. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

60. In the tenth, 'out of compassion' means on account of compassion for the Elder Girimānanda. 'Eye disease' and so on should be understood by way of their physical basis. Indeed, for the sensitive matter produced by kamma, there is no such thing as disease. 'Ear disease' means a disease in the outer ear. 'Nose disease' means a disease in the outer nose. 'Nail disease' means a disease in a place scratched by nails. 'Arisen from bile' means originated from bile. It is said that they are thirty-two. This same method applies to those arisen from phlegm and so on. 'Born of seasonal change' means diseases that arise from a change of season, from excessive heat or cold. 'Born of imbalanced conduct' means those born from imbalanced conduct, such as standing or sitting for too long. 'Caused by assault' means those born from an assault by others, such as killing or binding. 'Born of kamma-result' means those produced from the result of powerful kamma. 'Peaceful' means peaceful due to the pacification of lust and so on. 'Sublime' due to its nature of being unsurpassed. The rest has a clear meaning everywhere.

Sacittavaggo paṭhamo.

The First Chapter on One's Own Mind.

(7) 2. Yamakavaggo

(7) 2. The Chapter on Pairs

1. Avijjāsuttādivaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Discourse on Ignorance, and so forth

61-62. Dutiyassa paṭhame sāhāranti sapaccayaṃ. Vijjāvimuttinti phalañāṇañceva sesasampayuttadhamme ca. Bojjhaṅgāti maggabojjhaṅgā. Dutiye bhavataṇhāyāti bhavapatthanāya. Evaṃ dvīsupi suttesu vaṭṭameva kathitaṃ, vaṭṭañcettha paṭhame sutte avijjāmūlakaṃ vaṭṭaṃ kathitaṃ, dutiye taṇhāmūlakaṃ.

61-62. In the first [sutta] of the second [vagga]: `sāhāraṃ` means with a condition. `Vijjāvimutti` refers to both fruition-knowledge and the remaining associated states. `Bojjhaṅgā` means the path enlightenment factors. In the second [sutta], `bhavataṇhāya` means longing for existence. Thus, in both suttas, the cycle of existence is taught. And here, in the first sutta, the cycle rooted in ignorance is taught; in the second, that rooted in craving.

3-4. Niṭṭhaṅgatasuttādivaṇṇanā

3-4. Commentary on the Niṭṭhaṅgata Sutta, and so forth

63-64. Tatiye [V 3.0315][P 5.0044] niṭṭhaṃ gatāti nibbematikā. Idha niṭṭhāti imasmiṃyeva loke parinibbānaṃ. Idha vihāyāti imaṃ lokaṃ vijahitvā suddhāvāsabrahmalokaṃ. Catutthe aveccappasannāti acalappasādena sampannā. Sotāpannāti ariyamaggasotaṃ āpannā.

63-64. In the third [sutta]: `niṭṭhaṃ gatā` means free from doubt. `Idha niṭṭhā` means final Nibbāna in this very world. `Idha vihāya` means having abandoned this world for the Suddhāvāsa Brahmā world. In the fourth [sutta]: `aveccappasannā` means endowed with unwavering confidence. `Sotāpannā` means those who have reached the stream of the noble path.

5-7. Paṭhamasukhasuttādivaṇṇanā

5-7. Commentary on the First Discourse on Happiness, and so forth

65-67. Pañcame [M 3.0318] vaṭṭamūlakaṃ sukhadukkhaṃ pucchitaṃ, chaṭṭhe sāsanamūlakaṃ. Sattame naḷakapānanti atīte bodhisattassa ovāde ṭhatvā vānarayūthena naḷehi udakassa pītaṭṭhāne māpitattā evaṃladdhanāmo nigamo. Tuṇhībhūtaṃ tuṇhībhūtanti yaṃ yaṃ disaṃ anuviloketi, tattha tattha tuṇhībhūtameva. Anuviloketvāti tato tato viloketvā. Piṭṭhi me āgilāyatīti kasmā āgilāyati? Bhagavato hi cha vassāni mahāpadhānaṃ padahantassa mahantaṃ kāyadukkhaṃ ahosi, athassa aparabhāge mahallakakāle piṭṭhivāto uppajji. Upādinnakasarīrassa ṭhānanisajjādīhi appamattakena ābādhena na sakkā kenaci bhavituṃ. Taṃ gahetvāpi therassa okāsakaraṇatthaṃ evamāha. Saṅghāṭiṃ paññāpetvā ekamante patirūpaṭṭhāne paññattassa kappiyamañcassa upari attharitvā.

65-67. In the fifth [sutta], happiness and suffering rooted in the cycle of existence was asked about; in the sixth, that rooted in the teaching. In the seventh, `Naḷakapānaṃ` is a market town that received its name because it was created at the place where, in the past, a troop of monkeys, abiding by the Bodhisatta's instruction, drank water with reeds. `Tuṇhībhūtaṃ tuṇhībhūtaṃ` means: whichever direction he looks, in that very direction it is silent. `Anuviloketvā` means having looked here and there. As for 'My back is aching': why does it ache? For the Blessed One, while striving the great striving for six years, there was great bodily suffering. Then, in a later part of his life, in old age, a wind-illness in the back arose. For one with a body produced by clinging, it is not possible to be without some minor ailment arising from standing, sitting, and so on. Taking this into account, he spoke thus in order to give the Elder an opportunity. `Saṅghāṭiṃ paññāpetvā` means having spread it on top of an allowable couch that had been prepared in a suitable place to one side.

9-10. Kathāvatthusuttadvayavaṇṇanā

9-10. Commentary on the Two Discourses on Topics of Discussion

69-70. Navame tiracchānakathanti aniyyānikattā saggamokkhamaggānaṃ tiracchānabhūtaṃ kathaṃ. Tattha rājānaṃ ārabbha “mahāsammato mandhātā dhammāsoko [P 5.0045] evaṃmahānubhāvo”tiādinā nayena pavattakathā rājakathā. Esa nayo corakathādīsu. Tesu “asuko rājā abhirūpo dassanīyo”tiādinā gehasitakathāva tiracchānakathā hoti, “sopi nāma evaṃmahānubhāvo khayaṃ gato”ti evaṃ pavattā pana kammaṭṭhānabhāve tiṭṭhati. Coresupi “mūladevo evaṃmahānubhāvo, meghadevo evaṃmahānubhāvo”ti tesaṃ kammaṃ paṭicca “aho sūrā”ti gehasitakathāva tiracchānakathā. Yuddhesupi bhāratayuddhādīsu “asukena asuko evaṃ mārito evaṃ viddho”ti kammassādavaseneva kathā tiracchānakathā, “tepi nāma khayaṃ gatā”ti evaṃ pavattā pana sabbattha kammaṭṭhānameva [V 3.0316] hoti. Apica annādīsu “evaṃ vaṇṇavantaṃ rasavantaṃ phassasampannaṃ khādimha bhuñjimha pivimha paribhuñjimhā”ti kāmarasassādavasena kathetuṃ na vaṭṭati, sātthakaṃ pana katvā “pubbe evaṃ vaṇṇādisampannaṃ annaṃ pānaṃ vatthaṃ yānaṃ mālaṃ gandhaṃ sīlavantānaṃ adamha, cetiyaṃ pūjimhā”ti kathetuṃ vaṭṭati.

69-70. In the ninth [sutta], 'animal talk' (`tiracchānakathaṃ`) is talk that is contrary to the paths to heaven and liberation because it is not conducive to release. In this context, talk concerning a king—such as, 'Mahāsammata, Mandhātā, and Dhammāsoka were of such great power'—is 'royal talk.' This same principle applies to talk about thieves and so on. Among these, talk based on household life, such as, 'Such-and-such a king is handsome and pleasing to see,' is 'animal talk.' However, a reflection that proceeds thus, 'Even one of such great power has come to destruction,' stands as a basis for meditation. Regarding thieves, too, talk based on household life, such as, 'Mūladeva was of such great power, Meghadeva was of such great power,' and in reference to their deeds, 'Ah, how brave!' is 'animal talk.' In talk about battles, too, such as the Bhārata war, talk that proceeds from enjoyment of the action, such as, 'So-and-so was killed by so-and-so in this way; he was struck in that way,' is 'animal talk.' But a reflection that proceeds thus, 'They, too, have come to destruction,' serves as a basis for meditation in all cases. Furthermore, concerning food and so on, it is not proper to speak from sensual enjoyment, saying, 'We ate, consumed, drank, and used food of such color, taste, and pleasant touch.' Rather, making it meaningful, it is proper to say, 'In the past, we gave food, drink, clothing, vehicles, garlands, and perfumes of such fine quality to the virtuous, and we made offerings to the cetiya.'

Ñātikathādīsupi “amhākaṃ ñātakā sūrā samatthā”ti vā “pubbe mayaṃ evaṃ vicitrehi yānehi vicarimhā”ti vā assādavasena vattuṃ na vaṭṭati[M 3.0319], sātthakaṃ pana katvā “tepi no ñātakā khayaṃ gatā”ti vā “pubbe mayaṃ evarūpā upāhanā saṅghassa adamhā”ti vā kathetabbaṃ. Gāmakathāpi suniviṭṭhadunniviṭṭhasubhikkhadubbhikkhādivasena [P 5.0046] vā “asukagāmavāsino sūrā samatthā”ti vā evaṃ assādavaseneva na vaṭṭati, sātthakaṃ pana katvā “saddhā pasannā”ti vā “khayavayaṃ gatā”ti vā vattuṃ vaṭṭati. Nigamanagarajanapadakathāsupi eseva nayo.

In talk about relatives and so on, it is not proper to speak with delight, saying, “Our relatives are brave and capable,” or “In the past, we traveled in such splendid vehicles.” Instead, making it meaningful, one should speak thus: “Those relatives of ours have passed away,” or “In the past, we gave such footwear to the Saṅgha.” Similarly, in village talk, it is not proper to speak with delight about whether a village is well-established or poorly-established, experiencing good harvests or bad harvests, and so forth, or to say, “The residents of such-and-such village are brave and capable.” Instead, making it meaningful, one should speak thus: “They have faith and are confident,” or “They have come to ruin and destruction.” The same principle applies to discussions about market towns, cities, and districts.

Itthikathāpi vaṇṇasaṇṭhānādīni paṭicca assādavasena na vaṭṭati, “saddhā pasannā khayaṃ gatā”ti evameva vaṭṭati. Sūrakathāpi “nandimitto nāma yodho sūro ahosī”ti assādavaseneva na vaṭṭati, “saddho ahosi khayaṃ gato”ti evameva vaṭṭati. Surākathanti pāḷiyaṃ pana anekavidhaṃ majjakathaṃ assādavasena kathetuṃ na vaṭṭati, ādīnavavaseneva vattuṃ vaṭṭati. Visikhākathāpi “asukavisikhā suniviṭṭhā dunniviṭṭhā sūrā samatthā”ti assādavaseneva na vaṭṭati, “saddhā pasannā khayaṃ gatā”ti vaṭṭati. Kumbhaṭṭhānakathā nāma kūṭaṭṭhānakathā udakatitthakathā vuccati (dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.17; ma. ni. aṭṭha. 2.223; saṃ. ni. aṭṭha. 3.5.1080). Kumbhadāsikathā vā. Sāpi “pāsādikā naccituṃ gāyituṃ chekā”ti assādavasena na vaṭṭati, “saddhā pasannā”tiādinā nayeneva vaṭṭati.

Regarding talk about women, it is not proper to speak with delight in dependence on their beauty, form, and so on; it is only proper to speak thus: 'She had faith, was confident, and has passed away.' Regarding talk about heroes, too, it is not proper to speak with delight, saying, 'The warrior named Nandimitta was a hero'; it is only proper to speak thus: 'He was faithful and has passed away.' Regarding the Pāḷi term `surākathaṃ` (talk about liquor), it is not proper to speak about various kinds of intoxicating drinks with delight; it is proper to speak only in terms of their dangers. Regarding talk about streets, too, it is not proper to speak with delight, saying, 'Such-and-such a street is well-laid out or poorly-laid out; its people are brave and capable'; it is proper to say: 'They had faith, were confident, and have passed away.' 'Talk about a water-pot place' (`kumbhaṭṭhānakathā`) is said to be talk about a potter's place (`kūṭaṭṭhānakathā`) or talk about a watering place (`udakatitthakathā`). Or it is talk about a water-carrying slave girl (`kumbhadāsikathā`). Regarding her, too, it is not proper to speak with delight, saying, 'She is charming and skilled in dancing and singing'; it is proper to speak only according to the principle, 'She had faith, was confident,' and so on.

Pubbapetakathā nāma atītañātikathā. Tattha vattamānañātikathāsadisova vinicchayo. Nānattakathā nāma purimapacchimakathāvimuttā avasesā nānāsabhāvā tiracchānakathā. Lokakkhāyikā nāma “ayaṃ loko kena nimmito? Asukena nāma [P 5.0047] nimmito. Kāko seto aṭṭhīnaṃ setattā, balākā rattā lohitassa rattattā”tievamādikā lokāyatavitaṇḍasallāpakathā. Samuddakkhāyikā nāma kasmā samuddo sāgaroti. Sāgaradevena khatattā [V 3.0317] sāgaro, khato meti hatthamuddāya niveditattā samuddotievamādikā niratthakā samuddakkhāyanakathā. Bhavoti vuddhi, abhavoti hāni. Iti bhavo iti abhavoti yaṃ vā taṃ vā niratthakakāraṇaṃ vatvā pavattitakathā itibhavābhavakathā nāma.

“Talk about former departed ones” (pubbapetakathā) is talk concerning relatives who have passed away. In this matter, the decision is similar to that for talk concerning living relatives. “Miscellaneous talk” (nānattakathā) is the remaining animal talk of various natures, which is separate from talk of the past and future. “Cosmological talk” (lokakkhāyikā) is talk that speaks the words found in the Lokāyata and Vitaṇḍa treatises, such as: “By whom was this world created? It was created by so-and-so. A crow is white because of the whiteness of its bones; a heron is red because of the redness of its blood.” “Ocean talk” (samuddakkhāyikā) is useless talk proclaiming about the ocean, such as when asked, “Why is the ocean called ‘sāgara’?” answering, “It is called ‘sāgara’ because it was dug by King Sāgara,” and “It is called ‘samudda’ because it was made known by a hand gesture saying, ‘It was dug by me.’” ‘Bhava’ is growth; ‘abhava’ is decline. Talk that is made to proceed after stating some useless reason or other, saying, “Thus it is growth,” “Thus it is decline,” is called “talk about this growth and decline” (itibhavābhavakathā).

Tejasā tejanti attano tejasā tesaṃ tejaṃ. Pariyādiyeyyāthāti khepetvā gahetvā abhibhaveyyātha. Tatridaṃ vatthu – eko piṇḍapātiko [M 3.0320] mahātheraṃ pucchi – “bhante, tejasā tejaṃ pariyādiyamānā bhikkhū kiṃ karontī”ti. Thero āha – āvuso, kiñcideva ātape ṭhapetvā yathā chāyā heṭṭhā na otarati, uddhaṃyeva gacchati tathā karonti. Dasame pāsaṃsāni ṭhānānīti pasaṃsāvahāni kāraṇāni. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

“Tejasā tejaṃ” means: with one's own radiance, the radiance of those. “Pariyādiyeyyātha” means: having exhausted it, having taken it, you should overpower it. Herein, this is the story: A certain monk who undertook the ascetic practice of alms-food gathering asked a great elder, “Venerable sir, what do monks do who are exhausting the radiance of others with their own radiance?” The elder said, “Friend, having placed some object in the sun, they act in such a way that its shadow does not descend below but goes only upward.” In the tenth sutta, “pāsaṃsāni ṭhānāni” means causes that bring praise. The rest has an obvious meaning everywhere.

Yamakavaggo dutiyo.

The Yamaka Chapter, the Second.

(8) 3. Ākaṅkhavaggo

3. The Ākaṅkha Chapter

1. Ākaṅkhasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Ākaṅkha Sutta

71. Tatiyassa paṭhame sampannasīlāti paripuṇṇasīlā, sīlasamaṅgino vā hutvāti attho. Tattha dvīhi kāraṇehi sampannasīlatā hoti sīlavipattiyā ca [P 5.0048] ādīnavadassanena, sīlasampattiyā ca ānisaṃsadassanena. Tadubhayampi visuddhimagge (visuddhi. 1.9, 21) vitthāritaṃ. Tattha “sampannasīlā”ti ettāvatā kira bhagavā catupārisuddhisīlaṃ uddisitvā “pātimokkhasaṃvarasaṃvutā”ti iminā tattha jeṭṭhakasīlaṃ vitthāretvā dassesīti dīpavihāravāsī sumanatthero āha. Antevāsiko panassa tepiṭakacūḷanāgatthero āha – ubhayatthapi pātimokkhasaṃvarova bhagavatā vutto. Pātimokkhasaṃvaroyeva hi sīlaṃ, itarāni pana tīṇi sīlanti vuttaṭṭhānaṃ atthīti ananujānanto vatvā āha – indriyasaṃvaro nāma chadvārārakkhāmattakameva, ājīvapārisuddhi dhammena samena paccayuppattimattakaṃ, paccayasannissitaṃ paṭiladdhapaccaye [V 3.0318] idamatthanti paccavekkhitvā paribhuñjanamattakaṃ. Nippariyāyena pātimokkhasaṃvarova sīlaṃ. Yassa so bhinno, ayaṃ sīsacchinno viya puriso hatthapāde sesāni rakkhissatīti na vattabbo. Yassa pana so arogo, ayaṃ acchinnasīso viya puriso jīvitaṃ sesāni puna pākatikāni kātuṃ sakkoti. Tasmā “sampannasīlā”ti iminā pātimokkhasaṃvaraṃ uddisitvā “sampannapātimokkhā”ti tasseva vevacanaṃ vatvā taṃ vitthāretvā dassento pātimokkhasaṃvarasaṃvutātiādimāha. Tattha pātimokkhasaṃvarasaṃvuttātiādīni vuttatthāneva. Ākaṅkheyya ceti [M 3.0321] idaṃ kasmā āraddhanti? Sīlānisaṃsadassanatthaṃ[P 5.0049]. Sacepi acirapabbajitānaṃ vā duppaññānaṃ vā evamassa “bhagavā ‘sīlaṃ pūretha sīlaṃ pūrethā’ti vadati, ko nu kho sīlapūraṇe ānisaṃso, ko viseso, kā vaḍḍhī”ti tesaṃ dasa ānisaṃse dassetuṃ evamāha – “appeva nāma etaṃ sabrahmacārīnaṃ piyamanāpatādiāsavakkhayapariyosānaṃ ānisaṃsaṃ sutvāpi sīlaṃ paripūreyyu”nti.

1. In the first sutta of the third chapter, `sampannasīlā` means having perfected virtue, or in other words, having become endowed with virtue. Herein, the perfection of virtue occurs for two reasons: by seeing the danger in the corruption of virtue and by seeing the benefit in the perfection of virtue. Both of these are elaborated upon in the Visuddhimagga. Here, it is said that by the phrase “endowed with virtue,” the Blessed One indicated the fourfold purification virtue, and by “restrained by the Pātimokkha restraint,” he elaborated upon and showed the principal virtue therein; thus said the Elder Sumata, a resident of Dīpavihāra. But his resident pupil, the Elder Tepiṭaka Cūḷanāga, said: “In both places, only the Pātimokkha restraint was spoken of by the Blessed One. For the Pātimokkha restraint alone is virtue.” Not accepting that there is any place where the other three are taught as ‘virtue,’ he said: “Sense restraint is merely the guarding of the six doors; purity of livelihood is merely the production of requisites righteously and properly; virtue concerning requisites is merely using the obtained requisites after reflecting, ‘This is for this purpose.’ Unconditionally, the Pātimokkha restraint alone is virtue. For whom that is broken, it cannot be said of this person that he will guard his hands, feet, and the remaining virtues, just like a man with his head cut off. But for whom that is unimpaired, this person is able to make his life and the remaining virtues normal again, just like a man whose head is not cut off. Therefore, by ‘endowed with virtue,’ having indicated the Pātimokkha restraint, and by ‘perfect in the Pātimokkha,’ having spoken a synonym for that very thing, and wishing to show it by elaborating, he said ‘restrained by the Pātimokkha restraint,’ etc.” Herein, the meaning of ‘restrained by the Pātimokkha restraint,’ etc., is as has been stated. Why was “And if one should wish” begun? For the purpose of showing the benefits of virtue. If it should occur to the newly ordained or to those of little wisdom, “The Blessed One says, ‘Fulfill virtue, fulfill virtue!’ But what indeed is the benefit in fulfilling virtue? What is the distinction? What is the growth?”—it was to show them the ten benefits that he spoke thus: “Perhaps, having heard this benefit—which begins with being dear and agreeable to one's companions in the holy life and culminates in the destruction of the taints—they might indeed fulfill virtue.”

Tattha ākaṅkheyya ceti yadi iccheyya. Piyo cassanti piyacakkhūhi sampassitabbo, sinehuppattiyā padaṭṭhānabhūto bhaveyyaṃ. Manāpoti tesaṃ manavaḍḍhanako, tesaṃ vā manena pattabbo, mettacittena pharitabboti attho. Garūti tesaṃ garuṭṭhāniyo pāsāṇacchattasadiso. Bhāvanīyoti “addhāyamāyasmā jānaṃ jānāti passaṃ passatī”ti evaṃ sambhāvanīyo. Sīlesvevassa paripūrakārīti catupārisuddhisīlesuyeva paripūrakārī assa, anūnena ākārena samannāgato bhaveyyāti vuttaṃ hoti. Ajjhattaṃ cetosamathamanuyuttoti attano cittasamathe yutto. Anirākatajjhānoti bahi anīhaṭajjhāno, avināsitajjhāno vā. Vipassanāyāti sattavidhāya anupassanāya. Brūhetā suññāgārānanti vaḍḍhetā suññāgārānaṃ. Ettha ca samathavipassanāvasena kammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvā rattindivaṃ suññāgāraṃ pavisitvā nisīdamāno bhikkhu “brūhetā suññāgārāna”nti veditabbo. Ayamettha saṅkhepo[P 5.0050], vitthāro pana icchantena majjhimanikāyaṭṭhakathāya (ma. ni. aṭṭha. 1.64 ādayo) ākaṅkheyyasuttavaṇṇanāya oloketabbo.

Therein, `ākaṅkheyya ce` means “if one should wish.” `Piyo c'assaṃ ti` means: “I should be one to be well looked upon with loving eyes; I should become a proximate cause for the arising of affection.” `Manāpo` means one who increases their joy, or in other words, one who is to be attained by their mind, to be pervaded by a mind of loving-kindness. `Garū` means one who stands in a position of respect for them, like a stone umbrella. `Bhāvanīyo` means one who is to be esteemed thus: “Surely, this venerable one knows what is to be known and sees what is to be seen.” `Sīlesvevassa paripūrakārī` means: he should be one who fulfills only the fourfold purification virtues; it is said that he should be endowed with them in a manner that is not deficient. `Ajjhattaṃ cetosamathamanuyutto` means yoked to the calming of one's own mind. `Anirākatajjhāno` means one whose jhāna is not brought outside, or one whose jhāna is not destroyed. `Vipassanāya` means by the seven kinds of contemplation. `Brūhetā suññāgārānaṃ` means an augmenter of empty dwellings. And here, a monk who, having taken up a meditation subject by way of serenity and insight, enters an empty dwelling day and night and sits, is to be known as “an augmenter of empty dwellings.” This is the summary here; but for the detailed explanation, one who wishes should look at the commentary on the Ākaṅkheyya Sutta in the Majjhima Nikāya Aṭṭhakathā.

Lābhīti ettha na bhagavā lābhanimittaṃ sīlādiparipūraṇaṃ katheti. Bhagavā hi “ghāsesanaṃ chinnakatho, na vācaṃ payutaṃ bhaṇe”ti (su. ni. 716) evaṃ sāvake ovadati. So kathaṃ lābhanimittaṃ sīlādiparipūraṇaṃ katheyya. Puggalajjhāsayavasena panetaṃ vuttaṃ. Yesañhi evaṃ ajjhāsayo [V 3.0319] bhaveyya “sace mayaṃ catūhi paccayehi na kilameyyāma, sīlāni paripūretuṃ sakkuṇeyyāmā”ti, tesaṃ ajjhāsayavasenevamāha. Apica sarasānisaṃso esa sīlassa yadidaṃ cattāro paccayā nāma. Tathā hi paṇḍitamanussā koṭṭhādīsu ṭhapitaṃ nīharitvā attanāpi aparibhuñjitvā sīlavantānaṃ dentīti sīlassa sarasānisaṃsadassanatthampetaṃ vuttaṃ.

Herein, with the word `Lābhī`, the Blessed One does not teach the perfecting of virtue and so forth for the sake of gain. For the Blessed One advises his disciples thus: 'Being one with talk cut off, one should not speak speech connected with seeking food' (Su. Ni. 716). How then could he teach the perfecting of virtue and so forth for the sake of gain? But this was said in accordance with the inclination of individuals. For there are those whose inclination might be thus: 'If we were not troubled by the four requisites, we would be able to perfect our virtues.' It is in accordance with their inclination that he spoke thus. Moreover, these four requisites are a tangible benefit of virtue. For thus, wise people, having taken out what is stored in granaries and so forth, and without consuming it themselves, give to the virtuous. Thus, this was also said to show the tangible benefit of virtue.

Tatiyavāre [M 3.0322] yesāhanti yesaṃ ahaṃ. Tesaṃ te kārāti tesaṃ devānaṃ vā manussānaṃ vā te mayi katā paccayadānakārā. Mahapphalā hontu mahānisaṃsāti lokiyasukhena phalabhūtena mahapphalā, lokuttarena mahānisaṃsā. Ubhayaṃ vā etaṃ ekatthameva. Sīlādiguṇayuttassa hi kaṭacchubhikkhāpi pañcaratanamattāya bhūmiyā paṇṇasālāpi katvā dinnā anekāni kappasahassāni duggativinipātato rakkhati, pariyosāne ca amatāya dhātuyā parinibbānassa paccayo hoti. “Khīrodanaṃ [P 5.0051] ahamadāsi”ntiādīni (vi. va. 413) cettha vatthūni. Sakalameva vā petavatthu vimānavatthu ca sādhakaṃ.

In the third repetition, `yesāhaṃ` is `yesaṃ ahaṃ`. `Tesaṃ te kārā` means: those acts of giving the requisites done to me by those devas or humans. `Mahapphalā hontu mahānisaṃsā` means: greatly fruitful through worldly happiness which is the fruit, and of great benefit through the supramundane. Or both of these have one and the same meaning. For, to one endowed with virtues such as morality, even a ladleful of almsfood given, or a leaf-hut built on a piece of land measuring five cubits and given, protects from falling into a bad destination for many thousands of aeons, and in the end becomes a condition for the final extinguishment by means of the deathless element. Herein, 'I gave milk-rice,' and so forth (Vi. Va. 413) are the examples. Or, the entire Petavatthu and Vimānavatthu are the proof.

Catutthavāre petāti peccabhavaṃ gatā. Ñātīti sassusasurapakkhikā. Sālohitāti ekalohitabaddhā pitipitāmahādayo. Kālaṅkatāti matā. Tesaṃ tanti tesaṃ taṃ mayi pasannacittaṃ, taṃ vā pasannena cittena anussaraṇaṃ. Yassa hi bhikkhuno kālakato pitā vā mātā vā “amhākaṃ ñātakatthero sīlavā kalyāṇadhammo”ti pasannacitto hutvā taṃ bhikkhuṃ anussarati, tassa so cittappasādopi taṃ anussaraṇamattampi mahapphalaṃ mahānisaṃsameva hoti.
