In the fourth repetition, `petā` means those who have gone to the next world. `Ñātī` means those on the side of the mother-in-law and father-in-law. `Sālohitā` means those bound by the same blood, such as father, grandfather, and so on. `Kālaṅkatā` means dead. `Tesaṃ taṃ` means their pleased mind towards me, or that recollection with a pleased mind. For if a monk's deceased father or mother recollects that monk, having become pleased in mind thinking, 'Our kinsman, the elder, is virtuous, of noble character,' then for that deceased person, that very confidence of mind and even the mere recollection are of great fruit and great benefit indeed.

Aratiratisahoti nekkhammapaṭipattiyā aratiyā kāmaguṇesu ratiyā ca saho abhibhavitā ajjhottharitā. Bhayabheravasahoti ettha bhayaṃ cittutrāsopi ārammaṇampi, bheravaṃ ārammaṇameva.

`Aratiratisaho` means one who endures, overcomes, and overwhelms both discontent with the practice of renunciation and delight in sensual pleasures. Herein, regarding `bhayabheravasaho`: `bhaya` is both terror of the mind and its object; `bherava` is the object alone.

2. Kaṇṭakasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Kaṇṭaka Sutta

72. Dutiye abhiññātehīti gaganamajjhe puṇṇacando viya sūriyo viya ñātehi pākaṭehi. Parapurāyāti paraṃ vuccati pacchimabhāgo, purāti purimabhāgo, purato dhāvantena pacchato [V 3.0320] anubandhantena ca mahāparivārenāti attho. Kaṇṭakoti vijjhanaṭṭhena kaṇṭako. Visūkadassananti visūkabhūtaṃ dassanaṃ. Mātugāmūpacāroti [P 5.0052] mātugāmassa samīpacāritā.

2. In the Second Sutta, `abhiññātehi` means known, manifest, like the full moon in the middle of the sky and like the sun. As for `parapurāya`, `paraṃ` is said to be the latter part, and `purā` is the former part; the meaning is: with a large retinue running in front and following behind. `Kaṇṭako` is a thorn in the sense of piercing. `Visūkadassanaṃ` means a contrary view that has become a thorn. `Mātugāmūpacāro` means the state of frequenting the vicinity of a woman.

3-4. Iṭṭhadhammasuttādivaṇṇanā

3-4. Commentary on the Iṭṭhadhamma Sutta and Others

73-74. Tatiye [M 3.0323] vaṇṇoti sarīravaṇṇo. Dhammāti nava lokuttaradhammā. Catutthe ariyāyāti apothujjanikāya, sīlādīhi missakattā evaṃ vuttaṃ. Sārādāyī ca hoti varadāyīti sārassa ca varassa ca ādāyako hoti. Yo kāyassa sāro, yañcassa varaṃ, taṃ gaṇhātīti attho.

72. In the Third Sutta, `vaṇṇo` is the complexion of the body. `Dhammā` are the nine supramundane Dhammas. In the Fourth Sutta, `ariyāya` means not belonging to ordinary people; it is said thus because of being mixed with morality and other virtues. `Sārādāyī ca hoti varadāyī` means one who takes what is excellent and what is desirable. The meaning is: whatever excellent quality there is for that noble person, and whatever desirable quality there is, that he takes.

5. Migasālāsuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Migasālā Sutta

75. Pañcamassa ādimhi tāva yaṃ vattabbaṃ, taṃ chakkanipāte vuttameva. Dussīlo hotītiādīsu pana dussīloti nissīlo. Cetovimuttinti phalasamādhiṃ. Paññāvimuttinti phalañāṇaṃ. Nappajānātīti uggahaparipucchāvasena na jānāti. Dussīlyaṃ aparisesaṃ nirujjhatīti ettha pañca dussīlyāni tāva sotāpattimaggena pahīyanti, dasa arahattamaggena. Phalakkhaṇe tāni pahīnāni nāma honti. Phalakkhaṇaṃ sandhāya idha “nirujjhatī”ti vuttaṃ. Puthujjanassa sīlaṃ pañcahi kāraṇehi bhijjati pārājikāpajjanena sikkhāpaccakkhānena titthiyapakkhandanena arahattena maraṇenāti. Tattha purimā tayo bhāvanāparihānāya saṃvattanti, catuttho vaḍḍhiyā, pañcamo neva hānāya na vaḍḍhiyā. Kathaṃ panetaṃ arahattena sīlaṃ bhijjatīti? Puthujjanassa hi sīlaṃ accantakusalameva hoti, arahattamaggo ca kusalākusalakammakkhayāya saṃvattatīti evaṃ tena taṃ bhijjati. Savanenapi [P 5.0053] akataṃ hotīti sotabbayuttakaṃ assutaṃ hoti. Bāhusaccenapi akataṃ hotīti ettha bāhusaccanti vīriyaṃ. Vīriyena kattabbayuttakaṃ akataṃ hoti, tassa akatattā saggatopi maggatopi parihāyati. Diṭṭhiyāpi appaṭividdhaṃ hotīti diṭṭhiyā paṭivijjhitabbaṃ appaṭividdhaṃ hoti apaccakkhakataṃ. Sāmayikampi vimuttiṃ na labhatīti kālānukālaṃ dhammassavanaṃ nissāya pītipāmojjaṃ na labhati. Hānāya paretīti hānāya pavattati.

75. Regarding the beginning of the fifth sutta, what should be said has already been stated in the Book of the Sixes. In passages such as dussīlo hoti ('he is immoral'), dussīlo means without virtue (nissīlo). Cetovimuttiṃ ('liberation of mind') means the concentration of fruition. Paññāvimuttiṃ ('liberation by wisdom') means the knowledge of fruition. Nappajānāti ('he does not understand') means he does not know by way of study and inquiry. Regarding the passage dussīlyaṃ aparisesaṃ nirujjhati ('immorality ceases without remainder'), here, five kinds of immorality are abandoned by the path of stream-entry, and ten by the path of arahantship. At the moment of fruition, they are said to be abandoned. With reference to the moment of fruition, it is said here, 'ceases'. The virtue of an ordinary person is broken for five reasons: by committing a pārājika offense, by renouncing the training, by going over to the heretics, by attaining arahantship, and by death. Among these, the first three lead to a decline in development, the fourth leads to growth, and the fifth leads neither to decline nor to growth. But how is virtue broken by attaining arahantship? The virtue of an ordinary person is purely wholesome, and the path of arahantship leads to the exhaustion of wholesome and unwholesome kamma; thus it is broken by it. Savanenapi akataṃ hoti ('it is not accomplished even by hearing') means what is fit to be heard remains unheard. Regarding bāhusaccenapi akataṃ hoti ('it is not accomplished even by great learning'), here bāhusaccaṃ ('great learning') means effort. What is fit to be done through effort remains undone; because it is undone, he falls away from heaven and from the path. Diṭṭhiyāpi appaṭividdhaṃ hoti ('it is not penetrated even by wisdom') means what should be penetrated by wisdom remains unpenetrated, not directly experienced. Sāmayikampi vimuttiṃ na labhati ('he does not attain even temporary liberation') means he does not obtain joy and gladness by relying on listening to the Dhamma from time to time. Hānāya paretīti hānāya pavattati ('he is bound for decline' means he proceeds toward decline).

Yathābhūtaṃ pajānātīti “sotāpattiphalaṃ patvā pañcavidhaṃ dussīlyaṃ aparisesaṃ nirujjhatī”ti uggahaparipucchāvasena jānāti. Tassa savanenapi kataṃ hotīti sotabbayuttakaṃ sutaṃ [V 3.0321] hoti. Bāhusaccenapi kataṃ hotīti [M 3.0324] vīriyena kattabbayuttakaṃ antamaso dubbalavipassanāmattakampi kataṃ hoti. Diṭṭhiyāpi suppaṭividdhaṃ hotīti antamaso lokiyapaññāyapi paccayapaṭivedho kato hoti. Imassa hi puggalassa paññā sīlaṃ paridhovati, so paññāparidhotena visesaṃ pāpuṇāti.

He understands things as they really are: he knows by way of study and inquiry that 'having attained the fruit of stream-entry, the five kinds of immorality cease without remainder.' For him, it is accomplished even by hearing, meaning what is fit to be heard has been heard. It is accomplished even by great learning, meaning what is fit to be done by effort—at least a mere weak insight—has been done. It is well-penetrated even by wisdom, meaning at least the penetration of conditions has been done even with mundane wisdom. Indeed, for this person, wisdom cleanses virtue, and through virtue cleansed by wisdom, he attains distinction.

Pamāṇikāti puggalesu pamāṇaggāhakā. Pamiṇantīti pametuṃ tuletuṃ arahanti. Eko hīnoti eko guṇehi hīno. Paṇītoti eko guṇehi paṇīto uttamo. Taṃ hīti taṃ pamāṇakaraṇaṃ. Abhikkantataroti sundarataro. Paṇītataroti uttamataro. Dhammasoto nibbahatīti sūraṃ hutvā pavattamānaṃ vipassanāñāṇaṃ nibbahati, ariyabhūmiṃ pāpeti. Tadantaraṃ [P 5.0054] ko jāneyyāti taṃ evaṃ kāraṇaṃ ko jāneyya. Sīlavā hotīti lokiyasīlena sīlavā hoti. Yatthassa taṃ sīlanti arahattavimuttiṃ patvā sīlaṃ aparisesampi nirujjhati nāma, tattha yutti vuttāyeva. Ito paresu dvīsu aṅgesu anāgāmiphalaṃ vimutti nāma, pañcame arahattameva. Sesamettha vuttanayānusāreneva veditabbaṃ. Chaṭṭhaṃ uttānatthameva.

Pamāṇikā means those who take the measure of individuals. Pamiṇanti means they are fit to measure or to weigh. Eko hīno means one is inferior in qualities. Paṇīto means one is superior, excellent in qualities. Taṃ hi means that act of measuring. Abhikkantataro means more beautiful. Paṇītataroti means more excellent. Dhammasoto nibbahati ('the stream of Dhamma carries one along') means the insight knowledge, arising heroically, carries one forth; it leads one to the state of the noble ones. Tadantaraṃ ko jāneyya ('who could know the difference between them?') means who could know that reason? Sīlavā hoti ('he is virtuous') means he is virtuous with mundane virtue. As for yatthassa taṃ sīlaṃ ('wherein that virtue of his...'), having attained the liberation of arahantship, virtue is said to cease even without remainder; the reasoning for this has already been stated. In the two subsequent factors, the fruit of non-returning is called 'liberation'; in the fifth, it is arahantship itself. The rest should be understood here according to the method already explained. The sixth sutta is clear in meaning.

7. Kākasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Crow Discourse

77. Sattame dhaṃsīti guṇadhaṃsako. Kassaci guṇaṃ anādiyitvā hatthenapi gahito tassa sīsepi vaccaṃ karoti. Pagabbhoti pāgabbhiyena samannāgato. Tintiṇoti tintiṇaṃ vuccati taṇhā, tāya samannāgato, āsaṅkābahulo vā. Luddoti dāruṇo. Akāruṇikoti nikkāruṇiko. Dubbaloti abalo appathāmo. Oravitāti oravayutto oravanto carati. Necayikoti nicayakaro.

77. In the seventh, dhaṃsī means a destroyer of virtues. Not taking anyone's virtue into account, even when caught by the hand, he defecates on that person's head. Pagabbho means possessed of boldness. Tintiṇo means that craving is called 'tamarind'; he is possessed of it, or he is full of apprehension. Luddo means harsh. Akāruṇiko means without compassion. Dubbalo means without strength, without power. Oravitā means endowed with base lamentation; he wanders about lamenting basely. Necayiko means one who makes accumulations.

9. Āghātavatthusuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Discourse on the Grounds for Resentment

79. Navame aṭṭhāneti akāraṇe. Sacittakapavattiyañhi “anatthaṃ me acarī”tiādi kāraṇaṃ bhaveyya, khāṇupahaṭādīsu taṃ natthi. Tasmā tattha āghāto aṭṭhāne āghāto nāma. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

79. In the ninth, aṭṭhāne means without a cause. For in the case of an occurrence involving a being with a mind, there could be a reason such as "they have done me harm," etc., but in cases like being struck by a stump, there is no such reason. Therefore, resentment in that case is called resentment without a cause. The rest is clear in meaning throughout.

Ākaṅkhavaggo tatiyo.

The Chapter on Aspirations, the Third.

(9) 4. Theravaggo

(9) 4. The Chapter on Elders

1-3. Vāhanasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-3. Commentary on the Discourse on Vehicles, etc.

81-83. Catutthassa [V 3.0322][M 3.0325][P 5.0055] paṭhame vimariyādīkatenāti kilesamariyādaṃ bhinditvā vimariyādaṃ katena. Dutiyaṃ uttānatthameva. Tatiye no ca payirupāsitāti na upaṭṭhāti.

81-83. In the first of the fourth, vimariyādīkatena means by one who has broken the boundary of defilements, having made it unbounded. The second is clear in meaning. In the third, no ca payirupāsitā means does not attend upon.

4. Byākaraṇasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Discourse on Declarations

84. Catutthe jhāyī samāpattikusaloti jhānehi ca sampanno samāpattiyañca cheko. Irīṇanti tucchabhāvaṃ. Vicinanti guṇavicinataṃ nigguṇabhāvaṃ. Atha vā irīṇasaṅkhātaṃ araññaṃ vicinasaṅkhātaṃ mahāgahanañca āpanno viya hoti. Anayanti avaḍḍhiṃ. Byasananti vināsaṃ. Anayabyasananti avaḍḍhivināsaṃ. Kiṃ nu khoti kena kāraṇena.

84. In the fourth, jhāyī samāpattikusalo means endowed with the jhānas and skilled in the attainments. Irīṇaṃ means the state of emptiness. Vicinaṃ means the state of being undistinguished by virtues, the state of being without virtues. Alternatively, it is as if one has reached a forest called Iriṇa and a great thicket called Vicina. Anayaṃ means non-growth. Byasanaṃ means destruction. Anayabyasanaṃ means non-growth and destruction. Kiṃ nu kho means for what reason.

5-6. Katthīsuttādivaṇṇanā

5-6. Commentary on the Katthī Discourse, etc.

85-86. Pañcame katthī hoti vikatthīti katthanasīlo hoti vikatthanasīlo, vivaṭaṃ katvā katheti. Na santatakārīti na satatakārī. Chaṭṭhe adhimānikoti anadhigate adhigatamānena samannāgato. Adhimānasaccoti adhigatamānameva saccato vadati.

85-86. In the fifth, katthī hoti vikatthī means one is of a nature to boast and disparage; he speaks having made it manifest. Na santatakārī means not one who acts constantly. In the sixth, adhimāniko means possessed of the conceit 'it has been attained' concerning what has not been attained. Adhimānasacco means he speaks of the very conceit 'it has been attained' as truth.

7. Nappiyasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Discourse on the Unpleasant

87. Sattame adhikaraṇiko hotīti adhikaraṇakārako hoti. Na piyatāyāti na piyabhāvāya. Na garutāyāti na garubhāvāya. Na [P 5.0056] sāmaññāyāti na samaṇadhammabhāvāya. Na ekībhāvāyāti na nirantarabhāvāya. Dhammānaṃ na nisāmakajātikoti navannaṃ lokuttaradhammānaṃ na nisāmanasabhāvo na upadhāraṇasabhāvo. Na paṭisallānoti na paṭisallīno. Sāṭheyyānīti saṭhabhāvo. Kūṭeyyānīti kūṭabhāvo. Jimheyyānīti na ujubhāvā. Vaṅkeyyānīti vaṅkabhāvā.

87. In the seventh, adhikaraṇiko hoti means one is a maker of disputes. Na piyatāya means not for the sake of being dear. Na garutāya means not for the sake of being respected. Na sāmaññāya means not for the sake of the state of a recluse's qualities. Na ekībhāvāya means not for the sake of being without division. Dhammānaṃ na nisāmakajātiko means he is not of a nature to heed or bear in mind the nine supramundane Dhammas. Na paṭisallāno means not one who dwells in solitude. Sāṭheyyāni means the state of being deceitful. Kūṭeyyāni means the state of being fraudulent. Jimheyyāni means not the state of being straight. Vaṅkeyyāni means the state of being crooked.

8. Akkosakasuttavaṇṇanā

8. The Commentary on the Discourse on the Abuser

88. Aṭṭhame [M 3.0326][V 3.0323] akkosakaparibhāsako ariyūpavādī sabrahmacārinanti ettha sabrahmacāripadaṃ akkosakaparibhāsakapadehi yojetabbaṃ “akkosako sabrahmacārīnaṃ, paribhāsako sabrahmacārīna”nti. Ariyānaṃ pana guṇe chindissāmīti antimavatthunā upavadanto ariyūpavādī nāma hoti. Saddhammassa na vodāyantīti sikkhāttayasaṅkhātā sāsanasaddhammā assa vodānaṃ na gacchanti. Rogātaṅkanti ettha rogova kicchājīvitabhāvakaraṇena ātaṅkoti veditabbo.

88. In the eighth sutta, regarding 'an abuser, a reviler, a slanderer of noble ones, towards fellow practitioners,' here the term 'fellow practitioners' should be connected with the terms 'abuser' and 'reviler' as: 'an abuser of fellow practitioners, a reviler of fellow practitioners.' Furthermore, one who slanders with the ultimate matter, thinking, 'I will cut off the virtues of the noble ones,' is called 'a slanderer of noble ones.' As for 'the good Dhamma does not become pure,' this means the good Dhamma of the Teaching, designated as the threefold training, does not attain purity for him. Regarding 'disease and affliction,' here it should be understood that disease itself is an 'affliction' because it causes the state of having a difficult livelihood.

9. Kokālikasuttavaṇṇanā

9. The Commentary on the Kokālika Sutta

89. Navame kokāliko bhikkhu yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkamīti koyaṃ kokāliko, kasmā ca upasaṅkami? Ayaṃ kira kokālikaraṭṭhe kokālikanagare kokālikaseṭṭhissa putto pabbajitvā pitarā kārite vihāre vasati cūḷakokālikoti nāmena, na pana devadattassa sisso. So hi brāhmaṇaputto [P 5.0057] mahākokāliko nāma. Bhagavati pana sāvatthiyaṃ viharante dve aggasāvakā pañcamattehi bhikkhusatehi saddhiṃ janapadacārikaṃ caramānā upakaṭṭhāya vassūpanāyikāya vivekavāsaṃ vasitukāmā te bhikkhū uyyojetvā attano pattacīvaramādāya tasmiṃ janapade taṃ nagaraṃ patvā vihāraṃ agamiṃsu. Tattha nesaṃ kokāliko vattaṃ akāsi. Tepi tena saddhiṃ sammoditvā, “āvuso, mayaṃ idha temāsaṃ vasissāma, mā no kassaci ārocesī”ti paṭiññaṃ gahetvā vasiṃsu. Vasitvā pavāraṇādivase pavāretvā “gacchāma mayaṃ, āvuso”ti kokālikaṃ āpucchiṃsu. Kokāliko “ajja, āvuso, ekadivasaṃ vasitvā sve gamissathā”ti vatvā dutiyadivase nagaraṃ pavisitvā manusse āmantesi – “āvuso, tumhe dve aggasāvake idha āgantvā vasamānepi na jānātha, na te koci paccayenapi nimantetī”ti. Nagaravāsino “kahaṃ, bhante, therā, kasmā no nārocayitthā”ti? Kiṃ, āvuso, ārocitena, kiṃ na passatha dve bhikkhū therāsane nisīdante, ete aggasāvakāti. Te khippaṃ sannipatitvā sappiphāṇitādīni ceva cīvaradussāni ca saṃhariṃsu.

89. In the ninth sutta, regarding 'the monk Kokālika approached the Blessed One': Who is this Kokālika, and why did he approach? It is said that this was the son of the merchant Kokālika in the country of Kokālika. Having gone forth, he lived in a monastery built by his father and was known by the name Cūḷakokālika. He was not, however, the disciple of Devadatta. That one was a brahmin's son, named Mahākokālika. Now, while the Blessed One was dwelling in Sāvatthī, the two chief disciples, while wandering through the country with about five hundred monks, desired to dwell in seclusion as the rains-residence was approaching. Having sent those monks away, they took their own bowls and robes and, arriving at that city in that country, went to the monastery. There, Kokālika performed his duties for them. They too, having exchanged friendly greetings with him, took a promise from him, saying, 'Friend, we will dwell here for the three months. Do not inform anyone about us,' and so they dwelt there. Having dwelt there, on the day of the Pavāraṇā, after performing the Pavāraṇā ceremony, they took their leave of Kokālika, saying, 'Friend, we are going.' Kokālika said, 'Friends, stay for one day today and go tomorrow.' The next day, he entered the city and addressed the people: 'Friends, the two chief disciples came and dwelt here, yet you did not know. No one invited them for any requisite.' The city-dwellers asked, 'Venerable sir, where are the elders? Why did you not inform us?' 'Friends, what is the use of informing? Do you not see the two monks sitting in the elder's seat? They are the chief disciples.' They quickly gathered and collected ghee, molasses, and so on, and also cloth for robes.

Kokāliko [M 3.0327] cintesi – “paramappicchā aggasāvakā payuttavācāya uppannalābhaṃ na sādiyissanti, asādiyantā ‘āvāsikassa dethā’ti vakkhantī”ti taṃ lābhaṃ gāhāpetvā therānaṃ [V 3.0324] santikaṃ agamāsi. Therā disvāva “ime paccayā neva amhākaṃ, na kokālikassa kappantī”ti paṭikkhipitvā pakkamiṃsu. Kokāliko “kathañhi nāma sayaṃ aggaṇhantā mayhampi adāpetvā pakkamissantī”ti āghātaṃ uppādesi[P 5.0058]. Tepi bhagavato santikaṃ gantvā bhagavantaṃ vanditvā puna attano parisaṃ ādāya janapadacārikaṃ carantā anupubbena tasmiṃ raṭṭhe tameva nagaraṃ paccāgamiṃsu. Nāgarā there sañjānitvā saha parikkhārehi dānaṃ sajjetvā nagaramajjhe maṇḍapaṃ katvā dānaṃ adaṃsu, therānañca parikkhāre upanāmesuṃ. Therā bhikkhusaṅghassa niyyādayiṃsu. Taṃ disvā kokāliko cintesi – “ime pubbe appicchā ahesuṃ, idāni pāpicchā jātā, pubbepi appicchasantuṭṭhapavivittasadisāva maññe”ti there upasaṅkamitvā, “āvuso, tumhe pubbe appicchā viya, idāni pana pāpabhikkhū jātatthā”ti vatvā “mūlaṭṭhāneyeva nesaṃ patiṭṭhaṃ bhindissāmī”ti taramānarūpo nikkhamitvā yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami. Ayamesa kokāliko, iminā ca kāraṇena upasaṅkamīti veditabbo.

Kokālika thought: 'The chief disciples are of extremely few desires; they will not consent to a gain that has arisen through suggestive speech. Not consenting, they will say, "Give it to the resident monk."' Having had that gain brought, he went to the presence of the elders. The elders, upon seeing it, rejected it, saying, 'These requisites are not suitable for us, nor for Kokālika,' and they departed. Kokālika produced resentment, thinking, 'How is it that, not taking it themselves, they depart without even having it given to me?' They too went to the Blessed One, paid homage, and then, taking their retinue, went wandering through the country. In due course, they returned to that very city in that country. The city-dwellers, recognizing the elders, prepared alms along with requisites, and having made a pavilion in the middle of the city, they gave the alms and offered the requisites to the elders. The elders entrusted them to the Sangha of monks. Seeing this, Kokālika thought: 'Previously they were of few desires; now they have become of evil desires. I thought that previously they were of few desires, content, and like secluded persons.' Approaching the elders, he said, 'Friends, previously you were as if of few desires, but now you have become wicked monks.' Then, thinking, 'I will destroy their standing right at the source,' he left in a hurry and approached the Blessed One. This is that Kokālika, and it should be understood that he approached for this reason.

Bhagavā taṃ turitaturitaṃ āgacchantaṃ disvāva āvajjento aññāsi “ayaṃ aggasāvake akkositukāmo āgato, sakkā nu kho paṭisedhetu”nti. Tato “na sakkā paṭisedhetuṃ, theresu aparajjhitvā āgato, ekaṃsena pana padumaniraye nibbattissatī”ti disvā “sāriputtamoggallānepi nāma garahantaṃ sutvā na nisedhetī”ti vādamocanatthaṃ ariyūpavādassa ca mahāsāvajjabhāvadassanatthaṃ mā hevanti tikkhattuṃ paṭisedhesi. Tattha mā hevanti mā evaṃ abhaṇi. Saddhāyikoti saddhāya āgamakaro pasādāvaho, saddhātabbavacano vā. Paccayikoti pattiyāyitabbavacano.

The Blessed One, seeing him coming very hurriedly, upon directing his mind, knew: 'This one has come wishing to abuse the chief disciples. Is it possible to prevent him?' Then, seeing, 'It is not possible to prevent him; he has come having wronged the elders, and he will definitely be reborn in the Paduma hell,' and in order to be free from the accusation that 'Having heard him reviling even Sāriputta and Moggallāna, he does not prevent him,' and also to show the great faultiness of slandering noble ones, he forbade him three times, saying, 'Do not speak thus.' Therein, 'Do not speak thus' means 'Do not say so.' 'Inspiring faith' (saddhāyika) means one who is a source of faith, who brings confidence, or one whose word is to be believed. 'Trustworthy' (paccayika) means one whose word is to be relied upon.

Pakkāmīti [P 5.0059] kammānubhāvena codiyamāno pakkāmi. Okāsakatañhi kammaṃ na sakkā paṭibāhituṃ. Acirapakkantassāti pakkantassa sato nacireneva. Sabbo kāyo phuṭo ahosīti kesaggamattampi okāsaṃ avajjetvā sakalasarīraṃ aṭṭhīni bhinditvā uggatāhi pīḷakāhi ajjhotthaṭaṃ [M 3.0328] ahosi. Yasmā pana buddhānubhāvena tathārūpaṃ kammaṃ buddhānaṃ sammukhībhāve vipākaṃ dātuṃ na sakkoti, dassanūpacāre vijahitamatte deti, tasmā tassa acirapakkantassa pīḷakā uṭṭhahiṃsu. Kalāyamattiyoti caṇakamattiyo. Beluvasalāṭukamattiyoti taruṇabeluvamattiyo. Pabhijjiṃsūti bhijjiṃsu. Tāsu bhinnāsu sakalasarīraṃ panasapakkaṃ viya ahosi. So pakkena gattena jetavanadvārakoṭṭhake visagilito maccho viya kadalipattesu nipajji. Atha dhammassavanatthaṃ āgatāgatā [V 3.0325] manussā “dhi kokālika, dhi kokālika, ayuttamakāsi, attanoyeva mukhaṃ nissāya anayabyasanaṃ pattosī”ti āhaṃsu. Tesaṃ saddaṃ sutvā ārakkhadevatā dhikkāramakaṃsu, ārakkhadevatānaṃ ākāsadevatāti iminā upāyena yāva akaniṭṭhabhavanā ekadhikkāro udapādi.

“He departed” means, being urged on by the power of his kamma, he departed. For kamma that has been given an opportunity cannot be warded off. “Soon after he departed” means not long after he had departed. “His entire body was covered” means without leaving a space even the size of a hair's tip, his whole body was covered with boils that erupted, breaking his bones. However, because such kamma cannot bear fruit in the presence of the Buddhas due to their power, and it bears fruit only when one has left their range of sight, boils therefore arose for him soon after he departed. “The size of a chickpea” means the size of a wild chickpea. “The size of a young beluva fruit” means the size of a tender beluva fruit. “They burst” means they split open. When they burst, his entire body became like a ripe jackfruit. With his festering body, he lay on banana leaves at the gatehouse of Jetavana like a fish that has swallowed poison. Then, people who came again and again to hear the Dhamma said, “Shame on you, Kokālika! Shame on you, Kokālika! You have done what is improper. Relying on your own mouth, you have come to ruin and disaster.” Hearing their words, the guardian deities uttered their disapproval. Hearing the sound of the guardian deities, the earth-dwelling deities uttered their disapproval. In this way, a single cry of disapproval arose, extending up to the Akaniṭṭha realm.

Turūti kokālikassa upajjhāyo turutthero nāma anāgāmiphalaṃ vatvā brahmaloke nibbatto. So bhummaṭṭhadevatā ādiṃ katvā “ayuttaṃ kokālikena kataṃ aggasāvake antimavatthunā abbhācikkhantenā”ti paramparāya brahmalokasampattaṃ [P 5.0060] taṃ saddaṃ sutvā “mā mayhaṃ passantasseva varāko nassi, ovadissāmi naṃ theresu cittappasādatthāyā”ti āgantvā tassa purato aṭṭhāsi. Taṃ sandhāyetaṃ vuttaṃ – “turū paccekabrahmā”ti. Pesalāti piyasīlā. Kosi tvaṃ, āvusoti nisinnakova kabarakkhīni ummīletvā evamāha. Passa yāvañca te idaṃ aparaddhati yattakaṃ tayā aparaddhaṃ, attano nalāṭe mahāgaṇḍaṃ apassanto sāsapamattāya pīḷakāya maṃ codetabbaṃ maññasīti āha.

“Turū” refers to Kokālika’s preceptor, the elder named Turu, who, having attained the fruit of non-returning, was reborn in the Suddhāvāsa Brahma world. Starting with the earth-dwelling deities, the news spread, “Kokālika has done what is improper by falsely accusing the chief disciples with a final matter.” Hearing that sound which had reached the Brahma world in succession, he thought, “Let not this wretched being perish while I am watching. I will advise him for the purpose of inspiring confidence in the elders,” and he came and stood before him. In reference to this, it was said: “Turū, the Paccekabrahmā.” “Pesalā” means of amiable virtue. While still seated, Kokālika opened his mottled eyes and said, “Who are you, friend?” The Brahmā replied, “See how much you have offended! Not seeing the great boil on your own forehead, you think I should be accused for a boil the size of a mustard seed!”

Atha naṃ “adiṭṭhippatto ayaṃ kokāliko, gilitaviso viya na kassaci vacanaṃ na karissatī”ti ñatvā purisassa hītiādimāha. Tattha kuṭhārīti kuṭhārisadisā pharusavācā. Chindatīti kusalamūlasaṅkhāte mūleyeva nikantati. Nindiyanti ninditabbaṃ dussīlapuggalaṃ. Pasaṃsatīti uttamatthe sambhāvetvā khīṇāsavoti vadati. Taṃ vā nindati yo pasaṃsiyoti yo vā pasaṃsitabbo khīṇāsavo, taṃ antimavatthunā codento “dussīlo aya”nti vadati. Vicināti mukhena so kalinti so taṃ aparādhaṃ [M 3.0329] mukhena vicināti nāma. Kalinā [P 5.0061] tenāti tena aparādhena sukhaṃ na vindati. Nindiyapasaṃsāya hi pasaṃsiyanindāya ca samakova vipāko.

Then, knowing, “This Kokālika has come to ruin; like one who has swallowed poison, he will not heed anyone's words,” he recited the verse beginning “Purisassa hi...”. Therein, “kuṭhārī” means harsh speech like an axe. “Chindati” means it cuts the very roots, which are called the roots of wholesomeness. “Nindiyam” refers to a blameworthy, immoral person. “Pasaṃsati” means, praising him in the highest sense, that of Arahantship, he says, “He is one whose cankers are destroyed.” “Or he blames one who is praiseworthy” means, censuring a praiseworthy one whose cankers are destroyed with a final matter, he says, “This one is immoral.” “Vicināti mukhena so kaliṃ” means that person accumulates that fault with his mouth. “Kalinā tena” means because of that fault, he does not find happiness. For the result of praising the blameworthy and of blaming the praiseworthy is indeed the same.

Sabbassāpi sahāpi attanāti sabbena sakena dhanenapi attanāpi saddhiṃ yo akkhesu dhanaparājayo nāma, ayaṃ appamattako aparādho. Yo sugatesūti yo pana sammaggatesu puggalesu cittaṃ dūseyya, ayaṃ cittapadosova tato kalito mahantataro kali.

“The loss of all one's possessions, together with oneself, in gambling is a minor fault. But whoever should corrupt their mind towards the Sugatas—that is, towards persons who have gone well—this corruption of mind is a far greater fault than that.”

Idāni tassa mahantatarabhāvaṃ dassento sataṃ sahassānantiādimāha. Tattha sataṃ sahassānanti [V 3.0326] nirabbudagaṇanāya satasahassañca. Chattiṃsatīti aparāni chattiṃsati nirabbudāni. Pañca cāti abbudagaṇanāya pañca abbudāni. Yamariyagarahīti yaṃ ariye garahanto nirayaṃ upapajjati, tattha ettakaṃ āyuppamāṇanti attho.

Now, wishing to show the greater nature of that fault, he said the words beginning “sataṃ sahassānaṃ...”. Therein, “sataṃ sahassānaṃ” means one hundred thousand by the reckoning of Nirabbudas. “Chattiṃsati” means another thirty-six Nirabbudas. “Pañca ca” means five Abbudas by the reckoning of Abbudas. “Yam ariyagarahī” means: the hell into which one who reviles the noble ones is reborn—in that Paduma hell, such is the measure of the lifespan. This is the meaning.

Kālamakāsīti upajjhāye pakkante kālaṃ akāsi. Padumanirayanti pāṭiyekko padumanirayo nāma natthi, avīcimahānirayasmiṃyeva pana padumagaṇanāya paccitabbe ekasmiṃ ṭhāne nibbatti.

“He passed away” means: when the preceptor had departed, he died. As for the Paduma hell, there is no separate hell named Paduma; rather, it is rebirth in a certain place within the great Avīci hell where one is tormented according to the Paduma reckoning.

Vīsatikhārikoti māgadhakena patthena cattāro patthā, kosalaraṭṭhe eko pattho hoti. Tena patthena cattāro patthā āḷhakaṃ[P 5.0062], cattāri āḷhakāni doṇaṃ, catudoṇā mānikā, catumānikā khārī, tāya khāriyā vīsatikhāriko. Tilavāhoti māgadhakānaṃ sukhumatilānaṃ tilasakaṭaṃ. Abbudo nirayoti abbudo nāma pāṭiyekko nirayo natthi, avīcimhiyeva pana abbudagaṇanāya paccitabbaṭṭhānassetaṃ nāmaṃ. Nirabbudādīsupi eseva nayo.

Regarding “twenty khārikas”: A pattha in the Kosala country is equivalent to four Magadhan patthas. By that Magadhan pattha, four patthas make an āḷhaka, four āḷhakas a doṇa, four doṇas a mānikā, and four mānikās a khārī. Twenty such khārīs is the measure. “A cartload of sesame seeds” refers to a cartload of fine Magadhan sesame seeds. “The Abbuda hell”: there is no separate hell named Abbuda; rather, this is the name for a place in Avīci itself where one is tormented according to the Abbuda reckoning. The same method applies to Nirabbuda and the others.

Vassagaṇanāpi panettha evaṃ veditabbā – yatheva hi sataṃ satasahassāni koṭi hoti, evaṃ sataṃ satasahassakoṭiyo pakoṭi nāma hoti, sataṃ satasahassapakoṭiyo koṭipakoṭi nāma, sataṃ satasahassakoṭipakoṭiyo nahutaṃ, sataṃ satasahassanahutāni ninnahutaṃ, sataṃ satasahassaninnahutāni ekaṃ abbudaṃ, tato vīsatiguṇaṃ nirabbudaṃ, esa nayo sabbatthāti. Dasamaṃ heṭṭhā vuttanayeneva veditabbaṃ. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

The counting of years here should also be understood thus: just as a hundred hundred-thousands make a koṭi, so a hundred hundred-thousand koṭis make a pakoṭi; a hundred hundred-thousand pakoṭis make a koṭipakoṭi; a hundred hundred-thousand koṭipakoṭis make a nahuta; a hundred hundred-thousand nahutas make a ninnahuta; a hundred hundred-thousand ninnahutas make one abbuda; and twenty times that is a nirabbuda. This is the method in all cases. The tenth sutta should be understood according to the method stated previously. The remainder is of clear meaning everywhere.

Theravaggo catuttho.

The Fourth Chapter on the Elders.

(10) 5. Upālivaggo

(10) 5. The Upāli Section.

1-2. Kāmabhogīsuttādivaṇṇanā

1-2. The Commentary on the Kāmabhogī Sutta and Others.

91-92. Pañcamassa [V 3.0327][M 3.0330] paṭhame sāhasenāti sāhasiyakammena. Dutiye bhayānīti cittutrāsabhayāni. Verānīti akusalaverapuggalaverāni. Ariyo cassa ñāyoti saha vipassanāya maggo. Iti imasmiṃ sati idaṃ hotīti evaṃ imasmiṃ avijjādike kāraṇe sati idaṃ saṅkhārādikaṃ phalaṃ hoti. Imassuppādā idaṃ uppajjatīti yo yassa sahajātapaccayo hoti, tassa uppādā itaraṃ uppajjati nāma. Imasmiṃ asatīti avijjādike kāraṇe asati saṅkhārādikaṃ phalaṃ na hoti. Imassa [P 5.0063] nirodhāti kāraṇassa appavattiyā phalassa appavatti hoti.

In the first [sutta] of the fifth [vagga], `sāhasena` means by a violent act. In the second [sutta], `bhayāni` means fears that are terrors of the mind. `Verāni` means enmities that are unwholesome and enmities that are personal. `Ariyo cassa ñāyo` means the path together with insight. `Iti imasmiṃ sati idaṃ hoti` means: thus, when this cause, such as ignorance, exists, this result, such as formations, arises. `Imassuppādā idaṃ uppajjati` means: whatever is a co-nascent condition for another, due to the arising of that, the other is said to arise. `Imasmiṃ asati` means: when the cause, such as ignorance, does not exist, the result, such as formations, does not arise. `Imassa nirodhā` means: due to the non-occurrence of the cause, there is the non-occurrence of the result.

3. Kiṃdiṭṭhikasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Kiṃdiṭṭhika Sutta.

93. Tatiye saṇṭhāpesunti iriyāpathampi vacanapathampi saṇṭhāpesuṃ. Appasaddavinītāti appasaddena mattabhāṇinā satthārā vinītā. Paratoghosapaccayā vāti parassa vā vacanakāraṇā. Cetayitāti pakappitā. Maṅkubhūtāti domanassappattā nittejā. Pattakkhandhāti patitakkhandhā. Sahadhammenāti sahetukena kāraṇena vacanena.

93. In the third [sutta], `saṇṭhāpesuṃ` means they well established both posture and the path of speech. `Appasaddavinītā` means disciplined by the Teacher who speaks with measure and little sound. `Paratoghosapaccayā vā` means or because of the speech of another. `Cetayitā` means incited. `Maṅkubhūtā` means having become displeased and without energy. `Pattakkhandhā` means with drooping shoulders. `Sahadhammena` means with a statement that has reason and cause.

4. Vajjiyamāhitasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Vajjiyamāhita Sutta.

94. Catutthe vajjiyamāhitoti evaṃnāmako. Sabbaṃ tapanti sabbameva dukkarakārikaṃ. Sabbaṃ tapassinti sabbaṃ tapanissitakaṃ. Lūkhājīvinti dukkarakārikajīvikānuyogaṃ anuyuttaṃ. Gārayhanti garahitabbayuttakaṃ. Pasaṃsiyanti pasaṃsitabbayuttakaṃ. Venayikoti sayaṃ avinīto aññehi vinetabbo. Apaññattikoti na kiñci paññāpetuṃ sakkoti. Atha vā venayikoti sattavināsako. Apaññattikoti apaccakkhaṃ nibbānaṃ paññāpeti, sayaṃkatādīsu kiñci paññāpetuṃ na sakkoti. Na so bhagavā venayikoti so bhagavā evaṃ yāthāvato ñatvā [P 5.0064] kusalākusalaṃ [V 3.0328] paññāpento na aññena vinetabbo na aññasikkhito. Ye ca dhamme upādāya satto paññāpiyati, tesaṃ paññāpanato na sattavināsako, suvinīto susikkhito sattavināyakoti attho. Tassa ca paññattiyo [M 3.0331] sapaññattiyoyevāti dasseti. Vimuttiṃ vimuccato akusalā dhammāti micchādiṭṭhisaṅkhātaṃ cittassa adhimuttiṃ adhimuccato akusalā dhammā vaḍḍhanti nāma, taṃ sandhāyetaṃ vuttaṃ. Sāsane pana cittassa vimuttisaṅkhāto vimutti kusalānaṃyeva paccayo hoti.

94. In the fourth [sutta], `vajjiyamāhito` is one so named. `Sabbaṃ tapaṃ` means all austerity which is difficult to perform. `Sabbaṃ tapassiṃ` means one who is dependent on all austerity. `Lūkhājīviṃ` means one who is devoted to the practice of a livelihood that is difficult to perform. `Gārayhaṃ` means that which is fit to be blamed. `Pasaṃsiyaṃ` means that which is fit to be praised. `Venayiko` means one who, being undisciplined himself, is to be disciplined by others. `Apaññattiko` means one who is unable to designate anything. Alternatively, `venayiko` means a destroyer of beings. `Apaññattiko` means one who designates a Nibbāna that is not directly realized, and is unable to designate anything among things self-made, etc. `Na so bhagavā venayiko` means: that Blessed One, knowing thus according to reality and designating the wholesome and unwholesome, is not to be disciplined by another, not trained by another. And because he designates those things on account of which a 'being' is designated, he is not a destroyer of beings, but is well-disciplined, well-trained, and a guide of beings. This is the meaning. `Vimuttiṃ vimuccato akusalā dhammā`: this was said in reference to the fact that for a person who resolves on the resolve of the mind reckoned as wrong view, unwholesome states are said to increase. But in the Dispensation, liberation, which is reckoned as the mind's liberation, is a condition for wholesome states only.

5. Uttiyasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Uttiya Sutta.

95. Pañcame tuṇhī ahosīti sattūpaladdhiyaṃ ṭhatvā apucchaṃ pucchatīti tuṇhī ahosi. Sabbasāmukkaṃsikaṃ vata meti mayā sabbapucchānaṃ uttamapucchaṃ pucchito samaṇo gotamo saṃsādeti no vissajjeti, nūna na visahati na sakkoti vissajjetunti evaṃ pāpikaṃ diṭṭhiṃ mā paṭilabhīti. Tadassāti taṃ evaṃ uppannaṃ diṭṭhigataṃ bhaveyya. Paccantimanti yasmā majjhimadese nagarassa uddhāpādīni thirāni vā hontu dubbalāni vā, sabbaso vā pana mā hontu, corāsaṅkā na hoti. Tasmā taṃ aggahetvā “paccantimaṃ nagara”nti āha. Daḷhuddhāpanti [P 5.0065] thirapākārapādaṃ. Daḷhapākāratoraṇanti thirapākārañceva thirapiṭṭhisaṅghāṭañca. Ekadvāranti kasmā āha? Bahudvārasmiñhi nagare bahūhi paṇḍitadovārikehi bhavitabbaṃ, ekadvāre ekova vaṭṭati. Tathāgatassa ca paññāya añño sadiso natthi. Tasmā satthu paṇḍitabhāvassa opammatthaṃ ekaṃyeva dovārikaṃ dassetuṃ “ekadvāra”nti āha. Paṇḍitoti paṇḍiccena samannāgato. Byattoti veyyattiyena samannāgato. Medhāvīti ṭhānuppattiyapaññāsaṅkhātāya medhāya samannāgato. Anupariyāyapathanti anupariyāyanāmakaṃ maggaṃ. Pākārasandhinti dvinnaṃ iṭṭhakānaṃ apagataṭṭhānaṃ. Pākāravivaranti pākārassa chinnaṭṭhānaṃ. Tadevetaṃ pañhanti taṃyeva “sassato loko”tiādinā nayena puṭṭhaṃ ṭhapanīyapañhaṃ punapi pucchi. Sabbo ca tena lokoti sattūpaladdhiyaṃyeva ṭhatvā aññenākārena pucchatīti dasseti.

95. In the fifth [sutta], 'he remained silent' (tuṇhī ahosi) means he was silent with the thought: 'Standing in the perception of a being, he asks what should not be asked.' [He was also silent] lest [the questioner] should adopt such a wrong view as: 'The ascetic Gotama, having been asked by me the supreme question among all questions, prevaricates and does not answer; surely he is unable, he cannot answer.' 'That would be his' (tadassa) means that wrong view, having thus arisen, would be. As for 'border' (paccantimaṃ): because in the middle country, whether the ramparts and so on of a city are strong or weak, or even if they are entirely absent, there is no fear of thieves. Therefore, not taking that as an example, he said, 'a border town.' 'A strong rampart' (daḷhuddhāpaṃ) means a firm wall-base. 'Strong walls and gate-towers' (daḷhapākāratoraṇaṃ) means both firm walls and a firm door-frame. Why did he say 'with one gate' (ekadvāraṃ)? Because in a city with many gates, there must be many wise gatekeepers, but in a city with one gate, only one is suitable. And there is no other equal to the Tathāgata in wisdom. Therefore, to provide a simile for the Teacher's state of wisdom, to show just one gatekeeper, he said, 'with one gate.' 'Wise' (paṇḍito) means endowed with wisdom. 'Accomplished' (byatto) means endowed with skill. 'Intelligent' (medhāvī) means endowed with the intelligence known as wisdom that arises on the spot. 'The patrol path' (anupariyāyapathaṃ) means the path for patrolling in sequence. 'A crevice in the wall' (pākārasandhiṃ) means the place where two bricks are separated. 'A breach in the wall' (pākāravivaraṃ) means a broken place of the wall. 'That very question' (tadevetaṃ pañhaṃ) means he again asked that very same question to be set aside, which was asked in the manner of 'Is the world eternal?', etc. The phrase 'and the whole world by him' (sabbo ca tena loko) shows that, standing only in the perception of a being, he asks in another way.

6. Kokanudasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Kokanuda Sutta.

96. Chaṭṭhe pubbāpayamānoti pubbasadisāni nirudakāni kurumāno. Kvettha, āvusoti ko [V 3.0329] ettha, āvuso. Yāvatā, āvuso, diṭṭhīti [M 3.0332] yattikā dvāsaṭṭhividhāpi diṭṭhi nāma atthi. Yāvatā diṭṭhiṭṭhānanti “khandhāpi diṭṭhiṭṭhānaṃ, avijjāpi, phassopi, saññāpi, vitakkopi ayonisomanasikāropi, pāpamittopi[P 5.0066], paratoghosopi diṭṭhiṭṭhāna”nti evaṃ yattakaṃ aṭṭhavidhampi diṭṭhiṭṭhānaṃ diṭṭhikāraṇaṃ nāma atthi. Diṭṭhādhiṭṭhānanti diṭṭhīnaṃ adhiṭṭhānaṃ, adhiṭhatvā adhibhavitvā pavattāya diṭṭhiyā etaṃ nāmaṃ. Diṭṭhipariyuṭṭhānanti “katamāni aṭṭhārasa diṭṭhipariyuṭṭhānāni? Yā diṭṭhi diṭṭhigataṃ diṭṭhigahanaṃ diṭṭhikantāraṃ diṭṭhivisūkaṃ diṭṭhivipphanditaṃ diṭṭhisaṃyojanaṃ diṭṭhisallaṃ diṭṭhisambādho diṭṭhipalibodho diṭṭhibandhanaṃ diṭṭhipapāto diṭṭhānusayo diṭṭhisantāpo diṭṭhipariḷāho diṭṭhigantho diṭṭhupādānaṃ diṭṭhābhiniveso diṭṭhiparāmāso. Imāni aṭṭhārasa diṭṭhipariyuṭṭhānānī”ti evaṃ vuttaṃ diṭṭhipariyuṭṭhānaṃ. Samuṭṭhānanti diṭṭhiṭṭhānasseva vevacanaṃ. Vuttañhetaṃ – “khandhā paccayo diṭṭhīnaṃ upādāya samuṭṭhānaṭṭhenā”ti (paṭi. ma. 1.124) sabbaṃ vitthāretabbaṃ. Sotāpattimaggo pana diṭṭhisamugghāto nāma sabbadiṭṭhīnaṃ samugghātakattā. Tamahanti taṃ sabbaṃ ahaṃ jānāmi. Kyāhaṃ vakkhāmīti kiṃkāraṇā ahaṃ vakkhāmi.

96. In the sixth, pubbāpayamāno means making bodies similar to before. Kvettha, āvuso is ko ettha, āvuso (Who is here, friend?). Yāvatā, āvuso, diṭṭhi (As far as views go, friend) means whatever sixty-two kinds of views exist. Yāvatā diṭṭhiṭṭhānaṃ (As far as the basis of views) means: ‘The aggregates are a basis for views, as are ignorance, contact, perception, thought, unwise attention, bad friends, and the voice of another’—thus, whatever eight kinds of basis for views exist, that is called the cause of views. Diṭṭhādhiṭṭhānaṃ (The foundation of views) means the establishing of views; this is the name for a view that arises having been established and having dominated. Diṭṭhipariyuṭṭhānaṃ (Obsession with views) means the obsession with views spoken of thus: ‘What are the eighteen obsessions with views? The view that is a wrong view, a thicket of views, a wilderness of views, a distortion of views, a writhing of views, a fetter of views, a dart of views, a congestion of views, a hindrance of views, a bondage of views, a precipice of views, an underlying tendency to views, the distress of views, the fever of views, the knot of views, the clinging to views, the insistence on views, the adherence to views. These are the eighteen obsessions with views.’ Samuṭṭhānaṃ (Origin) is just a synonym for the basis of views. For it is said: ‘The aggregates are a condition for views in the sense of being their origin through clinging’—all this should be elaborated. The path of stream-entry, however, is called the ‘eradicator of views’ because it eradicates all views. Tamahaṃ (That I) means ‘I know all that.’ Kyāhaṃ vakkhāmi (Why should I speak?) means ‘For what reason should I speak?’

7-8. Āhuneyyasuttādivaṇṇanā

7–8. Commentary on the Āhuneyya Sutta, etc.

97-98. Sattame sammādiṭṭhikoti yāthāvadiṭṭhiko. Aṭṭhame adhikaraṇasamuppādavūpasamakusaloti catunnaṃ adhikaraṇānaṃ mūlaṃ gahetvā vūpasamena samuppādavūpasamakusalo hoti.

97–98. In the seventh, sammādiṭṭhiko means one who has a correct view. In the eighth, adhikaraṇasamuppādavūpasamakusalo means one is skilled in the pacification of the arising of the four legal questions by grasping their root.

9. Upālisuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Upāli Sutta

99. Navame [P 5.0067] durabhisambhavānīti sambhavituṃ dukkhāni dussahāni, na sakkā appesakkhehi ajjhogāhitunti vuttaṃ hoti. Araññavanapatthānīti araññāni ca vanapatthāni ca. Āraññakaṅganipphādanena araññāni, gāmantaṃ atikkamitvā manussānaṃ anupacāraṭṭhānabhāvena vanapatthāni. Pantānīti pariyantāni atidūrāni. Dukkaraṃ pavivekanti kāyaviveko dukkaro. Durabhiramanti abhiramituṃ na sukaraṃ. Ekatteti ekībhāve. Kiṃ dasseti? Kāyaviveke katepi tattha cittaṃ abhiramāpetuṃ dukkaraṃ. Dvayaṃdvayārāmo hi ayaṃ lokoti. Haranti maññeti haranti viya ghasanti viya. Manoti cittaṃ. Samādhiṃ alabhamānassāti upacārasamādhiṃ vā appanāsamādhiṃ vā alabhantassa[M 3.0333]. Kiṃ dasseti? Īdisassa bhikkhuno [V 3.0330] tiṇapaṇṇamigādisaddehi vividhehi ca bhīsanakehi vanāni cittaṃ vikkhipanti maññeti. Saṃsīdissatīti kāmavitakkena saṃsīdissati. Uplavissatīti byāpādavihiṃsāvitakkehi uddhaṃ plavissati.

99. In the ninth, durabhisambhavāni means they are difficult to come to be, hard to endure, and cannot be entered into by those of little power—so it is said. Araññavanapatthāni means forests and jungle thickets. Because of fulfilling the ascetic practice of dwelling in the forest, they are called ‘forests’; because they are places beyond the outskirts of a village, not frequented by humans, they are called ‘jungle thickets.’ Pantāni means remote and extremely distant. Dukkaraṃ pavivekaṃ means physical seclusion is difficult. Durabhiramaṃ means it is not easy to delight in. Ekatte means in solitude. What does this show? Even when physical seclusion is accomplished, it is difficult to make the mind delight therein, for this world delights in pairs. Haranti maññe means as if they carry away or devour. Mano means the mind. Samādhiṃ alabhamānassa means for one not obtaining either access concentration or absorption concentration. What does this show? For such a monk, the forests, with the sounds of grass, leaves, animals, and various frightening things, seem to scatter the mind. Saṃsīdissati means one will sink due to sensual thought. Uplavissati means one will float up due to thoughts of ill will and harm.

Kaṇṇasaṃdhovikanti kaṇṇe dhovantena kīḷitabbaṃ. Piṭṭhisaṃdhovikanti piṭṭhiṃ dhovantena kīḷitabbaṃ. Tattha [P 5.0068] udakaṃ soṇḍāya gahetvā dvīsu kaṇṇesu āsiñcanaṃ kaṇṇasaṃdhovikā nāma, piṭṭhiyaṃ āsiñcanaṃ piṭṭhisaṃdhovikā nāma. Gādhaṃ vindatīti patiṭṭhaṃ labhati. Ko cāhaṃ ko ca hatthināgoti ahaṃ ko, hatthināgo ko, ahampi tiracchānagato, ayampi, mayhampi cattāro pādā, imassapi, nanu ubhopi mayaṃ samasamāti.

Kaṇṇasaṃdhovikā means playing by washing the ears. Piṭṭhisaṃdhovikā means playing by washing the back. Therein, taking water with the trunk and pouring it into both ears is called kaṇṇasaṃdhovikā, and pouring it on the back is called piṭṭhisaṃdhovikā. Gādhaṃ vindati means one obtains a foothold. Ko cāhaṃ ko ca hatthināgo means: ‘Who am I, and who is this great elephant? I too am an animal, and so is this one. I too have four feet, and so does this one. Are we not both equal?’

Vaṅkakanti kumārakānaṃ kīḷanakaṃ khuddakanaṅgalaṃ. Ghaṭikanti dīghadaṇḍakena rassadaṇḍakaṃ paharaṇakīḷaṃ. Mokkhacikanti saṃparivattakakīḷaṃ, ākāse daṇḍakaṃ gahetvā bhūmiyaṃ vā sīsaṃ ṭhapetvā heṭṭhupariyabhāvena parivattanakīḷanti vuttaṃ hoti. Ciṅgulakanti tālapaṇṇādīhi kataṃ vātappahārena paribbhamanacakkaṃ. Pattāḷhakaṃ vuccati paṇṇanāḷi, tāya vālukādīni minantā kīḷanti. Rathakanti khuddakarathaṃ. Dhanukanti khuddakadhanumeva.

Vaṅkaka is a small toy plough for boys. Ghaṭika is a game of striking a short stick with a long one. Mokkhacika is a game of turning somersaults; it is said to be a game of turning upside down, either by taking a stick in the air or by placing one’s head on the ground. Ciṅgulaka is a pinwheel made of palm leaves or the like that revolves when struck by the wind. Pattāḷhaka is called a leaf-measure; they play by measuring sand and so on with it. Rathaka is a small toy chariot. Dhanuka is just a small toy bow.

Idha kho pana voti ettha voti nipātamattaṃ, idha kho panāti attho. Iṅgha tvaṃ, upāli, saṅghe viharāhīti ettha iṅghāti codanatthe nipāto. Tena theraṃ saṅghamajjhe vihāratthāya codeti, nāssa araññavāsaṃ anujānāti. Kasmā? Araññasenāsane [P 5.0069] vasato kirassa vāsadhurameva pūrissati, na ganthadhuraṃ. Saṅghamajjhe vasanto pana dve dhurāni pūretvā arahattaṃ pāpuṇissati, vinayapiṭake ca pāmokkho bhavissati. Athassāhaṃ parisamajjhe pubbapatthanaṃ pubbābhinīhārañca kathetvā imaṃ bhikkhuṃ vinayadharānaṃ aggaṭṭhāne ṭhapessāmīti imamatthaṃ passamāno satthā therassa araññavāsaṃ nānujānīti. Dasamaṃ uttānatthamevāti.

In idha kho pana vo, vo is merely a particle; the meaning is idha kho pana (here, however). In iṅgha tvaṃ, upāli, saṅghe viharāhi (‘Come now, Upāli, dwell in the Saṅgha’), iṅgha is a particle with the meaning of urging. Thereby, he urges the elder to dwell in the midst of the Saṅgha; he does not permit him to live in the forest. Why? Because for one dwelling in a forest abode, it is said only the burden of practice will be fulfilled, not the burden of study. But dwelling in the midst of the Saṅgha, he will fulfill the two burdens, attain arahantship, and become foremost in the Vinaya Piṭaka. ‘Then, in the midst of the assembly, having spoken of his past aspiration and past resolve, I will place this monk in the foremost position among the upholders of the Vinaya’—seeing this significance, the Teacher did not permit the elder to live in the forest. The tenth is of obvious meaning.

Upālivaggo pañcamo.

The Upāli Chapter, the Fifth.

Dutiyapaṇṇāsakaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.

The Second Fifty is completed.

3. Tatiyapaṇṇāsakaṃ

3. The Third Fifty

(11) 1. Samaṇasaññāvaggo

(11) 1. The Chapter on the Perceptions of an Ascetic

1. Samaṇasaññāsuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Samaṇasaññā Sutta

101. Tatiyassa [M 3.0334][V 3.0331] paṭhame samaṇasaññāti samaṇānaṃ uppajjanakasaññā. Santatakārīti nirantarakārī. Abyāpajjhoti niddukkho. Idamatthaṃtissa hotīti idamatthaṃ ime paccayāti evamassa jīvitaparikkhāresu hoti, paccavekkhitaparibhogaṃ paribhuñjatīti attho. Dutiyaṃ uttānatthameva.

101. In the first sutta of the third paṇṇāsaka, ‘samaṇasaññā’ is the perception that arises for ascetics. ‘Santatakārī’ is one who acts without ceasing. ‘Abyāpajjho’ is one without suffering. As for ‘idamatthaṃtissa hoti,’ it means: ‘These requisites are for this purpose’—thus it is for him regarding the requisites of life; he uses them with reflection. This is the meaning. The second sutta has a clear meaning.

3. Micchattasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Explanation of the Sutta on Wrongness

103. Tatiye virādhanā hotīti saggato maggato ca virajjhanaṃ hoti. No ārādhanāti na sampādanā na paripūrakāritā hoti. Pahotīti pavattati.

103. In the third sutta, ‘virādhanā hoti’ means there is a falling away from heaven and from the path. ‘No ārādhanā’ means there is no accomplishment, no fulfillment. ‘Pahoti’ means it proceeds.

4-5. Bījasuttādivaṇṇanā

4–5. Explanation of the Bīja Sutta, etc.

104-105. Catutthe yathādiṭṭhi samattaṃ samādinnanti diṭṭhānurūpena paripuṇṇaṃ samādinnaṃ sakalaṃ gahitaṃ. Cetanāti tīsu dvāresu nibbattitacetanāva gahitā. Patthanāti “evarūpo siya”nti evaṃ patthanā. Paṇidhīti [P 5.0070] “devo vā bhavissāmi devaññataro vā”ti cittaṭṭhapanā. Saṅkhārāti sampayuttakasaṅkhārā. Pañcame purecārikaṭṭhena pubbaṅgamā. Anvadevāti taṃ anubandhamānameva.

In the fourth sutta, ‘yathādiṭṭhi samattaṃ samādinnaṃ’ (undertaken completely according to one's view) means that what has been fully undertaken in accordance with one's view is entirely grasped. By ‘cetanā’ (volition), the volition that arises in the three doors is taken. ‘Patthanā’ (longing) is the wish, “May I become such a one.” ‘Paṇidhi’ (aspiration) is the fixing of the mind with the thought, “I shall become a god or some other divine being.” ‘Saṅkhārā’ (formations) are the associated formations. In the fifth sutta, ‘pubbaṅgamā’ (preceding) is so in the sense of being a forerunner. ‘Anvadevā’ (following) means just following after it.

6. Nijjarasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Explanation of the Nijjarā Sutta

106. Chaṭṭhe [V 3.0332] nijjaravatthūnīti nijjarakāraṇāni. Micchādiṭṭhi nijjiṇṇā hotīti ayaṃ heṭṭhā vipassanāyapi nijjiṇṇā eva pahīnā. Kasmā puna gahitāti? Asamucchinnattā. Vipassanāya hi kiñcāpi nijjiṇṇā, na pana samucchinnā. Maggo pana uppajjitvā taṃ samucchindati, na puna vuṭṭhātuṃ deti. Tasmā puna gahitā. Evaṃ sabbapadesu yojetabbo. Ettha ca sammāvimuttipaccayā catusaṭṭhi dhammā bhāvanāpāripūriṃ gacchanti. Katame catusaṭṭhi? Sotāpattimaggakkhaṇe adhimokkhaṭṭhena [M 3.0335] saddhindriyaṃ paripūrati, paggahaṭṭhena vīriyindriyaṃ, upaṭṭhānaṭṭhena satindriyaṃ, avikkhepaṭṭhena samādhindriyaṃ, dassanaṭṭhena paññindriyaṃ paripūreti, vijānanaṭṭhena manindriyaṃ, abhinandanaṭṭhena somanassindriyaṃ, pavattasantatiādhipateyyaṭṭhena jīvitindriyaṃ paripūrati…pe… arahattaphalakkhaṇe adhimokkhaṭṭhena saddhindriyaṃ…pe… pavattasantatiādhipateyyaṭṭhena jīvitindriyaṃ paripūratīti evaṃ catūsu ca maggesu catūsu ca phalesu aṭṭhaṭṭha hutvā catusaṭṭhi dhammā pāripūriṃ gacchanti.

106. In the sixth sutta, ‘nijjaravatthūni’ means the causes of elimination. ‘Micchādiṭṭhi nijjiṇṇā hoti’ (wrong view is eliminated): this is abandoned even earlier by insight. Why then is it taken up again? Because it is not uprooted. For although it is eliminated by insight, it is not, however, uprooted. But the path arises and uproots it, not allowing it to rise again. Therefore it is taken up again. This method should be applied in all instances. Here, sixty-four qualities that are conditions for right liberation reach the perfection of development. What are the sixty-four? At the moment of the path of stream-entry, the faculty of faith is perfected in the sense of conviction, the faculty of energy in the sense of exertion, the faculty of mindfulness in the sense of establishing, the faculty of concentration in the sense of non-distraction, the faculty of wisdom in the sense of seeing, the faculty of mind in the sense of knowing, the faculty of joy in the sense of rejoicing, and the faculty of life in the sense of mastery over the continuity of the process… and so on… At the moment of the fruition of arahantship, the faculty of faith is perfected in the sense of conviction… and so on… the faculty of life is perfected in the sense of mastery over the continuity of the process. Thus, in the four paths and four fruits, there being eight in each, sixty-four qualities reach perfection.

7. Dhovanāsuttavaṇṇanā

7. Explanation of the Dhovana Sutta

107. Sattame [P 5.0071] dhovananti aṭṭhidhovanaṃ. Tasmiñhi janapade manussā ñātake mate na jhāpenti, āvāṭaṃ pana khaṇitvā bhūmiyaṃ nidahanti. Atha nesaṃ pūtibhūtānaṃ aṭṭhīni nīharitvā dhovitvā paṭipāṭiyā ussāpetvā gandhamālehi pūjetvā ṭhapenti. Nakkhatte patte tāni aṭṭhīni gahetvā rodanti paridevanti, tato nakkhattaṃ kīḷanti.

107. In the seventh sutta, ‘washing’ means the washing of bones. Indeed, in that country, when their relatives die, people do not cremate them; instead, they dig a pit and bury them in the ground. Then, after they have become putrid, they take out their bones, wash them, set them up in order, honor them with perfumes and garlands, and keep them. When a festival arrives, they take those bones and, weeping and lamenting, they then celebrate the festival.

8-10. Tikicchakasuttādivaṇṇanā

8–10. Explanation of the Physician Sutta, etc.

108-110. Aṭṭhame virecananti dosanīharaṇabhesajjaṃ. Virittā hotīti nīhaṭā hoti panuditā. Navame vamananti vamanakaraṇabhesajjaṃ. Dasame niddhamanīyāti niddhamitabbā. Niddhantāti niddhamitā.

In the eighth sutta, ‘virecana’ (purgation) means medicine for expelling ailments. ‘Virittā hoti’ (is purged) means it is expelled, driven away. In the ninth, ‘vamana’ (emesis) means medicine that causes vomiting. In the tenth, ‘niddhamanīyā’ (to be expelled) means they are things that should be expelled. ‘Niddhantā’ (expelled) means they have been expelled.

11. Paṭhamaasekhasuttavaṇṇanā

11. Explanation of the First Asekha Sutta

111. Ekādasame [V 3.0333] aṅgaparipūraṇatthaṃ sammādiṭṭhiyeva sammāñāṇanti vuttā. Evamete sabbepi arahattaphaladhammā asekhā, asekhassa pavattattā paccavekkhaṇañāṇampi asekhanti vuttaṃ.

111. In the eleventh sutta, for the purpose of completing the factors, right view itself is said to be right knowledge. Thus, all these are states of the fruit of Arahantship, states beyond training. Because they occur in one who is beyond training (asekha), the knowledge of reviewing is also said to be ‘beyond training’.

12. Dutiyaasekhasuttavaṇṇanā

12. Explanation of the Second Asekha Sutta

112. Dvādasame asekhiyāti asekhāyeva, asekhasantakā vā. Iminā suttena khīṇāsavova kathitoti.

112. In the twelfth sutta, ‘asekhiyā’ (pertaining to one beyond training) means they are indeed states beyond training, or they are the property of one beyond training. By this sutta, only the one whose taints are destroyed is spoken of.

Samaṇasaññāvaggo paṭhamo.

The Chapter on the Perceptions of an Ascetic is the first.

(12) 2. Paccorohaṇivaggo

(12) 2. The Chapter on Descending

1-2. Adhammasuttadvayavaṇṇanā

1–2. Explanation of the Two Suttas on the Non-Dhamma

113-114. Dutiyassa [M 3.0336] paṭhame pāṭiyekkaṃ pucchā ca vissajjanā ca katā. Dutiye ekatova.

In the first of the second vagga, the question and the answer are given separately. In the second, they are together.

3. Tatiyaadhammasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Explanation of the Third Adhamma Sutta

115. Tatiye uddesaṃ uddisitvāti mātikaṃ nikkhipitvā. Satthu [P 5.0072] ceva saṃvaṇṇitoti pañcasu ṭhānesu etadagge ṭhapentena satthārā saṃvaṇṇito. Sambhāvitoti guṇasambhāvanāya sambhāvito. Pahotīti sakkoti. Atisitvāti atikkamitvā. Jānaṃ jānātīti jānitabbakaṃ [V 3.0334] jānāti. Passaṃ passatīti passitabbakaṃ passati. Cakkhubhūtoti cakkhu viya bhūto jāto nibbatto. Ñāṇabhūtoti ñāṇasabhāvo. Dhammabhūtoti dhammasabhāvo. Brahmabhūtoti seṭṭhasabhāvo. Vattāti vattuṃ samattho. Pavattāti pavattetuṃ samattho. Atthassa ninnetāti atthaṃ nīharitvā dassetā. Yathā no bhagavāti yathā amhākaṃ bhagavā byākareyya.

115. In the third sutta, ‘having set forth the summary’ means having laid down the matrix. ‘Praised by the Teacher’ means praised by the Teacher who established him foremost in five instances. ‘Sambhāvito’ (esteemed) means esteemed through the manifestation of his qualities. ‘Pahoti’ (is capable) means he is able. ‘Atiyitvā’ (having surpassed) means having transcended. ‘Knowing, he knows’ means he knows what should be known. ‘Seeing, he sees’ means he sees what should be seen. ‘Cakkhubhūto’ (become vision) means become like an eye, born, produced. ‘Ñāṇabhūto’ (become knowledge) means having the nature of knowledge. ‘Dhammabhūto’ (become Dhamma) means having the nature of the scriptural Dhamma. ‘Brahmabhūto’ (become sublime) means having a sublime nature. ‘Vattā’ (a speaker) means capable of speaking. ‘Pavattā’ (a proclaimer) means capable of proclaiming. ‘Atthassa ninnetā’ (a revealer of meaning) means one who draws out and shows the meaning, that is, the Four Noble Truths. ‘Yathā no Bhagavā’ (as our Blessed One) means as our Blessed One would explain.

4. Ajitasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Explanation of the Ajita Sutta

116. Catutthe ajitoti evaṃnāmako. Cittaṭṭhānasatānīti cittuppādasatāni. Yehīti yehi cittaṭṭhānasatehi anuyuñjiyamānā. Upāraddhāva jānanti upāraddhasmāti viraddhā niggahitā evaṃ jānanti “viraddhā mayaṃ, niggahitā mayaṃ, āropito no doso”ti. Paṇḍitavatthūnīti paṇḍitabhāvatthāya kāraṇāni.

116. In the fourth sutta, ‘Ajita’ means one so named. ‘A hundred states of mind’ means a hundred arisings of consciousness. ‘By which’ means by which hundred arisings of consciousness being questioned. ‘Upāraddhāva jānanti upāraddhasmā’ means: having failed, being reproached, they know thus: “We have failed, we are reproached, fault has been placed upon us.” ‘Grounds for the wise’ means reasons for the sake of becoming wise.

5-6. Saṅgāravasuttādivaṇṇanā

5–6. Explanation of the Saṅgārava Sutta, etc.

117-118. Pañcame orimaṃ tīranti lokiyaṃ orimatīraṃ. Pārimaṃ tīranti lokuttaraṃ pārimatīraṃ. Pāragāminoti nibbānagāmino. Tīramevānudhāvatīti sakkāyadiṭṭhitīraṃyeva anudhāvati. Dhamme [P 5.0073] dhammānuvattinoti sammā akkhāte navavidhe lokuttaradhamme anudhammavattino, tassa dhammassānucchavikāya sahasīlāya pubbabhāgapaṭipattiyā pavattamānā. Maccudheyyaṃ [M 3.0337] suduttaranti maccuno ṭhānabhūtaṃ tebhūmakavaṭṭaṃ suduttaraṃ taritvā. Pāramessantīti nibbānaṃ pāpuṇissanti.

In the fifth sutta, 'the near shore' means the worldly is the near shore. 'The far shore' means the supramundane is the far shore. 'Gone to the far shore' means gone to Nibbāna. 'He runs along the shore' means he runs along only the shore of identity-view. 'Dhamma-followers in the Dhamma' means those who act in accordance with the Dhamma in the well-proclaimed, ninefold supramundane Dhamma, being engaged in the preliminary practice together with virtue that is suitable to that Dhamma. 'The realm of Death, very hard to cross' means having crossed the round of existence in the three planes, which is the domain of Death and is very hard to cross. 'They will go to the far shore' means they will attain Nibbāna.

Okā anokamāgammāti vaṭṭato vivaṭṭaṃ āgamma. Viveke yattha dūramanti yasmiṃ kāyacittaupadhiviveke durabhiramaṃ, tatrābhiratimiccheyya. Hitvā kāmeti duvidhepi kāme pahāya. Akiñcanoti nippalibodho. Ādānapaṭinissageti gahaṇapaṭinissaggasaṅkhāte nibbāne. Anupādāya ye ratāti catūhi upādānehi kiñcipi anupādiyitvā ye abhiratā. Parinibbutāti te apaccayaparinibbānena parinibbutā nāmāti veditabbā. Chaṭṭhaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ desitaṃ.

'Having gone from home to homelessness' means having come from the round of existence to that which is free from the round. 'In seclusion, where it is hard to delight' means in the seclusion of body, mind, and substrata, in which it is hard to delight; there one should wish for delight. 'Having abandoned sensual pleasures' means having abandoned both kinds of sensual pleasures. 'Possessing nothing' means being without impediment. 'In the relinquishing of grasping' means in Nibbāna, which is called the relinquishing of grasping. 'Those who delight without clinging' means those who delight, not clinging to anything by means of the four kinds of clinging. 'Fully extinguished' means they are to be known as fully extinguished by the unconditioned parinibbāna. The sixth sutta was taught to the monks.

7-8. Paccorohaṇīsuttadvayavaṇṇanā

7–8. Commentary on the Two Paccorohaṇī Suttas

119-120. Sattame [V 3.0335] paccorohaṇīti pāpassa paccorohaṇaṃ. Pattharitvāti santharitvā. Antarā ca velaṃ antarā ca agyāgāranti vālikārāsissa ca aggiagārassa ca antare. Aṭṭhamaṃ bhikkhusaṅghassa desitaṃ. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānatthamevāti.

In the seventh sutta, 'paccorohaṇī' means the descending from evil. 'Pattharitvā' means 'having spread out.' 'Between the shore and between the fire-house' means between the heap of sand and the fire-house. The eighth sutta was taught to the community of monks. The rest has a clear meaning everywhere.

Paccorohaṇivaggo dutiyo.

The Paccorohaṇī Chapter, the Second

(13) 3. Parisuddhavaggavaṇṇanā

13. 3. Commentary on the Parisuddha Chapter

123. Tatiyassa [P 5.0074] paṭhame parisuddhāti nimmalā. Pariyodātāti pabhassarā. Dutiyādīni uttānatthānevāti.

123. In the first sutta of the third chapter, 'parisuddha' means stainless. 'Pariyodāta' means luminous. The second and subsequent suttas are clear in meaning.

Parisuddhavaggo tatiyo.

The Parisuddha Chapter, the Third

(14) 4. Sādhuvaggavaṇṇanā

14. 4. Commentary on the Sādhu Chapter

134. Catutthassa paṭhame sādhunti bhaddakaṃ siliṭṭhakaṃ. Dutiyādīni uttānatthānevāti. Ariyamaggavaggo uttānatthoyevāti.

134. In the first sutta of the fourth chapter, 'sādhu' means good, auspicious, and well-joined. The second and subsequent suttas are clear in meaning. The Ariyamagga Chapter is indeed clear in meaning.

Sādhuvaggo catuttho.

The Sādhu Chapter, the Fourth

Tatiyapaṇṇāsakaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.

The Third Fifty is concluded.

4. Catutthapaṇṇāsakaṃ

4. The Fourth Fifty

155. Catutthassa [V 3.0336][M 3.0338] paṭhamādīni uttānatthānevāti.

155. The first and subsequent suttas of the fourth chapter are clear in meaning.

8. Kammanidānasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Kammanidāna Sutta

174. Aṭṭhame lobhahetukampīti pāṇātipātassa lobho upanissayakoṭiyā hetu hoti dosamohasampayuttopi. Iminā upāyena sabbattha attho veditabbo.

174. In the eighth sutta, 'lobhahetukaṃ' means that greed is a decisive support condition for the destruction of life, even when associated with hatred and delusion. The meaning should be understood in this way throughout.

9. Parikkamanasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Parikkamana Sutta

175. Navame parikkamanaṃ hotīti parivajjanaṃ hoti.

175. In the ninth sutta, 'parikkamanaṃ hoti' means 'avoidance occurs.'

10. Cundasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Cunda Sutta

176. Dasame kammāraputtassāti suvaṇṇakāraputtassa. Kassa no tvanti kassa nu tvaṃ. Pacchābhūmakāti pacchābhūmivāsikā. Kamaṇḍalukāti kamaṇḍaludhārino. Sevālamālikāti sevālamālā viya dhārenti. Sevālapaṭanivāsitātipi vuttameva. Udakorohakāti sāyatatiyakaṃ udakorohanānuyogamanuyuttā. Āmaseyyāsīti hatthena parimajjeyyāsi.

176. In the tenth sutta, 'kammāraputtassa' means 'of the goldsmith’s son.' 'Kassa no tvaṃ' means 'Whose are you?' 'Pacchābhūmakā' refers to those who live in the western lands. 'Kamaṇḍalukā' refers to those who carry water pots. 'Sevālamālikā' means 'they wear garlands of algae.' It is also said they are 'clothed in algae mats.' 'Udakorohakā' refers to those devoted to the practice of descending into water three times a day, including at evening. 'Āmaseyyāsi' means 'you would rub me with your hand.'

11. Jāṇussoṇisuttavaṇṇanā

11. Commentary on the Jāṇussoṇi Sutta

177. Ekādasame upakappatūti pāpuṇātu. Ṭhāneti okāse. No aṭṭhāneti no anokāse. Nerayikānaṃ [P 5.0075] āhāro nāma tattha nibbattanakammameva. Teneva hi te tattha yāpenti. Tiracchānayonikānaṃ pana tiṇapaṇṇādivasena āhāro veditabbo. Manussānaṃ odanakummāsādivasena[V 3.0337], devānaṃ sudhābhojanādivasena, pettivesayikānaṃ kheḷasiṅghāṇikādivasena. Yaṃ vā panassa ito anuppavecchantīti yaṃ tassa mittādayo ito dadantā anupavesenti. Pettivesayikā eva hi paradattūpajīvino honti, na aññesaṃ parehi dinnaṃ [M 3.0339] upakappati. Dāyakopi anipphaloti yaṃ sandhāya taṃ dānaṃ dinnaṃ, tassa upakappatu vā mā vā, dāyakena pana na sakkā nipphalena bhavituṃ, dāyako tassa dānassa vipākaṃ labhatiyeva.

177. In the eleventh sutta, 'upakappatu' means 'may it reach.' 'Ṭhāne' means 'in a suitable place.' 'No aṭṭhāne' means 'not in an unsuitable place.' For beings in hell, their food is merely the kamma that brought them to rebirth there; indeed, they subsist by that alone. For animals, food should be understood as grass, leaves, and so forth. For humans, it is rice, porridge, and the like. For devas, it is ambrosial food and such. For spirits in the realm of the departed, it is spittle, mucus, and so on. The phrase 'or what others send to them from here' refers to what their friends and others give from this world and convey to them. Indeed, only spirits in the realm of the departed live on what is given by others; what is given by others is not of use to those in other realms. The phrase 'and the donor is not without fruit' means that whether or not the gift given for a particular departed one benefits them, it is not possible for the donor to be without fruit; the donor certainly receives the result of that gift.

Aṭṭhānepi bhavaṃ gotamo parikappaṃ vadatīti anokāse uppannepi tasmiṃ ñātake bhavaṃ gotamo dānassa phalaṃ parikappetiyeva paññāpetiyevāti pucchati. Brāhmaṇassa hi “evaṃ dinnassa dānassa phalaṃ dāyako na labhatī”ti laddhi. Athassa bhagavā pañhaṃ paṭijānitvā “dāyako nāma yattha katthaci puññaphalūpajīviṭṭhāne nibbatto dānassa phalaṃ labhatiyevā”ti dassetuṃ idha brāhmaṇātiādimāha. So tattha lābhī hotīti so tattha hatthiyoniyaṃ nibbattopi maṅgalahatthiṭṭhānaṃ patvā lābhī hoti. Assādīsupi eseva nayo. Sādhuvaggo uttānatthoyevāti.

The question 'Even in an inappropriate situation, does the Venerable Gotama speak of the result of giving?' means: 'Even when that relative is reborn in an unsuitable place, does the Venerable Gotama still explain and declare the fruit of giving?' For the brahmin held the view that 'the giver does not receive the fruit of such a gift.' Then the Blessed One, having acknowledged his question, said, 'The giver, wherever reborn in a place sustained by the fruit of merit, indeed receives the fruit of the gift,' and to demonstrate this, he begins here with 'brahmin' and so on. The phrase 'he becomes a gainer there' means that even if reborn in the state of an elephant, having attained the position of a ceremonial elephant, he becomes a gainer. The same principle applies to horses and so on. The Sādhu Chapter is indeed clear in meaning.

Jāṇussoṇivaggo dutiyo.

The Jāṇussoṇi Chapter, the Second

Catutthapaṇṇāsakaṃ niṭṭhitaṃ.

The Fourth Fifty is concluded.

(21) 1. Karajakāyavaggo

21. 1. The Karajakāya Chapter

211. Pañcamassa [M 3.0340] paṭhamādīni uttānatthāneva.

211. The first and subsequent suttas of the fifth paṇṇāsaka are clear in meaning.

6. Saṃsappanīyasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Saṃsappanīya Sutta

216. Chaṭṭhe saṃsappanīyapariyāyaṃ vo, bhikkhave, dhammapariyāyanti saṃsappanassa kāraṇaṃ desanāsaṅkhātaṃ dhammadesanaṃ. Saṃsappatīti taṃ kammaṃ karonto āsappati parisappati vipphandati. Jimhā [P 5.0076] gatīti tena kammena yaṃ gatiṃ gamissati, sā jimhā hoti. Jimhupapattīti tassa yaṃ [V 3.0338] gatiṃ upapajjissati, sāpi jimhāva hoti. Saṃsappajātikāti saṃsappanasabhāvā. Bhūtā bhūtassa upapatti hotīti bhūtasmā sabhāvato vijjamānakammā sattassa nibbatti hoti. Phassā phusantīti vipākaphassā phusanti.

216. In the sixth sutta, 'the discourse on creeping, bhikkhus, is a discourse on the Dhamma' means a teaching of the Dhamma, which is a teaching on the cause of creeping. 'Creeping' means that one who performs that kamma creeps, crawls, and writhes. 'Crooked going' means that whatever destination one will go to by that kamma, that is crooked. 'Crooked rebirth' means that whatever destination one will be reborn into, that too is crooked. 'Of the nature of creeping' means having the nature of creeping. 'From what has come to be, there is the arising of what has come to be' means that from an existing nature, from existing kamma, the arising of a being occurs. 'Contacts touch' means resultant contacts touch.

7-8. Sañcetanikasuttadvayavaṇṇanā

7-8. Commentary on the Two Sañcetanika Suttas

217-218. Sattame sañcetanikānanti cetetvā pakappetvā katānaṃ. Upacitānanti citānaṃ vaḍḍhitānaṃ. Appaṭisaṃveditvāti tesaṃ kammānaṃ vipākaṃ avediyitvā. Byantībhāvanti vigatantabhāvaṃ tesaṃ kammānaṃ paricchedaparivaṭumatākaraṇaṃ. Tañca kho diṭṭheva dhammeti tañca kho vipākaṃ diṭṭhadhammavedanīyaṃ diṭṭheva dhamme. Upapajjanti upapajjavedanīyaṃ anantare attabhāve. Apare vā pariyāyeti aparapariyāyavedanīyaṃ pana saṃsārappavatte sati sahassimepi attabhāveti. Iminā idaṃ dasseti “saṃsārappavatte paṭiladdhavipākārahakamme na vijjati so jagatippadeso, yattha ṭhito mucceyya pāpakammā”ti. Tividhāti [P 5.0077] tippakārā. Kāyakammantasandosabyāpattīti kāyakammantasaṅkhātā vipatti. Iminā nayena sabbapadāni veditabbāni. Aṭṭhame apaṇṇako maṇīti samantato caturasso pāsako.

In the seventh [sutta], 'of intentional [kammas]' (sañcetanikānaṃ) means of those done after intending and planning. 'Of accumulated [kammas]' (upacitānaṃ) means of those that have been gathered and increased. 'Without having experienced' (appaṭisaṃveditvā) means without having experienced the result of those kammas. 'The making of an end' (byantībhāvaṃ) means the state of being pacified, the making of a limit or an end for those kammas. 'And that, indeed, in this very life' (tañca kho diṭṭheva dhamme) means that the result, being kamma to be experienced in the present life, is in this very life. 'In the next life' (upapajja) refers to kamma to be experienced in the immediately following existence. 'Or on a subsequent occasion' (apare vā pariyāye) refers to kamma to be experienced in a future life, while the round of saṃsāra continues, even in the thousandth existence. By this, this is shown: 'In the round of saṃsāra, there is no place on earth where one, standing, might be freed from evil kamma that has obtained its turn to give result.' 'Threefold' (tividhā) means of three kinds. 'Faulty transgression of bodily action' (kāyakammantasandosabyāpatti) means the failure designated as bodily action. By this method, all terms should be understood. In the eighth [sutta], 'an unfailing gem' (apaṇṇako maṇī) means a die that is square on all sides.

9. Karajakāyasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Karajakāya Sutta

219. Navame dukkhassāti vipākadukkhassa, vaṭṭadukkhasseva vā. Imasmiṃ sutte maṇiopammaṃ natthi. Evaṃ vigatābhijjhoti evanti nipātamattaṃ. Yathā vā mettaṃ [M 3.0341] bhāventā vigatābhijjhā bhavanti, evaṃ vigatābhijjho. Evamassa vigatābhijjhatādīhi nīvaraṇavikkhambhanaṃ dassetvā idāni akusalanissaraṇāni kathento mettāsahagatenātiādimāha. Appamāṇanti appamāṇasattārammaṇatāya ciṇṇavasitāya vā appamāṇaṃ. Pamāṇakataṃ kammaṃ nāma kāmāvacarakammaṃ. Na taṃ tatrāvatiṭṭhatīti taṃ mahogho parittaṃ udakaṃ viya attano okāsaṃ gahetvā ṭhātuṃ na sakkoti, atha kho naṃ oghe parittaṃ udakaṃ viya idameva appamāṇaṃ kammaṃ ajjhottharitvā attano vipākaṃ nibbatteti. Daharataggeti daharakālato paṭṭhāya.

219. In the ninth [sutta], 'of suffering' (dukkhassa) refers to the suffering of result, or of the suffering of the round of existence itself. In this sutta, there is no simile of a gem. In 'Thus, with covetousness departed' (evaṃ vigatābhijjho), 'evaṃ' is merely a particle. Or, just as those who develop loving-kindness become free from covetousness, so too, one is free from covetousness. Thus, having shown the suppression of the hindrances by means of being free from covetousness and so on, now, wishing to speak of the escapes from the unwholesome, the Teacher said 'accompanied by loving-kindness', and so on. 'Boundless' (appamāṇaṃ) is so called either because it has limitless beings as its object, or because of practiced mastery. What is called 'limited kamma' (pamāṇakataṃ kammaṃ) is kamma of the sense-sphere. 'It does not remain there' (na taṃ tatrāvatiṭṭhati) means that [limited kamma], like a small amount of water in a great flood, is unable to get an opportunity to establish itself; rather, this immeasurable kamma overwhelms it and produces its own result. 'From a young age' (daharatagge) means from the time of being young.

Nāyaṃ kāyo ādāyagamaniyoti imaṃ kāyaṃ gahetvā paralokaṃ gantuṃ nāma na sakkāti attho[V 3.0339]. Cittantaroti cittakāraṇo, atha vā citteneva antariko. Ekasseva hi cuticittassa anantarā dutiye paṭisandhicitte devo nāma hoti, nerayiko nāma [P 5.0078] hoti, tiracchānagato nāma hoti. Purimanayepi cittena kāraṇabhūtena devo nerayiko vā hotīti attho. Sabbaṃ taṃ idha vedanīyanti diṭṭhadhammavedanīyakoṭṭhāsavanetaṃ vuttaṃ. Na taṃ anugaṃ bhavissatīti mettāya upapajjavedanīyabhāvassa upacchinnattā upapajjavedanīyavasena na anugataṃ bhavissati. Idaṃ sotāpannasakadāgāmiariyapuggalānaṃ paccavekkhaṇaṃ veditabbaṃ. Anāgāmitāyāti jhānānāgāmitāya. Idhapaññassāti imasmiṃ sāsane paññā idhapaññā nāma, sāsanacaritāya ariyapaññāya ṭhitassa ariyasāvakassāti attho. Uttarivimuttinti arahattaṃ. Dasamaṃ uttānatthamevāti.

'This body is not to be taken along' (nāyaṃ kāyo ādāyagamaniyo) means that it is not possible to take this body and go to the next world. 'With mind as the intermediate' (cittantaro) means it has mind as its cause, or it is immediately preceded only by mind. For indeed, immediately after the single death-consciousness, in the second, the rebirth-consciousness, one becomes what is called a deva, one becomes what is called a hell-being, one is called an animal. In the previous method also, the meaning is that one becomes a deva or a hell-being by means of the mind which has become the cause. 'All that is to be experienced here' (sabbaṃ taṃ idha vedanīyaṃ) was spoken by way of the category of kamma to be experienced in the present life. 'It will not follow' (na taṃ anugaṃ bhavissati) means that because the state of being kamma to be experienced in the next life has been cut off by loving-kindness, it will not follow by way of kamma to be experienced in the next life. This should be known as the reflection of the noble persons who are stream-enterers and once-returners. 'For non-returning' (anāgāmitāya) means for the sake of non-returning by means of jhāna. 'Of one with wisdom here' (idhapaññassa) means of a noble disciple who is established in the noble wisdom that fares in this Dispensation; wisdom in this Dispensation is called 'wisdom here'. 'Further liberation' (uttarivimuttiṃ) means arahantship. The tenth [sutta] has only a clear meaning.

Karajakāyavaggo paṭhamo.

The Chapter on the Body Produced by Kamma is the first.

(22) 2. Sāmaññavaggavaṇṇanā

(22) 2. Commentary on the Chapter on the Ascetic

221. Dutiyassa paṭhamaṃ ādiṃ katvā sabbā peyyālatanti uttānatthāyevāti.

221. Beginning with the first [sutta] of the second [chapter], all the series of repetitions are indeed of clear meaning.

Manorathapūraṇiyā aṅguttaranikāya-aṭṭhakathāya

In the Manorathapūraṇī, the Commentary to the Aṅguttara Nikāya,

Dasakanipātassa saṃvaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Book of the Tens is completed.

. Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa.

Homage to the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One.

Aṅguttaranikāye

In the Aṅguttara Nikāya

Ekādasakanipāta-aṭṭhakathā

Commentary on the Book of the Elevens

1. Nissayavaggo

1. The Chapter on Reliance

1-6. Kimatthiyasuttādivaṇṇanā

1–6. Commentary on the Kimatthiya Sutta and Others

1-6. Ekādasakanipātassa [M 3.0343][V 3.0341][P 5.0079] paṭhamādīni heṭṭhā vuttanayāneva. Kevalañcettha ādito pañcasu nibbidāvirāgaṃ dvidhā bhinditvā ekādasaṅgāni katāni. Chaṭṭhe sikkhāpaccakkhānaṃ adhikaṃ.

1–6. The first and subsequent [suttas] of the Book of the Elevens are of the same method as that already explained in the preceding [commentary]. Indeed, here only, from the beginning in the five [suttas], disenchantment and dispassion are divided into two, making eleven factors. In the sixth, the renunciation of the training is additional.

7-8. Paṭhamasaññāsuttādivaṇṇanā

7–8. Commentary on the First Perception Sutta and Others

7-8. Sattame atthena atthoti atthena saddhimattho. Byañjanena byañjananti byañjanena saddhiṃ [V 3.0342] byañjanaṃ. Saṃsandissatīti sampavattissati. Samessatīti samānaṃ bhavissati. Na viggayhissatīti na virajjhissati. Aggapadasminti nibbāne. Aṭṭhame paccavekkhaṇā kathitā.

7–8. In the seventh [sutta], 'meaning with meaning' means meaning together with meaning. 'Phrase with phrase' means phrase together with phrase. 'Will blend' means will proceed together. 'Will agree' means will be similar. 'Will not conflict' means will not be opposed. 'In the foremost state' means in Nibbāna. In the eighth, reflection is taught.

9. Saddhasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Sutta on Faith

9. Navame doṇiyā baddhoti yavasassadoṇiyā samīpe baddho. Antaraṃ karitvāti abbhantare katvā. Jhāyatīti cinteti. Pajjhāyatīti ito cito ca nānappakārakaṃ jhāyati. Nijjhāyatīti nirantaravasena nibaddhaṃ jhāyati. Pathavimpi nissāya jhāyatīti samāpattiyaṃ sanikantikavasenetaṃ [P 5.0080] vuttaṃ. Samāpattiyañhi sanikantikattā esa khaḷuṅko nāma kato. Āpādīsupi eseva nayo.

9. In the ninth [sutta], 'bound to a trough' means bound near a trough of barley. 'Having made an interval' means having placed it inside. 'He contemplates' means he thinks. 'He contemplates deeply' means he contemplates in various ways, from here and there. 'He scrutinizes' means he contemplates continuously and without interruption. 'He contemplates relying even on earth'—this is said by way of having craving in attainment. Indeed, because of having craving in attainment, this one is called a 'stake'. In the case of water and so on, this is the same method.

Kathañca saddha ājānīyajhāyitaṃ hotīti kathaṃ kāraṇākāraṇaṃ jānantassa sindhavassa jhāyitaṃ hoti. Yathā iṇantiādīsu iṇasadisaṃ bandhanasadisaṃ dhanajānisadisaṃ kalisaṅkhātamahāparādhasadisañca katvā attano abhimukhassa patodassa ajjhoharaṇasaṅkhātaṃ patanaṃ vipassatīti attho. Neva pathaviṃ nissāya jhāyatīti samāpattisukhanikantiyā abhāvena pathaviārammaṇāya [M 3.0344] catukkapañcakajjhānasaññāya na jhāyati, niyantiyā abhāveneva so ājānīyo nāma hotīti. Jhāyati ca panāti nibbānārammaṇāya phalasamāpattiyā jhāyati.
